# World History Flashcards

> One flashcard per examinable item in the repository World History owners (basic + advanced), plus a concept card per owner file. IDs: WH-1001 onward by agent block.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia and other pages that loaded while building; confirmed items carry a ✅ source tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the cards. Time-sensitive facts carry as-of dates from the owner.

---

## WORLD HISTORY — Part 1: Enlightenment, Revolutions, Industrial Revolution, Vienna Settlement and Unifications

### GROUP — Enlightenment and Age of Revolutions (owner 01)

### Flashcard WH-1001 — Enlightenment as the intellectual climate of the Age of Revolutions
**Front:** What was the Enlightenment, and why is it not itself a revolution?
**Definition (learn):** A plural 18th-century European intellectual movement centred on reason, nature, humanity, freedom and happiness; it was the climate that made late-18th-century political revolutions thinkable and defensible.
**Breakdown:**
- Labels: "Age of Reason" / "Age of Enlightenment" for the 18th century (✅ owner 01, Old NCERT).
- Core themes: reason, secular inquiry, natural rights, liberty, representative government, criticism of privilege by birth, Church authority and autocracy (✅ owner 01; ✅ Britannica via owner).
- Slogans that gained force: liberty, equality, fraternity; alongside them freedom, democracy and nationalism (✅ owner 01).
- Not one organised movement with one programme (⚠️ owner 01 §8.9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Scientific advance and print culture in the 17th–18th centuries (✅ owner 01, Britannica). Wikipedia lead: a "period of intellectual and cultural flourishing in Europe", beginning in the late 17th century, stressing reason, empirical evidence, liberty, tolerance, progress and natural rights (✅ Wikipedia "The Enlightenment").
**Links:** WH-1002 (ideas-to-revolution chain), WH-1008 (ideas + crisis + mobilisation), WH-1015 (traps), WH-1033 (worldwide grammar of politics).
**Trap:** Enlightenment is NOT only anti-religion, and it did not automatically create democracy everywhere; it opened ideological space, while actual change depended on social conflict and state crisis (✅ owner 01 §5).
**Mains hook:** "The Enlightenment supplied the language, not the entire cause, of the Age of Revolutions." Discuss. Qualified line: ideas gave legitimacy; crisis gave momentum; mobilised groups gave force.
**Back:** *"Reason questioned, rights claimed, privilege exposed — a climate, not a coup."*

### Flashcard WH-1002 — Ideas-to-revolution chain (causal ladder)
**Front:** Trace the chain from Enlightenment science to American and French revolutions.
**Definition (learn):** The owner's causal sequence linking intellectual change to political rupture.
**Breakdown:**
- Scientific advance + print culture → reason questions Church, absolutism and hereditary privilege → natural rights + consent + sovereignty of the people → American Revolution (colonial self-government) + French Revolution (destruction of feudal privilege) → nationalism, constitutionalism and rights-based politics spread outward (✅ owner 01 §4).
- ⚠️ Ideas alone did not produce revolution; they gave language, legitimacy and programme to groups already angered by fiscal, social and political crises (✅ owner 01 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ladder of markers: 1689 Locke → 1748 Montesquieu → 1762 Rousseau → 1776 American Declaration → 1789 French Revolution begins (✅ owner 01 §1).
**Links:** WH-1001, WH-1008, WH-1009, WH-1011, WH-1015 to WH-1017 (doctrines).
**Trap:** Do not present the chain as deterministic; the arrows are necessary but not sufficient.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show transmission from thought to institution in a 10-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Science → reason → rights → two revolutions → nations."*

### Flashcard WH-1003 — Chronology ladder of Enlightenment markers
**Front:** What are the dated intellectual and political markers of the Enlightenment-to-revolution phase?
**Definition (learn):** Five dated milestones that anchor the period; each is a distinct marker, not one event.
**Breakdown:**
- 1689 — Locke, *Two Treatises of Government* (natural rights, limited government).
- 1748 — Montesquieu, *Spirit of Laws* (separation of powers).
- 1762 — Rousseau, *Social Contract* (popular sovereignty).
- 1776 — American Declaration of Independence (4 July 1776).
- 1789 — French Revolution begins.
- ⚠️ c. 1776–1848 — "Age of Revolutions" periodisation label, not a single event (✅ owner 01 §1; ⚠️ standard label).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Locke 1689 — Wikipedia main text gives 1689; its sidebar gives 1690 (✅ Wikipedia "The Enlightenment"; ⚠️ owner-vs-source minor conflict on sidebar, use 1689). Montesquieu 1748 and Rousseau 1762 confirmed (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1001, WH-1002, WH-1004, WH-1005, WH-1034.
**Trap:** Do not give publication dates other than these; ❌ invented later dates for Locke's or Rousseau's other works.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show that thought preceded rupture by nearly nine decades for Locke-to-1776, but only about three decades for Rousseau-to-1789.
**Back:** *"Locke eighty-nine, Montesquieu forty-eight, Rousseau sixty-two, then seventy-six and eighty-nine."*

### Flashcard WH-1004 — John Locke (natural rights, consent, limited government)
**Front:** Who was John Locke and what is his core contribution to the Enlightenment?
**Definition (learn):** English political philosopher whose *Two Treatises of Government* (1689) formulated natural rights, limited government and the consent of the governed.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural rights (life, liberty, property) and limited government (✅ owner 01 §3, §8.4).
- Consent of the governed: a government that violates rights may be lawfully resisted (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4 interpretive payoff).
- Institutional payoff: rebellion against a rights-violating government becomes lawful, not merely successful (⚠️ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *Two Treatises of Government*, 1689 (✅ Wikipedia; sidebar lists 1690). Anchor for the American Revolution (✅ owner 01 §3).
**Links:** WH-1001, WH-1003, WH-1016 (liberalism), WH-1002.
**Trap:** Locke's political context was 17th-century English, not a programme for mass democracy (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
**Mains hook:** Locke as the best anchor for consent-based rule and the American Declaration's rights language; qualify with the property limits of his thought.
**Back:** *"Locke: life, liberty, property — and rebellion when consent breaks."*

### Flashcard WH-1005 — Charles de Montesquieu (separation of powers)
**Front:** What does Montesquieu contribute to modern constitutionalism?
**Definition (learn):** French thinker whose *Spirit of Laws* (1748) gave the principle of separation of powers and constitutional balance its enduring form.
**Breakdown:**
- Separation of powers among branches (✅ owner 01 §3).
- ⚠️ He argued for balance among social orders as well as among branches (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
- Design principle later used in written constitutions (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *The Spirit of the Laws*, 1748 (✅ Wikipedia "The Enlightenment"; ✅ owner 01 §1).
**Links:** WH-1003, WH-1011, WH-1051 (U.S. Constitution, owner 02).
**Trap:** Do not reduce Montesquieu to "three branches"; his concern was checks on arbitrariness through the structure of power.
**Mains hook:** The best bridge from Enlightenment to modern institutional design; contrast with Rousseau's popular sovereignty.
**Back:** *"Montesquieu: divide power, check tyranny."*

### Flashcard WH-1006 — Jean-Jacques Rousseau (popular sovereignty and general will)
**Front:** What is Rousseau's core claim in *The Social Contract* (1762)?
**Definition (learn):** Rousseau argued that legitimate authority rests in popular sovereignty expressed through the general will.
**Breakdown:**
- Popular sovereignty; the social contract; the general will (✅ owner 01 §8.4).
- Makes "the people" the source of legitimate authority (⚠️ owner 01).
- ⚠️ The general will can justify coercion of dissenters, a point the Terror later illustrated (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *The Social Contract*, 1762 (✅ Wikipedia "The Enlightenment"). Owner paraphrases the opening line "Man is born free..." — paraphrase only, do not quote (✅ owner 01 §8.9).
**Links:** WH-1001, WH-1003, WH-1008, WH-1016 (liberalism) and WH-1018 (nationalism; the nation as a claim of the people).
**Trap:** Rousseau is not a liberal in the Lockean sense; popular sovereignty and individual rights can pull apart (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** "Popular sovereignty turned from a book into a mass political claim; but the general will could justify terror." Use both halves.
**Back:** *"Rousseau: the people's general will is the source — and its danger."*

### Flashcard WH-1007 — Voltaire (reason over clerical dogma)
**Front:** What is Voltaire's contribution and limit as an Enlightenment figure?
**Definition (learn):** French writer whose critique of clerical authority and dogma built a secular public sphere and civil liberty.
**Breakdown:**
- Critique of clerical authority; reason over dogma (✅ owner 01 §3).
- Opens space for a secular public sphere and civil liberty (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
- Works: *Letters on the English* (1733) and *Philosophical Dictionary* (1764) (✅ Wikipedia "The Enlightenment").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1733 and 1764 dates confirmed by Wikipedia. The Wikipedia article gives no date for the anti-clerical stance itself.
**Links:** WH-1001, WH-1015 (traps), WH-1009.
**Trap:** Anti-clericalism is not democracy; Voltaire was not a democrat (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
**Mains hook:** Use Voltaire for "civil liberty and secular debate" strands; do not stretch him to universal franchise.
**Back:** *"Voltaire: attack dogma, defend civil liberty — not universal suffrage."*

### Flashcard WH-1008 — Ideas + crisis + mobilisation (the causal formula)
**Front:** What is the owner's causal formula for the Age of Revolutions?
**Definition (learn):** Ideas provide legitimacy, crises provide momentum, and mobilised groups provide force (⚠️ owner 01 §2 synthesis).
**Breakdown:**
- Ideational view: ideas made old regimes illegitimate (⚠️ owner 01 §2).
- Social-economic view: fiscal crisis, class resentment, colonial grievance, food stress (⚠️ owner 01 §2).
- State-crisis view: weak monarchies or empires failed to absorb reform pressure (⚠️ owner 01 §2).
- Best balanced answer: ideas, grievance and political opportunity interacted (⚠️ owner 01 §2).
- Old NCERT: discontent alone is not enough; revolutionary thinking and ideals must precede revolutionary action (✅ owner 01 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No numeric data; the formula is an analytic synthesis.
**Links:** WH-1001, WH-1009, WH-1002, WH-1034.
**Trap:** Do not present one view as the sole cause; the highest-scoring answers synthesise.
**Mains hook:** "The Enlightenment supplied the language, not the entire cause." Use the formula to weigh ideas against material crisis.
**Back:** *"Ideas legitimise, crises ignite, groups carry it."*

### Flashcard WH-1009 — Ideas vs interests: form, timing, depth
**Front:** What is the examinable resolution of the "ideas versus material interests" debate?
**Definition (learn):** Form from ideas, timing from crisis, depth from social conflict (⚠️ owner 01 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Ideas were decisive: ✅ Old NCERT links new thought to overthrowing autocracy and abolishing privilege; ✅ both the American and French declarations are rights documents. Counter: ideas circulated for decades; enlightened absolutists adopted reform language without revolution (⚠️ owner 01 §8.5).
- Material/fiscal crisis was decisive: ✅ British war debt after 1763 produced colonial taxation dispute (basic/02); ✅ French monarchy toward bankruptcy and bread crisis (basic/03). Counter: fiscal crises had occurred before without revolution (⚠️ owner 01).
- Social conflict was decisive: ✅ bourgeois wealth without status; ✅ peasant burden of dues and taxes; ✅ artisan and urban unrest. Counter: groups wanted incompatible things (⚠️ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No numbers; cross-owner dates 1763 and bread crisis per basic/02 and basic/03.
**Links:** WH-1008, WH-1011, WH-1012.
**Trap:** Do not choose one side without evasion; the answer must name all three roles.
**Mains hook:** 20-mark debate: "Ideas supplied the language, not the cause." Discuss; answer with form-timing-depth.
**Back:** *"Ideas gave the form, crisis the clock, conflict the depth."*

### Flashcard WH-1010 — Transmission channels of Enlightenment ideas
**Front:** Through which channels did Enlightenment ideas travel into politics?
**Definition (learn):** Four routes by which ideas left elite circles and became political slogans.
**Breakdown:**
- Print and pamphlets: revolutionary writing as politically catalytic (✅ owner 01 adv §3, Old NCERT).
- Atlantic example: the American Revolution showed rights-language could found a republic; France and Latin America watched the precedent (✅ owner 01 adv §3).
- Public debate: reason, inquiry and secular argument forced authority to justify itself publicly (✅ Britannica via owner).
- War and diplomacy: soldiers, diplomats and reformers carried examples across borders (⚠️ owner 01 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numerical.
**Links:** WH-1011, WH-1033, WH-1028.
**Trap:** Transmission explains why revolutions became transnational, not local; do not claim direct straight-line influence on every later movement.
**Mains hook:** Show how print and precedent turned a climate into a transnational wave.
**Back:** *"Print, precedent, public talk, and war carried the idea across borders."*

### Flashcard WH-1011 — American vs French Revolution: what each overthrew
**Front:** How do the American and French revolutions differ in target, depth and document?
**Definition (learn):** The American break was with external imperial authority; the French was with an internal social and legal order of privilege (✅ owner 01 §4; ✅ owner 02/03 cross-check).
**Breakdown:**
- Immediate target: America — imperial control by Britain; France — absolutism + feudal privilege inside France (✅ owner 01 adv §4).
- Social depth: America — political separation first; France — political, social and legal restructuring (✅ owner 01 adv §4).
- Document: America — Declaration of Independence; France — Declaration of the Rights of Man and Citizen (✅ owner 01 adv §4).
- Historical weight: America — republican precedent; France — far more radical destruction of old-order legitimacy (⚠️ owner 01 adv §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1776 (America), 1789 (France) (✅ owner 01 §1).
**Links:** WH-1012, WH-1002, WH-1032 (verdict), WH-1039 (American Revolution concept card).
**Trap:** Do not flatten the two into one "Atlantic revolution" (⚠️ owner 01 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** The compact line: "America internationalised republican constitutionalism; France universalised the language of citizenship, nation and anti-feudal transformation" (⚠️ owner 01 adv §4).
**Back:** *"America broke a link; France broke a structure."*

### Flashcard WH-1012 — Atlantic comparison: six dimensions without flattening
**Front:** Which six dimensions should a comparison of American and French revolutions cover?
**Definition (learn):** A disciplined comparison grid from owner 01 §8.6.
**Breakdown:**
- What was overthrown: ✅ external imperial authority (America) vs internal social and legal order (France).
- Feudal/seigneurial burden: ⚠️ largely absent in colonies vs ✅ central French target (dues, tithes, tax exemptions).
- Social violence: ⚠️ comparatively contained vs ✅ Terror phase 1793–94.
- Outcome form: ✅ federal republic with written constitution and Bill of Rights (1789) vs ✅ Republic → Directory → Napoleonic empire, legal equality retained.
- Slavery: ✅ survived the American Revolution vs ✅ abolition attempted in revolutionary period, reversed under Napoleon.
- Common inheritance: rights-language, consent, written constitutionalism vs rights-language, popular sovereignty, modern nation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Terror 1793–94 (✅ owner 01); Bill of Rights 1791 (✅ owner 02 §9).
**Links:** WH-1011, WH-1013, WH-1087 (Napoleonic Code), WH-1052 (Bill of Rights).
**Trap:** The causal arrow runs Enlightenment → both revolutions in parallel; America did not "cause" France; it "influenced and encouraged" it (✅ owner 01 §8.6 sequencing caution).
**Mains hook:** 20-mark comparison: shared vocabulary is real; social depth is not.
**Back:** *"Same words, different depth: link versus structure, slavery abolished in one, reversed in the other."*

### Flashcard WH-1013 — Physiocrats (taxes require consent)
**Front:** What did the Physiocrats contribute to the Enlightenment's politics of property and tax?
**Definition (learn):** An 18th-century economic current whose argument that taxes require consent and that property and economic freedom matter linked fiscal politics to consent.
**Breakdown:**
- Taxes require consent; property and economic freedom matter (✅ owner 01 §3).
- Helps explain anti-feudal and anti-privilege politics (✅ owner 01 §3).
- Links fiscal politics to consent: the exact fault line of 1765 and 1789 (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
- A minority current, not the whole Enlightenment (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no dates for the Physiocrats; the 1765 and 1789 fault line is the owner's analytic link (⚠️ owner 01 §8.4).
**Links:** WH-1001, WH-1009, WH-1016 (liberalism), WH-1025 (capitalism forms).
**Trap:** Do not present the Physiocrats as the mainstream or "the Enlightenment" as a whole.
**Mains hook:** Use the Physiocrats to show that fiscal grievance and consent theory were intertwined before 1789.
**Back:** *"No tax without consent — the fiscal side of Enlightenment."*

### Flashcard WH-1014 — Exclusions and limits of Enlightenment rights
**Front:** What limits did Enlightenment rights-language face in practice?
**Definition (learn):** Universal rights-language coexisted with restricted political citizenship; the Enlightenment produced a universal grammar administered by a restricted electorate (⚠️ owner 01 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Women: NCERT devotes a section to whether women had a revolution; political citizenship was not extended equally (✅ owner 01 §8.7).
- Enslaved people: abolition collided with colonial slave economies (✅ owner 01 §8.7).
- Workers and artisans gained political voice without economic equality (✅ Old NCERT via owner 01 §8.7).
- Property qualifications, not universal franchise, were the norm in first constitutional settlements (⚠️ owner 01 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No figures; exclusions by property, sex, slavery, race and colonial status (✅ owner 01 §9 control).
**Links:** WH-1012, WH-1016 (liberalism limits), WH-1030.
**Trap:** Do not call Enlightenment thinkers democrats in the modern universal-franchise sense.
**Mains hook:** Critical line: "a universal grammar administered by a restricted electorate." Use for the "limits" dimension of any 15/20-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Universal words, restricted votes."*

### Flashcard WH-1015 — Enlightenment traps (close options)
**Front:** Which common statements about the Enlightenment should be rejected?
**Definition (learn):** Three high-yield factual traps from owner 01 §5 and §6.
**Breakdown:**
- ❌ Enlightenment = only anti-religion → ✅ broader: reason, natural law, rights, science, constitutionalism, secular public debate.
- ❌ Enlightenment automatically created democracy everywhere → ✅ it opened ideological space; change depended on social conflict and state crisis.
- ❌ Nationalism was identical to liberalism from the start → ✅ they overlapped in this phase but are not the same concept.
- ❌ Enlightenment was purely French → ✅ European in reach, though French thinkers were central to its idiom (⚠️ owner 01 adv §6).
- ❌ Nationalism appears only after 1815 → ✅ Old NCERT links it to the 18th-century ideological world (✅ owner 01 adv §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1001, WH-1009, WH-1018 (nationalism), WH-1016 (liberalism).
**Trap:** These are standard MCQ close-option pairs; the first option in each pair is the wrong one.
**Mains hook:** Use the correction in an introduction to show definitional discipline.
**Back:** *"Not only anti-faith, not automatic democracy, not the same as nation."*

### Flashcard WH-1016 — Liberalism (consent, limited government, rights)
**Front:** What is liberalism as a doctrine, and what is its social base and institutional form?
**Definition (learn):** Government exists by consent, is limited by law and must protect individual rights; careers should follow ability rather than birth (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(a)).
**Breakdown:**
- Named anchors: Locke (natural rights, limited government, consent); Montesquieu (separation of powers); Physiocrats (taxes require consent); American and French declarations as rights documents (✅ owner 01 §9.3(a)).
- Social base: propertied and professional classes excluded from political power by hereditary privilege (⚠️ owner 01).
- Institutional form: written constitutions, separation of powers, representative assemblies, rule of law, legal equality, free press (⚠️ owner 01).
- Effects: abolition of privilege by birth and of Church ideological authority; legal equality survived the Napoleonic reaction (✅ owner 01 §9.3(a); ✅ basic/03).
- Limits: property-qualified franchise; compatible with slavery and empire (✅ owner 01 §9.3(a)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1776 (American Declaration), 1789 (French Declaration) (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** WH-1004, WH-1005, WH-1014, WH-1015, WH-1023 (socialism vs communism vs social democracy), WH-1030.
**Trap:** Nineteenth-century liberalism was not democratic in the modern sense (✅ owner 01 §9.3(a)).
**Mains hook:** Liberalism's achievement was the form of the constitution; its limit was the franchise.
**Back:** *"Consent, law, rights — property first, universal vote later."*

### Flashcard WH-1017 — Conservatism (order, gradualism, restoration)
**Front:** What is conservatism as a reasoned governing position in the owner's sense?
**Definition (learn):** A reasoned position that political order, religion, property and inherited institutions are achievements easier to destroy than to rebuild; change should be gradual (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(b)).
**Breakdown:**
- Named anchors: Congress of Vienna settlement and Concert of Europe as institutional case; Holy Alliance and Carlsbad Decrees as repressive instruments (✅ owner 01 §9.3(b); basic/05).
- Social base: monarchies, aristocracies and established churches facing revolutionary and Napoleonic upheaval (⚠️ owner 01).
- Institutional form: restored monarchies, censorship, policing of universities and press, congress diplomacy, balance of power (⚠️ owner 01).
- Effect: decades of great-power peace after 1815 alongside systematic repression of liberal and national movements (✅ owner 01; basic/05 §7.4–7.6).
- Limit: conservatism adapted as well as repressed (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(b)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-1815 peace; Holy Alliance and Carlsbad Decrees (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** WH-1171 (Vienna settlement, owner 05), WH-1016, WH-1030.
**Trap:** "Conservatives opposed change" is the wrong formula; conservatism is a reasoned position, not mere resistance (✅ owner 01 §9.1).
**Mains hook:** Use the Vienna settlement as the case for conservatism's adaptation and repression.
**Back:** *"Conservatism: order is earned, change is slow, repression is a tool."*

### Flashcard WH-1018 — Nationalism (nation as the unit of sovereignty)
**Front:** What is nationalism as a doctrine, and what is its institutional form?
**Definition (learn):** The nation, a people sharing language, culture, history or civic allegiance, is the proper unit of sovereignty (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(c)).
**Breakdown:**
- Named anchors: NCERT treats nationalism as a major outcome alongside liberty, equality and fraternity (✅ owner 01 §2); Mazzini, Garibaldi, Cavour, Bismarck (basic/06, ✅ owner 01 §9.3(c)); the French Revolution as the moment the nation became a political claim (basic/03).
- Social base: peoples divided among dynastic states or under foreign rule; later colonised peoples (⚠️ owner 01).
- Institutional form: the nation-state, national conscription and citizenship, national education and language policy, self-determination as an international principle (⚠️ owner 01; basic/10).
- Effects: unification of Italy and Germany; redrawing of Europe's map twice over (basic/06, basic/10); decisive political resource of anti-colonial movements (basic/18).
- Limit: nationalism fused with conservatism, racial doctrine and anti-colonial socialism — same word, different politics (⚠️ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the cross-referenced owners.
**Links:** WH-1015, WH-1209 (unification concept, owner 06), WH-1239 (map verdict).
**Trap:** Nationalism and liberalism overlapped but are not the same concept (✅ owner 01 §5).
**Mains hook:** Nationalism was the political claim that redrew Europe; its later fusion with other doctrines made it ambivalent.
**Back:** *"One nation, one sovereignty — liberal, conservative, racial or anti-colonial."*

### Flashcard WH-1019 — Capitalism (private ownership and profit)
**Front:** How does Lowe define capitalism, and what are its named anchors?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe's minimal definition: the system operates on private ownership of a country's wealth; private enterprise pursues profit; private wealth preserves its power (✅ owner 01 §9.2 point 3, citing Norman Lowe).
**Breakdown:**
- Named anchors: old NCERT links the Industrial Revolution to the growth of capitalism, the factory system and steam power (✅ owner 01 §9.3(d), basic/04 §2); Physiocrats supply the 18th-century argument for economic freedom and property (✅ owner 01 §3).
- Social base: merchants, manufacturers and financiers constrained by guild regulation, privilege and arbitrary taxation (⚠️ owner 01).
- Institutional form: private property and contract law, wage labour, joint-stock companies, stock exchanges, banking and credit (⚠️ owner 01).
- Effects: urbanisation, a capitalist class and a wage-earning class, class politics, enormous productive capacity; long hours, low wages, women's and children's labour and slum conditions in early phase (✅ owner 01 §9.3(d), basic/04 §5).
- Limit: recurrent instability — Great Depression (basic/20 §9) and 2008 crisis (basic/20 §8.5) (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Lowe's definition quoted via owner (✅ Norman Lowe, as reported in owner 01).
**Links:** WH-1025 (forms of capitalism), WH-1024 (doctrine vs regime), WH-1026 (trade unions), WH-1029.
**Trap:** Never write "capitalism means free markets"; specify which capitalism and which decades (⚠️ owner 01 §9.2).
**Mains hook:** Capitalism produced expansion and insecurity together; its outcomes depend on the regulatory regime.
**Back:** *"Private wealth, private profit — but which capitalism, which decade?"*

### Flashcard WH-1020 — Socialism: utopian and Marxian currents
**Front:** What is the core proposition of socialism, and how did the utopian and Marxian currents differ?
**Definition (learn):** Industrial society produces wealth and poverty together; the remedy is social rather than private control of productive property (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(e)).
**Breakdown:**
- Utopian/early current: moral criticism, model communities and cooperatives rather than theory of historical necessity (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(e)).
- Sourced example of non-Marxian socialism: Huey Long's "Share Our Wealth" in 1930s Louisiana — redistribution by direct guarantee, financed by taxing the rich (✅ owner 01 §9.3(e); basic/20 §9.7).
- Marxian current: collective ownership, centrally planned economy, working-class interests safeguarded by state social policy (✅ Lowe via owner); "dictatorship of the proletariat"; distributive principle "from each according to his ability, to each according to his needs" (✅ Lowe via owner).
- Social base: the industrial working class created by mechanisation and the factory system — landless, toolless, dependent (✅ owner 01; basic/04 §5).
- Institutional form: socialist parties, trade unions, cooperatives and, in the Marxian line, a revolutionary party seizing state power (⚠️ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Marx "had never described" how a socialist economy should be run (✅ Lowe via owner) — explains Bolshevik policy disputes.
**Links:** WH-1021 (communism), WH-1023 (definitional discipline), WH-1029 (effects), WH-1026.
**Trap:** Do not name early European utopian socialists (Saint-Simon, Fourier, Owen) as sourced facts of this folder; owner 01 forbids it, and no source is held here.
**Mains hook:** Socialism arose as the political answer to a social fact: a class with nothing to sell but its labour.
**Back:** *"Moral socialism first, Marx's system next — both answer the propertyless."*

### Flashcard WH-1021 — Communism: revolution, dictatorship of the proletariat, and the peasant mismatch
**Front:** What is communism as a doctrine, and what is the most examinable mismatch in its history?
**Definition (learn):** Collective ownership, central planning and state social policy for the working class, achieved by revolution and exercised through the dictatorship of the proletariat (✅ Lowe via owner 01 §9.3(f)).
**Breakdown:**
- Named anchors: the world's first communist government was set up in Russia in 1917 (✅ owner 01 §9.3(f)); Russian Social Democrat Labour Party split into Mensheviks (strict Marxists believing in proletarian revolution) and Bolsheviks; Lowe records Lenin "moving away from Marxism" (✅ owner 01 §9.3(f)).
- Social base: in Russia and China, predominantly peasant societies in state crisis, not the mature industrial proletariat Marx wrote about (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(f)).
- Institutional form: single ruling party, state ownership, central planning, security apparatus, official ideology (⚠️ owner 01).
- Effect: rapid state-led industrialisation and mass literacy and welfare alongside famine, terror and destruction of pluralism (✅ basic/13 §9.7 via owner); organising principle of one side of the Cold War (basic/15).
- Limits: communism never monolithic — Chinese accused Russians of "revisionism"; Yugoslavia developed its own model (✅ owner 01 §9.3(f)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1917 Russia (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** WH-1020, WH-1022, WH-1023, WH-1024 (doctrine vs regime), WH-1032 (citation trap).
**Trap:** Communism is a doctrine; the USSR is evidence of Soviet communism in practice, not its definition (⚠️ owner 01 §9.2 point 2).
**Mains hook:** The peasant base in Russia and China is the examinable mismatch between Marx's model and twentieth-century communism.
**Back:** *"Marx's proletariat, Lenin's peasants, one-party state."*

### Flashcard WH-1022 — Revisionism, parliamentary socialism and social democracy
**Front:** What is social democracy and what is the sourced meaning of "revisionism"?
**Definition (learn):** Capitalism can be reformed rather than overthrown; the working class should use the vote, union and parliament, and socialism arrives by accumulation of reforms (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(g)).
**Breakdown:**
- Sourced meaning of "revisionism" (Lowe, from the Chinese attack on Khrushchev): "revising or reinterpreting the teachings of Marx and Lenin to suit their own needs" (✅ owner 01 §9.3(g)).
- German Social Democrats (SPD): Friedrich Ebert became first president of the Weimar Republic; SPD largest single party in Germany's first fully democratic election (✅ owner 01 §9.3(g)).
- Vienna: Social Democrats set up welfare and housing projects for workers (✅ owner 01 §9.3(g)).
- Nazi Germany: SPD alone voted against the Enabling Act (441 to 94); Social Democrats were among the first political prisoners (✅ owner 01 §9.3(g)).
- Japan's Socialist Party became the Social Democratic Party of Japan in 1991 (✅ owner 01 §9.3(g)).
- Weimar failure: communist refusal to work with the SPD (✅ Lowe via owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Enabling Act vote 441 to 94; Social Democratic Party of Japan 1991 (✅ owner 01 §9.7 figures list).
**Links:** WH-1021, WH-1023, WH-1030, WH-1028.
**Trap:** Do not name Bernstein, Kautsky or European revisionist theorists — the folder's sourced meaning is Sino-Soviet (✅ owner 01 §9.7).
**Mains hook:** Revisionism is almost always a term of abuse used by those who claim orthodoxy; the socialist family quarrel helped open the door for the Nazis in Weimar.
**Back:** *"Social democrats vote and bargain; communists call it revisionism; the Nazis profit from the quarrel."*

### Flashcard WH-1023 — Socialism vs communism vs social democracy (definitional discipline)
**Front:** How do socialism, communism and social democracy differ on claim, method and relation to the state?
**Definition (learn):** Socialism is the genus; communism and social democracy are two species that disagree about method and about pluralism (⚠️ owner 01 §9.2 point 1).
**Breakdown:**
- Core claim: socialism — collective or social ownership, distribution by need (⚠️); communism — collective ownership, central planning, state social policy (✅ Lowe); social democracy — capitalist production retained but regulated, redistributed and socially provided (⚠️).
- Method: socialism — ranges from gradual to revolutionary (⚠️); communism — revolutionary (⚠️ Bolshevik position rested on dictatorship of the proletariat; ✅ Lowe); social democracy — parliamentary and electoral (⚠️).
- Relation to state: socialism — varies; communism — single-party state directing planned economy (✅ Lowe via owner); social democracy — pluralist with competing parties and protected opposition (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numerical.
**Links:** WH-1020, WH-1021, WH-1022, WH-1030 (comparison grid).
**Trap:** Do not use the three words as synonyms; ❌ "socialism = USSR" (see WH-1024).
**Mains hook:** "One diagnosis, three prescriptions": they agree capitalist ownership produces insecurity and inequality, and divide over revolution, regulation or redefinition of ownership. The answer to that question decided whether pluralism survived.
**Back:** *"Socialism is the family; communism and social democracy are its quarrelling branches — method and pluralism."*

### Flashcard WH-1024 — Doctrine is not a regime (trap)
**Front:** Why must a doctrine not be defined by the regime that claims it?
**Definition (learn):** The USSR's record is evidence about Soviet communism in practice, not a definition of communism or socialism (✅ owner 01 §9.2 point 2).
**Breakdown:**
- Lowe: socialists in the USA attacked Roosevelt's New Deal for not going far enough (✅ owner 01 §9.2; basic/20 §9.7).
- German Social Democrats were imprisoned in concentration camps and were the only party to vote against the Enabling Act (✅ owner 01).
- Soviet and Chinese communists accused each other of betraying the same doctrine (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Enabling Act 441 to 94 (✅ owner 01 §9.7).
**Links:** WH-1021, WH-1022, WH-1023, WH-1030.
**Trap:** Write "socialism in the Soviet form" or "socialism in the social-democratic form", never "socialism, i.e. the USSR" (✅ owner 01 §9.2).
**Mains hook:** Doctrine-versus-regime distinction is the first step of any socialism or communism answer.
**Back:** *"Judge the doctrine by its promise, the regime by its record."*

### Flashcard WH-1025 — Capitalism has forms, not one model
**Front:** What four historically distinct forms of capitalism does the owner identify?
**Definition (learn):** Capitalism is a family of forms; the form and its regulatory regime determine social outcomes (✅ owner 01 §9.2 point 3).
**Breakdown:**
- Laissez-faire capitalism (owner 01 §8 and basic/04).
- Regulated and welfare-supported capitalism of the New Deal and post-war welfare states (basic/20 §9.6–9.7).
- State-directed capitalism in the Japanese latecomer model (basic/04 §8.6A).
- Deregulated, globally integrated capitalism of the late 20th century that produced the 2008 crisis (basic/20 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2008 crisis (✅ owner 01 cross-ref basic/20).
**Links:** WH-1019, WH-1026, WH-1027, WH-1030 (comparison 2).
**Trap:** Never write that capitalism means free markets (⚠️ owner 01 §9.2).
**Mains hook:** "Neither system can be judged on ownership alone" — the decisive variable is whether political institutions corrected the system's characteristic failure.
**Back:** *"Laissez-faire, New Deal, Japanese, Global — four capitalisms."*

### Flashcard WH-1026 — Trade unions and the labour movement
**Front:** What are the key dated markers and effects of trade unions and labour movements?
**Definition (learn):** Workers' bargaining power is collective or it does not exist (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(h)).
**Breakdown:**
- Anti-union laws repealed in England in 1824, making union growth possible (✅ owner 01 §9.3(h)).
- Chartism in the 1830s–40s carried working-class demands into the constitutional arena; it did not achieve its immediate aims (✅ owner 01 §9.3(h)).
- American Federation of Labor and the IWW as rival strategies in the USA (✅ owner 01 §9.3(h)).
- Collective bargaining accepted under the New Deal; General Motors and US Steel forced to recognise unions (✅ owner 01 §9.3(h)).
- Solidarity in Poland, a trade union, opened the collapse of communism in August 1988 (✅ owner 01; basic/21).
- First Factory Act (1802) and later Factory Acts (✅ owner 01 §9.3(h)).
- Fair Labour Standards Act (1938): maximum hours, minimum wage in low-paid trades, most child labour illegal (✅ owner 01 §9.3(h)).
- Nazi Germany abolished trade unions; South Africa forbade black workers to strike from 1911 until 1979 (✅ owner 01; basic/18 §7.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1802 (First Factory Act), 1824 (repeal), 1830s–40s (Chartism), 1938 (FLSA), August 1988 (Solidarity) (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** WH-1020, WH-1025, WH-1028 (Progressive programme), WH-1027 (welfare state).
**Trap:** Chartism's immediate aims were not achieved; FLSA (1938) is not the same as the Social Security Act (1935).
**Mains hook:** Unions are a test of a regime, not merely an actor within it.
**Back:** *"1802 factories, 1824 unions, 1938 hours, 1988 Poland."*

### Flashcard WH-1027 — Welfare state: war or socialism? (two causal accounts)
**Front:** Was the welfare state a product of war or of socialist pressure?
**Definition (learn):** The welfare state owes its citizens a floor of security as a matter of right; the owner's answer is a synthesis of two sourced causal accounts (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(i)).
**Breakdown:**
- Account A — product of socialist and labour pressure: American socialist movement forced insurance, hours and child-labour reform before 1914; Vienna Social Democratic welfare; trade-union pressure behind labour law. Cannot explain: welfare states built where socialists did not govern; 1935 Act came from a president attacked by socialists as too cautious (⚠️ owner 01).
- Account B — product of war and depression: Beveridge is a 1942 wartime document; 1935 Act followed the Depression; Lowe's summary that governments promised "welfare in return for the respect and obedience of its citizens". Cannot explain: pre-1914 reform agenda (⚠️ owner 01).
- Synthesis: socialist and labour pressure supplied the programme; depression and total war supplied political possibility and administrative machinery (⚠️ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Beveridge Report 1942 ("a plan for introducing a Welfare State", stimulated by the war in Britain); US Social Security Act 1935; Fair Deal (✅ owner 01 §9.3(i), §9.7).
**Links:** WH-1026, WH-1028, WH-1029, WH-1030.
**Trap:** Do not name any national welfare-state statute other than those sourced (NHS, British 1945–51 legislation are not sourced here) (✅ owner 01 §9.7).
**Mains hook:** (20 marks) "The welfare state was a product of war, not of socialism." Critically examine. Graded verdict: programme from socialism; possibility from war and depression.
**Back:** *"Socialists wrote the plan; war and depression made it possible."*

### Flashcard WH-1028 — Welfare doctrine debate: Nozick, Rawls, Friedman and Hayek
**Front:** How do the conservative and liberal/social-contract positions on welfare differ, and who are the named texts?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's one fully sourced ideological debate, reported from Lowe (✅ owner 01 §9.3(j)).
**Breakdown:**
- Conservative/right: rugged individualism and self-help; taxation as invasion of private property; named anchors Robert Nozick, *Anarchy, State and Utopia* (1974), property rights and minimal state; Friedman and Hayek on economics (✅ owner 01 §9.3(j)).
- Liberal/social-contract: rugged individualism tempered by social contract; state provides basic welfare in return for respect and obedience; named anchor John Rawls, *A Theory of Justice* (1973), duty of government to provide welfare and redistribution (✅ owner 01 §9.3(j)).
- Institutional consequence: Roosevelt's New Deal and Johnson's Great Society (✅ owner 01).
- From the 1980s, monetarist theories of Milton Friedman and Friedrich Hayek opposed socialism and welfare state (✅ owner 01 §9.3(i)); Hayek, *The Road to Serfdom*, 1944 (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nozick 1974; Rawls 1973; Hayek 1944 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** WH-1027, WH-1026, WH-1029.
**Trap:** No verbatim quotations from these thinkers are held; paraphrase only (✅ owner 01 §9.3(j) caution).
**Mains hook:** Use the property-rights objection at its strongest before answering it; connect the liberal social-contract line back to Locke and Rousseau.
**Back:** *"Nozick guards property, Rawls guards the floor, Hayek fears the road."*

### Flashcard WH-1029 — Socialism's effect on society without taking power
**Front:** What measurable effect did socialist ideas have on society, and where?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's best evidence unit: socialist ideas changed law and policy in countries where socialists never took power (⚠️ owner 01 §9.3(e)).
**Breakdown:**
- American socialist movement "publicised the need for reform and influenced both major parties" (✅ Lowe via owner 01).
- American Progressive programme included eight-hour day, prohibition of child labour, votes for women and a national system of social insurance (✅ owner 01 §9.3(e)).
- Limit: American socialist vote remained small even as it grew; Debs's 1912 vote more than doubled his previous total (✅ owner 01).
- Doctrinal split over method (⚠️ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Debs 1908: just over 400,000 votes; 1912: 900,672 votes (✅ owner 01 §9.7); 1912 result — Wilson 6.3 million, Roosevelt 4.1 million, Taft 3.5 million (✅ owner 01 §9.7); nine US states had votes for women by 1914; federal women's suffrage 1920 (✅ owner 01 §9.7).
**Links:** WH-1020, WH-1026, WH-1027, WH-1031.
**Trap:** Influence is not power; the largest effect was where socialism did not take power.
**Mains hook:** 15-mark spine: "Trace the rise of socialist ideas and assess their effect on society." Verdict: socialism's largest effect on society was achieved where it did not take power.
**Back:** *"Eight hours, child labour, women's vote, social insurance — won without power."*

### Flashcard WH-1030 — Four doctrines compared on six questions
**Front:** How do liberalism, conservatism, socialism (social-democratic form) and communism compare on ownership, legitimacy, social problem, method, pluralism and institutional output?
**Definition (learn):** Comparison 1 of owner 01 §9.4 — the grid for any "compare ideologies" demand.
**Breakdown:**
- Ownership: liberalism — private owners; conservatism — established owners, property as social ballast; social democracy — mixed, socially regulated and taxed; communism — collective, centrally planned.
- Legitimacy: liberalism — consent and constitutional limits; conservatism — continuity, tradition; social democracy — electoral majority; communism — party as vanguard.
- Central social problem: liberalism — arbitrary power; conservatism — disorder and rupture; social democracy — insecurity and inequality within capitalism; communism — class exploitation itself.
- Method: liberalism — reform through law; conservatism — gradual adaptation; social democracy — legislation and bargaining; communism — revolution.
- Pluralism: liberalism — constitutive; conservatism — accepted within limits; social democracy — constitutive; communism — rejected in practice.
- Characteristic output: liberalism — constitutions, rights, franchise; conservatism — concert diplomacy, restored monarchies, censorship; social democracy — labour law, social insurance, welfare state; communism — one-party state, planned economy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Grid values are owner-analytic (⚠️ owner 01 §9.4) except marked ✅ cells.
**Links:** WH-1016 to WH-1023.
**Trap:** The cells that are analytic (⚠️) must not be quoted as sourced facts.
**Mains hook:** Use the grid to structure a 20-mark comparison, then add a named example for each cell.
**Back:** *"Own, legitimise, diagnose, change, tolerate, deliver."*

### Flashcard WH-1031 — Permitted figures and factual-risk controls (owner 01)
**Front:** Which numbers may be cited from the political-philosophies clause?
**Definition (learn):** The owner allows only Lowe's figures; no membership, vote-share or coverage numbers beyond these (✅ owner 01 §9.7).
**Breakdown:**
- American Socialist Party about 100,000 members by 1910 (✅ owner 01).
- Debs just over 400,000 votes in 1908 and 900,672 in 1912 (✅ owner 01).
- 1912 result: Wilson 6.3 million; Roosevelt 4.1 million; Taft 3.5 million (✅ owner 01).
- Nine US states with votes for women by 1914; federal women's suffrage 1920 (✅ owner 01).
- Enabling Act passed 441 to 94 (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** WH-1022, WH-1029, WH-1024.
**Trap:** Do not give figures beyond these from the owner's source base.
**Mains hook:** Use a single figure as evidence (not decoration) inside a claim → evidence → analysis chain.
**Back:** *"Only Lowe's numbers: 100,000, 900,672, 441–94, 1920."*

### Flashcard WH-1032 — Marx and Engels citation restriction and the contested Bolshevik policy
**Front:** Which Marxian texts and quotations may and may not be cited from this folder?
**Definition (learn):** Only two Marxian quotations appear in the folder's source base, both reported by Lowe (✅ owner 01 §9.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Allowed: "dictatorship of the proletariat"; "from each according to his ability, to each according to his needs" (✅ Lowe via owner 01).
- Not allowed: *The Communist Manifesto*, *Das Kapital*, or any publication date — none sourced (✅ owner 01 §9.7).
- Lowe: Marx "had never described" how a socialist economy should be run, which made Bolshevik policy contested in the Politburo (✅ owner 01 §9.3(e)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond quotations.
**Links:** WH-1020, WH-1021.
**Trap:** Do not quote Marx or Engels beyond these two phrases; paraphrase the rest.
**Mains hook:** The unspecified economic blueprint of Marx is why twentieth-century communism improvised.
**Back:** *"Two quotations, no citations — paraphrase the rest."*

### Flashcard WH-1033 — Enlightenment as a worldwide grammar of modern politics
**Front:** In what sense did the Age of Revolutions create a world-historical inheritance?
**Definition (learn):** The revolutions created a worldwide grammar of modern politics, not a direct straight-line influence on every movement (⚠️ owner 01 adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Old NCERT: ideas spread beyond their place of origin, influencing peoples under oppressive governments (✅ owner 01 adv §5).
- Later 19th-century constitutional and nationalist politics across Europe drew on rights-sovereignty vocabulary (⚠️ owner 01 adv §5).
- Shared vocabulary: liberty, equality, citizenship, representation, sovereignty (⚠️ owner 01 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numerical.
**Links:** WH-1010, WH-1012, WH-1018.
**Trap:** Do not overclaim direct influence on every anti-colonial movement.
**Mains hook:** "A worldwide grammar of modern politics" is the qualified formulation for a conclusion.
**Back:** *"A grammar shared, not a script copied."*

### Flashcard WH-1034 — Age of Revolutions as periodisation, not a single event
**Front:** Is the "Age of Revolutions" a single event or a label, and what period does it cover?
**Definition (learn):** A standard periodisation label linking Atlantic and European upheavals; acceptable only when clearly presented as periodisation (⚠️ owner 01 adv §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Period label c. 1776–1848 (⚠️ owner 01 §1).
- Tied above all to the late 18th century, especially America and France (✅ owner 01 adv §1, §6).
- Not a term to reduce to "middle class replaced nobility" in one line (✅ owner 01 adv §6 trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1776–1848 (⚠️ standard label).
**Links:** WH-1003, WH-1002, WH-1008.
**Trap:** Never reduce to "middle class replaced nobility".
**Mains hook:** Distinguish intellectual change (Enlightenment) from political rupture (Age of Revolutions) in the introduction.
**Back:** *"A label for 1776–1848, not a single revolution."*

### Flashcard WH-1035 — Ideational view vs state-crisis view (debate pair)
**Front:** How does the ideational view of revolution differ from the state-crisis view?
**Definition (learn):** Two causal positions in the historiography of the Age of Revolutions (⚠️ owner 01 adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Ideational view: Enlightenment ideas made old regimes illegitimate; use for intro but do not stop there (⚠️ owner 01 adv §2).
- State-crisis view: weak monarchies or empires failed to absorb reform pressures; strong for comparing America and France (⚠️ owner 01 adv §2).
- Best balanced answer: ideas + grievance + political opportunity (⚠️ owner 01 adv §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1008, WH-1009, WH-1011.
**Trap:** Do not present the ideational view as the complete explanation.
**Mains hook:** Use the state-crisis view to explain why America and France diverged.
**Back:** *"Ideas make it lawful; state failure makes it possible."*

### Flashcard WH-1036 — Verdict scaffolds for political philosophies and welfare
**Front:** What qualified verdicts are ready for the political-philosophies clause?
**Definition (learn):** Ready-made one-sentence verdicts from owner 01 §9.6.
**Breakdown:**
- Rise and effect of socialist ideas: socialism arose from a class with nothing to sell but its labour; its measurable effect lies in labour law, social insurance and franchise reform forced on states that did not themselves become socialist (⚠️ owner 01 §9.6).
- Socialism, communism, social democracy: one diagnosis, three prescriptions (⚠️ owner 01 §9.6).
- Capitalism's effect on society: greatest expansion of productive capacity and the insecurity that made socialism necessary; most stable form absorbed its critics' demands (⚠️ owner 01 §9.6).
- Welfare state: written by socialists and labour movements; enacted by governments that feared what depression and war had revealed; contested as prosperity returned (⚠️ owner 01 §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1029, WH-1023, WH-1025, WH-1027.
**Trap:** Verdicts must not claim that any one ideology caused a war or revolution on its own (owner 01 §9.7).
**Mains hook:** End a 20-mark answer on the graded verdict.
**Back:** *"Diagnosis shared, prescriptions divided; the welfare floor was fought over."*

### Flashcard WH-1037 — 15-mark socialist effects spine
**Front:** What is the ready-made structure for a 15-mark answer on the rise and effect of socialist ideas?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 01 §9.5 spine 1.
**Breakdown:**
- Industrial society creates a propertyless wage-earning class (basic/04 §5).
- Early moral and redistributive socialism → Marxian turn with distinctive claims → split over method.
- Effects: unions and Chartism; American Progressive programme; Vienna's municipal welfare; labour law; social insurance.
- Limits: small electoral share in some countries; fratricidal split; difference between influence and power.
- Verdict: socialism's largest effect on society was achieved where it did not take power (⚠️ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** See WH-1029.
**Links:** WH-1020, WH-1026, WH-1029, WH-1036.
**Trap:** Do not forget the limits and the doctrinal split; a list of effects alone scores less.
**Mains hook:** This is the spine for 15-mark questions; 20-mark add a comparison from WH-1030.
**Back:** *"Class, moral socialism, Marx, split, effects, limits, verdict."*

### Flashcard WH-1038 — 2019 GS-I cross-cutting demand: American and French revolutions
**Front:** Which PYQ routes into owner 01 and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's verified PYQ ledger shows zero direct overview-only routes; the 2019 GS-I question is cross-owned by Topics 02 and 03 (✅ owner 01 §9; ✅ owner 02 adv).
**Breakdown:**
- 2019 GS-I question 13: "American and French Revolutions and the foundations of the modern world", 15 marks, 250 words (✅ owner 02 adv PYQ integration).
- Owner 01 says it is cross-cutting; not relabelled here (✅ owner 01 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019; 15 marks; 250 words (✅ owner 02 adv).
**Links:** WH-1011, WH-1012, WH-1039 (American Revolution concept card).
**Trap:** Do not assume a PYQ is overview-only when the ledger routes it to a revolution owner.
**Mains hook:** Prepare context, core dimensions, evidence, counterpoint and conclusion.
**Back:** *"2019: two revolutions, one question, three owners."*

### GROUP — American Revolution (owner 02)

### Flashcard WH-1039 — American Revolution as war of independence and constitutional revolution
**Front:** What kind of revolution was the American Revolution, and what is its core idea?
**Definition (learn):** A struggle by 13 British colonies against an imperial system that blocked their economic and political autonomy; both a war of independence and a constitutional revolution (✅ owner 02 §2; ✅ Old NCERT).
**Breakdown:**
- Old NCERT: 13 colonies vs imperial system (✅ owner 02 §2).
- Library of Congress: background is Britain's war debt after 1763 and new attempts to tax, regulate and station troops in the colonies (✅ owner 02 §2).
- Declaration of Independence: "all men are created equal"; inalienable rights to life, liberty and the pursuit of happiness (✅ owner 02 §2, National Archives transcript).
- ⚠️ Both a war of independence and a constitutional revolution (⚠️ owner 02 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1763 war-debt background; 1776 Declaration; 1783 Paris; 1789 Constitution (✅ owner 02 §1).
**Links:** WH-1040 (timeline), WH-1041 (causes), WH-1057 (how revolutionary), WH-1038 (2019 PYQ).
**Trap:** Not only a tax revolt; it became a struggle over sovereignty, representation and nationhood (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Mains hook:** "A political revolution with a constitutional method and a social settlement it declined to disturb" (⚠️ owner 02 adv §8.5).
**Back:** *"Thirteen colonies, one claim of right, a written constitution."*

### Flashcard WH-1040 — American Revolution chronology ladder (1763–1789)
**Front:** Give the dated stages of the American Revolution from 1763 to 1789.
**Definition (learn):** The owner's mini-timeline with one function per date (✅ owner 02 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- 1763 — Seven Years' War ends; Britain tightens imperial control to recover costs (✅ LoC via owner 02).
- 1765 — Stamp Act (✅ owner 02).
- 1773 — Boston Tea Party (✅ owner 02).
- 1774 — First Continental Congress at Philadelphia (✅ owner 02).
- 1775 — Lexington and Concord; armed struggle begins (✅ owner 02).
- 4 July 1776 — Declaration of Independence adopted (✅ owner 02).
- 1777–78 — Saratoga and French alliance; war turns international (✅ LoC/Britannica via owner 02).
- 1781 — Cornwallis surrenders at Yorktown (✅ owner 02).
- 1783 — Treaty of Paris: Britain recognises independence (✅ owner 02).
- 1789 — U.S. Constitution comes into effect (✅ owner 02).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above; the Bill of Rights is dated 1791 in owner 02 §9 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** WH-1041, WH-1042, WH-1043 to WH-1052 (individual stage cards).
**Trap:** Keep the stages separate: Saratoga (1777) internationalised the war; Yorktown (1781) gave surrender; Paris (1783) gave legal recognition.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show escalation from protest to international war to constitutional settlement.
**Back:** *"Sixty-three, sixty-five, seventy-three, seventy-four, seventy-five, seventy-six, seventy-seven, eighty-one, eighty-three, eighty-nine."*

### Flashcard WH-1041 — Causes of the American Revolution as a system
**Front:** What were the five interacting causes of the American Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Causes operated as an interacting system: mercantilist restriction, taxation, settlement limits, existing self-government and Enlightenment ideas (✅ owner 02 §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Mercantilist restrictions: colonies forced into British ships, markets and manufactures (✅ Old NCERT via owner 02).
- Taxation: Stamp Act and duties without colonial representation — "no taxation without representation" (✅ owner 02 §3).
- Westward restrictions: limits on moving west (✅ Old NCERT via owner 02).
- Political self-government: colonies already had local assemblies; conflict over who may tax and legislate (✅ owner 02 §3).
- Enlightenment ideas: Locke, Jefferson, Paine and rights-language legitimised rebellion (✅ owner 02 §3).
- Fiscal origin: Britain's war debt after 1763 (✅ LoC via owner 02).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1763 fiscal reordering; 1765 Stamp Act (✅ owner 02 §8.4).
**Links:** WH-1042 (Stamp Act), WH-1039, WH-1009 (ideas vs interests), WH-1053 (Paine), WH-1055 (Jefferson).
**Trap:** Many colonists still profited within the imperial system; the sums were not crushing, the principle was (⚠️ owner 02 §8.4).
**Mains hook:** 10-mark: "Analyse the political and economic causes." Name one economic and one political-ideological cause with evidence.
**Back:** *"Trade, tax, land, self-rule, ideas."*

### Flashcard WH-1042 — Stamp Act 1765 and taxation without representation
**Front:** What was the Stamp Act (1765) and why did it matter?
**Definition (learn):** A tax on colonial transactions that turned a fiscal dispute into a sovereignty dispute (✅ owner 02 §1; ⚠️ owner 02 §8.4 analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- Duties and taxes without colonial representation (✅ owner 02 §3).
- Colonists radicalised around taxation and representation (✅ owner 02 §1).
- Representation meant colonial assemblies, not universal franchise (⚠️ owner 02 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1765 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** WH-1041, WH-1040, WH-1043.
**Trap:** "No taxation without representation" was about who legislates for the colonies, not about universal suffrage.
**Mains hook:** Use Stamp Act to illustrate the escalation from fiscal grievance to constitutional claim.
**Back:** *"Stamp of 1765: tax first, representation later."*

### Flashcard WH-1043 — Boston Tea Party 1773
**Front:** What was the Boston Tea Party and what did it signal?
**Definition (learn):** A 1773 symbolic defiance that made the tea dispute an open confrontation with imperial authority (✅ owner 02 §1; ✅ owner 02 adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1773 (✅ owner 02 §6 date control).
- Consequence: punitive British response and continental coordination (✅ owner 02 §4 course).
- Symbolic defiance against imperial authority (✅ owner 02 adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773.
**Links:** WH-1042, WH-1044, WH-1041.
**Trap:** Do not date the Boston Tea Party loosely: 1773 (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Use it as the step from boycott into punitive response.
**Back:** *"Seventy-three: tea into harbour, empire into crisis."*

### Flashcard WH-1044 — First Continental Congress 1774
**Front:** What did the First Continental Congress at Philadelphia (1774) achieve?
**Definition (learn):** The colonial coordination body in 1774 that let the colonies act politically together (✅ owner 02 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Venue: Philadelphia (✅ owner 02).
- Function: colonies coordinate politically (✅ owner 02 §1).
- Followed by continental militia mobilisation (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1774.
**Links:** WH-1043, WH-1045, WH-1050 (Articles phase 1781).
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1774 First Continental Congress with the later 1781 Articles union phase (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Show the first institutional step of colonial union before armed war.
**Back:** *"Seventy-four: colonies talk as one."*

### Flashcard WH-1045 — Lexington and Concord 1775
**Front:** What do Lexington and Concord mark?
**Definition (learn):** The start of armed conflict in the American Revolution (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1775 (✅ owner 02 §6).
- Militia war phase followed under George Washington's command (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1775.
**Links:** WH-1044, WH-1046, WH-1054 (Washington).
**Trap:** Do not date Lexington loosely: 1775 (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Armed struggle began before independence was declared.
**Back:** *"Seventy-five: first shots, then a nation."*

### Flashcard WH-1046 — Declaration of Independence (4 July 1776)
**Front:** What did the Declaration of Independence (4 July 1776) assert and who drafted it?
**Definition (learn):** The colonial protest turned into a rights-based claim to statehood, drafted by Thomas Jefferson (✅ owner 02 §5, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Adopted 4 July 1776 (✅ owner 02 §1).
- All men "created equal"; inalienable rights to life, liberty and the pursuit of happiness (✅ National Archives transcript via owner 02 §2).
- Turns colonial rights-claim into rights-based claim to statehood (✅ owner 02 §5).
- Rights as the moral basis of secession (✅ owner 02 adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 July 1776; drafted by Jefferson (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Links:** WH-1055 (Jefferson), WH-1039, WH-1050, WH-1057.
**Trap:** Declaration of Independence is not the Constitution; it proclaims independence, the Constitution structures the state (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Mains hook:** The Declaration shows rights-language founding a secession; its exclusions are the qualification.
**Back:** *"Seventy-six: all men created equal, independence declared."*

### Flashcard WH-1047 — Saratoga and the French alliance (1777–78)
**Front:** Why was Saratoga decisive for the international character of the war?
**Definition (learn):** After colonial victory at Saratoga, French assistance became decisive; the war was internationalised (✅ owner 02 adv §4; ✅ LoC/Britannica via owner 02 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- 1777–78: Saratoga and French alliance (✅ owner 02 §1).
- Spain and the Dutch also hurt Britain (✅ owner 02 §4).
- Lafayette is named in the French phase (✅ owner 02 §4).
- After 1778 the conflict was international (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1777–78 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** WH-1048 (Yorktown), WH-1040, WH-1062 (Spain and Dutch), WH-1063 (Lafayette).
**Trap:** Do not say Britain fought only the colonies; after 1778 it fought a wider war (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Mains hook:** Saratoga shows colonial resistance could survive and win external support.
**Back:** *"Seventy-seven, seventy-eight: France joins, Britain fights everywhere."*

### Flashcard WH-1048 — Yorktown 1781 (Cornwallis surrenders)
**Front:** What was the military turning point at Yorktown in 1781?
**Definition (learn):** Cornwallis's surrender at Yorktown in 1781, the decisive military turning point (✅ owner 02 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1781 (✅ owner 02 §1).
- Commander surrendering: Cornwallis (✅ owner 02 §4).
- Yorktown supplied decisive surrender; Paris supplied legal recognition (✅ owner 02 §9 mechanism control).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1781.
**Links:** WH-1047, WH-1049, WH-1054.
**Trap:** Military victory mattered, but Yorktown alone did not legally create the United States; recognition came with Paris (✅ owner 02 adv §6).
**Mains hook:** Use Yorktown and Paris as the military and legal stages of one settlement.
**Back:** *"Eighty-one: Cornwallis surrenders, but the law comes later."*

### Flashcard WH-1049 — Treaty of Paris 1783
**Front:** What did the Treaty of Paris (1783) do?
**Definition (learn):** Britain recognised American independence; the imperial loss of colonies and the republican precedent survived (✅ owner 02 §1; owner 02 adv §1).
**Breakdown:**
- 1783 recognition of independence (✅ owner 02 §6).
- Legal recognition as distinct from military victory (✅ owner 02 adv §6).
- Empire loses colonies; republican precedent survives (✅ owner 02 adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1783.
**Links:** WH-1048, WH-1050, WH-1051.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Paris (1783) with the 1789 Constitution.
**Mains hook:** Paris as the legal end of the war and the start of constitutional building.
**Back:** *"Eighty-three: Britain signs, America stands."*

### Flashcard WH-1050 — Articles of Confederation phase (1781) and the union before the Constitution
**Front:** What was the 1781 union phase before the 1789 Constitution?
**Definition (learn):** The period when colonies cooperated as states under a common framework, preceding the federal constitution (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Articles / national union phase (1781) (✅ owner 02 §5).
- Federal division of power: the 1781 union phase preceded the federal republic (⚠️ owner 02 adv §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1781.
**Links:** WH-1051, WH-1044, WH-1049.
**Trap:** The 1781 Articles phase is distinct from the 1774 First Continental Congress.
**Mains hook:** Institutional continuity from confederation to federation.
**Back:** *"Eighty-one: states united, not yet federal."*

### Flashcard WH-1051 — U.S. Constitution 1789: federal republic
**Front:** What did the U.S. Constitution (in effect 1789) establish?
**Definition (learn):** A federal republican order, not a monarchy; the state's powers enumerated in a written text (✅ owner 02 §5, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- In effect 1789 (✅ owner 02 §1).
- Federal republic, not a monarchy (✅ owner 02 §6).
- Written, supreme constitution: state powers enumerated (⚠️ owner 02 adv §8.6).
- Federal division of power (✅ owner 02 adv §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1789.
**Links:** WH-1052, WH-1046, WH-1049, WH-1058.
**Trap:** Do not describe the Constitution as the Declaration; the Constitution structures the state, the Declaration proclaims independence (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Mains hook:** The Constitution is the novelty of the American revolution: written constitutionalism and federalism.
**Back:** *"Eighty-nine: a written federal republic, not a king."*

### Flashcard WH-1052 — Bill of Rights (1791)
**Front:** What does the Bill of Rights protect and when was it adopted?
**Definition (learn):** The first ten amendments that entrench speech, press, religion and legal safeguards against government (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Protects speech, press, religion and legal safeguards (✅ owner 02 §5).
- Date: 1791 (✅ owner 02 basic §9 date control).
- Rights become judicially defensible, not merely proclaimed (⚠️ owner 02 adv §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1791 (✅ owner 02 §9).
**Links:** WH-1051, WH-1058, WH-1012 (Atlantic comparison bill of rights).
**Trap:** The Bill of Rights is not the Constitution's text itself; it followed it. Do not give a different date from 1791.
**Mains hook:** Entrenched rights are a major export of the American revolution.
**Back:** *"Ninety-one: rights written down against the state."*

### Flashcard WH-1053 — Thomas Paine and Common Sense
**Front:** What role did Thomas Paine play in the American Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Paine's *Common Sense* is a major ideological text of the revolution and part of the rights-language that legitimised resistance (✅ owner 02 adv §6; ✅ owner 02 §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Named with Locke and Jefferson as a rights-language source (✅ owner 02 §3).
- Protest phase names: Thomas Paine and colonial assemblies (✅ owner 02 §4).
- *Common Sense* as major ideological text (✅ owner 02 adv §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No date in owner text; ⚠️ do not add.
**Links:** WH-1041, WH-1046, WH-1055.
**Trap:** Do not assign Paine's authorship to the Declaration (Jefferson drafted it).
**Mains hook:** Use Paine as the vehicle that spread rights-language to ordinary colonists.
**Back:** *"Paine popularised; Jefferson drafted."*

### Flashcard WH-1054 — George Washington and the American command
**Front:** What role did George Washington play?
**Definition (learn):** Washington led the American forces (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Breakdown:**
- War phase: Lexington, militia war, Washington's command (✅ owner 02 §4).
- Names to remember list (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Command during the war, 1775–1781 window per owner phase (⚠️ dates not stated in owner; the war-phase window is from 1775 to Yorktown).
**Links:** WH-1045, WH-1048.
**Trap:** Washington led the forces; he did not draft the Declaration (Jefferson did).
**Mains hook:** Military command was necessary but not sufficient; recognition came through diplomacy and Paris.
**Back:** *"Washington commands the war; Jefferson drafts the words."*

### Flashcard WH-1055 — Thomas Jefferson and the Declaration
**Front:** What did Jefferson do in the American Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Jefferson drafted the Declaration of Independence (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Declaration drafter (✅ owner 02 §6).
- Named with Locke and Paine as rights-language source (✅ owner 02 §3).
- Settlement phase names Jefferson and the Philadelphia Convention (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1776 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** WH-1046, WH-1053, WH-1041.
**Trap:** Do not conflate Jefferson with Washington; Jefferson drafted, Washington commanded.
**Mains hook:** Use Jefferson as the rights-language author in a 10-mark cause answer.
**Back:** *"Jefferson writes, Washington fights."*

### Flashcard WH-1056 — Westward restriction and Native dispossession
**Front:** How did settlement restrictions and westward expansion affect Native peoples and the revolution's coalition?
**Definition (learn):** Old NCERT notes anger over limits on westward movement; independence removed the imperial restraint on westward settlement (✅ owner 02 §3; ⚠️ owner 02 adv §8.4–8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Settlement restriction added a land grievance and widened the coalition beyond merchants (⚠️ owner 02 §8.4).
- Westward expansion meant dispossession of Native peoples (⚠️ owner 02 §8.4).
- Native American land dispossession accelerated after independence (⚠️ owner 02 adv §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No figures (✅ owner 02 §8.8 bans invented figures).
**Links:** WH-1041, WH-1057, WH-1059.
**Trap:** Do not present the westward grievance as a Native grievance; it was a colonist grievance with Native dispossession as consequence.
**Mains hook:** For social consequences, name dispossession explicitly.
**Back:** *"Land blocked, then land taken."*

### Flashcard WH-1057 — How revolutionary was the American Revolution? (radical vs conservative ledger)
**Front:** Which features were radical and which were continuous in the American Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** A ledger of novelties vs continuities that answers "how revolutionary?" (✅ owner 02 adv §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Radical: rights-based claim to statehood (✅ 1776 Declaration); written constitution creating federal republic (✅ 1789); entrenched liberties (✅ Bill of Rights); rejection of monarchy (✅ owner 02); successful anti-imperial precedent (✅ Paris 1783).
- Conservative: slavery survived (⚠️ see basic/08 for Haiti contrast); property and status qualifications limited participation (⚠️); existing colonial elites largely kept social leadership (⚠️); Native dispossession accelerated (⚠️); women gained no equivalent political status (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** WH-1039, WH-1058, WH-1059, WH-1064.
**Trap:** Do not say the Revolution was fully democratic in 1776 or 1789 (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Mains hook:** "Revolutionary in constitutional form, reformist in social content" (⚠️ owner 02 adv §8.7).
**Back:** *"Radical in constitution, conservative in society."*

### Flashcard WH-1058 — Exportable models of the American Revolution
**Front:** Which five models did the American Revolution export to the modern world?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 02 adv §8.6 lists the five exportable models (⚠️ owner analytic framing; each evidence item ✅).
**Breakdown:**
- Written, supreme constitution (✅ 1789 Constitution).
- Federal division of power (✅ federal republic; 1781 union preceded it).
- Entrenched bill of rights (✅ Bill of Rights).
- Legitimate revolt against empire (✅ Declaration's rights-based justification).
- Internationalised anti-colonial war (✅ Saratoga and French alliance 1777–78).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1789, 1791, 1776, 1777–78 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** WH-1051, WH-1052, WH-1047, WH-1050.
**Trap:** Models were exported with their exclusions; do not present them as universal fulfilment.
**Mains hook:** The American contribution was procedural: a people binds its rulers to a text.
**Back:** *"Constitution, federation, rights, revolt, alliance."*

### Flashcard WH-1059 — Exclusions: slavery, property, women and Native peoples
**Front:** What exclusions survived the American Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Political sovereignty changed while slavery, Native dispossession, property limits and women's exclusion survived (✅ owner 02 adv; ✅ owner 02 §9 mechanism control).
**Breakdown:**
- Slavery survived the Revolution; abolition was not achieved (✅ owner 02 §8.8).
- Native American political inclusion remained minimal; dispossession accelerated (✅ owner 02 adv §3).
- Women: political inclusion remained minimal (✅ owner 02 adv §3).
- Property-holding white men gained voice but franchise was still limited by property (✅ owner 02 adv §3).
- Enslaved people: contradiction exposed; slavery continued until Civil War-era abolition (✅ owner 02 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Civil War-era abolition (✅ owner 02 adv §3 — the date is not in the owner).
**Links:** WH-1014 (Enlightenment exclusions), WH-1057, WH-1062 ...
**Trap:** Do not write "American Revolution abolished slavery" — it did not (✅ owner 02 adv §6).
**Mains hook:** The rhetoric of universal rights outran the social order.
**Back:** *"Slavery stayed, property limited, women outside, Natives displaced."*

### Flashcard WH-1060 — Declaration vs Constitution vs Bill of Rights (close-option trap)
**Front:** How do the 1776 Declaration, the 1789 Constitution and the Bill of Rights differ?
**Definition (learn):** Three distinct documents, each doing a different job (✅ owner 02 §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Declaration (1776): proclaims independence and rights-based claim to statehood.
- Constitution (in effect 1789): structures the federal republic.
- Bill of Rights (1791): entrenches speech, press, religion and legal safeguards.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1776, 1789, 1791 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** WH-1046, WH-1051, WH-1052.
**Trap:** ❌ Declaration and Constitution are the same document → ✅ different functions.
**Mains hook:** A useful two-part sentence in any introduction.
**Back:** *"Declare, structure, protect."*

### Flashcard WH-1061 — American Revolution traps (MCQ close options)
**Front:** Which statements about the American Revolution must be rejected?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's must-know traps (✅ owner 02 §6, §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- ❌ It was only a tax revolt → ✅ sovereignty, representation and nationhood.
- ❌ Britain fought only the colonies → ✅ after 1778 the conflict was international.
- ❌ The Revolution established democracy → ✅ a federal republic with restricted franchise.
- ❌ It abolished slavery → ✅ it did not.
- ❌ Troop numbers, casualty and revenue figures → ✅ none sourced in owner (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1778 (international turn).
**Links:** WH-1041, WH-1047, WH-1059, WH-1060.
**Trap:** These are standard MCQ pairs.
**Mains hook:** Use a trap-correction in an introduction to signal precision.
**Back:** *"Not just tax, not all-British, not democratic, not abolition."*

### Flashcard WH-1062 — Spain and the Dutch and the wider war
**Front:** Who else hurt Britain during the American war?
**Definition (learn):** Spain and the Dutch joined in weakening Britain after the Franco-American alliance (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- International phase: Saratoga helped bring France in; Spain and the Dutch also hurt Britain (✅ owner 02 §4).
- Imperial power can be broken by combining local resistance with great-power rivalry (⚠️ owner 02 adv §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates in owner for Spain and the Dutch.
**Links:** WH-1047, WH-1058.
**Trap:** Do not overstate Spain's and the Dutch role; the owner names them without dates.
**Mains hook:** Great-power rivalry as the lever of colonial success.
**Back:** *"France joins; Spain and the Dutch press Britain."*

### Flashcard WH-1063 — Lafayette and the French phase
**Front:** Who is named as a figure in the French phase of the American war?
**Definition (learn):** Lafayette is named in the owner's names list for the international phase (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Phase: International phase (Saratoga helps bring France in) (✅ owner 02 §4).
- Lafayette is listed with Cornwallis (✅ owner 02 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates in owner.
**Links:** WH-1047, WH-1048.
**Trap:** Do not invent Lafayette's career dates; ⚠️ not in owner.
**Mains hook:** Name the French contribution in the internationalisation section of a 15-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Lafayette: France's face in the war."*

### Flashcard WH-1064 — How revolutionary? four views
**Front:** What are the four historiographical views on how revolutionary the American Revolution was?
**Definition (learn):** Four historiographical positions in owner 02 adv §2 (⚠️ analytic).
**Breakdown:**
- National-liberation view: colony broke from empire and created a republic (⚠️).
- Constitutional view: limited government, federalism, rights, written constitution (⚠️).
- Social-limits view: slavery survived; women, Native Americans and most Blacks excluded (⚠️).
- Balanced conclusion: politically radical, socially incomplete (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1057, WH-1059, WH-1039.
**Trap:** Do not claim the revolution was socially radical; the balanced line is politically radical, socially incomplete.
**Mains hook:** End with the balanced conclusion unless the question asks specifically for a view.
**Back:** *"Nation, constitution, limits, balance."*

### Flashcard WH-1065 — Phase structure: protest, war, international, settlement
**Front:** What are the four phases of the American Revolution, and which names belong to each?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 02 §4 phase table (✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Protest phase: Stamp Act agitation, boycott, Boston Tea Party; Paine and colonial assemblies.
- War phase: Lexington, militia war, Washington's command.
- International phase: Saratoga helps bring France in; Spain and the Dutch hurt Britain; Lafayette and Cornwallis.
- Settlement phase: Paris peace and constitutional framing; Jefferson and the Philadelphia Convention.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Phases as above.
**Links:** WH-1040, WH-1042, WH-1045, WH-1047, WH-1049, WH-1051.
**Trap:** Keep the phases distinct; do not merge Paris with the Constitution.
**Mains hook:** A four-phase narrative fits a 15-mark chronology.
**Back:** *"Protest, war, world, settlement."*

### Flashcard WH-1066 — American Revolution mark-scaled answer structures and verdicts
**Front:** How should a 10, 15 or 20-mark answer on the American Revolution be built?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 02 §8.3 structures and §8.7 verdicts (⚠️ analytic).
**Breakdown:**
