- 10 marks: thesis → two causes (one economic, one ideological) → constitutional outcome → one exclusion → verdict.
- 15 marks: thesis → causes → escalation → internationalisation → constitutional settlement → exclusions → verdict.
- 20 marks: all of the above plus significance and disciplined French comparison.
- Verdict: "A revolt over the terms of empire became a revolution over the source of authority."
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1041, WH-1057, WH-1064, WH-1011.
**Trap:** Do not describe the Constitution's contents when asked for constitutional significance; explain novelty.
**Mains hook:** Use the 20-mark frame with the French comparison.
**Back:** *"Ten, fifteen, twenty — causes, escalation, outcome, verdict."*

### Flashcard WH-1067 — American Revolution: thesis templates and foundation verdict
**Front:** What is the foundation-of-the-modern-world thesis for American Revolution answers?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 02 §8.2 thesis (⚠️ analytic).
**Breakdown:**
- "Radical in what it created, conservative in what it left standing."
- "Procedural rather than social: a people wrote the terms of its government and held rulers to that text."
- "Began as a constitutional dispute over who may tax and legislate; became national revolution when Britain answered a claim of right with coercion."
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1057, WH-1058, WH-1041.
**Trap:** Do not present the American Revolution as a social revolution; it was constitutional in form.
**Mains hook:** The procedural line is the examinable novelty.
**Back:** *"Created radically, left conservatively, written procedurally."*

### Flashcard WH-1068 — Sequencing caution: American and French revolutions in parallel
**Front:** Did the American Revolution cause the French Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** No. The Enlightenment is the cause of both in parallel; the American Revolution influenced and encouraged the French (✅ owner 01 §8.6 caution, cross-ref owner 02 adv §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Causal arrow: Enlightenment → both revolutions in parallel (✅ owner 01 §8.6 from master chronology).
- Write "influenced and encouraged", not "caused" (✅ owner 01 §8.6).
- Distinct contributions: America — constitutional form and federal republic; France — social destruction of privilege, citizenship and the modern nation (✅ owner 02 adv §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1011, WH-1012, WH-1038.
**Trap:** Do not merge the two into one "Atlantic revolution".
**Mains hook:** The 2019 PYQ requires separate contributions and a shared inheritance.
**Back:** *"Influenced, not caused; parallel, not sequential."*

### Flashcard WH-1069 — Factual-risk controls for the American Revolution
**Front:** Which factual claims about the American Revolution must be avoided?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 02 §8.8 controls (✅ owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Do not conflate 1776 Declaration with the Constitution or Bill of Rights.
- Do not say the Revolution established democracy; it established a federal republic with restricted franchise.
- Do not claim abolition of slavery.
- No troop numbers, casualty figures or tax revenue figures are sourced.
- Do not quote the Declaration beyond phrases verified (National Archives).
- Do not date Boston Tea Party or Lexington loosely.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1773, 1775 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** WH-1060, WH-1061, WH-1043, WH-1045.
**Trap:** Any number not in the owner is ⚠️ and should be omitted.
**Mains hook:** Precision is itself an examiner signal.
**Back:** *"No troops, no tax totals, no abolition, no loose dates."*

### GROUP — French Revolution and Napoleon (owner 03)

### Flashcard WH-1070 — French Revolution as social, political and ideological rupture
**Front:** What kind of rupture was the French Revolution, and how should its phases be taught?
**Definition (learn):** A social, political and ideological rupture in the Old Regime, unfolding in phases from constitutional monarchy to Napoleonic empire (✅ owner 03 basic §1, §4; ⚠️ owner 03 adv §7).
**Breakdown:**
- Phases: 1789–91 (National Assembly, Bastille, Rights of Man, constitutional monarchy); 1792–94 (republic, war, Jacobins, Terror); 1795–99 (Directory); 1799–1815 (Napoleon) (✅ owner 03 §4).
- Causal grammar: fiscal crisis + unequal estates + bread stress + Enlightenment attack on privilege → 1789 rupture → war, counter-revolutionary fear and radicalisation → Jacobin republic → Directory → Napoleon (✅ owner 03 §4 diagram).
- Owner-specified purpose: the strongest answer themes are destruction of feudal privilege, citizenship, popular sovereignty, nationalism, radicalisation under war and the Napoleonic legacy (⚠️ owner 03 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1789 to 1815 (Waterloo) (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Links:** WH-1071 (estates), WH-1075 (fiscal crisis), WH-1101 (causal ladder), WH-1102 (phase table), WH-1103 (traps).
**Trap:** Do not present the Revolution as a single-stage event of 1789 only; UPSC expects phases (✅ owner 03 §6).
**Mains hook:** "Destruction of feudal privilege, citizenship and the nation" as the answer's three content lines; phases as the structure.
**Back:** *"Rupture in phases: privilege falls, republic radicalises, Napoleon consolidates."*

### Flashcard WH-1071 — The three estates and unequal privilege
**Front:** What were the three estates of pre-revolutionary France and what did each enjoy or bear?
**Definition (learn):** The legal orders of Old Regime France in which privilege, not wealth as such, determined the burden (✅ owner 03 basic §3).
**Breakdown:**
- First Estate: clergy enjoyed privilege and tax exemptions; linked the Church to the old order (✅ owner 03 §3).
- Second Estate: nobility held privilege, office and much land (✅ owner 03 §3).
- Third Estate: common people, about 95% of population in old NCERT, carried the tax burden and political exclusion (✅ owner 03 §3; ✅ old NCERT).
- Third Estate was internally divided; bourgeois and peasant aims were not identical (⚠️ owner 03 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ~95% of population in Third Estate (✅ old NCERT via owner 03 §3).
**Links:** WH-1072, WH-1073, WH-1074, WH-1111.
**Trap:** The Third Estate "represented the overwhelming majority but lacked privilege" (✅ owner 03 §6) — do not treat it as a homogeneous class.
**Mains hook:** Target of 1789 was legal privilege, not wealth as such (✅ owner 03 §8.4 analysis).
**Back:** *"Clergy and nobles exempt; the Third Estate pays."*

### Flashcard WH-1072 — The struggle to survive: peasantry, dues and bread
**Front:** Why did peasant distress and the bread crisis matter in 1789?
**Definition (learn):** Rent, dues, taxes and bad harvests made peasant life insecure; subsistence stress mobilised the crowd (✅ owner 03 §3; ✅ NCERT "The struggle to survive").
**Breakdown:**
- Peasantry: rent, dues, taxes and bad harvests; anti-feudal anger (✅ owner 03 §3).
- Peasants wanted relief from dues, rents and feudal burdens; major anti-feudal gains (✅ owner 03 adv §2).
- Bread crisis supplied the mass force behind elite constitutionalism (✅ owner 03 §8.4).
- Bread riots are not by themselves revolutionary programmes (⚠️ owner 03 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric in owner.
**Links:** WH-1071, WH-1075, WH-1073.
**Trap:** Do not equate bread riots with a revolutionary programme.
**Mains hook:** Subsistence stress explains the mass, not the ideology; combine with WH-1075.
**Back:** *"Dues weigh the peasant; bread decides the crowd."*

### Flashcard WH-1073 — Bourgeoisie: wealth without status
**Front:** What did the bourgeoisie want and gain in the French Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** The middle class whose wealth lacked matching status or power; it led institutional rupture (✅ owner 03 §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Wanted: end of privilege, office by merit, property rights (✅ owner 03 adv §2).
- Gains: law, status and institutional power (✅ owner 03 adv §2).
- Political ambition met social grievance (✅ NCERT "A growing middle class envisages an end to privileges" via owner 03 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1071, WH-1074, WH-1097 (popular sovereignty).
**Trap:** Do not write the Revolution as "only bourgeois victory" (✅ owner 03 §6).
**Mains hook:** Bourgeois leadership plus popular radicalisation makes a multi-class revolution.
**Back:** *"Money without rank made the middle class angry."*

### Flashcard WH-1074 — Urban workers, artisans and the sans-culottes
**Front:** What role did urban workers and the Paris crowd play in the Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** The urban poor pressed for cheap bread, political voice and social equality and drove the radical phase (✅ owner 03 §3; ✅ owner 03 adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Long hours, poverty, secret societies and unrest (✅ owner 03 §3).
- Sans-culotte politics: cheap bread, political voice, social equality (✅ owner 03 adv §2).
- Helped the radical phase; economic equality remained incomplete (✅ owner 03 adv §2).
- Jacobin republicanism rested on sans-culotte Paris (⚠️ owner 03 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1072, WH-1082, WH-1083.
**Trap:** Workers and artisans gained political voice without economic equality (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
**Mains hook:** Use the crowd to show the Revolution rested on more than elite thinkers (✅ owner 03 adv §2).
**Back:** *"Bread and voice for the Paris crowd, not yet equality."*

### Flashcard WH-1075 — Fiscal crisis and the Estates-General (May 1789)
**Front:** Why did the fiscal crisis open the political door in 1789?
**Definition (learn):** The monarchy was financially troubled and pushed towards bankruptcy, and the Estates-General was summoned in May 1789 (✅ old NCERT via owner 03 §3, §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- Old NCERT: the monarchy was financially troubled and pushed toward bankruptcy (✅ owner 03 §3).
- Estates-General summoned: May 1789 (✅ owner 03 §1, §8.4).
- Explains "why in 1789" and not earlier (⚠️ owner 03 §8.4).
- Fiscal crisis had been survived before; it became fatal only with ideology and subsistence stress (⚠️ owner 03 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** May 1789 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** WH-1072, WH-1076, WH-1008 (ideas + crisis formula in Part 1 of this file).
**Trap:** Fiscal crisis alone is not a sufficient cause.
**Mains hook:** Fiscal breakdown is the timing variable; ideology gave legitimacy; subsistence gave force (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Empty treasury, May 1789 summons."*

### Flashcard WH-1076 — 17 June 1789: Third Estate becomes the National Assembly
**Front:** What happened on 17 June 1789 and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** The Third Estate declared itself the National Assembly, shifting sovereignty from king to nation (✅ owner 03 §1; ✅ Wikipedia "French Revolution" gives 17 June).
**Breakdown:**
- Third Estate declared itself National Assembly on 17 June 1789 (✅ owner 03 §1; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Sovereignty began shifting from king to nation (✅ owner 03 §1).
- Popular sovereignty: a permanent question of constituent power (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17 June 1789 (✅ owner 03; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1077, WH-1097.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 17 June National Assembly with the Tennis Court Oath (20 June).
**Mains hook:** The first formal claim of sovereignty by the nation rather than the monarch.
**Back:** *"Seventeen June: the Third Estate becomes the nation."*

### Flashcard WH-1077 — Tennis Court Oath (20 June 1789)
**Front:** What was the Tennis Court Oath and what did it establish?
**Definition (learn):** A pledge in which constitution-making became the revolutionary claim (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 20 June 1789 (✅ owner 03 §1; the Wikipedia article implies 20 June without stating it — ⚠️ exact day as implied).
- Clergy joined the Third Estate by 19 June, per Wikipedia (✅ Wikipedia).
- Constitution-making became the revolutionary claim (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 20 June 1789.
**Links:** WH-1076, WH-1078.
**Trap:** Tennis Court Oath is a constitution pledge; do not confuse it with the storming of the Bastille.
**Mains hook:** Shows the revolution claimed the right to write the constitution itself.
**Back:** *"Twenty June: no dissolution until a constitution."*

### Flashcard WH-1078 — Storming of the Bastille (14 July 1789)
**Front:** What was the Bastille and why did its storming matter?
**Definition (learn):** The storming of 14 July 1789 that made the fall of autocracy a mass symbol (✅ owner 03 §1; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 14 July 1789 (✅ owner 03 §6; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Symbolic value mattered more than prisoner count (✅ owner 03 §6 trap).
- Fall of autocracy became a mass symbol (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 14 July 1789 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1079, WH-1076.
**Trap:** ❌ "Bastille mattered because prisoners were many" → ✅ its symbolic value.
**Mains hook:** Symbolism as the mass mobilisation of the Revolution.
**Back:** *"Fourteen July: a prison falls, a symbol rises."*

### Flashcard WH-1079 — Declaration of the Rights of Man and Citizen (August 1789)
**Front:** What did the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen proclaim, and what did it not do?
**Definition (learn):** Proclaimed equality before law and rights of citizenship; it transformed law and legitimacy but did not end inequality (✅ owner 03 §1, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Date: August 1789, approved 26 August 1789 (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 03 §1 "Aug 1789").
- Equality before law and rights of citizenship (✅ owner 03 §6).
- Rights became a public standard to measure exclusion (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Did not grant universal suffrage in 1789 (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 26 August 1789 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1014 (exclusions in Part 1), WH-1095 (rights as public standard), WH-1012 (Atlantic comparison).
**Trap:** Do not claim the Declaration granted universal suffrage or ended all inequality (✅ owner 03 §8.8, §6).
**Mains hook:** The Declaration is a standard, not a settlement.
**Back:** *"Equal before law, not yet equal at the ballot."*

### Flashcard WH-1080 — Constitutional monarchy phase (1791)
**Front:** What characterised the 1791 phase of the Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** The old regime was legally broken but instability continued under a constitutional monarchy (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Phase 1789–91: National Assembly, Bastille, Rights of Man, constitutional monarchy; end of legal privilege begins (✅ owner 03 §4).
- 1791: constitutional monarchy phase; old regime legally broken but instability continues (✅ owner 03 §1).
- Unresolved fiscal, religious and war crises (⚠️ owner 03 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1791 (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Links:** WH-1070, WH-1081, WH-1102.
**Trap:** 1791 constitutional monarchy is not the end of the revolution.
**Mains hook:** Why the constitutional settlement of 1791 could not hold.
**Back:** *"Ninety-one: a king under a constitution, and no peace."*

### Flashcard WH-1081 — Monarchy abolished and republic declared (September 1792)
**Front:** When was the French monarchy abolished and the republic declared?
**Definition (learn):** The Revolution turned more radical when the monarchy was abolished and a republic declared (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- September 1792: monarchy abolished, republic declared (✅ Wikipedia "French Revolution": "September" 1792).
- Non-propertied men gained equal political rights in the 1792–93 phase (✅ old NCERT via owner 03 §8.5; ⚠️ owner 03 adv §6 says 1792).
- Republic, war, Jacobins, Terror follow (✅ owner 03 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** September 1792.
**Links:** WH-1082, WH-1083, WH-1095.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1792 republic with the 1804 empire.
**Mains hook:** Radicalisation followed the republic, under war pressure.
**Back:** *"Ninety-two: king gone, republic declared."*

### Flashcard WH-1082 — Execution of Louis XVI (January 1793)
**Front:** When was Louis XVI executed and what does it mark?
**Definition (learn):** The regicide of January 1793, which the Wikipedia article gives as "January 1793" (✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- January 1793 (✅ Wikipedia; regicide is listed in the owner's date control, ✅ owner 03 §9).
- Marks the radicalisation of the republic under war and counter-revolutionary fear (⚠️ owner 03 §4 synthesis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** January 1793 (exact day not stated in the fetched text; ⚠️).
**Links:** WH-1081, WH-1083.
**Trap:** Do not date regicide to 1792; the republic was declared in 1792.
**Mains hook:** Use regicide to show radicalisation under war.
**Back:** *"Ninety-three: the king is executed."*

### Flashcard WH-1083 — Jacobins and the Reign of Terror (1793–94)
**Front:** Who were the Jacobins and why did the Terror happen?
**Definition (learn):** Jacobins were associated with radical republicanism and the Reign of Terror; defence of the revolution and coercion combined (✅ owner 03 §6, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Jacobins believed in direct democracy and, fearing danger, adopted harsh measures (✅ old NCERT via owner 03 adv §3).
- Old NCERT: about 17,000 tried and executed in 14 months (✅ owner 03 adv §3).
- Reign of Terror ended July 1794 with the Thermidorian reaction (✅ Wikipedia "French Revolution").
- Terror as three causes: war emergency, ideological logic, factional struggle (⚠️ owner 03 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1793–94; ~17,000 executed (in 14 months, old NCERT); ended July 1794 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1084, WH-1093, WH-1074, WH-1081.
**Trap:** Terror neither "necessary" nor "insane" as a complete verdict; state the cost (⚠️ owner 03 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** The Terror is the first warning of rights invoked against rights.
**Back:** *"Seventeen thousand in fourteen months; July 1794 ends it."*

### Flashcard WH-1084 — Jacobin constitution: rights, insurrection, work
**Front:** What did the Jacobin constitution provide, according to old NCERT?
**Definition (learn):** Universal male political rights, the right of insurrection and the principle that government must provide work or livelihood (✅ old NCERT via owner 03 adv §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Universal male political rights (✅ owner 03 adv §3).
- Right of insurrection (✅ owner 03 adv §3).
- Principle that government must provide work or livelihood (✅ owner 03 adv §3).
- Jacobin constitution coexisted with the Terror; the Terror cannot be dismissed as mere madness (⚠️ owner 03 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Universal male suffrage in the Jacobin constitution; ⚠️ its implementation is not in the owner.
**Links:** WH-1083, WH-1079, WH-1074.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the Jacobin constitutional provisions with their implementation under the Terror.
**Mains hook:** Mass democracy, war emergency and coercive violence in one package (⚠️ owner 03 adv §3).
**Back:** *"Male vote, right to rise, work for all — under the knife."*

### Flashcard WH-1085 — Directory (1795–99): instability and army
**Front:** What characterised the Directory and why did it weaken civilian rule?
**Definition (learn):** The 1795–99 regime of propertied republicans that retreated from the Terror but faced instability (✅ owner 03 §4, §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- 1795–99: Directory (✅ owner 03 §1, §4).
- Retreat from Terror (✅ owner 03 §8.6).
- Instability and fatigue weakened civilian rule; power shifted toward the army (✅ owner 03 §4, §8.6).
- Propertied republicans as the coalition (⚠️ owner 03 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1795–99 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** WH-1086, WH-1083, WH-1102.
**Trap:** The Directory is not Napoleon's rule; it is the bridge between republic and coup.
**Mains hook:** Instability shifted power to the army; Napoleon's coup was the result.
**Back:** *"Ninety-five to ninety-nine: propertied republic, weak and tired."*

### Flashcard WH-1086 — Coup of 18 Brumaire (9 November 1799)
**Front:** When and by whom was the Brumaire coup carried out?
**Definition (learn):** Napoleon's coup that started the authoritarian-consolidating phase (✅ owner 03 §1; ✅ Wikipedia gives "9 November 1799").
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 9 November 1799 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: 1799 Napoleon's coup (✅ Britannica via owner 03 §1).
- Revolution enters a new authoritarian-consolidating phase (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 9 November 1799 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1085, WH-1087.
**Trap:** Do not date Brumaire to 1804 — 1804 is the empire and Code.
**Mains hook:** The army's rise under the Directory led to Brumaire.
**Back:** *"Ninety-nine, Brumaire: the army takes the state."*

### Flashcard WH-1087 — Napoleonic Code (1804): legal equality survives
**Front:** What did the Napoleonic Code (1804) preserve?
**Definition (learn):** Legal equality and anti-feudal reform codified and carried beyond France (✅ owner 03 §5, §6; ✅ Britannica via owner 03 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Napoleon proclaimed emperor, 1804 (✅ owner 03 §1).
- Legal equality and modern civil order preserved (✅ old NCERT and Britannica via owner 03 §5).
- Mechanism of transmission: codification carried the settlement beyond France (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
- The same Code narrowed married women's legal position; transmission was selective (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1804 (✅ owner 03; ✅ Britannica via owner).
**Links:** WH-1088, WH-1099, WH-1011.
**Trap:** Do not date the Napoleonic Code to the 1790s; it is 1804 (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Equality before law as the durable transplant of the Revolution.
**Back:** *"Eighteen-oh-four: the Code keeps equality, narrows women."*

### Flashcard WH-1088 — Napoleon: heir or gravedigger?
**Front:** How should Napoleon be assessed as heir to or destroyer of the Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Napoleon as heir (legal codification, merit, anti-feudal reform) and limiter (empire, censorship, conquest, concentration of power) (✅ owner 03 §5 and §4 tables).
**Breakdown:**
- Preserved: abolition of feudal remnants; Napoleonic Code; careers open to talent; nationalism and anti-feudal reform spread across Europe (✅ owner 03 adv §4).
- Curtailed: republic ended; empire and censorship; war became imperial conquest; popular sovereignty subordinated (✅ owner 03 adv §4).
- Split verdict: "Heir to the social revolution, betrayer of the political revolution" (⚠️ owner 03 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1799–1815 (✅ owner 03 §4).
**Links:** WH-1089, WH-1086, WH-1087, WH-1105.
**Trap:** Napoleon did not simply restore the old order; he preserved anti-feudal legal change (✅ owner 03 §6).
**Mains hook:** Napoleon as the Revolution's consolidator and reverser (20-mark evaluation).
**Back:** *"Heir of the code, gravedigger of the republic."*

### Flashcard WH-1089 — Napoleon stabilised the social revolution and reversed its politics
**Front:** What is the best formulation of Napoleon's relationship to the revolution?
**Definition (learn):** "Napoleon stabilised the social revolution while reversing much of its political republicanism" (⚠️ owner 03 adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Social settlement kept: legal equality, anti-feudal reform (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Political republicanism reversed: censorship, dynasty, war (✅ owner 03 §9 mechanism control).
- Napoleon did not end all revolutionary change (✅ owner 03 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1088, WH-1087, WH-1090.
**Trap:** "Napoleon ended all revolutionary change" is wrong (✅ owner 03 §6).
**Mains hook:** Use this to answer "Was Napoleon the continuer or betrayer?" (✅ owner 03 §7).
**Back:** *"Keep the social, end the republic."*

### Flashcard WH-1090 — Waterloo (1815): end of the Napoleonic phase
**Front:** What does Waterloo (1815) mark in the French Revolution's timeline?
**Definition (learn):** The end of the Napoleonic phase; the revolutionary legacy remained (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- 1815: Waterloo; Napoleonic phase ends (✅ owner 03 §1).
- Transition to Vienna (Topic 05 owner) and restoration (✅ owner 03 §9 boundary).
- Revolutionary legacy remains (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1815 (✅ owner 03 §9 date control).
**Links:** WH-1089, WH-1100 (non-linear legacy ladder), WH-1171 (Vienna settlement).
**Trap:** Waterloo ends the Napoleonic phase, not the revolutionary legacy.
**Mains hook:** Link to the Vienna settlement as a restoration response.
**Back:** *"Fifteen: Napoleon falls; the Revolution's ideas stay."*

### Flashcard WH-1091 — "Did women have a revolution?" (gendered limits of citizenship)
**Front:** What does the NCERT question "Did women have a revolution?" test?
**Definition (learn):** Women were central to revolutionary action yet denied equal political citizenship (✅ NCERT section via owner 03 §2, §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Women central to revolutionary action (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Denied equal political citizenship (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- The Code narrowed married women's legal position (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
- Citizenship gendered and property-conditioned by turns (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1014 (exclusions), WH-1096 (citizenship), WH-1087.
**Trap:** Do not present 1789 as universal citizenship for women.
**Mains hook:** The exclusion must appear in the same answer as the proclamation.
**Back:** *"Central to the act, outside the vote."*

### Flashcard WH-1092 — Abolition of slavery in French colonies: contradiction
**Front:** How did the French Revolution's abolition of slavery in the colonies proceed and reverse?
**Definition (learn):** Universal rights collided with the colonial slave economy; abolition in the revolutionary period was contested and later reversed under Napoleon before final abolition in 1848 (✅ owner 03 §8.5; ✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: abolition attempted in the revolutionary period, reversed under Napoleon (✅ owner 01 §8.6; ✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Final French abolition: 1848 (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
- ⚠️ The 1794 decree and the 1802 reinstatement are standard knowledge not confirmed by the fetched text; the Wikipedia section was truncated — ⚠️.
- The revolutionary contradiction is a vital check on any simplistic narrative (✅ owner 03 adv §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1848 final abolition (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Links:** WH-1091, WH-1014, WH-1059 (American slavery survived).
**Trap:** Do not say the Revolution "abolished slavery permanently" (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Universal rights and empire collided; neither revolution resolved slavery.
**Back:** *"Abolished, reversed, abolished again in 1848."*

### Flashcard WH-1093 — Terror: three causes, one cost
**Front:** How should the Terror be explained?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's handling rule: explain the Terror as war emergency plus ideological logic plus factional struggle; refuse both "necessary" and "insane" as complete verdicts (✅ owner 03 §8.6, ⚠️ analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- War emergency and counter-revolutionary fear (✅ owner 03 §4).
- Ideological logic of direct democracy and the republic (✅ owner 03 adv §3).
- Factional struggle among revolutionaries (⚠️ owner 03 §8.6).
- State the cost explicitly (⚠️ owner 03 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ~17,000 in 14 months (✅ old NCERT).
**Links:** WH-1083, WH-1084, WH-1108.
**Trap:** Emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification (✅ owner 03 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** The Terror as the classic example of rights invoked against rights.
**Back:** *"War, ideology, faction — cost stated."*

### Flashcard WH-1094 — Enduring relevance: transmission of principles, not institutions (2025 PYQ)
**Front:** What is the owner's answer frame for the 2025 GS-I question on enduring relevance?
**Definition (learn):** "The French Revolution has enduring relevance to the contemporary world. Explain. (Answer in 250 words)" — verified PYQ wording, 15 marks (✅ owner 03 adv §7).
**Breakdown:**
- Answer through enduring principles and political vocabulary: legal equality, citizenship, popular sovereignty, secular public authority and nationalism (⚠️ owner 03 adv §7).
- Qualify: women, colonial subjects and poorer citizens did not immediately receive equal power (⚠️ owner 03 adv §7).
- "Enduring relevance" does not mean institutions survive unchanged (⚠️ owner 03 adv §7).
- Verdict: relevant as the origin of the standard by which states are judged, and of its critique (⚠️ owner 03 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-I; 15 marks; 250 words (✅ owner 03 basic §9 PYQ integration).
**Links:** WH-1095, WH-1096, WH-1097, WH-1098, WH-1099, WH-1100.
**Trap:** Do not claim 1789's institutions survive intact.
**Mains hook:** The whole answer is "principles, not institutions, with exclusions in the same paragraph."
**Back:** *"Principles travel, institutions don't — exclusions included."*

### Flashcard WH-1095 — Rights as public standard
**Front:** What enduring principle does the Declaration of 1789 supply?
**Definition (learn):** Rights declarations became the normal form in which political legitimacy is claimed and criticised; the Declaration created a standard against which exclusion could be measured (✅ owner 03 §8.5; ⚠️ analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- Declaration proclaimed equality before law and rights of citizenship, August 1789 (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Rights-declarations as normal form of legitimacy (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
- Declaration did not end inequality; it created a measurable standard (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1789 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** WH-1079, WH-1094.
**Trap:** Do not present the Declaration as universal immediately.
**Mains hook:** Measured exclusion is the critical use of rights.
**Back:** *"A yardstick, not a settlement."*

### Flashcard WH-1096 — Citizenship replacing subjecthood
**Front:** How did the Revolution change the political unit from subject to citizen?
**Definition (learn):** Shift from subject-of-a-ruler to citizen-with-claims; equal political rights for non-propertied men dated by old NCERT to the 1792–93 phase (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- New language, symbols and forms of address remade everyday life (✅ NCERT via owner 03 §8.5).
- Equal political rights for non-propertied men: 1792–93 phase (✅ old NCERT via owner 03 §8.5).
- Citizenship gendered and property-conditioned by turns; contested, not conferred once (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1792–93 (✅ old NCERT via owner).
**Links:** WH-1091, WH-1081, WH-1094.
**Trap:** Do not present citizenship as conferred once.
**Mains hook:** Citizenship as a contested category.
**Back:** *"From subject to claimant."*

### Flashcard WH-1097 — Popular sovereignty and constituent power
**Front:** Why is popular sovereignty a permanent question, not a guarantee of liberty?
**Definition (learn):** The Third Estate's declaration and the Tennis Court Oath made constituent power the question "who may make the constitution" (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- 17 June 1789: Third Estate declares National Assembly (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Tennis Court Oath (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Monarchy abolished 1792 (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Invoked by the Terror as well as by liberals; not a guarantee of liberty (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17 June 1789; 1792.
**Links:** WH-1076, WH-1077, WH-1006 (Rousseau, in Part 1 file), WH-1083.
**Trap:** Popular sovereignty does not by itself guarantee liberty.
**Mains hook:** The Terror shows the danger of the general will (see Part 1 Rousseau card).
**Back:** *"Who writes the constitution is the permanent question."*

### Flashcard WH-1098 — Secular public authority and anti-clericalism
**Front:** How did the Revolution contribute to the argument for secular public authority?
**Definition (learn):** The Revolution attacked the ideological authority of the Church and the First Estate's privileges (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Public office and law need no religious sanction (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
- Revolutionary anti-clericalism was itself coercive; secularism has plural later forms (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1071 (First Estate), WH-1007 (Voltaire, Part 1).
**Trap:** Secularism is not identical with revolutionary anti-clericalism.
**Mains hook:** Qualify the claim with the coercion of anti-clericalism.
**Back:** *"Law without priests — but not without force."*

### Flashcard WH-1099 — Equality before law as durable transplant (Napoleonic Code)
**Front:** How did codification transmit the Revolution's legal settlement beyond France?
**Definition (learn):** The Code preserved legal equality and anti-feudal reform; transmission was selective (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Napoleonic Code (1804) preserved legal equality and anti-feudal reform (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Old NCERT: feudal laws annulled and gains survived Napoleon's seizure of power (✅ owner 03 §5).
- The Code narrowed married women's legal position (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1804 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** WH-1087, WH-1091, WH-1094.
**Trap:** Transmission is selective; do not present the Code as purely progressive.
**Mains hook:** Shows the mechanism of transmission.
**Back:** *"Codes carry equality — and its limits."*

### Flashcard WH-1100 — Non-linear legacy ladder (1789 to 19th-century nationalism)
**Front:** Why must the French Revolution's legacy be drawn as a non-linear ladder?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's ladder refuses a straight-line "1789 → modern democracy" story (✅ owner 03 §8.5; ⚠️ analysis).
**Breakdown:**
- 1789 rights claim → 1793–94 Terror (rights invoked against rights) → 1799–1815 Napoleon (social settlement kept, republic ended) → 1815 Vienna restoration → 1830 / 1848 renewed revolutions → 19th-century nationalism and constitutionalism (✅ owner 03 §8.5).
- Principles survived through repeated contestation, defeat and re-assertion (⚠️ owner 03 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1789, 1793–94, 1799–1815, 1815, 1830, 1848.
**Links:** WH-1090, WH-1094, WH-1101.
**Trap:** The Revolution did not survive through uninterrupted institutional continuity.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to qualify any "legacy" answer.
**Back:** *"Rights, Terror, Napoleon, Vienna, 1830, 1848 — contested, not continuous."*

### Flashcard WH-1101 — The causal ladder: fiscal crisis, estates, bread, Enlightenment → 1789 → Napoleon
**Front:** Trace the causal chain of the French Revolution from crisis to Napoleon.
**Definition (learn):** The owner's diagram of the revolutionary sequence (✅ owner 03 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Inputs: fiscal crisis + unequal estates + bread stress + Enlightenment attack on privilege (✅ owner 03 §4).
- 1789 political rupture (✅ owner 03 §4).
- War + counter-revolutionary fear + radicalisation (✅ owner 03 §4).
- Jacobin republic → Directory → Napoleon (✅ owner 03 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1789; 1792–94; 1795–99; 1799–1815.
**Links:** WH-1070, WH-1075, WH-1102, WH-1008.
**Trap:** Ideas alone do not explain the timing (⚠️ owner 03 §8.2).
**Mains hook:** Causal thesis: ideas converted fiscal-agrarian crisis into a challenge to legitimacy of privilege (⚠️ owner 03 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Money, estates, bread and ideas — then war, republic, Napoleon."*

### Flashcard WH-1102 — Phase table: coalitions and what each achieved
**Front:** Which social coalition led each phase of the French Revolution, and why did each give way?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 03 §8.6 evidence bank (⚠️ coalitions analytic; achievements ✅).
**Breakdown:**
- 1789–91: bourgeois-led constitutionalism with peasant and crowd pressure; achieved end of legal privilege, the Declaration and constitutional monarchy; gave way on unresolved fiscal, religious and war crises.
- 1792–94: Jacobin republicanism on sans-culotte Paris; achieved republic, wider male political rights and national defence; gave way as emergency coercion outlived its justification.
- 1795–99: propertied republicans; retreat from Terror; gave way as instability shifted power to the army.
- 1799–1815: army, notables and codifying state; Napoleonic Code, careers open to talent, anti-feudal reform exported; gave way to empire, censorship and conquest.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Phase dates as above.
**Links:** WH-1080, WH-1083, WH-1085, WH-1088.
**Trap:** Keep each coalition distinct; do not say "the bourgeoisie made the Revolution" for all phases.
**Mains hook:** A phase table is the quickest structure for a "trace the phases" answer.
**Back:** *"Liberal-bourgeois, Jacobin, propertied, army."*

### Flashcard WH-1103 — French Revolution traps: phases, Bastille, Declaration, Napoleon
**Front:** Which close-option claims about the French Revolution must be rejected?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's §6 traps (✅ owner 03 §6).
**Breakdown:**
- ❌ Single-stage 1789 event → ✅ phases.
- ❌ Bastille mattered because prisoners were many → ✅ symbolic value.
- ❌ Declaration of Rights immediately ended inequality → ✅ it transformed law and legitimacy; practice conflict-ridden.
- ❌ Napoleon simply restored the old order → ✅ preserved anti-feudal legal change while ending the republic.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1789, 1799–1815.
**Links:** WH-1070, WH-1078, WH-1079, WH-1088.
**Trap:** These are standard MCQ close-option pairs.
**Mains hook:** Use one correction in the introduction to show precision.
**Back:** *"Phases, symbols, law not equality, Code not restoration."*

### Flashcard WH-1104 — French Revolution traps: bourgeois, Terror, Napoleon's end
**Front:** Which further claims about the French Revolution must be rejected?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 03 adv §6 traps (✅).
**Breakdown:**
- ❌ French Revolution = only bourgeois victory → ✅ popular classes drove radicalisation.
- ❌ Terror proves the Revolution failed → ✅ show both survival-context and coercive cost.
- ❌ Napoleon ended all revolutionary change → ✅ he ended the republic, not the anti-feudal transformation.
- ❌ Full social equality achieved → ✅ workers and artisans lacked economic equality.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1073, WH-1074, WH-1083, WH-1089.
**Trap:** Standard MCQ traps.
**Mains hook:** Use to qualify the "transformation" thesis.
**Back:** *"Not only bourgeois, not proof of failure, not all change ended."*

### Flashcard WH-1105 — Verdict: Napoleon as heir to the social revolution, betrayer of the political one
**Front:** What is the split verdict on Napoleon?
**Definition (learn):** "Heir to the social revolution, betrayer of the political revolution — which is precisely why the Restoration could not simply undo 1789" (✅ owner 03 §8.7 verdict scaffold).
**Breakdown:**
- Social revolution: legal status, anti-feudal settlement (✅ owner 03 adv §4).
- Political revolution: republic and popular sovereignty subordinated (✅ owner 03 adv §4).
- The verdict also explains why Vienna could not undo 1789 (⚠️ owner 03 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1799–1815.
**Links:** WH-1088, WH-1089, WH-1100.
**Trap:** Do not choose one side without evidence (⚠️ owner 03 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** Probable question: "Evaluate the argument that Napoleon completed the social revolution while aborting the political revolution."
**Back:** *"Social heir, political betrayer."*

### Flashcard WH-1106 — Verdict: how far did the Revolution transform society?
**Front:** What is the measured verdict on the Revolution's social transformation?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's verdict scaffold (⚠️ owner 03 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Completely at the level of legal status and political legitimacy.
- Substantially for the peasantry through the destruction of feudal dues.
- Only partially for urban workers.
- Least of all for women and colonial subjects.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1072, WH-1074, WH-1091, WH-1092.
**Trap:** Do not write "it changed everything" unqualified (⚠️ owner 03 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** Use this as a 15-mark conclusion.
**Back:** *"Law total, peasants substantial, workers partial, women least."*

### Flashcard WH-1107 — Factual-risk controls for the French Revolution
**Front:** Which French Revolution claims must be avoided or kept precise?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's §8.8 controls (✅ owner 03 §8.8).
**Breakdown:**
- Casualty figure for the Terror: use the sourced ~17,000 executed in ~14 months (old NCERT); do not invent totals.
- Declaration did not grant universal suffrage in 1789.
- Abolition of slavery was not permanent; reversed under Napoleon; final French abolition 1848.
- Napoleonic Code is 1804, not the 1790s.
- Do not say the Revolution caused any specific later revolution; say "influenced" or "supplied vocabulary".
- Do not quote Rousseau, Robespierre or the Declaration verbatim unless verified.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17,000; 14 months; 1804; 1848.
**Links:** WH-1083, WH-1087, WH-1092, WH-1068.
**Trap:** Standard factual-risk MCQs.
**Mains hook:** Precision in numbers signals discipline.
**Back:** *"Seventeen thousand, fourteen months, eighteen-oh-four, forty-eight."*

### Flashcard WH-1108 — 2025 GS-I PYQ wording and route (French Revolution)
**Front:** What is the verified PYQ route for the French Revolution's enduring relevance?
**Definition (learn):** Verified paper wording: "The French Revolution has enduring relevance to the contemporary world. Explain. (Answer in 250 words)" (✅ owner 03 adv §7, §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Paper: GS-I, 2025, Q 13 (✅ owner 03 §9 PYQ integration).
- Directive: Explain, 15 marks, 250 words (✅ owner 03 §9).
- Owner requirement: context, core dimensions, evidence/examples, counterpoint and concise conclusion (✅ owner 03 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025; 15 marks; 250 words.
**Links:** WH-1094, WH-1038 (2019 PYQ).
**Trap:** Do not convert it into an objective question; the ledger shows no objective key.
**Mains hook:** Use the principles frame, qualify with exclusions.
**Back:** *"Twenty-five: enduring principles, not enduring institutions."*

### Flashcard WH-1109 — Mechanism control: structural privilege, fiscal breakdown, subsistence, political trigger
**Front:** How do structural privilege, fiscal breakdown and subsistence stress differ from political triggers?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 03 §9 mechanism control: these are distinct layers of causation (✅ owner 03 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Structural privilege: legal entrenchment of Estates (✅).
- Fiscal breakdown: monarchy pushed to bankruptcy (✅).
- Subsistence stress: bread crisis and tax burden (✅).
- Political triggers and popular agency: Estates-General, Assembly, Bastille (✅ owner 03 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** May 1789; 14 July 1789.
**Links:** WH-1071, WH-1075, WH-1072, WH-1078.
**Trap:** Do not conflate structural cause with trigger.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish layers of causation in a 15-mark causes answer.
**Back:** *"Structure, money, bread, trigger."*

### Flashcard WH-1110 — Napoleon's legal equality, censorship, dynasty and war
**Front:** What did Napoleon restrict while preserving legal equality and administrative rationalisation?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's mechanism control: Napoleon restricted sovereignty through censorship, dynasty and war (✅ owner 03 §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Preserved legal equality and administrative rationalisation (✅ owner 03 §9).
- Restricted sovereignty via censorship (✅ owner 03 §9).
- Dynasty: empire and dynastic rule (✅ owner 03 §9).
- War: imperial conquest (✅ owner 03 adv §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1804 (empire).
**Links:** WH-1087, WH-1088, WH-1089.
**Trap:** Napoleon's empire is not the Republic.
**Mains hook:** Use as the restriction side of the heir/gravedigger verdict.
**Back:** *"Censor, dynast, conquer — keep the code."*

### Flashcard WH-1111 — Who led and who lost: social forces ledger
**Front:** Which social forces gained and lost in the French Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 03 adv §2 ledger (✅).
**Breakdown:**
- Bourgeoisie: gains in law, status, institutional power.
- Peasantry: major anti-feudal gains.
- Urban workers / sans-culotte: radical phase help; economic equality incomplete.
- Jacobins: saved revolution militarily; associated with Terror.
- Army under Napoleon: vehicle for authoritarian consolidation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1071, WH-1073, WH-1074, WH-1083, WH-1085.
**Trap:** Don't present one group as the sole winner.
**Mains hook:** The revolution rested on elite thinkers and popular classes together.
**Back:** *"Bourgeois law, peasant dues, crowd bread, Jacobin war, army rule."*

### Flashcard WH-1112 — Terror and democracy: why the question is tricky
**Front:** Why is the Terror a difficult link between democracy and coercion?
**Definition (learn):** The Jacobins believed in direct democracy and, fearing danger, used harsh measures; the Terror joined mass democracy, war emergency and coercive violence (✅ owner 03 adv §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Direct democracy belief (✅ old NCERT).
- Harsh measures (✅ old NCERT).
- Universal male rights in the Jacobin constitution (✅).
- Joined three things in one unstable package (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ~17,000 in 14 months (✅ old NCERT).
**Links:** WH-1083, WH-1084, WH-1093.
**Trap:** Do not romanticise or dismiss the Terror as mere madness.
**Mains hook:** Use as the centrepiece of the "rights against rights" answer.
**Back:** *"Democracy with a knife in hand."*

### GROUP — Industrial Revolution (owner 04)

### Flashcard WH-1113 — Industrial Revolution as technological and social revolution
**Front:** What is the Industrial Revolution, and why must it be written as both a technological and a social revolution?
**Definition (learn):** The rapid changes in the later 18th century that revolutionised production and created an industrial economy; transition from agrarian and handicraft economy to industry and machine manufacture (✅ old NCERT; ✅ Britannica via owner 04 §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Links directly to capitalism, the factory system and steam power (✅ owner 04 §2).
- Technological side: mechanised textiles, steam power, iron and coal (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Social side: urbanisation, new classes, harsh labour regime, state regulation (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives "c. 1750" as the beginning in England (✅ old NCERT). Wikipedia gives "around 1760" as the start and "by about 1840" for spread to the continent and US (✅ Wikipedia "Industrial Revolution"). ⚠️ Owner-vs-source difference of about a decade: use "c. 1750–60 onward in England" and avoid a single year (owner §8.9).
**Links:** WH-1114, WH-1115, WH-1131, WH-1132.
**Trap:** Do not reduce the Industrial Revolution to machines alone; UPSC expects social effects (✅ owner 04 §6).
**Mains hook:** "A technological revolution that became a social question." Use both halves in the introduction.
**Back:** *"Machines made it; the social question made it political."*

### Flashcard WH-1114 — Industrial Revolution mini-timeline (1764–1830s)
**Front:** What are the dated markers of the Industrial Revolution in the owner's timeline?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 04 §1 chronology ladder with the function of each marker (✅ owner 04 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- c. 1750 — Industrial Revolution begins in England (✅ old NCERT).
- 1764 — Hargreaves' spinning jenny (✅ owner 04).
- 1769 — Arkwright's water frame and Watt's steam-engine breakthrough era (✅ owner 04).
- 1785 — Cartwright's power loom (✅ owner 04).
- 1793 — Eli Whitney's cotton gin (✅ owner 04).
- 1802 — First Factory Act in England (✅ owner 04).
- 1824 — Anti-union laws repealed (✅ old NCERT).
- 1830 — First railway for passengers (✅ old NCERT via owner 04; the fetched Wikipedia text did not reach the railway section).
- 1830s–40s — Chartism (✅ owner 04).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** WH-1122 to WH-1127, WH-1134, WH-1135, WH-1136, WH-1141.
**Trap:** Keep inventions, legal status and effects as separate markers (✅ owner 04 §9 date control).
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show mechanisation then labour response.
**Back:** *"Sixty-four jenny, sixty-nine water and steam, eighty-five loom, ninety-three gin, oh-two Act."*

### Flashcard WH-1115 — Why England first? A conjunction of conditions
**Front:** Why did the Industrial Revolution begin in England?
**Definition (learn):** Britain industrialised not because of one advantage but because several conditions were simultaneously available: capital, coal, released labour, agricultural surplus, markets and a favourable legal-political order (⚠️ owner 04 §8.2 analysis; ✅ conditions from old NCERT).
**Breakdown:**
- Capital accumulation (WH-1116).
- Coal and iron (WH-1117).
- Released labour via enclosure (WH-1118).
- Agricultural surplus (WH-1119).
- Shipping and markets (WH-1120).
- Stable political order (WH-1121).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric (owner bans figures).
**Links:** WH-1113, WH-1116 to WH-1121, WH-1139.
**Trap:** ❌ Industrialisation began equally across Europe → ✅ began in England; spread unevenly (✅ owner 04 §6, §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Thesis: "a conjunction, not a cause." Name four or five conditions with how each operated.
**Back:** *"Capital, coal, cheap hands, bread, ships, law — all at once."*

### Flashcard WH-1116 — Condition: capital accumulation from trade and colonies
**Front:** How did capital accumulation make British industrialisation financeable?
**Definition (learn):** Expanding trade, colonies and plundered wealth supplied investible capital (✅ old NCERT via owner 04 §3, §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- Made fixed investment in mills and engines financeable before returns arrived (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
- Colonial profit is one source among several; do not make empire the single cause (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1115, WH-1139.
**Trap:** Do not say empire alone financed British industry.
**Mains hook:** Use as one of the conditions in the "why England first" answer.
**Back:** *"Trade and plunder financed the first machines."*

### Flashcard WH-1117 — Condition: coal and iron
**Front:** How did coal and iron support the early Industrial Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** England had coal and iron in abundance; coal removed the ceiling that water power imposed on mill siting and scale (✅ owner 04 §3; ⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- Machinery and fuel base (✅ owner 04 §3).
- Coal and iron support machinery production (✅ owner 04 §4 "Metals").
- Resources alone explain nothing; other regions had coal and did not industrialise first (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1115, WH-1127 (steam engine).
**Trap:** Resource abundance is necessary context, not sufficient cause.
**Mains hook:** Coal's role was to lift the water-power constraint.
**Back:** *"Coal lifted the water ceiling."*

### Flashcard WH-1118 — Condition: enclosure and released labour
**Front:** How did enclosure supply factory labour?
**Definition (learn):** The enclosure movement created landless labour available for factories; rural displacement fed industry (✅ owner 04 §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Released workers had no alternative subsistence, so they accepted factory time-discipline (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
- Enclosure was a long, contested process, not a single event (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1115, WH-1133, WH-1113.
**Trap:** Do not date enclosure to one event.
**Mains hook:** Released labour connects agricultural change to factory discipline.
**Back:** *"Land taken, hands released, factories filled."*

### Flashcard WH-1119 — Condition: agricultural surplus
**Front:** How did agricultural change support industrial towns?
**Definition (learn):** Crop rotation, manuring and new implements raised output and fed a non-farming urban population (✅ owner 04 §4 Agriculture; ⚠️ owner 04 §8.4 on unevenness).
**Breakdown:**
- More food and labour for towns (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Improvement was regionally uneven (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** WH-1115, WH-1130.
**Trap:** Do not present agricultural improvement as uniform across England.
**Mains hook:** Surplus is the food side of the urban-industrial equation.
**Back:** *"Surplus feeds the town that works the loom."*

### Flashcard WH-1120 — Condition: markets, shipping and colonial demand
**Front:** How did overseas trade and colonies widen demand for industrial output?
**Definition (learn):** Overseas trade and colonies widened demand, justifying production beyond local need (✅ owner 04 §3, §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- Scale and export incentive (✅ owner 04 §3).
- Precondition for scale (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
- Domestic demand also mattered; do not reduce it to exports (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1115, WH-1129, WH-1139.
**Trap:** Do not reduce demand to exports only.
**Mains hook:** Markets create the scale precondition; domestic demand is the other half.
**Back:** *"Ships and colonies widen demand; home demand matters too."*

### Flashcard WH-1121 — Condition: stable political-legal order
**Front:** Why did the post-17th-century English state suit industrial enterprise?
**Definition (learn):** The English state was more favourable to commerce, lowering the risk of expropriation and enforcing contracts (✅ owner 04 §3; ⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- Lower political friction for enterprise (✅ owner 04 §3).
- Stability is comparative, not absolute (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric; "post-17th-century" (✅ owner 04 §8.4).
**Links:** WH-1115, WH-1019 (capitalism doctrine, Part 1).
**Trap:** Do not present England as politically stable in an absolute sense.
**Mains hook:** Legal order and contract enforcement as the fifth condition.
**Back:** *"Contracts enforced, property safer, enterprise freer."*

### Flashcard WH-1122 — Spinning jenny (James Hargreaves, 1764)
**Front:** What did the spinning jenny do and who invented it?
**Definition (learn):** A textile machine that made faster spinning possible; invented by James Hargreaves in 1764 (✅ owner 04 §1; ✅ Wikipedia "Industrial Revolution").
**Breakdown:**
- Sector: textiles (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Effect: cotton-textile output rises sharply (✅ owner 04 §4).
- First step of the textile mechanisation sequence (✅ owner 04 §8.5 step 1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1764; James Hargreaves (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1114, WH-1123, WH-1124, WH-1125.
**Trap:** Hargreaves is linked to the spinning jenny, not the power loom (Cartwright).
**Mains hook:** Textiles led the first wave of mechanisation.
**Back:** *"Sixty-four, Hargreaves, spin many threads."*

### Flashcard WH-1123 — Water frame (Richard Arkwright, patented 1769)
**Front:** What is the water frame and who developed it?
**Definition (learn):** A water-powered spinning machine associated with Arkwright; patented 1769, used at Cromford in 1771 (✅ Wikipedia "Industrial Revolution").
**Breakdown:**
- Textile machine: water frame (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Water power — the limit that coal later removed (✅ owner 04 §8.4 WH-1117).
- Owner lists "Arkwright's water-frame" in 1769 (✅ owner 04 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Patent 1769; Cromford 1771 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1122, WH-1117, WH-1125.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the water frame (water-powered) with Watt's steam engine.
**Mains hook:** Water-powered mills tied industry to rivers, which steam later freed.
**Back:** *"Arkwright's water frame: sixty-nine, Cromford seventy-one."*

### Flashcard WH-1124 — Spinning mule (Samuel Crompton, 1779)
**Front:** Who invented the spinning mule and when?
**Definition (learn):** A spinning machine invented by Samuel Crompton in 1779 (✅ Wikipedia "Industrial Revolution").
**Breakdown:**
- Textile sector mechanisation (✅ Wikipedia).
- Not listed in the owner's 1764–1793 timeline — ⚠️ added from the fetched source as a textile milestone.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1779; Samuel Crompton (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-1122, WH-1123, WH-1125.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the mule with the jenny or the water frame.
**Mains hook:** The mule combined the jenny and water-frame principles (⚠️ standard knowledge).
**Back:** *"Seventy-nine, Crompton's mule."*

### Flashcard WH-1125 — Power loom (Edmund Cartwright, 1785) and later looms
**Front:** Who patented the power loom and what were the later loom developments?
**Definition (learn):** Cartwright's power loom, patented 1785, accelerated weaving (✅ owner 04 §1, §6; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Edmund Cartwright (✅ owner 04 §6).
- Samuel Horrocks patented a loom in 1813; Richard Roberts improved it in 1822 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1785; 1813; 1822 (✅ Wikipedia for the later two).
**Links:** WH-1122, WH-1124, WH-1126.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Cartwright (loom) with Whitney (gin).
**Mains hook:** Weaving accelerated after spinning, closing the textile chain.
**Back:** *"Eighty-five Cartwright, thirteen Horrocks, twenty-two Roberts."*

### Flashcard WH-1126 — Cotton gin (Eli Whitney, 1793, United States)
**Front:** What did the cotton gin do and where was it invented?
**Definition (learn):** A machine that separated cotton fibre quickly, invented by Eli Whitney in 1793 in the United States (✅ owner 04 §1, §8.9).
**Breakdown:**
- Raw cotton processing speeds up sharply (✅ owner 04 §1).
- Cotton fibre separation becomes much faster (✅ owner 04 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1793 (✅ owner 04 §1; Wikipedia excerpt gave no year, ⚠️ owner date retained).
**Links:** WH-1125, WH-1113, WH-1058 (US export models, Part 1 file).
**Trap:** ❌ Cotton gin was a British invention → ✅ Eli Whitney, United States (✅ owner 04 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** The gin links British textiles to American slavery-based cotton supply — ⚠️ analytical link.
**Back:** *"Ninety-three, Whitney, not British."*

### Flashcard WH-1127 — Steam engine (James Watt)
**Front:** What role did James Watt's steam engine play, and what is the factual caution?
**Definition (learn):** Watt's steam engine freed production from the limits of muscle and water power (✅ owner 04 §4); Watt's improvements were the decisive breakthrough for industrial use (✅ owner 04 §8.9).
**Breakdown:**
- Energy breakthrough: production leaves limits of muscle and water (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Perfected by 1778 with Matthew Boulton's backing; double-acting rotative type in 1783; rotary engine in 1782 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner date control lists "Watt patent (1769)" (✅ owner 04 §9); the fetched Wikipedia text gave no patent year (⚠️ 1769 not confirmed by fetched text).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Perfected by 1778 (✅ Wikipedia); patent 1769 (owner date control).
**Links:** WH-1117, WH-1123, WH-1125.
**Trap:** ❌ Watt invented the steam engine solely → ✅ he improved and made it usable; Newcomen-type engines preceded (⚠️ standard knowledge).
**Mains hook:** Steam removed the siting and scale limit (see WH-1131 evidence step 2).
**Back:** *"Watt improved; steam freed the mill from the river."*

### Flashcard WH-1128 — Factory system replaces the putting-out system
**Front:** How did the factory system differ from the domestic or putting-out system?
**Definition (learn):** The factory system replaced the domestic or putting-out system; standardised, supervised production raised output per worker (✅ owner 04 §6, §8.5 step 3).
**Breakdown:**
- Factory discipline and steam power together (✅ owner 04 §2).
- Output per worker beyond artisan capacity (⚠️ owner 04 §8.5).
- Long hours, low wages and child labour in the early factory regime (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1127, WH-1133, WH-1131.
**Trap:** Do not present the factory as only a machine space; it is a labour regime (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
**Mains hook:** "Why did the factory mark a decisive break?" — discipline plus scale.
**Back:** *"Home work to factory bell."*

### Flashcard WH-1129 — Transport: canals, macadam roads, steam boats and railways
**Front:** What transport changes lowered delivered cost in the Industrial Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Railways, canals, macadam roads and steam boats moved raw materials and finished goods faster (✅ owner 04 §4, §8.5 step 4).
**Breakdown:**
- Canals and macadam roads (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Steam boats (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Railways: first passenger railway 1830 (✅ owner 04 §1; ✅ old NCERT).
- Cost advantage survives the journey to distant markets (⚠️ owner 04 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1830 (✅ old NCERT).
**Links:** WH-1141 to WH-1144, WH-1120.
**Trap:** Railways followed the first mechanisation wave; do not present them as its cause (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Transport completed the national market where one existed.
**Back:** *"Canals, macadam, steam boats, then the 1830 railway."*

### Flashcard WH-1130 — Agricultural change: crop rotation, manuring and implements
**Front:** Which agricultural changes supported the industrial economy?
**Definition (learn):** Crop rotation, manuring and new implements raised food output and labour supply for towns (✅ owner 04 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- More food and labour for towns (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Regionally uneven (⚠️ owner 04 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1119, WH-1131.
**Trap:** Do not present agriculture as the same across England.
**Mains hook:** Agriculture is a hidden condition of the urban factory.
**Back:** *"Rotate, manure, implement — feed the factory."*

### Flashcard WH-1131 — Causal chain of the Industrial Revolution
**Front:** Trace the owner's cause-mechanism-consequence chain from capital to labour protest.
**Definition (learn):** Owner 04 §4 diagram (✅ owner 04 §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Capital + coal/iron + labour + markets → textile mechanisation → factory system + steam power → more output + urbanisation + new working class → labour protest + social legislation + imperial expansion (✅ owner 04 §4).
- Evidence steps: mechanisation concentrates on textiles → steam removes siting limit → factory replaces putting-out → transport lowers cost → structural pressure outward (✅ owner 04 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1115, WH-1128, WH-1132, WH-1133, WH-1134.
**Trap:** Do not stop at machines; the chain runs through labour and state (✅ owner 04 §6).
**Mains hook:** Use as the spine for a 15-mark causal answer.
**Back:** *"Capital, coal, labour, markets — machines — factory — people — law — empire."*

### Flashcard WH-1132 — Urbanisation and new classes
**Front:** How did industrialisation change class and city?
**Definition (learn):** Economic life shifted from village to city; capitalists and wage workers became central (✅ owner 04 §5, §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Industrial towns dominate modern life (✅ owner 04 §5).
- Class politics deepens (✅ owner 04 §5).
- Early slum conditions and public health problems (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
- Urbanisation concentrated in specific industrial regions (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** WH-1133, WH-1137, WH-1019 (capitalism), WH-1020 (socialism).
**Trap:** Do not give urban population figures; none sourced.
**Mains hook:** Class politics as the social consequence.
**Back:** *"Village empties, town fills, two classes face off."*

### Flashcard WH-1133 — Harsh labour regime and the social question
**Front:** What did the early factory regime do to workers, women and children?
**Definition (learn):** Long hours, low wages, women and child labour; the social question emerges (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Long hours and low wages (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
- Women's and children's labour central to early factories (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
- Women's factory work coexisted with domestic and outwork labour (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7).
- Landless, toolless workers dependent on employers (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None numeric.
**Links:** WH-1118, WH-1134, WH-1135, WH-1136, WH-1020.
**Trap:** Industrialisation did not automatically improve workers' lives (✅ owner 04 §6).
**Mains hook:** The social question is the bridge to the labour laws.
**Back:** *"Long hours, low wage, child and woman at the loom."*

### Flashcard WH-1134 — First Factory Act (1802) and the state's first intervention
**Front:** What is the significance of the First Factory Act (1802)?
**Definition (learn):** The first English labour regulation under which the state began to restrain industry; laissez-faire eroded by evidence of harm (✅ owner 04 §1, §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- 1802 First Factory Act (✅ owner 04 §1).
- Later Factory Acts (✅ owner 04 §5).
- Early Acts were weakly enforced (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7).
- Labour regulation began slowly (✅ owner 04 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1802 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** WH-1133, WH-1135, WH-1026 (trade unions, Part 1), WH-1027 (welfare).
**Trap:** Do not overstate early enforcement.
**Mains hook:** The first Factory Act is the state's entry into the social question.
**Back:** *"Oh-two: the state first steps into the factory."*

### Flashcard WH-1135 — Anti-union laws repealed (1824)
**Front:** What did the 1824 repeal of anti-union laws make possible?
**Definition (learn):** England repealed anti-union laws in 1824, making trade-union growth possible (✅ old NCERT; ✅ owner 04 §1, §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Trade-union growth becomes possible (✅ owner 04 §1).
- Collective labour organisation (✅ owner 04 §5).
- Union legalisation reference in Japan contrast (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1824.
**Links:** WH-1026, WH-1136, WH-1145.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1824 repeal with the 1802 Factory Act.
**Mains hook:** Union legalisation is the political answer to the social question.
**Back:** *"Twenty-four: unions may organise."*

### Flashcard WH-1136 — Chartism (1830s–40s)
**Front:** What was Chartism and what did it achieve?
**Definition (learn):** A working-class movement in the 1830s–40s carrying demands for political rights into the constitutional arena (✅ owner 04 §1, §8.7; ✅ owner 01 §9.3(h)).
**Breakdown:**
- Working-class political rights enter the agenda (✅ owner 04 §1).
- Social question becomes a constitutional question about who may vote (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7).
- Chartism did not achieve its immediate aims (✅ owner 04 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1830s–40s.
**Links:** WH-1135, WH-1026, WH-1074 (sans-culotte parallel, Part 1 file).
**Trap:** Do not claim Chartism succeeded in its immediate demands.
**Mains hook:** The social question turned into constitutional demands.
**Back:** *"Thirties to forties: the vote goes on the agenda."*

### Flashcard WH-1137 — Environmental side of coal industrialisation
**Front:** What environmental and health effects of coal-based production can a 20-mark answer mention?
**Definition (learn):** Coal-based production concentrated smoke, waterway pollution and occupational disease in industrial districts (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Smoke and waterway pollution (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7).
- Occupational disease (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7).
- Urban public health, housing and sanitation become state problems (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No figures (⚠️ owner 04 §8.7 bans invented emission or mortality figures).
**Links:** WH-1132, WH-1117.
**Trap:** Keep it qualitative.
**Mains hook:** Connects industrialisation to modern environmental history.
**Back:** *"Coal smoke: the first environmental bill."*

### Flashcard WH-1138 — Industrial Revolution traps (MCQ close options)
**Front:** Which statements about the Industrial Revolution must be rejected?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 04 §6 traps (✅ owner 04 §6).
**Breakdown:**
- ❌ Machines alone → ✅ social effects too.
- ❌ Started equally across Europe → ✅ England first; uneven spread.
- ❌ Steam engine alone caused everything → ✅ capital, labour, resources, agriculture and markets also mattered.
- ❌ Industrialisation automatically improved workers' lives → ✅ early severe exploitation.
- ❌ Factory = the domestic system → ✅ factory replaced putting-out.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1113, WH-1115, WH-1127, WH-1133.
**Trap:** These are standard close-option pairs.
**Mains hook:** Use one correction in the introduction.
**Back:** *"Not only machines, not even, not steam alone, not automatic."*

### Flashcard WH-1139 — Verdicts: mixed blessing, why Britain, and empire
**Front:** What qualified verdicts apply to the Industrial Revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 04 §8.8 scaffolds (⚠️ analytic).
**Breakdown:**
- Mixed blessing: gains general and delayed, costs concentrated and immediate; political response took the form of labour law and the franchise.
- Why Britain: remove any single element and the sequence slows; remove secure markets and cheap coal together and it does not begin.
- Industry and empire: raised the stakes of empire rather than inventing the impulse.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1115, WH-1131, WH-1134, WH-1152.
**Trap:** Do not say "industry caused empire" (owner 04 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** Use the verdicts as conclusions to 10, 15 and 20-mark answers.
**Back:** *"Gains late, costs early; empire raised the stakes."*

### Flashcard WH-1140 — Boundary rule: Indian deindustrialisation belongs to Modern Indian History
**Front:** Can the Industrial Revolution owner answer the Indian deindustrialisation question?
**Definition (learn):** No. This file supplies only the world/English mechanism; the India verdict belongs to Modern Indian History basic/07 (✅ owner 04 §8.5 boundary).
**Breakdown:**
- Excluded from this file: colonial tariff asymmetry, loss of court patronage, revenue policy, transport penetration, regional craft variation, scholarly dispute (✅ owner 04 §8.5).
- Hand-off sentence: English mechanisation, steam and cheap transport made machine cloth structurally cheaper; whether that declined Indian handicrafts depends on colonial policy, patronage and demand inside India (✅ owner 04 §8.5).
- Modern Indian History owner: `Modern-Indian-History/basic/07_Economic-Impact-of-British-Rule.md` (✅ owner 04 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in this file.
**Links:** WH-1164 (hand-off sentence), WH-1162 (PYQ bridge).
**Trap:** ❌ "The Industrial Revolution destroyed Indian handicrafts" is a directive failure on a "how far" question (✅ owner 04 §8.5).
**Mains hook:** Use the hand-off sentence then switch to the India owner.
**Back:** *"Cheaper machine cloth is pressure; policy decides the damage."*

### Flashcard WH-1141 — Railways in Britain (early industrialiser)
**Front:** What did railways do for Britain, and what is the causal caution?
**Definition (learn):** Railways completed the transport revolution alongside canals and macadam roads (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- First passenger railway 1830 (✅ owner 04 §8.6; ✅ old NCERT).
- Integrated a national market, cheapened coal and raw-material movement (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6).
- Pulled forward iron, coal and engineering demand (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6).
- Railways followed the first mechanisation wave; not its cause (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1830.
**Links:** WH-1129, WH-1142.
**Trap:** Do not present railways as the cause of industrialisation.
**Mains hook:** The British railway is the integration case in the railway comparison.
**Back:** *"Thirty: the railway completes the national market."*

### Flashcard WH-1142 — Railways in continental Europe (catch-up and state promotion)
**Front:** How did railways serve catch-up industrialisation in continental Europe?
**Definition (learn):** Industrial growth after 1815 with often state-promoted railway building; railways served national integration and, in Germany, market unification before state unification (✅ owner 04 §8.6; ⚠️ timing varied).
**Breakdown:**
- Countries: France, Belgium, Germany (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
- Germany: market unified before political unification (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
- Timing and state involvement varied by country (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Industrial growth after 1815 (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
**Links:** WH-1141, WH-1143, WH-1223 (Bismarck).
**Trap:** Do not claim the German market was unified by a state that did not yet exist.
**Mains hook:** Railways can unify a market before a state.
**Back:** *"Germany's rails unite the market before the state."*

### Flashcard WH-1143 — Railways in the USA and the settler frontier
**Front:** How did railways affect the USA settler frontier?
**Definition (learn):** Rapid growth after independence and especially after 1870; railways opened interior land and bound a continental market (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Interior land opened to settlement and commercial agriculture (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
- Dispossession of indigenous populations (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6; ✅ owner 02 §8.4 Native dispossession).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** After 1870 (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
**Links:** WH-1056 (Native dispossession, Part 1 file), WH-1141.
**Trap:** Say the dispossession if the question asks for social effects.
**Mains hook:** Railways as the continental-market binder with a social cost.
**Back:** *"Land opened, peoples displaced, market bound."*

### Flashcard WH-1144 — Railways in Russia, Japan and the colonies
**Front:** How did state-assisted and colonial railways differ from the market-led cases?
**Definition (learn):** Russia and Japan built later, state-assisted railways for modernisation and strategic reach; colonial railways moved export commodities to ports (✅ owner 04 §8.6; ⚠️ colony detail).
**Breakdown:**
- Russia/Japan: instruments of deliberate state modernisation and strategic reach; compressed modernisation carried heavier social strain (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6).
- Colonies: laid to move export commodities to ports and administrative-military traffic (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6).
- Same technology deepened export dependence rather than a home market (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1141, WH-1145, WH-1158.
**Trap:** For India, cross-link Modern Indian History basic/07; do not supply Indian figures here (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
**Mains hook:** The railway test: who controlled it and what the economy was for.
**Back:** *"State rails build power; colonial rails carry exports."*

### Flashcard WH-1145 — Britain vs Japan: who initiated industrialisation
**Front:** What is the organising contrast between Britain and Japan?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 04 §8.6A one-line contrast (⚠️ analytic): Britain industrialised because private actors found it profitable; Japan industrialised because the state decided it was necessary.
**Breakdown:**
- Britain: private enterprise responding to capital, coal, released labour, markets and a commercial legal order (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Japan: the state; after Commodore Matthew Perry forced trade treaties from 1853 the treaties were felt as a national humiliation; from the Meiji restoration of 1868 the government drove the programme (✅ owner 04 §8.6A; ✅ Lowe via owner).
- Why it matters: Japan's industrialisation can be dated to a year; Britain's cannot (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1853 (Perry); 1868 (Meiji restoration) (✅ owner 04 §8.9).
**Links:** WH-1146, WH-1155, WH-1156.
**Trap:** Do not dateline Japanese industrialisation to 1853 alone; the effective starting date is 1868 (⚠️ owner 04 §8.2).
**Mains hook:** "Britain's industrialisation had no author; Japan's had a policy."
**Back:** *"Britain by profit, Japan by necessity; Perry 1853, Meiji 1868."*

### Flashcard WH-1146 — Japan's motive: security against Western occupation
**Front:** What was the Japanese motive for industrialisation?
**Definition (learn):** Security: the government decided that the best way to prevent western powers from treating Japan as China was to occupy neighbouring territories (✅ Lowe via owner 04 §8.6A).
**Breakdown:**
- Motive: security, not profit (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Explains why industrialisation was tied to armed force from the outset (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Britain's motive: profit and market opportunity (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1145, WH-1152, WH-1155.
**Trap:** Do not present Japan's motive as purely economic.
**Mains hook:** The security motive ties Japanese industry to the military.
**Back:** *"Japan industrialised to avoid being China."*

### Flashcard WH-1147 — Japan: imported technology and leading sectors (cotton and silk)
**Front:** How did Japan acquire technology and which sectors led?
**Definition (learn):** Japan imported and adapted proven foreign techniques and started modern industries like cotton and silk manufacture (✅ Lowe via owner 04 §8.6A; ⚠️ latecomer advantage).
**Breakdown:**
- Technology: imported and adapted (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Leading sector: cotton and silk (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Britain: domestically generated technology; textiles first (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Latecomer advantage: skip the invention stage and buy the result (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner text.
**Links:** WH-1122 to WH-1127, WH-1153.
**Trap:** Concede the textile similarity before stressing the contrast (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6A).
**Mains hook:** Latecomer advantage is a transferable point.
**Back:** *"Copy, adapt, and start with silk and cotton."*

### Flashcard WH-1148 — Japan's constitution (1889) and Diet (1890)
**Front:** What political institutions did Japan build to carry industrialisation?
**Definition (learn):** A constitution formally accepted in 1889 and an elected lower house, the Diet, first meeting in 1890, with a cabinet, house of peers and Privy Council (✅ Lowe via owner 04 §8.6A).
**Breakdown:**
- Centralisation came first; a parliamentary system modelled on Germany's constitution (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Institution-building preceded industrial society (⚠️ owner 04 §8.6A).
- System was far from democratic: the emperor could dissolve the Diet, decided war and peace and was commander-in-chief (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1889; 1890 (✅ owner 04 §8.9).
**Links:** WH-1145, WH-1149, WH-1151.
**Trap:** Do not date the constitution other than 1889 or the Diet other than 1890.
**Mains hook:** Institutions built to industrialise, not after.
**Back:** *"Eighty-nine constitution, ninety Diet, emperor in command."*

### Flashcard WH-1149 — Japan's railways and road programme from 1868
**Front:** What infrastructure programme did the Japanese government pursue from 1868?
**Definition (learn):** From 1868 the Japanese embarked on a policy of building railways and improving the road system as an explicit government programme (✅ Lowe via owner 04 §8.6A).
**Breakdown:**
- Same technology as Britain, opposite initiating logic (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Railways and roads were instruments of state programme (✅ owner 04 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1868 (✅ owner 04 §8.9).
**Links:** WH-1141, WH-1145, WH-1144.
**Trap:** Do not present Japanese railway financing details; not sourced (⚠️ owner 04 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** State chose the arteries and sectors.
**Back:** *"Sixty-eight: the state builds the rails."*

### Flashcard WH-1150 — Japan: concentrated business tied to the state (zaibatsu marked ⚠️)
**Front:** How should Japanese business structure be described, and what must not be named?
**Definition (learn):** Concentrated business power tied to the state; Lowe records politicians who took bribes from big business and workers blaming "the government and big business" after 1929 (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
**Breakdown:**
- Observable fact: concentration (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
- Standard term: zaibatsu (⚠️ marked, not sourced in this folder).
- Do not name individual houses, founding dates, ownership shares (✅ owner 04 §8.9 bans).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** After 1929 (✅ owner 04 §8.6A).
**Links:** WH-1145, WH-1151, WH-1152.
**Trap:** Do not claim Meiji founded model enterprises and sold them to private groups (✅ owner 04 §8.9 bans this unsourced account).
**Mains hook:** Concentration plus state ties explain Japan's corruption and militarism narrative.
**Back:** *"Big business and government, bound together — name no house."*

### Flashcard WH-1151 — Japan's labour suppression and the 1921 and 1929 collapse
**Front:** How did Japan deal with labour and what fragility did the 1921 and 1929 shocks reveal?
