**Definition (learn):** Italy became a middling power; Germany became the strongest continental power, which fed the 1914 problem (⚠️ owner 06 §8.4; cross-ref basic/09).
**Breakdown:**
- Germany: strongest continental military-economic power (⚠️ owner 06 §8.4).
- Italy: middling power with weak state capacity (⚠️ owner 06 §8.4).
- Habsburg nationality crisis and Alsace-Lorraine grievance (⚠️ owner 06 §8.7).
- Proclamation did not complete social integration or make 1914 inevitable (✅ owner 06 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1914 (cross-ref).
**Links:** WH-1233, WH-1238, WH-1239.
**Trap:** Do not say unification made 1914 inevitable (✅ owner 06 §9).
**Mains hook:** Long-term consequence chain for 20-mark answers.
**Back:** *"Germany too strong, Italy too weak — the road to 1914 is not inevitable."*

### Flashcard WH-1235 — Germany's federal-authoritarian form
**Front:** How should the German Empire's constitutional form be described?
**Definition (learn):** A federal-authoritarian form: a modern parliament combined with Prussian dominance and monarchical control of army and foreign policy (⚠️ owner 06 §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Not simply undemocratic; not simply constitutional — both (⚠️ owner 06 §8.6).
- Germany unified under authoritarian-monarchical structure, not 1848 liberal hopes (⚠️ owner 06 adv §3).
- Monarchical control of the army and foreign policy (⚠️ owner 06 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1871.
**Links:** WH-1227, WH-1236, WH-1209.
**Trap:** ❌ German unification fulfilled the 1848 liberal dream → ✅ it fulfilled unity under conservative-Prussian dominance (✅ owner 06 adv §6).
**Mains hook:** Use the form to explain later militarism (⚠️ owner 06 adv §3).
**Back:** *"Parliament yes, Prussia decides."*

### Flashcard WH-1236 — Germany's internal minorities and Catholic and socialist suspects
**Front:** Which minorities did the German Empire contain?
**Definition (learn):** The Empire contained Polish, Danish and French-speaking (Alsace-Lorraine) populations, and treated Catholics and socialists as suspect national communities (⚠️ owner 06 §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Polish, Danish and French-speaking populations (⚠️ owner 06 §8.6).
- Catholics and later socialists treated as suspect (⚠️ owner 06 §8.6).
- Kulturkampf — ⚠️ analytical reference only; no detailed source in this folder (⚠️ owner 06 §8.6, §8.8).
- Anti-Socialist legislation — ⚠️ analytical reference only (⚠️ owner 06 §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1235, WH-1221, WH-1237.
**Trap:** Do not name Kulturkampf as a sourced fact of this folder (✅ owner 06 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Name the minorities to show the nation's incomplete integration.
**Back:** *"Poles, Danes, Alsatians, Catholics and socialists — the suspects."*

### Flashcard WH-1237 — The excluded Germans: small-German unification and the Anschluss root
**Front:** Why was German unification "small German" and what did it leave out?
**Definition (learn):** Austria was excluded in 1866, so unification was small German in form; the exclusion is the root of the 1938 Anschluss question (✅ owner 06 §8.6; cross-ref basic/11).
**Breakdown:**
- Austria excluded in 1866 (✅ owner 06 §8.6).
- Small German form (✅ owner 06 §8.6).
- 1938 Anschluss question — cross-ref basic/11 (✅ owner 06 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1866; 1938 (cross-ref).
**Links:** WH-1225, WH-1223, WH-1238.
**Trap:** Do not present 1938 as a Vienna-era event; it is later (cross-ref).
**Mains hook:** The exclusion explains a 20th-century question.
**Back:** *"Sixty-six exclusion: small Germany, Austrian questions later."*

### Flashcard WH-1238 — Alsace-Lorraine (1871) and French revanchism
**Front:** What was France's permanent grievance from 1871?
**Definition (learn):** Alsace-Lorraine lost in 1871; French revanchism a named source of friction in 1914 (✅ owner 06 §8.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Alsace-Lorraine lost in 1871 (✅ owner 06 §8.6).
- French revanchism a named source of 1914 friction (✅ owner 06 §8.6).
- Clearest long causal link in the folder (✅ owner 06 §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1871.
**Links:** WH-1226, WH-1234, WH-1239.
**Trap:** Do not date the loss before 1871.
**Mains hook:** The long causal link from 1871 to 1914.
**Back:** *"Seventy-one: Alsace lost, revenge remembered."*

### Flashcard WH-1239 — Map verdict: national states replaced dynastic legitimacy
**Front:** How did nationalism reshape the map, according to the owner's verdict?
**Definition (learn):** Nationalism replaced a settlement based on dynastic legitimacy with one based on national states; created the Alsace-Lorraine grievance and the Habsburg nationality crisis (⚠️ owner 06 §8.7).
**Breakdown:**
- Dynastic legitimacy (Vienna) → national states (WH-1175 contrast) (⚠️ owner 06 §8.7).
- Alsace-Lorraine grievance (WH-1238).
- Habsburg nationality crisis that broke Europe in 1914 (⚠️ owner 06 §8.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1914 (cross-ref).
**Links:** WH-1175, WH-1238, WH-1234.
**Trap:** Do not present the Habsburg crisis as the sole cause of 1914.
**Mains hook:** Use this verdict for "How did nationalism reshape the map?"
**Back:** *"Dynasty replaced by nation — and two grievances for 1914."*

### Flashcard WH-1240 — Unification traps (MCQ close options)
**Front:** Which claims about unification must be rejected?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 06 §6 and §8.8 traps (✅).
**Breakdown:**
- ❌ Mazzini completed unification → ✅ Cavour, Garibaldi, Victor Emmanuel II.
- ❌ Austria led German unification → ✅ Prussia did, after 1866.
- ❌ Rome joined in 1861 → ✅ 1870.
- ❌ Germany unified by popular assemblies → ✅ Prussian power and war.
- ❌ Italian unification purely democratic-republican → ✅ monarchy completed.
- ❌ Bismarck created nationalism → ✅ used it instrumentally.
- ❌ Garibaldi and Bismarck same role → ✅ volunteer-revolutionary vs state strategist.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1861; 1870; 1866; 1871.
**Links:** WH-1211, WH-1218, WH-1227, WH-1223, WH-1213.
**Trap:** Standard MCQ pairs.
**Mains hook:** Use one correction in the introduction.
**Back:** *"Not Mazzini, not Austria, not 1861, not assemblies, not Garibaldi as Bismarck."*

### Flashcard WH-1241 — Factual-risk controls for unification
**Front:** Which claims about unification must be avoided or bounded?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 06 §8.8 and §10 controls (✅ owner).
**Breakdown:**
- No Bismarck quotation (blood and iron or otherwise).
- Bismarck dates: minister-president 1862; Empire January 1871.
- Kulturkampf, Mezzogiorno, anti-socialist legislation: analytical reference only.
- No Bismarck quotation; no objective key.
- Do not give population, income or literacy figures.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1862; January 1871.
**Links:** WH-1223, WH-1228, WH-1236.
**Trap:** Bounded references must be marked ⚠️.
**Mains hook:** Precision in bounds defends the answer.
**Back:** *"No Bismarck quote, no Kulturkampf as fact, no figures."*

### Flashcard WH-1242 — Mark-scaled structures for unification answers
**Front:** How should 10, 15 and 20-mark unification answers be built?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 06 §8.3 structures (⚠️ analytic).
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: thesis → two axes (core state, method) with evidence → one limit → verdict.
- 15 marks: thesis → shared obstacle (Vienna, Austria) → Italy's sequence → Germany's sequence → decisive difference → limits → verdict.
- 20 marks: all of the above plus popular-versus-elite question, minorities and post-1871 consequences for European power → verdict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1230, WH-1231, WH-1235, WH-1236.
**Trap:** Do not stop at 1871 — the limits are the 20-mark difference (✅ owner 06 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** Continue past the proclamation.
**Back:** *"Ten: two axes. Fifteen: sequence. Twenty: continue past 1871."*

### Flashcard WH-1243 — Verdict scaffolds for comparing unifications
**Front:** What are the ready verdicts for "compare", "ideas or state power" and "map"?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 06 §8.7 verdicts (⚠️ analytic).
**Breakdown:**
- Compare: same enemy, same era, opposite methods; Italy combined diplomacy with insurrection and remained weak; Germany concentrated power and became too strong for the system that produced it.
- Ideas or state power: ideas made it thinkable; state power made it happen.
- Map: national states replaced dynastic legitimacy, creating Alsace-Lorraine and the Habsburg crisis.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1230, WH-1232, WH-1239.
**Trap:** Keep the verdict tied to the question's wording.
**Mains hook:** Three verdicts for three common demands.
**Back:** *"Same enemy, opposite methods; ideas thinkable, power real."*

### Flashcard WH-1244 — Nationalism does not always produce democracy first
**Front:** What general lesson do unification cases teach for GS-I?
**Definition (learn):** Nationalism does not always produce democracy first; sometimes it produces strong nation-states through elite-led consolidation (⚠️ owner 06 adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Italy and Germany: 19th-century European state-building projects (⚠️ owner 06 adv §5).
- Do not collapse European national unification and Indian anti-colonial nationalism into one template (⚠️ owner 06 adv §5).
- Cross-ref for Indian comparison: Modern-Indian-History basic/14 (⚠️ owner 06 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** WH-1232, WH-1239.
**Trap:** Do not claim a template across European and Indian nationalisms.
**Mains hook:** Use for a comparative GS-I question with a bounded India link.
**Back:** *"Nation first, democracy later — not a single template."*

### Flashcard WH-1245 — PYQ control for unification
**Front:** What is the PYQ status of the unification owner?
**Definition (learn):** Zero direct topic-only routes in 2018–2025; comparison and from-above/from-below questions remain original practice (✅ owner 06 §10).
**Breakdown:**
- No Bismarck quotation, objective key or current linkage invented (✅ owner 06 §10).
- Probable questions: compare processes; how nationalism reshaped the map; ideas or state power (✅ owner 06 §7).
- Boundary: Topic 05 owns Vienna; Topic 07 owns Imperialism; Topic 09 owns pre-1914 alliances and outbreak (✅ owner 06 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018–2025.
**Links:** WH-1209, WH-1232, WH-1203.
**Trap:** Do not invent a PYQ for unification.
**Mains hook:** "Redrawal of national boundaries" as the syllabus clause (✅ owner 06 §7).
**Back:** *"No PYQ invented; practise the boundary clause."*

## WORLD HISTORY — Part 2: Imperialism, Latin America, WWI, Interwar Order, Fascism and the Russian Revolution

### GROUP — New Imperialism and Scramble for Africa (basic 07 + advanced 07)

### Flashcard WH-2001 — New Imperialism: core concept (basic 07)
**Front:** What distinguishes "New Imperialism" (late 19th century) from earlier European influence in Africa?
**Definition (learn):** The late-19th-century phase in which industrial European powers pursued direct political control of inland African territory, not merely coastal trading posts or informal leverage.
**Breakdown:**
- Earlier: coastal trade, forts and informal influence.
- New: effective occupation, administration, boundary-making and extraction of resources inland.
- Rate of change is the proof of "new".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** About 10 per cent of African territory under European control in the 1870s; about 90 per cent by 1914 (Britannica figures as cited in ✅ owner 07 basic §2; not independently fetched). Only Ethiopia and Liberia remained independent in 1914 (✅ owner 07 basic §1).
**Links:** WH-2002, WH-2010, WH-2019 (motive debate); WH-2025 (social consequences); Modern-Indian-History basic/05 for the Indian parallel.
**Trap:** Do not describe New Imperialism as merely "civilising" or merely "economic". Its defining feature is direct inland rule.
**Mains hook:** "New Imperialism was new in the depth of control, not in the fact of expansion." Qualify with motive weighting in WH-2019.
**Back:** *"Coast to core: 10 per cent in 1870s, 90 per cent by 1914."*

### Flashcard WH-2002 — The 10 to 90 per cent rate of change
**Front:** Why is the change from about 10 per cent to about 90 per cent of African territory under European control the key number for this topic?
**Definition (learn):** The benchmark that proves the speed of partition: most of Africa was still largely outside effective European control in the 1870s and was almost entirely partitioned by 1914.
**Breakdown:**
- 1870s: about 10 per cent under European control.
- 1914: about 90 per cent under European control.
- Only Ethiopia and Liberia remained independent (1914).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 per cent (1870s), 90 per cent (1914) (✅ owner 07 basic §2 and advanced §8.4, citing Britannica). Not independently fetched; keep attribution.
**Links:** WH-2001; WH-2010 (Berlin rules); WH-2017.
**Trap:** Do not write that the whole continent was partitioned in 1885. Berlin set the rules; the 1870s-1914 expansion did the partitioning.
**Mains hook:** Use the figures to prove "new" imperialism. A high-scoring answer pairs the number with a mechanism, such as effective occupation.
**Back:** *"Ten to ninety in forty years: the pace is the proof."*

### Flashcard WH-2003 — Economic driver of the Scramble
**Front:** What economic motive drove European expansion into Africa?
**Definition (learn):** Industrial powers sought raw materials, markets and outlets for capital, tying empire to industrial capitalism.
**Breakdown:**
- Raw materials for factories.
- Markets for manufactured goods.
- Colonies reorganised for extraction (cash crops, mines, labour).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 basic §3 (Economic row); ✅ owner 07 advanced §1 (motive debate).
**Links:** WH-2001; WH-2019 (motive debate); cross-link basic/04 Industrial Revolution.
**Trap:** Economic motive was central but not exclusive. Not all colonies were profitable, and profit was often concentrated in few hands (⚠️ owner 07 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "Capitalism was the structural driver, but prestige and strategy set the timing." Use WH-2019.
**Back:** *"Factories want raw stuff and buyers: that is the engine."*

### Flashcard WH-2004 — Strategic driver: Suez and Egypt
**Front:** Which strategic developments made Africa's coasts and routes important to European powers?
**Definition (learn):** Strategic value of sea routes and key territories shaped which African areas were taken first.
**Breakdown:**
- Suez Canal opened in 1869: routes to Asia gained weight.
- British occupation of Egypt in 1882: strategic-financial imperialism became direct rule.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Suez Canal 1869; Britain occupies Egypt 1882 (✅ owner 07 basic §1; ✅ owner 07 advanced §2 on Britannica linking strategic claims and resources).
**Links:** WH-2008 (Suez); WH-2009 (Egypt); WH-2019.
**Trap:** Strategy often followed financial entanglement rather than preceding it (⚠️ owner 07 basic §8.4). Do not present Egypt 1882 as a pure strategic first move.
**Mains hook:** Strategy explains which territories were taken first; it does not by itself explain the economic reorganisation that followed.
**Back:** *"Canal in 1869, Egypt in 1882: routes first, then rule."*

### Flashcard WH-2005 — Prestige driver: unified Germany and Italy
**Front:** How did national prestige drive colonial competition?
**Definition (learn):** Recently unified states sought empire as a mark of great-power status.
**Breakdown:**
- Germany (unified 1871) and Italy (recently unified) sought colonies.
- Empire became a badge of great-power standing.
- Bismarck backed German claims in Africa in 1884.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Bismarck backed German claims in 1884 (✅ owner 07 basic §1 and §8.4). Germany's unification date 1871 is ⚠️ standard knowledge, not from the owner.
**Links:** WH-2010 (Berlin); WH-2019; WH-2048 (Triple Entente context).
**Trap:** Prestige explains the timing and speed of the rush after 1884. It cannot explain the economic reorganisation that followed (⚠️ owner 07 basic §8.4).
**Mains hook:** "Prestige set the clock; capitalism set the direction."
**Back:** *"Late unifiers wanted badges: colonies were status."*

### Flashcard WH-2006 — Technology as the permissive cause
**Front:** What role did technology and medicine play in the Scramble?
**Definition (learn):** Technology converted European intent into feasibility; it explains capability, not motive.
**Breakdown:**
- Steam transport.
- Improved weapons.
- Medicines that improved European survival in Africa.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 basic §3 (Technology row); ⚠️ the "permissive" framing is an owner synthesis (§8.4).
**Links:** WH-2001; WH-2003; WH-2018.
**Trap:** Technology explains capability, never intent (⚠️ owner 07 basic §8.4). Do not present it as a motive.
**Mains hook:** "Technology removed the obstacle; ideology dissolved the objection."
**Back:** *"Steam and guns made it possible; they never made it wanted."*

### Flashcard WH-2007 — Ideology: the civilising mission
**Front:** How did ideology justify expansion, and why must it be read critically?
**Definition (learn):** "Civilising mission" and racial-superiority rhetoric provided domestic legitimacy for conquest and shaped the form of rule.
**Breakdown:**
- Belief: some sincerely held the civilising ideal.
- Instrument: the rhetoric masked coercion and extraction.
- Owner treats ideology as both belief and instrument (advanced §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 basic §3 and §8.4; ✅ owner 07 advanced §1 debate matrix.
**Links:** WH-2012 (Congo); WH-2015 (Herero and Nama); WH-2022 (source critique).
**Trap:** Do not treat "civilising mission" as purely humanitarian. It coexisted with racial superiority and extraction (✅ owner 07 advanced §6).
**Mains hook:** "The civilising mission is best tested against recorded conduct, such as the Herero and Nama genocide."
**Back:** *"Civilise in words, extract in deeds."*

### Flashcard WH-2008 — Suez Canal (1869)
**Front:** When was the Suez Canal opened, and why did it matter for Africa?
**Definition (learn):** The 1869 canal shortened the sea route between Europe and Asia and raised the strategic value of Egypt and the Red Sea coasts.
**Breakdown:**
- Opened 1869.
- Increased the value of sea routes and their guardians.
- Preceded British occupation of Egypt (1882).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1869 (✅ owner 07 basic §1).
**Links:** WH-2004; WH-2009.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the canal's opening (1869) with the British occupation of Egypt (1882).
**Mains hook:** Use as the strategic-route example, but qualify it with financial entanglement.
**Back:** *"Sixty-nine opened the door to the sea route."*

### Flashcard WH-2009 — British occupation of Egypt (1882)
**Front:** What did the British occupation of Egypt (1882) signify?
**Definition (learn):** The point at which strategic-financial imperialism in Egypt became direct rule.
**Breakdown:**
- Earlier financial entanglement.
- 1882: direct British control.
- A strategic gateway to India and the east, linked to the Suez route.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1882 (✅ owner 07 basic §1). The "gateway to India" gloss is ⚠️ standard knowledge.
**Links:** WH-2004; WH-2008; Modern-Indian-History basic/05 for Indian linkage.
**Trap:** Egypt's occupation is an example of finance preceding rule, not a clean strategic first move (⚠️ owner 07 basic §8.4).
**Mains hook:** Strategy and finance together, not one of them alone.
**Back:** *"Eighty-two: finance becomes flag."*

### Flashcard WH-2010 — Berlin Conference: dates and participants
**Front:** When did the Berlin Conference meet, how many countries attended, and what legal instrument did it produce?
**Definition (learn):** A meeting of interested European powers that set rules for claiming African territory and recognised their legitimacy.
**Breakdown:**
- Opened 15 November 1884; closed 26 February 1885 after adjournment.
- Fourteen countries took part; no African states were invited.
- The General Act was signed on 26 February 1885.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 15 Nov 1884–26 Feb 1885; 14 countries (✅ owner 07 basic §4; ✅ Wikipedia: Berlin Conference, fetched). No African states invited (✅ Wikipedia). Six of the fourteen had no African zones of interest (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-2011 (effective occupation); WH-2012 (Congo); WH-2018 (trap); WH-2025.
**Trap:** Do not say the conference partitioned Africa. It standardised the recognition rules; conquest and delimitation continued (✅ owner 07 basic §6 and §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Berlin was decisive as a legitimising rulebook and negligible as a cartographer" (owner verdict scaffold, §8.8).
**Back:** *"Fourteen states, Nov 84 to Feb 85: rules, not maps."*

### Flashcard WH-2011 — Effective occupation
**Front:** What was the principle of "effective occupation" agreed at Berlin?
**Definition (learn):** A claimant power had to establish authority in the territory to make its claim recognisable against rivals.
**Breakdown:**
- Authority had to be established throughout the territory (Wikipedia wording).
- In practice: treaties with local leaders, a flag, an administration and a police force.
- Claimants had to notify other signatories.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 basic §4 and §8.5 (effective occupation). ✅ Wikipedia: Berlin Conference (fetched) on the mechanics.
**Links:** WH-2010; WH-2012; WH-2019 (advanced mechanisms).
**Trap:** ⚠️ Owner 07 presents effective occupation generally; the fetched Wikipedia summary states the rule applied to coastal lands. Flag this scope question; do not present coastal-only as settled without checking the Act.
**Mains hook:** "Effective occupation gave conquest the look of law: the flag and the police were the legal evidence."
**Back:** *"Flag, police, treaty: occupation made legal."*

### Flashcard WH-2012 — Congo Free State and Leopold II
**Front:** How is the Congo Free State linked to Leopold II and the Berlin Conference?
**Definition (learn):** A private-imperial venture under Leopold II that the Berlin Conference recognised in part, without giving him sovereignty.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner view: the Congo Free State was recognised under Leopold II (✅ owner 07 basic §4).
- Wikipedia: the Act confirmed lands held by Leopold II's International Congo Society; the name "Congo Free State" came on 1 August 1885 and does not appear in the Act; the conference did not make Leopold sovereign.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 August 1885 (name); 26 Feb 1885 (Act) (✅ Wikipedia). Owner date control consistent.
**Links:** WH-2010; WH-2007 (ideology); WH-2019.
**Trap:** ⚠️ CONFLICT: owner 07 says "recognised under Leopold II"; Wikipedia says the Act did not name the state and did not make Leopold sovereign. Use the Wikipedia wording in exams.
**Mains hook:** Berlin legitimised a private-imperial venture but did nothing to prevent atrocity (✅ owner 07 basic §8.5).
**Back:** *"Leopold's society got the lands; the name came later."*

### Flashcard WH-2013 — Partition pattern by power
**Front:** Which European powers held which main African zones after the Scramble?
**Definition (learn):** A map-by-power ladder for boundary and partition questions.
**Breakdown:**
- Britain: Egypt, Sudan, South Africa, Nigeria, East African holdings.
- France: much of West and Equatorial Africa, Algeria, Madagascar.
- Belgium: Congo.
- Germany: Togo, Cameroon, German East Africa, German South West Africa.
- Portugal: Angola, Mozambique.
- Italy: Eritrea, Somalia; defeated at Adwa in Ethiopia.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 basic §4 table and §8.5. Algeria as French is ✅ owner-sourced.
**Links:** WH-2014 (Adwa); WH-2017 (Ethiopia and Liberia); WH-2025.
**Trap:** Do not list Ethiopia as Italian. Italy failed there. Do not list Liberia as colonised; it remained independent.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show the "boundary" question: partition followed power, not local identity.
**Back:** *"Brits Egypt-to-Cape, French West, Belgians Congo, Germans Togo-Cameroon."*

### Flashcard WH-2014 — Adwa (1896): African victory
**Front:** What happened at Adwa in 1896, and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** Ethiopia's decisive victory over Italy, proving African conquest was not automatic.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1 March 1896 (✅ Wikipedia: First Italo-Ethiopian War, fetched).
- Emperor: Menelik II (✅ Wikipedia).
- Result: decisive Ethiopian victory; Italian forces retreated into Eritrea (✅ Wikipedia).
- Treaty of Addis Ababa concluded October 1896 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 Mar 1896; Menelik II; Italy retreated into Eritrea.
**Links:** WH-2013; WH-2017; WH-2022 (agency).
**Trap:** Adwa is exceptional. Most African resistance was defeated (⚠️ owner 07 basic §8.6).
**Mains hook:** "Adwa shows contingency, not inevitability, in conquest."
**Back:** *"Ninety-six: Menelik beat Italy at Adwa."*

### Flashcard WH-2015 — Herero and Nama genocide (1904–08)
**Front:** What was the Herero and Nama genocide, and where and when did it occur?
**Definition (learn):** The German colonial suppression in German South West Africa, recognised as genocide of the Herero and Nama peoples, 1904–08.
**Breakdown:**
- Place: German South West Africa.
- Dates: 1904–08 (✅ owner 07 basic §1 and §8.6).
- Strongest single counter-evidence to the "civilising mission" claim (✅ owner 07 basic §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1904–08 (✅ owner 07 basic §1). No casualty figures are sourced in the owner (§8.9).
**Links:** WH-2007; WH-2016 (Maji Maji); WH-2022.
**Trap:** Name it precisely as genocide of the Herero and Nama. Do not generalise the term to all colonial violence (✅ owner 07 basic §8.6).
**Mains hook:** Test stated motive against recorded conduct.
**Back:** *"Oh-four to oh-eight: the civilising claim's worst witness."*

### Flashcard WH-2016 — Maji Maji uprising (1905–07)
**Front:** What was the Maji Maji uprising and why does it matter for resistance?
**Definition (learn):** A mass anti-colonial revolt in German East Africa, 1905–07, against labour and taxation demands.
**Breakdown:**
- Place: German East Africa.
- Dates: 1905–07 (✅ owner 07 basic §1 and §8.6).
- Resistance continued after conquest, not only before it.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1905–07 (✅ owner 07 basic). No casualty figure sourced (owner §8.9).
**Links:** WH-2015; WH-2022; WH-2025 (labour and taxation).
**Trap:** Do not give Maji Maji casualty numbers (⚠️ owner-restricted).
**Mains hook:** Resistance after conquest shows the colonial order was contested even when defeated.
**Back:** *"Oh-five to oh-seven: revolt against tax and labour."*

### Flashcard WH-2017 — Ethiopia and Liberia (1914)
**Front:** Which two African states remained independent in 1914?
**Definition (learn):** The only sovereign states left after the partition by 1914.
**Breakdown:**
- Ethiopia: defeated Italy at Adwa (1896).
- Liberia: independent state, not colonised.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1914 (✅ owner 07 basic §1 and §6).
**Links:** WH-2014; WH-2013.
**Trap:** Do not list other African states as independent in 1914 from this owner. Only these two are named.
**Mains hook:** The 90 per cent figure implies these two exceptions.
**Back:** *"Fourteen: two left, Ethiopia and Liberia."*

### Flashcard WH-2018 — Trap: Berlin did not draw every border
**Front:** Did the Berlin Conference alone draw Africa's colonial borders on one day?
**Definition (learn):** No. Berlin created recognition rules and legitimacy; delimitation and conquest continued afterwards.
**Breakdown:**
- Berlin: rules and the General Act.
- After Berlin: occupation, treaty-making and boundary commissions.
- Final lines were set over decades (⚠️ owner 07 basic §6 trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1884-85 Berlin; 1870s-1914 expansion ✅ owner 07.
**Links:** WH-2010; WH-2011; WH-2013.
**Trap:** Writing "Berlin divided Africa" is the central examiner trap for this topic.
**Mains hook:** "Berlin explains why partition went fast without war among Europeans, not where each line fell."
**Back:** *"Rules, not a one-day map."*

### Flashcard WH-2019 — Advanced: motive debate (advanced 07)
**Front:** What are the main historiographical positions on why New Imperialism happened?
**Definition (learn):** A debate between economic causes, prestige and strategy, and ideology, with the best synthesis treating economics as central but not exclusive.
**Breakdown:**
- Motive: capitalism versus prestige and strategy.
- Berlin: triggered the scramble versus regularised an ongoing process.
- Ideology: genuine belief versus instrument.
- Nature of empire: direct rule versus informal and chartered control.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §1–2.
**Links:** WH-2003 to WH-2007; WH-2020; WH-2010.
**Trap:** Never say imperialism was caused by one factor only. The owner expects a layered answer.
**Mains hook:** "Economic motives were central but not exclusive; Berlin accelerated and legitimated a process already under way."
**Back:** *"Money first, prestige and fear close behind."*

### Flashcard WH-2020 — Advanced: how colonial control worked
**Front:** What mechanisms made colonial control work in practice?
**Definition (learn):** Direct occupation, legal cover, river and trade rules, chartered or concessionary control, and variation between powers.
**Breakdown:**
- Effective occupation: legal cover for expansion (WH-2011).
- River and trade rules: Congo and Niger discussed as free waters.
- Chartered or concessionary control: Leopold's Congo (WH-2012).
- Variation: different powers ruled differently; avoid a single model.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §2 table.
**Links:** WH-2011; WH-2012; WH-2019.
**Trap:** "Imperialism = one uniform model" is wrong (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §2).
**Mains hook:** Distinguish the types of control: direct, chartered, informal.
**Back:** *"Occupy, trade, charter, vary."*

### Flashcard WH-2021 — Border legacy and the preservation principle
**Front:** Why did independent African states keep colonial borders, and what did that choice cost?
**Definition (learn):** A collective-security choice: recognising one secession would legitimise reopening every border.
**Breakdown:**
- Evidence is behavioural: the Organization of African Unity (1963) became the forum for inter-state disputes (✅ owner 07 basic §8.7A(a)).
- Nigeria's civil war: the UN, Commonwealth and OAU did not support Biafra's secession (✅ owner basic/18 §7.6A, cited).
- Katanga's secession reversed with UN troops in the Congo (✅ owner).
- Singapore's 1965 separation from Malaysia came by agreement (✅ owner basic/18 §7.5A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** OAU 1963; Biafra war (no date given in owner card); Singapore 1965.
**Links:** WH-2025; Topic 18 (decolonisation) for the border legacy.
**Trap:** Do not claim the OAU adopted a formal border-inviolability resolution or cite an article. The evidence is behavioural (⚠️ owner 07 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Arbitrariness of 1885 became a permanent constitutional condition because revision had no stopping point."
**Back:** *"Keep the lines: reopen one, reopen all."*

### Flashcard WH-2022 — Colonial-source critique
**Front:** How should an answer treat the colonial record on African societies?
**Definition (learn):** The colonial archive is usable but not neutral: it records European intention accurately and African societies through European categories.
**Breakdown:**
- Categories were created by administrators; "tribe" is an administrative artefact.
- Africans were excluded from the rules at Berlin.
- Justificatory framing tested against Herero, Nama and Maji Maji.
- Cross-check against recorded African action (Adwa, Samori Ture, Maji Maji).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 basic §8.7A(b). Owner names no colonial official, report or school; keep it that way.
**Links:** WH-2007; WH-2014; WH-2015; WH-2016.
**Trap:** Do not name specific colonial ethnographers or archives; none is sourced in the owner (§8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Read the colonial archive for what Europeans did and cross-check it against what Africans did."
**Back:** *"Usable, not neutral: check it against African action."*

### Flashcard WH-2023 — Trap: African resistance was not absent
**Front:** Did Africans resist colonial rule?
**Definition (learn):** Yes. Resistance was frequent, though often defeated, and some states resisted successfully.
**Breakdown:**
- Successful: Ethiopia at Adwa (1896).
- Sustained: Zulu resistance; Samori Ture in West Africa.
- Mass revolt after conquest: Maji Maji (1905–07).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 basic §5–6 and §8.6.
**Links:** WH-2014; WH-2016; WH-2022.
**Trap:** Do not write "Africans did not resist". Resistance was uneven because colonial powers had advantages in logistics and weapons (✅ owner 07 advanced §3).
**Mains hook:** "Assess African agency: resistance and collaboration, not passivity."
**Back:** *"Resisted, often lost, sometimes won."*

### Flashcard WH-2024 — Advanced: African agency and passive-victim trap
**Front:** Why is the "Europe acted, Africa received" model a weak answer?
**Definition (learn):** It flattens agency; the stronger formulation is that Europe imposed while African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered.
**Breakdown:**
- Do not write: Europe acted -> Africa received.
- Write: Europe imposed + African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered.
- Collaboration: chiefs, soldiers, clerks, interpreters (⚠️ owner 07 basic §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §3 and basic §8.6.
**Links:** WH-2023; WH-2022.
**Trap:** Collaboration is an analytical category, not a moral judgement (⚠️ owner 07 basic §8.6).
**Mains hook:** "Subordinated but never merely acted upon."
**Back:** *"Imposed, yet never merely received."*

### Flashcard WH-2025 — Social consequences of colonial rule in Africa
**Front:** What social consequences of colonial rule should a 15 or 20 mark answer include?
**Definition (learn):** Land and labour reorganisation, gender effects, administratively fixed authority, health and demography, and borders.
**Breakdown:**
- Land and labour: cash taxation and labour obligations pushed men into mines, plantations and porterage (⚠️ owner 07 §8.7).
- Gender: male migration shifted farming to women; colonial law and missions redefined marriage and property (⚠️).
- Authority: colonial administration invented or hardened chiefs into fixed categories (⚠️).
- Health: forced labour and disrupted food systems raised mortality in several regions (⚠️, qualitative only).
- Borders: boundaries rarely followed ethnic or political realities (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 basic §8.7 (evidence bank D).
**Links:** WH-2021; WH-2022; WH-2016.
**Trap:** Keep health claims qualitative; no figures are sourced (§8.7).
**Mains hook:** "The deepest legacy was structural: export economies, control administrations and fixed identities."
**Back:** *"Land, labour, chiefs, health, borders."*

### GROUP — Latin American Independence Movements (basic 08 + advanced 08)

### Flashcard WH-2026 — Latin American independence: core concept (basic 08)
**Front:** What was the common trigger and the basic structure of Latin American independence?
**Definition (learn):** Wars of liberation from Spain and Portugal (1808–1826), triggered by the Iberian crisis and fought by creole-led and popular forces with different aims.
**Breakdown:**
- Trigger: Napoleon's intervention in Spain (1808).
- Five distinct paths: northern South America, southern South America, Mexico, Brazil and Haiti (✅ owner 08 basic §4).
- Outcome: political sovereignty transferred; social hierarchy largely preserved (except Haiti).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 basic §1 and §4; ✅ owner 08 advanced §10.
**Links:** WH-2027 (trigger); WH-2037 (five-paths trap); WH-2039 (elite-transfer debate).
**Trap:** Do not treat all independence movements as identical republican revolts (✅ owner 08 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "Political independence transferred sovereignty without redistributing power."
**Back:** *"One trigger, five paths, one hierarchy left standing."*

### Flashcard WH-2027 — Iberian crisis of 1808
**Front:** What did Napoleon's 1808 intervention in Spain change for the Americas?
**Definition (learn):** The collapse of legitimacy at the imperial centre, which opened the colonial political opportunity.
**Breakdown:**
- 1808: Napoleon invades Spain (✅ owner 08 basic §1).
- Portuguese court shifts to Brazil in 1808 (✅ owner 08 advanced §5).
- Juntas form and challenge old authority (✅ owner 08 basic §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1808 (✅ owner 08 basic §1, §3).
**Links:** WH-2026; WH-2036 (Brazil).
**Trap:** The external shock opened the door; it did not create the grievances (⚠️ owner 08 basic §3).
**Mains hook:** "The wars were made possible by imperial collapse and made necessary by colonial hierarchy."
**Back:** *"Eighteen-oh-eight: the centre cracks, the colonies move."*

### Flashcard WH-2028 — Peninsulares versus creoles
**Front:** Who were peninsulares and creoles, and why did the rivalry drive independence?
**Definition (learn):** Peninsulares were European-born officeholders; creoles were American-born whites who were wealthy but politically restricted.
**Breakdown:**
- Peninsulares held top colonial offices (✅ owner 08 basic §2).
- Creoles supplied leadership, finance and officers; they became the ruling class after independence (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.4).
- Trade restrictions and monopoly added economic grievance (✅ owner 08 basic §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 basic §2 and advanced §8.4.
**Links:** WH-2029; WH-2041 (verdict).
**Trap:** Creoles were not the mass base; they led and benefited (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.4).
**Mains hook:** "The group that led the movement was the group that benefited from the old order."
**Back:** *"Spain-born rule, Americas-born resent."*

### Flashcard WH-2029 — Mestizos, Indigenous peoples and enslaved Africans
**Front:** Which groups formed the mass base of independence, and what did they gain?
**Definition (learn):** Subordinate groups who supplied the fighting and popular strength but gained unevenly from independence.
**Breakdown:**
- Mestizos: socially and politically subordinate; supplied much of the fighting strength (⚠️ owner 08 advanced §8.4).
- Indigenous peoples: mobilised in Mexico's popular phase under Hidalgo; land pressure often continued (⚠️).
- Enslaved Africans: decisive only in Haiti; emancipation was piecemeal and later elsewhere (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 basic §2; owner advanced §8.4 table.
**Links:** WH-2030 (Haiti); WH-2031 (Hidalgo); WH-2040 (comparison).
**Trap:** Formal legal change did not mean social ascent (⚠️ owner 08 advanced §8.4).
**Mains hook:** Use this table to answer "why independence did not produce equality".
**Back:** *"Fought in the masses, gained in the margins."*

### Flashcard WH-2030 — Haitian Revolution (1791–1804)
**Front:** What was the Haitian Revolution, and why is it the region's control case?
**Definition (learn):** An anti-colonial and anti-slavery revolution that ended French rule and created the first state founded by former slaves.
**Breakdown:**
- Begins 1791; independence 1804 (✅ owner 08 basic §1; ✅ owner 08 advanced §3).
- Leaders: Toussaint Louverture, Dessalines (✅ owner 08 basic §4).
- Only case where independence was social revolution: immediate emancipation (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1791 (begins); 1804 (independence); first free Black republic of the modern era (✅ owner 08 basic §1).
**Links:** WH-2027; WH-2029; WH-2041; WH-2045.
**Trap:** Do not date Haitian independence to 1791 (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.9). Haiti was not a footnote.
**Mains hook:** "Haiti shows what independence looks like when the enslaved, not the creoles, made it."
**Back:** *"Ninety-one begins, four ends: first Black republic."*

### Flashcard WH-2031 — Grito de Dolores (1810)
**Front:** When and where was the Grito de Dolores issued, and by whom?
**Definition (learn):** Hidalgo's call to revolt in Mexico, which turned the independence phase mass and regional.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 16 September 1810 (✅ Wikipedia: Mexican War of Independence, fetched).
- Place: Dolores; leader: Miguel Hidalgo (✅ owner 08 basic §6).
- Hidalgo executed in July 1811 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 16 Sept 1810; July 1811 (executed).
**Links:** WH-2032 (Mexico sequence); WH-2029.
**Trap:** Do not date the Grito to 1821 (Mexican independence). The cry was 1810.
**Mains hook:** The 1810 revolt started popular; the 1821 settlement was conservative.
**Back:** *"Sixteen September, ten: Dolores calls."*

### Flashcard WH-2032 — Mexico: from popular revolt to conservative settlement
**Front:** What was Mexico's path to independence, from Hidalgo to Iturbide?
**Definition (learn):** A sequence beginning in a popular and indigenous rising and ending with a conservative settlement led by a royalist officer.
**Breakdown:**
- Hidalgo (1810): popular rising.
- Morelos: continuation of the insurgency (✅ owner 08 basic §4).
- Iturbide: former royalist commander; Plan of Iguala (1821) with Guerrero (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Mexican independence achieved 1821 (war ended 27 September 1821; ✅ Wikipedia infobox).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1810; 1821 (Plan of Iguala; war end 27 Sept 1821). O'Donojú signed a treaty after entry into Mexico City (✅ Wikipedia; exact Córdoba date not confirmed).
**Links:** WH-2031; WH-2026.
**Trap:** Mexico ended in a conservative settlement, the sharpest reversal in the region (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.5). Do not present Mexico as a republican mass movement throughout.
**Mains hook:** "Mexico began as a popular rising and ended in a settlement the propertied classes could accept."
**Back:** *"Hidalgo rose, Iturbide ruled: ten to twenty-one."*

### Flashcard WH-2033 — Bolívar: northern South America
**Front:** What did Bolívar achieve in northern South America?
**Definition (learn):** A continental republican project in the north, built as both army and political project.
**Breakdown:**
- New Granada secured in 1819; Gran Colombia built (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.5).
- Venezuela, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Bolivia tied to his campaigns (✅ owner 08 basic §4).
- Bolivia emerges as an independent state in 1825 (✅ owner 08 basic §1).
- Gran Colombia's later fragmentation shows fragility (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1819; Gran Colombia; 1825 (Bolivia).
**Links:** WH-2034 (San Martín); WH-2042 (fragmentation); WH-2044.
**Trap:** Bolívar did not permanently unite Spanish America; his integrative vision did not endure (✅ owner 08 advanced §6).
**Mains hook:** "Bolívar built a political project as well as an army."
**Back:** *"Nineteen, Gran Colombia, twenty-five: north's giant."*

### Flashcard WH-2034 — San Martín: southern South America
**Front:** What was San Martín's role in southern South America?
**Definition (learn):** A disciplined regular-army liberation of the south from an external base.
**Breakdown:**
- Crossed the Andes into Chile in 1817 (✅ owner 08 basic §1, §4).
- Argentina, Chile and Peru central to his role (✅ owner 08 basic §4).
- Ayacucho in 1824 broke major royalist power (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1817 (Andes crossing); 1824 (Ayacucho).
**Links:** WH-2033; WH-2035.
**Trap:** Do not present Bolívar and San Martín as operating in the same theatre in the same way (✅ owner 08 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "Complementary rather than parallel; their theatres met in Peru."
**Back:** *"Seventeen across the Andes: south liberated by regulars."*

### Flashcard WH-2035 — Ayacucho (1824)
**Front:** What was the significance of Ayacucho in 1824?
**Definition (learn):** The battle that broke the major royalist military position in South America.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1824 (✅ owner 08 basic §5, §9).
- Effect: Spanish military position collapses (✅ owner 08 basic §1).
- Linked with San Martín's and Bolívar's theatres meeting in Peru (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1824 (only; do not vary, ✅ owner 08 advanced §8.9).
**Links:** WH-2034; WH-2033.
**Trap:** Do not date Ayacucho other than 1824 (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Military defeat decided the south-American outcome.
**Back:** *"Twenty-four: royal army falls."*

### Flashcard WH-2036 — Brazil under Pedro I (1822)
**Front:** How did Brazil become independent, and how did it differ from Spanish America?
**Definition (learn):** Brazil separated from Portugal in 1822 under Pedro I through a monarchical, comparatively non-violent path.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1822 (✅ owner 08 basic §5).
- Monarchy, not republic.
- Slavery persisted long after independence (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1822; Pedro I.
**Links:** WH-2027 (Portuguese court's shift); WH-2037 (trap).
**Trap:** Do not say Brazil followed the Spanish-American route (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Brazil's path was monarchical and less conventionally revolutionary."
**Back:** *"Twenty-two: monarch-led, slavery stays."*

### Flashcard WH-2037 — Trap: five paths, not four or six
**Front:** How many distinct independence paths does the owner recognise, and which are they?
**Definition (learn):** Five: Haiti, northern South America, southern South America, Mexico and Brazil.
**Breakdown:**
- Haiti (social revolution).
- Northern South America (Bolívar).
- Southern South America (San Martín).
- Mexico (popular then conservative).
- Brazil (monarchical).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 basic §4 and advanced §8.5 (count discipline note).
**Links:** WH-2026; WH-2030; WH-2033; WH-2032; WH-2036.
**Trap:** Do not describe the paths as four or six (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Name each path with its distinctive feature in one line.
**Back:** *"Haiti, north, south, Mexico, Brazil: five."*

### Flashcard WH-2038 — Caudillismo: the mechanism
**Front:** What is caudillismo, and what mechanism produced it?
**Definition (learn):** Personalist rule by a military strongman resting on armed clientage rather than institutions.
**Breakdown:**
- Long wars of liberation -> military authority won and held by commanders.
- Weak institutions inherited from colonial rule.
- Regional landowners fund private armed followings.
- Personal loyalty replaces institutional legitimacy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 advanced §4 (Norman Lowe continuity context); ⚠️ owner 08 advanced §8.6 chain.
**Links:** WH-2042 (fragmentation); WH-2045 (instability); Topic 19 for the 20th-century sequel.
**Trap:** Name the phenomenon and its mechanism. Do not name individual caudillos or their dates; none is sourced (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Independence produced states before nations, and armies before institutions."
**Back:** *"War-made strongmen, weak institutions, private armies."*

### Flashcard WH-2039 — Advanced: elite-transfer debate
**Front:** Was Latin American independence a liberation of peoples or a transfer of elite power?
**Definition (learn):** The debate between political decolonisation and social democratisation; the best synthesis is that political decolonisation outran social change.
**Breakdown:**
- Side one: ended European rule, created new states.
- Side two: transferred power from peninsulares to creoles.
- Synthesis: political decolonisation outran social democratisation (✅ owner 08 advanced §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 advanced §1 debate matrix.
**Links:** WH-2028; WH-2029; WH-2041; WH-2043.
**Trap:** Never write that independence "completed democracy". Democratic deepening remained uneven (✅ owner 08 advanced §6).
**Mains hook:** "Politically revolutionary but socially incomplete."
**Back:** *"Flag changed, hierarchy stayed."*

### Flashcard WH-2040 — Advanced: comparison with later decolonisation
**Front:** How does 1808–26 Latin American independence compare with 1945–75 Africa and Asia?
**Definition (learn):** A bounded comparison across trigger, leadership, colonial type, method, social outcome and post-independence problems.
**Breakdown:**
- Trigger: imperial collapse (1808) vs European exhaustion after 1945.
- Leaders: creole elites vs colonial nationalist movements.
- Method: prolonged wars vs mixed (Ghana negotiated; Algeria and Indo-China armed).
- Social outcome: hierarchy preserved vs varied.
- Post-independence: caudillismo vs arbitrary borders, weak institutions and Cold War interference.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 advanced §8.7 and basic §8.7 table; Topic 18 owns the Africa/Asia side.
**Links:** WH-2038; Topic 18 cards.
**Trap:** Do not treat "decolonisation" as one process (✅ owner 08 advanced §8.7).
**Mains hook:** "The same word covers a settler-elite secession in 1820 and an anti-colonial mass movement in 1960."
**Back:** *"Secession of creoles, not mass revolt: compare with care."*

### Flashcard WH-2041 — Verdict: independence and power
**Front:** What verdict does the owner give on why independence did not equalise society?
**Definition (learn):** The revolution was fought over who governs, not over how society is ordered.
**Breakdown:**
- Creoles removed peninsulares.
- Land, racial hierarchy and slavery largely persisted.
- Haiti asked the second question and produced a different outcome.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 advanced §8.8 verdict.
**Links:** WH-2028; WH-2030; WH-2039.
**Trap:** Do not blame "leaders were selfish" (owner: do not write this).
**Mains hook:** "Independence confirmed the creole landowner in place of the peninsular official."
**Back:** *"Who governs changed; how society is ordered did not."*

### Flashcard WH-2042 — Fragmentation: Gran Colombia and Central America
**Front:** Why did continental federations in Latin America break apart?
**Definition (learn):** Gran Colombia and the Central American union both broke up, because distance, weak communications and regional elite interests defeated continental federation.
**Breakdown:**
- Gran Colombia (Bolívar's project) fragmented.
- Central American union broke apart.
- Regional elite interests overrode continental structure.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 08 advanced §8.6 (no dates given).
**Links:** WH-2033; WH-2038.
**Trap:** Do not say Bolívar permanently united Spanish America (✅ owner 08 advanced §6).
**Mains hook:** Regional fragmentation as a post-independence condition, not a failure of ideas alone.
**Back:** *"Continental dreams, regional elites."*

### Flashcard WH-2043 — 1826 Amphictyonic Congress (Panama)
**Front:** What does the 2026 Panama bicentennial link support in this topic?
**Definition (learn):** A bounded reference to the 1826 Amphictyonic Congress as an expression of Bolívar's regional-cooperation project.
**Breakdown:**
- Congress: 1826 (owner-cited bicentennial linkage).
- Supports only Bolívar's regional-cooperation vision.
- Does not validate decorative ceremony detail.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1826 (✅ owner 08 advanced §10, PYQ/current block). The Panama bicentennial is a 2026 current-affairs peg, owner-only (no fetch).
**Links:** WH-2033; WH-2039.
**Trap:** Do not add ceremonial detail not sourced in the owner.
**Mains hook:** Regional cooperation was Bolívar's aim even when it did not survive.
**Back:** *"Twenty-six: Panama, Bolívar's regional dream."*

### Flashcard WH-2044 — Date ladder: Latin American independence
**Front:** Put these Latin American dates in order: Haiti 1804; Iberian crisis 1808; Grito 1810; Andes crossing 1817; New Granada 1819; Mexico 1821; Brazil 1822; Ayacucho 1824; Bolivia 1825.
**Definition (learn):** A date ladder to recall the sequence of stages across paths.
**Breakdown:**
- 1804: Haiti independence.
- 1808: Iberian crisis.
- 1810: Grito de Dolores (16 Sept).
- 1817: San Martín crosses the Andes.
- 1819: New Granada and Gran Colombia.
- 1821: Mexico (war ends 27 Sept 1821).
- 1822: Brazil under Pedro I.
- 1824: Ayacucho.
- 1825: Bolivia.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 basic §1 and advanced §10 (date control); Mexico 1810, 1821 ✅ Wikipedia fetched.
**Links:** WH-2026 to WH-2036.
**Trap:** Do not merge Ayacucho (1824) with Bolivia (1825) or Brazil (1822).
**Mains hook:** Use date stages to show sequence, not chronicle.
**Back:** *"Four, eight, ten, seventeen, nineteen, twenty-one, twenty-two, twenty-four, twenty-five."*

### Flashcard WH-2045 — Haiti as the control case
**Front:** Why is Haiti described as the "control case" of Latin American independence?
**Definition (learn):** Haiti shows what independence looked like when the enslaved made it, and why creole-led movements took care to avoid it.
**Breakdown:**
- Anti-colonial plus anti-slavery social revolution.
- Mainland Spanish America: anti-colonial, creole-led political revolution.
- Brazil: monarchical separation from Portugal.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 advanced §3 diagram; owner 08 basic §5.
**Links:** WH-2030; WH-2041; WH-2029.
**Trap:** Haiti was not a peripheral duplicate of the mainland cases (✅ owner 08 advanced §10).
**Mains hook:** "Haiti is the control case; the creole movements elsewhere ensured it was not repeated."
**Back:** *"Haiti is the one where society moved too."*

### GROUP — World in 1914 and the Outbreak of the First World War (basic 09 + advanced 09)

### Flashcard WH-2046 — Sarajevo as trigger, not sufficient cause (basic 09)
**Front:** What was the immediate trigger of the First World War, and why is it insufficient as an explanation?
**Definition (learn):** The assassination at Sarajevo lit the fuse; the continent had already been filled with alliance, nationalist and imperial tensions.
**Breakdown:**
- Trigger: Archduke Franz Ferdinand assassinated on 28 June 1914.
- Structural: alliance blocs, naval rivalry, imperial tension, nationalism.
- Escalation: July crisis transmitted choices across the system.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 28 June 1914 (✅ owner 09 basic mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-2052; WH-2053; WH-2061.
**Trap:** Do not reduce the cause of the war to alliances alone or to the assassination alone (✅ owner 09 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "Sarajevo lit the match; the continent had already been filled with fuel."
**Back:** *"Sarajevo ignites, the system explodes."*

### Flashcard WH-2047 — Triple Alliance (1882)
**Front:** Which powers formed the Triple Alliance, and what happened to Italy?
**Definition (learn):** The 1882 alliance of Germany, Austria-Hungary and Italy.
**Breakdown:**
- Members: Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy (✅ owner 09 basic §3).
- Italy later entered the war on the Allied side in 1915 (✅ owner 09 advanced §4 / basic §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1882 (✅ owner 09 basic mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-2048; WH-2063 (Italy trap).
**Trap:** Do not write that Italy fought from the start with Germany and Austria (✅ owner 09 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** Alliance membership was not automatic war obligation.
**Back:** *"Eighty-two: three in, Italy later out."*

### Flashcard WH-2048 — Triple Entente and its treaties
**Front:** What were the Franco-Russian alliance, Entente Cordiale and Anglo-Russian agreement, and when were they signed?
**Definition (learn):** The network that formed the opposing camp (Britain, France, Russia).
**Breakdown:**
- Franco-Russian alliance: 1894.
- Entente Cordiale (Britain-France): 1904.
- Anglo-Russian agreement: 1907; deepened German encirclement fears.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1894; 1904; 1907 (✅ owner 09 basic mini-timeline and §4).
**Links:** WH-2047; WH-2049; WH-2057.
**Trap:** Kennan saw the 1894 Franco-Russian alliance as sealing Europe's fate, which is a historiographical view, not a fact of war (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
**Mains hook:** The Entente converted a two-bloc system into encirclement anxiety.
**Back:** *"Ninety-four, four, seven: the other camp."*

### Flashcard WH-2049 — Moroccan and Agadir crises
**Front:** What were the Moroccan Crisis (1905–06) and Agadir Crisis (1911), and what did each show?
**Definition (learn):** Two tests of the Entente; each Germany lost diplomatically or raised anti-British feeling.
**Breakdown:**
- Moroccan Crisis 1905–06: tested the Entente; Germany suffered diplomatic defeat (✅ owner 09 basic §4).
- Agadir 1911: Britain backed France; anti-British feeling deepened in Germany (✅ owner 09 basic §4).
- Both show the system could manage crises without general war (✅ owner 09 advanced §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1905–06; 1911.
**Links:** WH-2050; WH-2057; WH-2058.
**Trap:** Do not date Agadir loosely; it is 1911 (✅ owner 09 basic §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Earlier crises were managed by the same logic, so the system did not require war."
**Back:** *"Oh-five to oh-six and eleven: tested, managed."*

### Flashcard WH-2050 — Bosnia Crisis (1908)
**Front:** What did the Bosnia Crisis of 1908 involve, and why did it matter?
**Definition (learn):** Austria annexed Bosnia; Serbia stayed bitter; Russia resolved not to suffer humiliation again.
**Breakdown:**
- 1908: Austria annexed Bosnia (✅ owner 09 basic §6).
- Austro-Serb hostility intensified (✅ owner 09 basic mini-timeline).
- Russia's resolution after 1908 shaped the 1914 calculus (✅ owner 09 basic §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1908.
**Links:** WH-2051; WH-2058.
**Trap:** Bosnia 1908 is not the same as the 1912–13 Balkan Wars (owner §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Each side scored regional change as a shift in the great-power ledger."
**Back:** *"Oh-eight: Bosnia annexed, Russia bitter."*

### Flashcard WH-2051 — Balkan Wars (1912–13)
**Front:** What did the Balkan Wars change for Serbia and Austria?
**Definition (learn):** Two wars in 1912–13 that strengthened Serbia and made Austria more determined to curb it.
**Breakdown:**
- Serbia emerged stronger (✅ owner 09 basic §4).
- Austria-Hungary alarmed and more determined to curb Serbia (✅ owner 09 basic §4).
- Serbian success threatened the Habsburg Empire's internal survival (⚠️ owner 09 basic §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1912–13 (First and Second Balkan Wars).
**Links:** WH-2050; WH-2052; WH-2058.
**Trap:** Do not date the Balkan Wars loosely (✅ owner 09 advanced §8 / basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Balkan nationalism converted a regional crisis into a continental one."
**Back:** *"Twelve-thirteen: Serbia grows, Vienna worries."*

### Flashcard WH-2052 — Assassination at Sarajevo (28 June 1914)
**Front:** Who was assassinated at Sarajevo, on what date, and by whom?
**Definition (learn):** The killing that Austria-Hungary used as the excuse for war on Serbia.
**Breakdown:**
- Victim: Archduke Franz Ferdinand.
- Date: 28 June 1914.
- Assassin: Gavrilo Princip (✅ owner 09 basic §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 28 June 1914 (✅ owner 09 basic mini-timeline and §4).
**Links:** WH-2046; WH-2053.
**Trap:** Do not treat the assassination as the complete cause (✅ owner 09 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** Use Sarajevo as opening paragraph trigger only.
**Back:** *"June 28: Princip, Franz Ferdinand."*

### Flashcard WH-2053 — July crisis escalation chain
**Front:** Trace the July 1914 escalation from Austria's declaration to Britain's entry.
**Definition (learn):** The sequence that converted a Balkan quarrel into a general war.
**Breakdown:**
- 28 July: Austria-Hungary declares war on Serbia (✅ owner 09 basic mini-timeline).
- Russia mobilises for Serbia.
- Germany backs Austria and mobilises.
- Germany attacks France through Belgium.
- Britain enters to defend Belgian neutrality (4 August) (✅ owner 09 basic §4 and advanced §8.6).
- Germany declares war on Russia (1 August) and France (3 August) (✅ owner 09 basic §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 28 July; 1 August; 3 August; 4 August 1914.
**Links:** WH-2046; WH-2055; WH-2056; WH-2058.
**Trap:** Do not use "Britain entered because of the alliance with France". Belgian neutrality was the legal-political trigger (⚠️ owner 09 basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** The chain shows operational timetables and contingent decisions, not a single pre-set plan.
**Back:** *"Serbia, Russia, Germany, Belgium, Britain: five steps."*

### Flashcard WH-2054 — German blank cheque
**Front:** What was the German "blank cheque" and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** German support for Austria-Hungary in July 1914 that enabled Austria's hard line on Serbia; Fischer gave it special weight.
**Breakdown:**
- Crucial enabling factor for Austria's hard line (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
- Fischer's argument: German leaders risked war (✅ owner 09 advanced §4b).
- Lowe's phrase "blank cheque" (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** July 1914 (✅ owner 09 advanced §2 and §7).
**Links:** WH-2053; WH-2059 (historiography); WH-2061.
**Trap:** Do not claim Germany planned the war for years; the folder records this as contested (✅ owner 09 basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Blank cheque shows a core German share of blame without absolving Vienna or Russia."
**Back:** *"Berlin backed Vienna without limit."*

### Flashcard WH-2055 — Schlieffen Plan (contested)
**Front:** What was the Schlieffen Plan, and why must it be presented with care?
**Definition (learn):** German mobilisation planning that required an attack through Belgium against France; treated as a fixed master-plan in older accounts but contested by Zuber.
**Breakdown:**
- German mobilisation planning required an attack through Belgium (✅ owner 09 basic §8.8).
- Zuber challenges a single fixed blueprint (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Ritter: planning was dangerously rigid (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the Belgian route.
**Links:** WH-2053; WH-2056; WH-2067.
**Trap:** Do not present the Schlieffen Plan as an uncontested fixed blueprint (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Mains hook:** "Rigid planning turned diplomacy into a race against the clock."
**Back:** *"Through Belgium, but not a fixed script."*

### Flashcard WH-2056 — Britain's entry (4 August 1914)
**Front:** Why did Britain enter the war on 4 August 1914?
**Definition (learn):** Britain entered after Germany violated Belgian neutrality; there was no binding alliance obligation of that kind.
**Breakdown:**
- Belgian neutrality was the immediate legal-political issue (✅ owner 09 basic §6).
- Balance-of-power threat: a hostile power on the Channel coast (✅ owner 09 basic §8.4).
- Naval rivalry with Germany (✅ owner 09 basic §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 August 1914.
**Links:** WH-2053; WH-2057; WH-2066.
**Trap:** Do not say Britain entered "because of the alliance with France" (✅ owner 09 basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Britain's entry is the best example where balance-of-power reading works (owner §8.4).
**Back:** *"Belgium, not a treaty: Britain's trigger."*

### Flashcard WH-2057 — 2024 PYQ: balance of power (15 marks, 250 words)
**Front:** How far is it correct to say that the First World War was fought essentially for the preservation of balance of power?
**Definition (learn):** The verified 2024 GS-I demand; 15 marks, 250 words, comment.
**Breakdown:**
- Balance explains the structure: the two blocs and encirclement fears.
- It does not explain every belligerent's motive or the timing of July 1914.
- Use nationalism, imperial insecurity, mobilisation rigidity and contingent July choices to qualify.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-I, Q12 (✅ owner 09 advanced §8 and basic §8.1 PYQ block).
**Links:** WH-2058; WH-2048; WH-2053.
**Trap:** A yes/no answer is wrong. Use a graded verdict: partly correct, and what it misses.
**Mains hook:** "Balance of power explains the system, not the motives or the timing."
**Back:** *"Structure yes, motive and timing no."*

### Flashcard WH-2058 — Balance of power: what it explains and what it misses
**Front:** Which parts of the 1914 war does balance-of-power logic explain, and which does it not?
**Definition (learn):** A two-sided evidence bank: explains the alliance structure and Britain's entry; cannot explain imperial survival, nationalism, revanchism, preventive war or operational rigidity.
**Breakdown:**
- Explains: two blocs; encirclement fears; naval ratio; Balkan power transfers.
- Misses: Habsburg survival; South Slav nationalism; French revanchism; German preventive timing; mobilisation timetables.
- Earlier crises (1905–06, 1908, 1911) show the system did not require war.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 09 basic §8.4–8.5.
**Links:** WH-2057; WH-2064; WH-2065.
**Trap:** Balance explains the structure, not every motive (✅ owner 09 basic §8.7).
**Mains hook:** "Necessary part of the explanation, not the essential one."
**Back:** *"Balance explains the room, not every person in it."*

### Flashcard WH-2059 — Historiography of the 1914 blame debate (advanced 09)
**Front:** Who are the main historians in the 1914 blame debate and what did each argue?
**Definition (learn):** A named historiography from Lowe, used as optional enrichment.
**Breakdown:**
- Kennan: alliance blocs made war highly likely.
- Fischer: Germany took most blame; leaders risked war deliberately.
- Koch: challenged weight given to the 1912 war council.
- Ritter: German mobilisation planning dangerously rigid.
- Taylor: railway timetables accelerated events.
- Zuber: challenged a fixed Schlieffen Plan.
- Turner: tragedy of miscalculation.
- Hamilton and Herwig: ultimate German responsibility.
- Williamson: Vienna's decision-making had its own autonomy.
- Rohl: Wilhelm II's recklessness mattered.
- Mulligan: Russian rearmament deserves serious weight.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 09 advanced §3 matrix.
**Links:** WH-2055; WH-2067; WH-2068.
**Trap:** Do not quote any historian verbatim; name positions only as in the owner (✅ owner 09 basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Use two or three historians to show a contested verdict.
**Back:** *"Kennan, Fischer, Zuber, Turner: four voices, one debate."*

### Flashcard WH-2060 — Advanced: was war inevitable?
**Front:** Was war inevitable once Europe split into two camps?
**Definition (learn):** No. Earlier crises did not produce general war and alliance obligations were not mechanically binding; July 1914 decisions mattered.
**Breakdown:**
- Earlier crises (1905–06, 1908, 1911) did not produce general war.
- Alliances created a dangerous framework, not a predetermined war.
- July decisions broke the pattern.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 09 advanced §4a.
**Links:** WH-2053; WH-2057; WH-2049.
**Trap:** "Alliances made war certain" is wrong (✅ owner 09 basic §8.1).
**Mains hook:** "Highly likely, but not inevitable."
**Back:** *"Likely by 1914, decided in July."*

### Flashcard WH-2061 — Two levels of responsibility
**Front:** How should an answer treat "who was responsible for the war"?
**Definition (learn):** Immediate responsibility lies with decision-makers, systemic responsibility lies with the alliance order.
**Breakdown:**
- Immediate: Vienna's decision for war and Berlin's backing (✅ owner 09 basic §8.7).
- Systemic: militarised alliance order where restraint looked like weakness (✅ owner 09 basic §8.7).
- Turner's view: miscalculation by Austria, Germany and Russia (✅ owner 09 advanced §4d).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the July sequence.
**Links:** WH-2054; WH-2059; WH-2062.
**Trap:** Naming one state in one sentence fails the "who was responsible" directive (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Mains hook:** "Two levels: decision-makers and the system."
**Back:** *"Vienna decided, the system allowed."*

### Flashcard WH-2062 — The 1914 world: power and political systems
**Front:** What was the state of the world in 1914, according to the owner?
**Definition (learn):** Europe still dominated world politics, with Germany the leading continental power, Britain the naval power, and the USA and Japan rising.
**Breakdown:**
- Germany: leading continental military-economic power.
- Britain: naval power with global empire.
- France: revanchist after 1871.
- Russia: expanding but insecure.
- Austria-Hungary: multinational empire under strain.
- Serbia: small, assertive, centre of South Slav nationalism.
- Political systems: Britain, France, USA democratic; Germany, Italy, Japan elected bodies with limited effective democracy; Russia and Austria-Hungary autocratic (✅ owner 09 basic §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 09 basic §2 (Lowe).
**Links:** WH-2065; WH-2064; WH-2046.
**Trap:** Do not write that Europe was a democracy-autocracy binary; Germany, Italy and Japan had elected bodies with limited democracy (✅ owner 09 basic §2).
**Mains hook:** Use the power table to explain why a regional crisis could widen.
**Back:** *"Germany steel, Britain sea, France grudge, Russia pride, Serbia pulse."*

### Flashcard WH-2063 — Trap: static two camps and Italy
**Front:** Does the two-camp map show a static alignment that automatically ended in war?
**Definition (learn):** No. Alliances were not identical automatic war plans, and Italy's 1915 switch disproves a static map.
**Breakdown:**
- Triple Alliance included Italy, which later joined the Allies (1915).
- Alignments were diplomatic instruments, not war commitments.
- Alliance obligations were not mechanically binding (✅ owner 09 advanced §4a).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1915 (Italy's entry on the Allied side, ✅ owner 09 basic §6).
**Links:** WH-2047; WH-2060.
**Trap:** Do not write that the alliance treaties obliged every signatory to fight (✅ owner 09 basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Alignment was a wager, not a binding script."
**Back:** *"Italy switched sides: the map was not destiny."*

### Flashcard WH-2064 — Alsace-Lorraine and French revanchism
**Front:** What did the loss of Alsace-Lorraine (1871) mean for French policy in 1914?
**Definition (learn):** A territorial-national grievance that made France revanchist and wanted the territory restored.
**Breakdown:**
- Loss in 1871.
- France wanted Alsace-Lorraine restored (✅ owner 09 basic §2).
- Revanchism is a territorial grievance, not a balancing objective (✅ owner 09 basic §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1871 (✅ owner 09 basic §2, §3, §8.5).
**Links:** WH-2058; WH-2062; Topic 06 (unification and Alsace-Lorraine's creation).
**Trap:** Revanchism does not fit a pure balance-of-power explanation (✅ owner 09 basic §8.5).
**Mains hook:** A grievance-based cause that balance-of-power theory cannot absorb.
**Back:** *"Seventy-one loss, fourteen wanting back."*

### Flashcard WH-2065 — Anglo-German naval rivalry
**Front:** Why did the Anglo-German naval race matter more than colonial rivalry?
**Definition (learn):** Naval competition, not colonies, was the direct friction between Britain and Germany.
**Breakdown:**
- Britain feared the rise of German naval power (✅ owner 09 basic §2).
- Lowe finds colonial rivalry less convincing as a direct cause (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
- Naval race was a power-ratio contest (✅ owner 09 basic §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No specific ratio figure is sourced in the owner (do not give one, §8.8).
**Links:** WH-2056; WH-2058; WH-2066.
**Trap:** Do not say Germany fought Britain because of colonies alone (✅ owner 09 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "Britain's concern was the relative naval balance, not territory."
**Back:** *"Ships, not colonies, pushed London."*

### Flashcard WH-2066 — Trap: colonies did not cause the war alone
**Front:** Did colonial rivalry alone cause the war between Britain and Germany?
**Definition (learn):** No. Naval rivalry was more direct, and colonial rivalry was present but less decisive by 1914.
**Breakdown:**
- Colonial rivalry: present, mention then qualify (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
- Naval race: serious, though less decisive by 1914.
- Economic rivalry: a Marxist explanation Lowe treats cautiously (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 09 basic §6 and advanced §2.
**Links:** WH-2065; WH-2056.
**Trap:** Colonies are not the central cause. The owner gives naval rivalry priority.
**Mains hook:** Use naval rivalry as the direct cause, colonial rivalry as a background.
**Back:** *"Colonies lit smaller fires."*

### Flashcard WH-2067 — Russian mobilisation and rearmament
**Front:** Why did Russian mobilisation and rearmament matter for the outbreak?
**Definition (learn):** Russia's prestige and Balkan position, and Russian mobilisation, helped make Germany feel pressed toward risk.
**Breakdown:**
- Russia backed Serbia; prestige and Dardanelles concern mattered (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
- Russia ordered general mobilisation after Austria moved on Serbia (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
- Mulligan: Russian rearmament deserves serious weight (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Russian general mobilisation after Austria's move (✅ owner 09 advanced §7; no date given).
**Links:** WH-2055; WH-2059; WH-2068.
**Trap:** Do not present Russia as a passive victim (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Mains hook:** "Russian mobilisation created the timetable pressure that German planning converted into war."
**Back:** *"Russia mobilised, Germany countered."*

### Flashcard WH-2068 — Turner: tragedy of miscalculation
**Front:** What is the "tragedy of miscalculation" interpretation of 1914?
**Definition (learn):** L. C. F. Turner argued that Austria, Germany and Russia all miscalculated, each expecting localisation or quick victory.
**Breakdown:**
- Austria assumed Russia would stay out (✅ owner 09 advanced §4d).
- Germany assumed pressure would localise the war.
- Russian and German leaders treated mobilisation too casually; generals trusted quick victory.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 09 advanced §4d.
**Links:** WH-2059; WH-2061.
**Trap:** Do not present miscalculation as the only explanation; it is a balancing view (✅ owner 09 advanced §4d).
**Mains hook:** "Balanced reply to a one-state blame answer."
**Back:** *"Each side guessed wrong."*

### GROUP — First World War and Aftermath (basic 10 + advanced 10)

### Flashcard WH-2069 — War as deadlock and political earthquake (basic 10)
**Front:** Why is the First World War described as both a military deadlock and a political earthquake?
**Definition (learn):** A prolonged industrial war of attrition that destroyed old empires, produced revolutions and left an unstable peace.
**Breakdown:**
- Military: trench stalemate, attrition, sea war and imperial manpower.
- Political: destruction of empires, revolutions in Germany and Russia, new successor states, League of Nations.
- Social: total war, women in factories, blockade hardship.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §1 and §4.
**Links:** WH-2070; WH-2077; WH-2087.
**Trap:** Do not treat "Versailles" as the whole peace settlement (owner §6).
**Mains hook:** "Military deadlock, political earthquake."
**Back:** *"Stalemate on land, quake in politics."*

### Flashcard WH-2070 — Battle of the Marne (September 1914)
**Front:** What did the Marne (September 1914) achieve?
**Definition (learn):** It ended the German hope of quick victory and locked the western front into trench warfare.
**Breakdown:**
- German advance halted at the Marne (✅ owner 10 basic §2).
- Trench stalemate followed (✅ owner 10 basic §2).
- Did not stop Eastern Front movement (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** September 1914 (✅ owner 10 basic mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-2069; WH-2071; WH-2075.
**Trap:** Do not generalise trenches to all theatres. The eastern front saw more movement (⚠️ owner 10 basic §8.4).
**Mains hook:** Marne is the start of the attrition logic.
**Back:** *"Marne: the quick war ends."*

### Flashcard WH-2071 — Gallipoli, Verdun and the Somme
**Front:** What were the key 1915 and 1916 battles, and what did they show?
**Definition (learn):** Gallipoli failed in 1915; Verdun and the Somme (1916) were attritional battles with huge casualties and limited gains.
**Breakdown:**
- Gallipoli: failed in 1915 (✅ owner 10 basic §6).
- Verdun and Somme 1916: attrition, long-term exhaustion of Central Powers (✅ owner 10 basic §8.4).
- Bulgaria joined Central Powers; Serbia overrun (1915) (✅ owner 10 basic §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1915; 1916 (no casualty totals; owner forbids them).
**Links:** WH-2070; WH-2075; WH-2088 (Haig debate).
**Trap:** Do not cite casualty totals for these battles (✅ owner 10 basic §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Attrition as deliberate strategy, not only failure.
**Back:** *"Fifteen Gallipoli, sixteen Verdun-Somme: the grind."*

### Flashcard WH-2072 — Unrestricted submarine warfare, convoys and US entry
**Front:** How did unrestricted submarine warfare backfire and bring the USA in?
**Definition (learn):** Germany's January 1917 resumption of unrestricted submarine warfare led to US entry in April 1917, and the convoy system blunted U-boat losses.
**Breakdown:**
- Germany resumed unrestricted submarine warfare: January 1917 (✅ owner 10 basic §6).
- USA entered: April 1917 (✅ owner 10 basic mini-timeline).
- Convoy system reduced losses (✅ owner 10 basic §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** January 1917; April 1917.
**Links:** WH-2073; WH-2075; WH-2076.
**Trap:** Do not say the USA decided the war by troop numbers alone; finance, food, shipping and morale mattered (✅ owner 10 basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** A tactical bid became a strategic catastrophe.
**Back:** *"Seventeen: U-boats in, Americans in."*

### Flashcard WH-2073 — Brest-Litovsk (March 1918)
**Front:** What was the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk, and what did it do for Russia?
**Definition (learn):** The March 1918 treaty that took Russia out of the First World War at a huge territorial cost.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: March 1918 (✅ owner 10 basic mini-timeline and 13 basic §4).
- Lenin argued space had to be sacrificed to gain time (✅ owner 13 basic §4).
- Germany had itself imposed Brest-Litovsk harshly on Russia (✅ owner 10 advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** March 1918.
**Links:** WH-2072; WH-2159 (Russia); WH-2076.
**Trap:** Do not date Brest-Litovsk loosely (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
**Mains hook:** Brest-Litovsk as the reason German forces could be redeployed, and as a cautionary example for the settlement.
**Back:** *"March 1918: Russia exits, huge cost."*

### Flashcard WH-2074 — Ludendorff offensive, armistice (11 November 1918)
**Front:** What happened in 1918 leading to the armistice of 11 November?
**Definition (learn):** Spring-to-August 1918 Ludendorff offensive failed; the Allied counter-offensive broke the Hindenburg Line and Central Powers collapsed.
**Breakdown:**
- Spring-August 1918: Ludendorff offensive fails (✅ owner 10 basic mini-timeline).
- Allied counter-attack breaks the Hindenburg Line (✅ owner 10 basic §2).
- Armistice 11 November 1918 (✅ owner 10 basic and §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 11 Nov 1918.
**Links:** WH-2076; WH-2077.
**Trap:** Germany asked for an armistice before deep invasion of German soil; do not say it was fully occupied (✅ owner 10 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** Military failure plus Allied unity under Foch, not invasion alone.
**Back:** *"Eleven-eleven, eighteen: Germany asks, Allies accept."*

### Flashcard WH-2075 — Why the war lasted
**Front:** Why did the war last so long, according to the owner's two-sided explanation?
**Definition (learn):** Balanced coalitions, defensive firepower, unyielding war aims, propaganda and global expansion.
**Breakdown:**
- Sides fairly evenly balanced.
- Defensive firepower outmatched offensive methods (✅ owner 10 basic §3).
- Strong war aims; unwillingness to compromise.
- Propaganda sustained morale and delayed collapse (⚠️ duration, not victory).
- Expansion into a global conflict with imperial manpower.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3 and §8.4.
**Links:** WH-2070; WH-2071; WH-2076.
**Trap:** Propaganda is a cause of duration, not of victory (⚠️ owner 10 basic §8.4).
**Mains hook:** "Neither side could convert local success into strategic breakthrough."
**Back:** *"Balanced, defended, unwilling, propagated, global."*

### Flashcard WH-2076 — Why the Central Powers lost
