**Front:** Why did the Central Powers lose the war?
**Definition (learn):** Cumulative causation across fronts, sea and economy: failed Schlieffen Plan, Allied sea power, submarine failure, American resources, Allied unity under Foch and collapse of allies.
**Breakdown:**
- Failure of Schlieffen Plan -> two-front war (✅ owner 10 basic §3).
- Allied sea power and blockade (✅).
- Submarine failure brought the USA in (✅).
- American resources compensated for Russia's withdrawal (✅).
- Allied leadership and unity under Foch improved in 1918 (✅).
- Allies collapsed first: Bulgaria, Austria-Hungary, Turkey (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3.
**Links:** WH-2072; WH-2073; WH-2074; WH-2075.
**Trap:** Do not say the Central Powers lost at one point or because of one battle (✅ owner 10 basic §8.1).
**Mains hook:** "Exhausted across a system: blockade, a second front, a failed submarine gamble and American resources."
**Back:** *"Two fronts, no sea, no sub, no reserve."*

### Flashcard WH-2077 — Total war and its social effects
**Front:** What made the First World War the first "total war", and what were its social effects?
**Definition (learn):** Mobilisation of whole populations, modern industry and state control, with women in factories and civilians exposed to blockade.
**Breakdown:**
- State control of industry, transport and food (✅ owner 10 basic §8.5).
- Women in factories (✅ owner 10 advanced §2).
- Civilians as targets through blockade (✅ owner 10 basic §8.5).
- Colonial manpower and territory (✅).
- Political revolution: Germany, Russia, Habsburg collapse (✅).
- Italy's postwar crisis, exploited by Mussolini (✅ owner 10 basic §4).
- Relative gain for USA and Japan (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §4 and §8.5 (evidence bank B).
**Links:** WH-2069; WH-2074; WH-2122 (fascism bridge).
**Trap:** Separate institutional effects (new states, League, Weimar, Bolshevism) from social effects (casualties, women, total-war governance, psychological shock) (⚠️ owner 10 basic §4).
**Mains hook:** "The war was won in shipyards, factories, food systems and state capacity."
**Back:** *"Whole societies, not just armies."*

### Flashcard WH-2078 — Versailles: German terms
**Front:** What were the main terms of the Treaty of Versailles (1919) for Germany?
**Definition (learn):** The Versailles settlement imposed territorial losses, disarmament, reparations and war-guilt liability on Germany.
**Breakdown:**
- Alsace-Lorraine returned to France (✅ owner 10 basic §5).
- West Prussia and Posen to Poland; Danzig a free city (✅).
- Saar under League administration for 15 years, French use of the coal mines (✅; Wikipedia confirms 15 years and French coal output).
- Union with Austria forbidden (✅).
- German colonies became League mandates (✅).
- Rhineland demilitarised (✅ owner 10 basic §6; Wikipedia fetched).
- Article 231 (see WH-2079).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1919; Saar 15 years (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Versailles, fetched).
**Links:** WH-2079; WH-2080; WH-2081; WH-2086.
**Trap:** Do not treat Versailles as the whole settlement. Name St Germain, Trianon, Neuilly, Sèvres and Lausanne (WH-2082 to WH-2085).
**Mains hook:** Use the Versailles terms to test whether German objections were fair (WH-2090).
**Back:** *"Land, army, Saar, Rhine, colonies, Article 231."*

### Flashcard WH-2079 — Article 231 and the "war-guilt clause"
**Front:** What did Article 231 say, and why is it called the "war-guilt clause"?
**Definition (learn):** The clause in which Germany and its allies accepted responsibility for causing Allied loss and damage, which became the legal basis for reparations and was politically read as a war-guilt clause.
**Breakdown:**
- Legal function: basis for reparations liability (✅ owner 10 basic §5 and advanced §8).
- Political meaning: "war-guilt clause" in German politics (✅ owner 10 advanced §5).
- Wikipedia: Germany accepted "responsibility of Germany and her allies for causing all the loss and damage" (✅ fetched).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Article 231 of the Treaty of Versailles.
**Links:** WH-2078; WH-2080; WH-2090.
**Trap:** Do not say the treaty text used the term "war-guilt clause" (✅ owner 10 basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Its immediate function was reparations liability; its political effect was resentment."
**Back:** *"Two-thirty-one: liability, read as guilt."*

### Flashcard WH-2080 — Reparations: amount and unit
**Front:** What was the German reparations sum, and what do the figures in the owner and in the fetched source mean?
**Definition (learn):** Reparations were fixed in 1921 under the London Schedule.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: fixed at £6600 million; later reduced by the Young Plan (✅ owner 10 basic §5, §8.8).
- Fetched: London Schedule (5 May 1921) set "a final reparation sum of 132 billion gold marks" for the Central Powers; Germany's own share was 50 billion gold marks, with credit for earlier payments bringing it to 41 billion (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Versailles).
- Keynes preferred £2000 million (✅ owner 10 advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5 May 1921 London Schedule; 132 billion gold marks (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-2081; WH-2093; WH-2105 (Young Plan).
**Trap:** ⚠️ UNIT CONFLICT: owner states £6600 million; the fetched source gives gold marks. The £6.6 billion sterling equivalent is not verified here; state the gold-mark figure first and the sterling only with attribution.
**Mains hook:** "The amount was judged too high by Keynes and others; the sum's unit and date matter for accuracy."
**Back:** *"Twenty-one: 132 billion marks, six-point-six in pounds."*

### Flashcard WH-2081 — Army limit and Rhineland terms
**Front:** What were the German army and Rhineland restrictions?
**Definition (learn):** A ceiling of 100,000 men, with demobilisation, and demilitarisation of the Rhineland.
**Breakdown:**
- Army: no more than 100,000 men in a maximum of seven infantry and three cavalry divisions; demobilisation by 31 March 1920 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- No conscription, tanks, submarines or military aircraft (✅ owner 10 basic §5).
- Rhineland demilitarised; fortifications within 50 km east of the river demolished; new construction forbidden (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Allied occupation staged: 5 years (Cologne), 10 years (Coblenz), 15 years (rest) (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 100,000; 31 March 1920; 50 km; 15 years.
**Links:** WH-2078; WH-2097 (Locarno, 1925).
**Trap:** Do not confuse the Rhineland demilitarisation with Locarno's western-frontier guarantee (WH-2103).
**Mains hook:** Disarmament was resented and hard to enforce (✅ owner 10 advanced §5).
**Back:** *"Hundred thousand, seven and three, fifty kilometres: the cage."*

### Flashcard WH-2082 — Treaty of St Germain (1919): Austria
**Front:** What did the Treaty of St Germain (1919) do to Austria?
**Definition (learn):** The settlement with Austria that dismembered the Habsburg lands.
**Breakdown:**
- Loss of Bohemia, Moravia, South Tyrol, Trieste and other territories (✅ owner 10 basic §5).
- Habsburg Empire collapsed into successor states (✅ owner 10 basic §4).
- Successor states small, mutually hostile, militarily weak (✅ owner 10 basic §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1919.
**Links:** WH-2083; WH-2084; WH-2087.
**Trap:** Do not confuse St Germain (Austria) with Trianon (Hungary) (⚠️ owner trap).
**Mains hook:** "Self-determination dissolved the losers' empires."
**Back:** *"Nineteen: Austria, Bohemia, Trieste go."*

### Flashcard WH-2083 — Treaty of Trianon (1920): Hungary
**Front:** What did the Treaty of Trianon (1920) take from Hungary?
**Definition (learn):** The settlement with Hungary that transferred Slovakia, Ruthenia, Croatia, Slovenia and Transylvania.
**Breakdown:**
- Slovakia, Ruthenia, Croatia, Slovenia, Transylvania lost (✅ owner 10 basic §5).
- Hungary's losses are listed in the owner's Trianon row (✅ owner 10 basic §8.6); no comparison of territory size is sourced.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1920.
**Links:** WH-2082; WH-2087.
**Trap:** Trianon is not Versailles; do not attribute its terms to Germany.
**Mains hook:** The settlement as a cluster of treaties.
**Back:** *"Twenty: Hungary shrinks."*

### Flashcard WH-2084 — Treaty of Neuilly (1919): Bulgaria
**Front:** What territory did Bulgaria lose under the Treaty of Neuilly (1919)?
**Definition (learn):** The settlement that gave territory to Greece, Yugoslavia and Romania.
**Breakdown:**
- Territory lost to Greece, Yugoslavia, Romania (✅ owner 10 basic §5).
- Part of the cluster of treaties.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1919.
**Links:** WH-2082; WH-2083.
**Trap:** Neuilly, not Versailles, governs Bulgaria (owner trap §6).
**Mains hook:** "Name each treaty and its state."
**Back:** *"Neuilly: Bulgaria's cut."*

### Flashcard WH-2085 — Sèvres and Lausanne: Turkey
**Front:** How did Sèvres (1920) and Lausanne (1923) differ for Turkey?
**Definition (learn):** Sèvres's terms were rejected by Turkish nationalists under Mustafa Kemal; Lausanne recognised Turkish sovereignty over eastern Thrace and Anatolia, including Smyrna/Izmir.
**Breakdown:**
- Sèvres (1920): imposed terms.
- Turkish nationalists under Mustafa Kemal rejected them.
- Lausanne (1923): recognised Turkish sovereignty over eastern Thrace and Anatolia, including Smyrna/Izmir (✅ owner 10 basic §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1920; 1923.
**Links:** WH-2086; WH-2087; WH-2092.
**Trap:** Do not say Turkey accepted Sèvres. Turkish resistance forced Lausanne (✅ owner 10 basic §8.8).
**Mains hook:** "Lausanne is the proof-case that the settlement rested on power, not consent."
**Back:** *"Twenty Sèvres rejected, twenty-three Lausanne."*

### Flashcard WH-2086 — Mandates: German colonies and Ottoman Arab territories
**Front:** What was the mandate system and how did it operate?
**Definition (learn):** German colonies and Ottoman Arab territories were transferred to victors as League mandates, with a stated purpose to "look after" them and prepare them for independence.
**Breakdown:**
- German colonies became League mandates (✅ owner 10 basic §5).
- Britain: Transjordan and Palestine; France: Syria (✅ owner 10 basic §8.6A).
- Commission supervision existed; enforcement did not (⚠️ owner 10 §8.6A).
- Transjordan independent 1946; Palestine mandate ended 1948 (✅ owner 10 §8.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1946 (Transjordan); 1948 (Palestine mandate ended).
**Links:** WH-2078; WH-2087; WH-2107 (League mandates).
**Trap:** Do not name mandate classes (A/B/C), Covenant articles or Permanent Mandates Commission procedures; none is sourced here (✅ owner 10 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "The mandate was the first instrument to make imperial rule conditional and temporary in principle."
**Back:** *"Look after them: mandates to victors."*

### Flashcard WH-2087 — Why empires collapsed: Habsburg, Ottoman, British
**Front:** Why did the war destroy some empires while the British survived?
**Definition (learn):** Empire collapse depended on whether the empire was the state itself; where empire was an overseas possession of a nation-state, defeat abroad did not dissolve the centre.
**Breakdown:**
- Habsburg (multinational dynastic empire): defeated and dismembered; successor states.
- Ottoman: defeated, Sèvres imposed then rejected; Turkish nation-state survived by force.
- British: victorious, enlarged in 1919, weakened later by WWII (✅ owner 10 advanced §5 and §8.6A(b)).
- Decisive variable: nationality and sovereignty as the same question (⚠️ owner 10 §8.6A(b)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1919–23; 1945 (end of maritime empires, ✅ owner 10 §8.6A(b)).
**Links:** WH-2082; WH-2085; WH-2086.
**Trap:** Do not claim the war "ended empire" generally; it destroyed the Habsburg and Ottoman empires and enlarged British and French ones.
**Mains hook:** "The war destroyed the empires in which nationality and sovereignty were the same question."
**Back:** *"Nation-states outlived, nation-empires broke."*

### Flashcard WH-2088 — Advanced: Haig, attrition and military judgment
**Front:** Was Haig a butcher or an adapting commander?
**Definition (learn):** Critics cite the Somme and Verdun casualties and "lions led by donkeys"; revisionists (J. P. Harris, Gary Sheffield) argue Haig learned, transformed the BEF and contributed to 1918.
**Breakdown:**
- Critique: Somme casualties, the big push, frontal assaults (✅ owner 10 advanced §3).
- Defence: learning curve, army transformation, combined methods, tanks (✅ owner 10 advanced §3).
- UPSC-safe conclusion: criticisable methods, but not a caricature (✅ owner 10 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1916 (Somme); 1918 (combined methods). No casualty totals.
**Links:** WH-2071; WH-2069.
**Trap:** Do not present Haig as a one-dimensional caricature (✅ owner 10 advanced §3).
**Mains hook:** "Haig's methods were costly; his learning curve complicates the verdict."
**Back:** *"Lions and donkeys, then learning."*

### Flashcard WH-2089 — Advanced: "lost generation" and memory
**Front:** Did an entire generation die in the First World War?
**Definition (learn):** Memory of a "lost generation", especially in France, exaggerates uniformity; Dan Todman questions the simplified memory.
**Breakdown:**
- Enormous death tolls, especially in France (✅ owner 10 advanced §4).
- Todman: public memory can exaggerate uniformity and totality (✅ owner 10 advanced §4).
- Distinguish emotional memory from demographic measurement (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No numbers sourced (owner).
**Links:** WH-2069; WH-2077.
**Trap:** Do not state that an entire generation was wiped out (✅ owner 10 advanced §4).
**Mains hook:** "Catastrophic, yet memory and measurement must be separated."
**Back:** *"Memory is not a census."*

### Flashcard WH-2090 — Advanced: Was Versailles too harsh?
**Front:** Were German objections to Versailles justified?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe lists German objections and then qualifies them: dictated peace, territory, colonies, disarmament, Article 231 and reparations.
**Breakdown:**
- Objections: dictated peace; lost colonies; disarmament; Article 231; reparations of £6600 million (✅ owner 10 advanced §5). [⚠️ unit conflict: see WH-2080]
- Qualifications: some territorial changes defensible on nationality grounds; Wilsonian ideas; Brest-Litovsk precedent (✅ owner 10 advanced §5).
- The 14 Points argument is not fully convincing as a German defence (✅ owner 10 advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 advanced §5.
**Links:** WH-2078; WH-2079; WH-2080; WH-2091.
**Trap:** "Versailles caused WWII" is too crude (✅ owner 10 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** "Harsh enough to embitter Germany, not enough to render it permanently incapable of recovery."
**Back:** *"Harsh to embitter, soft to recover."*

### Flashcard WH-2091 — Advanced: the wider settlement and its weaknesses
**Front:** Why did the peace settlement of 1919 fail to secure a durable peace?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe's list of weaknesses: revisionist and status-quo split, US non-participation, French and Italian grievance, Russia ignored, German economic strength, unresolved minorities.
**Breakdown:**
- Europe split between revisionist and status-quo states.
- USA never ratified or joined the League.
- France lost confidence when Anglo-American guarantees lapsed.
- Italy felt cheated; Russia ignored.
- Germany economically strong enough to recover and retaliate.
- Minority questions left unresolved.
- Softer view: depression and Hitler destroyed a recovery that might have worked (✅ owner 10 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 advanced §6.
**Links:** WH-2092; WH-2094; WH-2105.
**Trap:** Versailles alone did not explain the later war; the Depression and Hitler were later decisive factors (✅ owner 10 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** "The settlement created grievance without enforcement."
**Back:** *"No America, no enforcement, no peace."*

### Flashcard WH-2092 — Trap: Versailles is not the whole settlement
**Front:** What is the trap in treating "Versailles" as the entire 1919–23 settlement?
**Definition (learn):** The settlement was a cluster of treaties: Versailles (Germany), St Germain (Austria), Trianon (Hungary), Sèvres and Lausanne (Turkey), Neuilly (Bulgaria).
**Breakdown:**
- Germany: Versailles.
- Austria: St Germain.
- Hungary: Trianon.
- Turkey: Sèvres then Lausanne.
- Bulgaria: Neuilly.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §6 trap.
**Links:** WH-2078 to WH-2085.
**Trap:** The owner says UPSC can ask these distinctions; a single-treaty answer loses marks.
**Mains hook:** "The settlement was a system of treaties, not one document."
**Back:** *"Five treaties, five states."*

### Flashcard WH-2093 — Advanced: Young Plan and Keynes's figure
**Front:** How was the reparations figure later revised, and what did Keynes prefer?
**Definition (learn):** Reparations were later reduced by the Young Plan (1929) to £2000 million; Keynes preferred £2000 million as a fair sum.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: Young Plan reduced reparations (✅ owner 10 basic §6; owner 11 basic §4).
- Keynes preferred £2000 million (✅ owner 10 advanced §5).
- The Young Plan figure is owner-only; not fetched here (⚠️ unverified).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1929 (Young Plan); £2000 million.
**Links:** WH-2080; WH-2105.
**Trap:** Do not give a reparations figure other than £6600 million for the 1921 total (⚠️ see WH-2080 conflict) or £2000 million for the Young Plan (✅ owner 11 basic §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Reparations amount and enforcement together drove the 1920s stabilisation.
**Back:** *"Twenty-nine: two-thousand, the revised bill."*

### Flashcard WH-2094 — Four arguments on settlement instability
**Front:** Why was the 1919–23 settlement structurally unstable?
**Definition (learn):** Four arguments: grievance without disarmament of resentment; inconsistent self-determination; enforcement depended on will; small, weak successor states.
**Breakdown:**
- Article 231 became a revisionist rallying point.
- Self-determination applied inconsistently (German–Austrian union forbidden).
- Enforcement depended on great-power will, eroded by reparations diplomacy and the Depression.
- Successor states were small, mutually hostile and militarily weak, creating a power vacuum between Germany and the USSR.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §8.6 (four arguments, ⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** WH-2079; WH-2082; WH-2091; WH-2085 (Lausanne proof-case).
**Trap:** Treaty grievance neither explains the war's outbreak nor makes a second war automatic (✅ owner 10 basic §10).
**Mains hook:** "The settlement was unstable less because it was harsh than because it was unenforced."
**Back:** *"Grievance, inconsistency, weak enforcement, weak states."*

### GROUP — International Relations, 1919-39 (basic 11 + advanced 11)

### Flashcard WH-2095 — Interwar IR: core sequence (basic 11)
**Front:** What is the interwar IR sequence from 1919 to 1939?
**Definition (learn):** Peace settlement, League, limited reconciliation, Depression, aggression, appeasement and war.
**Breakdown:**
- 1920s: partial stability (Dawes, Locarno, Kellogg-Briand, Young).
- 1930s: breakdown through Manchuria, Abyssinia, Rhineland, Munich, Prague, Poland.
- Revisionist powers: Japan, Italy, Germany (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 11 basic §1.
**Links:** WH-2096; WH-2113 (Depression chain); WH-2112.
**Trap:** Do not treat the 1920s as pure peace or the 1930s as pure war. The owner gives a two-phase sequence.
**Mains hook:** "Failure of order: peace settlement, League, reconciliation, Depression, aggression, appeasement, war."
**Back:** *"Settle, League, reconcile, slump, invade, appease, war."*

### Flashcard WH-2096 — League of Nations: founding dates
**Front:** When did the League of Nations come into being, and when did its first Assembly and Council meet?
**Definition (learn):** The League's Covenant took effect with the Treaty of Versailles, and its organs began functioning in 1920.
**Breakdown:**
- Covenant in effect: 10 January 1920 (✅ owner 11 basic §6; ✅ Wikipedia: League of Nations, fetched).
- Council first met: 16 January 1920 (✅ Wikipedia).
- First Assembly: 15 November 1920, Geneva (✅ Wikipedia).
- Founding members: 42 by November 1920 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 Jan 1920; 16 Jan 1920; 15 Nov 1920.
**Links:** WH-2097; WH-2098; WH-2099.
**Trap:** Do not date the League's establishment other than 10 January 1920 (✅ owner 11 basic §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Institutional birth preceded the 1930s breakdown by a decade.
**Back:** *"Ten January twenty: League lives."*

### Flashcard WH-2097 — League organs and commissions
**Front:** What were the League's main organs and bodies?
**Definition (learn):** A structure of General Assembly, Council, Permanent Court of International Justice, Secretariat and commissions.
**Breakdown:**
- General Assembly: annual, all members, general policy and finances.
- Council: smaller executive for political disputes.
- Permanent Court of International Justice: legal disputes.
- Secretariat: administration.
- Commissions and committees: mandates, disarmament, minorities, labour, health, welfare (✅ owner 11 basic §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 11 basic §2 table.
**Links:** WH-2096; WH-2098; WH-2107.
**Trap:** The League's organs were later reused by the UN (✅ owner 11 advanced §2, Ruth Henig).
**Mains hook:** Institutional design existed; the enforcement weakness was political.
**Back:** *"Assembly talks, Council decides, Court judges, Secretariat files."*

### Flashcard WH-2098 — League's genuine achievements
**Front:** What did the League achieve that is not a peace-enforcement story?
**Definition (learn):** Technical and social successes that balance a failed security record.
**Breakdown:**
- ILO work on labour conditions.
- Refugee work under Fridtjof Nansen.
- Health work against epidemics (typhus).
- Saar administration and plebiscite.
- Settlements: Åland Islands, Upper Silesia, Greek-Bulgarian dispute, Mosul (✅ owner 11 basic §2 and §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 11 basic §2.
**Links:** WH-2097; WH-2099; WH-2112.
**Trap:** Do not confuse technical success with peace enforcement. Name both (✅ owner 11 basic §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "The League failed as a coercive security system, not as the first modern experiment in organised cooperation."
**Back:** *"ILO, Nansen, typhus, Saar: the League's good works."*

### Flashcard WH-2099 — League's structural weaknesses
**Front:** What structural weaknesses weakened the League?
**Definition (learn):** No army, unanimity, absent great powers, Versailles identity, and members unwilling to commit to fight aggressors.
**Breakdown:**
- Too closely tied to the Versailles order (✅ owner 11 basic §3).
- USA never joined.
- Unanimity rules; no League army (✅).
- Conference of Ambassadors often overshadowed it (✅).
- Members would not commit to fight aggressors (✅).
- Depression strengthened aggressive regimes (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 11 basic §3.
**Links:** WH-2096; WH-2100; WH-2106; WH-2107.
**Trap:** Do not say the League collapsed only because of Hitler; Manchuria and Abyssinia had already exposed it (✅ owner 11 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "Collective security needs prior commitment; those who mattered would not commit."
**Back:** *"No army, no unanimity, no America, no will."*

### Flashcard WH-2100 — League membership changes
**Front:** When did Germany, Japan, Italy, the USSR and Spain join or leave the League?
**Definition (learn):** A membership ladder across the interwar period.
**Breakdown:**
- Germany joined in 1926 (✅ owner 11 basic §6, §8.4); left in 1933 (✅ owner and Wikipedia).
- Japan left in 1933 (✅ owner; ✅ Wikipedia).
- USSR joined in 1934 (✅ owner 11 basic §3).
- Italy left in 1937 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
- Spain left in 1939 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1926; 1933; 1934; 1937; 1939.
**Links:** WH-2099; WH-2106.
**Trap:** Italy's exit is 1937, not 1933; the owner does not give Italy's exit date.
**Mains hook:** Membership shifts show the League losing aggressors and gaining Soviet presence late.
**Back:** *"Germany in twenty-six, out thirty-three; Italy out thirty-seven."*

### Flashcard WH-2101 — Washington Conferences (1921–22)
**Front:** What did the Washington Conferences of 1921–22 achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Naval limits and temporary Far Eastern easing.
**Breakdown:**
- Naval limits agreed (✅ owner 11 basic §4).
- Temporary Far Eastern easing (✅).
- Built-in weakness: did not restrain land forces or Japanese continental ambition (✅ owner 11 basic §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1921–22.
**Links:** WH-2102; WH-2106.
**Trap:** Do not claim naval limits stopped Japan's expansion on the mainland.
**Mains hook:** "Stability at sea did not restrain expansion on land."
**Back:** *"Twenty-one to twenty-two: ships limited, land unbound."*

### Flashcard WH-2102 — Dawes Plan (1924)
**Front:** What did the Dawes Plan of 1924 do, and what was its weakness?
**Definition (learn):** An American loan that made reparations manageable and led to French withdrawal from the Ruhr.
**Breakdown:**
- American loan (✅ owner 11 basic §4).
- Reparations made manageable (✅).
- French leave the Ruhr (✅).
- Built-in weakness: European stability depended on American credit (✅ owner 11 basic §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1924.
**Links:** WH-2103; WH-2113.
**Trap:** Dawes is not the Young Plan. Dawes 1924; Young 1929.
**Mains hook:** "American credit made the stabilisation hostage to the New York money market."
**Back:** *"Twenty-four: loans in, Ruhr out."*

### Flashcard WH-2103 — Locarno Treaties (1925)
**Front:** What did Locarno guarantee, and what did it leave out?
**Definition (learn):** Western frontiers of Germany were guaranteed; eastern frontiers were not.
**Breakdown:**
- Western frontiers guaranteed (✅ owner 11 basic §4).
- Eastern frontiers not guaranteed (✅ owner 11 basic §8.5).
- Locarno spirit rested on economic stability (✅ owner 11 basic §4 lesson).
- Stresemann's death and the Depression ended the spirit (✅ owner 11 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1925.
**Links:** WH-2104; WH-2105; WH-2120.
**Trap:** Locarno did not guarantee all German frontiers (✅ owner 11 basic §6 trap).
**Mains hook:** "Locarno reduced visible tension in the west but not the revisionist question in the east."
**Back:** *"Twenty-five: west guaranteed, east open."*

### Flashcard WH-2104 — Kellogg-Briand Pact (1928)
**Front:** What did the Kellogg-Briand Pact do, and what did it lack?
**Definition (learn):** A renunciation of war with no sanctions clause and no enforcement mechanism.
**Breakdown:**
- Renunciation of war: 1928 (✅ owner 11 basic §4).
- No sanctions clause (✅ owner 11 basic §8.5).
- No enforcement mechanism (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1928.
**Links:** WH-2103; WH-2099.
**Trap:** Do not say Kellogg-Briand prevented war by law. It had no enforcement (✅ owner 11 basic §6 and §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "A declaration without machinery."
**Back:** *"Twenty-eight: war renounced, nothing enforced."*

### Flashcard WH-2105 — Young Plan (1929) and the Depression
**Front:** What did the Young Plan do, and why did it come at a bad time?
**Definition (learn):** The 1929 plan that cut reparations to £2000 million, arriving as the Depression began.
**Breakdown:**
- Reparations cut to £2000 million (✅ owner 11 basic §4).
- Arrived as the Depression began (✅ owner 11 basic §8.5).
- Counter-check: owner figure; Young Plan not fetched (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1929; £2000 million.
**Links:** WH-2093; WH-2113.
**Trap:** Do not give a Young Plan figure other than £2000 million (✅ owner 11 basic §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Reparations relief came as the American credit that sustained it was withdrawn."
**Back:** *"Twenty-nine: two-thousand, too late."*

### Flashcard WH-2106 — Manchuria (1931)
**Front:** Why was the 1931 Manchurian crisis the first major interwar test of the League?
**Definition (learn):** Japan's successful defiance of the League, which kept its gains and demonstrated impunity.
**Breakdown:**
- Japan invaded Manchuria in 1931 (✅ owner 11 basic mini-timeline).
- First major successful defiance of the League (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
- Demonstrated impunity to other revisionist states (✅ owner 11 basic §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1931.
**Links:** WH-2099; WH-2100; WH-2107.
**Trap:** Do not say Manchuria was the first crisis of any kind; it was the first interwar test the League failed (✅ owner 11 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "A great power defied the League and kept its gains."
**Back:** *"Thirty-one: first defiance, first impunity."*

### Flashcard WH-2107 — Abyssinia (1935)
**Front:** Why did Abyssinia damage League prestige so badly?
**Definition (learn):** League sanctions proved half-hearted and ineffective, destroying prestige among the states the League needed.
**Breakdown:**
- Italy's attack on Abyssinia, 1935 (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
- Sanctions half-hearted and ineffective (✅).
- Most damaging blow to League prestige before the war (✅ owner 11 basic §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1935.
**Links:** WH-2099; WH-2106; WH-2109.
**Trap:** Abyssinia is not the same as Manchuria; the first was Japanese, the second Italian (✅ owners).
**Mains hook:** "Sanctions that were half-hearted taught aggressors the next revision would be cheaper."
**Back:** *"Thirty-five: sanctions shy, prestige shattered."*

### Flashcard WH-2108 — Anglo-German Naval Agreement (1935)
**Front:** What did the 1935 Anglo-German Naval Agreement break?
**Definition (learn):** A bilateral agreement that broke the Stresa Front and weakened united resistance to Germany.
**Breakdown:**
- Broke the Stresa Front (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
- Weakened anti-German unity (✅ owner 11 basic §6).
- Britain and France not acting as a bloc (✅ owner 11 advanced / basic §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1935.
**Links:** WH-2107; WH-2109; WH-2110.
**Trap:** Do not present the Naval Agreement as a collective-security instrument; it weakened unity.
**Mains hook:** "Appeasement began when a single bilateral deal broke a common front."
**Back:** *"Thirty-five: front broken, Stresa gone."*

### Flashcard WH-2109 — Rhineland (1936) and the Spanish Civil War (1936–39)
**Front:** What did the 1936 Rhineland remilitarisation and the Spanish Civil War teach Hitler?
**Definition (learn):** Protest without force, and non-intervention by Britain and France while Germany and Italy helped Franco.
**Breakdown:**
- Rhineland 1936: Hitler learned protest without force meant little (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
- Spanish Civil War 1936–39: Germany and Italy helped Franco; Britain and France stayed out (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1936; 1936–39.
**Links:** WH-2108; WH-2110; WH-2117; WH-2122.
**Trap:** Rhineland is not the same as the Anschluss (1938) (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
**Mains hook:** "Each unpunished act raised the aggressor's estimate of what was possible."
**Back:** *"Thirty-six: Rhineland shrugged, Spain watched."*

### Flashcard WH-2110 — Anschluss and Munich (1938)
**Front:** What happened at Anschluss and Munich in 1938?
**Definition (learn):** Austria was absorbed into the Reich without resistance from powers; at Munich Sudetenland was conceded while Czechs and USSR were excluded.
**Breakdown:**
- Anschluss 1938: absorbed into Reich (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
- Munich Sept 1938: Sudetenland conceded immediately (✅ owner 11 advanced §5).
- Czechs and USSR excluded from the decision (✅ owner 11 basic §5 and advanced §5).
- Munich accelerated destruction of Czechoslovakia (✅ owner 11 basic §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1938; September 1938.
**Links:** WH-2111; WH-2114; WH-2119.
**Trap:** Do not say Munich solved the Sudeten issue permanently (✅ owner 11 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "Munich destroyed the strongest central-European barrier to expansion."
**Back:** *"Thirty-eight: Austria taken, Sudetes given."*

### Flashcard WH-2111 — Prague (March 1939) and the Nazi-Soviet Pact (August 1939)
**Front:** Why did the March 1939 seizure of Prague and the August 1939 pact matter?
**Definition (learn):** Hitler seized non-German Czech territory, shattering the self-determination logic; the Nazi-Soviet Pact cleared the way for invasion of Poland.
**Breakdown:**
- March 1939: seized non-German territory (✅ owner 11 basic §5 and advanced §5).
- August 1939: Nazi-Soviet Pact (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
- September 1939: Poland invaded; Britain and France declare war (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** March 1939; Aug 1939; Sept 1939.
**Links:** WH-2110; WH-2115; WH-2116.
**Trap:** Do not say Britain and France declared war on Germany before the September 1939 invasion of Poland (✅ owner 11 basic §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Prague proved appeasement's own justification wrong."
**Back:** *"Thirty-nine: Prague, Pact, Poland."*

### Flashcard WH-2112 — Appeasement defined and why it looked rational
**Front:** What was appeasement, and why did contemporaries support it?
**Definition (learn):** The British-led policy of avoiding war by conceding demands that seemed not too unreasonable.
**Breakdown:**
- Fear of another catastrophic war; pacifist opinion after 1914–18 (✅ owner 11 advanced §4).
- Belief that Germany and Italy had genuine grievances; some Versailles terms seemed unfair (✅).
- Distrust of the League; preference for leader-to-leader diplomacy; fear of communist Russia (✅).
- British military unreadiness; buying time for rearmament (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 11 basic §5 and advanced §4.
**Links:** WH-2113; WH-2114; WH-2117.
**Trap:** Never write "appeasement = foolishness" without explaining why it seemed attractive (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** "A policy rational in motive and disastrous in effect."
**Back:** *"Peace-seeking, then wrong opponent."*

### Flashcard WH-2113 — Depression to collapse of collective security (chain)
**Front:** What is the economic-to-diplomatic transmission chain that ended collective security?
**Definition (learn):** American credit sustained European recovery; the Crash and withdrawal of loans produced a crisis, which fed Manchuria, Nazi power and Abyssinia.
**Breakdown:**
- American loans (Dawes 1924) -> European recovery and Locarno spirit.
- Wall Street crash and Depression (1929) -> loans withdrawn, unemployment, radicalisation.
- Manchuria 1931, Nazi power 1933, Abyssinia 1935 -> collective security collapses (✅ owner 11 basic §8.5 diagram).
- Depression mechanism owned by basic/20 §9 (✅ owner 11 basic §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1924; 1929; 1931; 1933; 1935.
**Links:** WH-2102; WH-2105; WH-2106; WH-2107.
**Trap:** Do not answer a Depression question from this chain alone (✅ owner 11 basic §8.5).
**Mains hook:** "Converts 'the Depression caused fascism' into a specific transmission mechanism."
**Back:** *"Loans in, loans out, leagues fall."*

### Flashcard WH-2114 — Advanced: Ruth Henig's corrective on the League
**Front:** Was the League a complete failure?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe cites Ruth Henig: the League failed as a peacekeeper but pioneered structures later used by the UN.
**Breakdown:**
- Failure: no army, weak sanctions, absent great powers, unwilling Britain and France (✅ owner 11 advanced §2).
- Success: refugee, labour and health work; institutional legacy to the UN (✅ owner 11 advanced §2).
- Structures: Assembly, Council, Secretariat, Court, social agencies (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 11 advanced §2.
**Links:** WH-2097; WH-2098; WH-2099.
**Trap:** Henig is a corrective, not a rejection of Lowe's failure verdict.
**Mains hook:** "Failed as a coercive security system, not as the first experiment in organised cooperation."
**Back:** *"Henig: failed guard, founded the UN's furniture."*

### Flashcard WH-2115 — Advanced: USSR and the Nazi-Soviet Pact
**Front:** Did the Soviet Union make war inevitable by signing the Nazi-Soviet Pact?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe records the charge and the Soviet defence, and treats the pact as a factor in the opportunity structure.
**Breakdown:**
- Charge: Stalin's pact enabled the invasion of Poland (✅ owner 11 advanced §6b).
- Defence: Britain and Poland distrusted the USSR; negotiations with the west were slow; the pact bought time (✅).
- Exclusion of USSR from Munich fed Soviet distrust (✅ owner 11 basic §8.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1939.
**Links:** WH-2111; WH-2110; WH-2116.
**Trap:** Do not claim the USSR caused the war; Lowe keeps Hitler's expansionism as the central driver (✅ owner 11 advanced §6c).
**Mains hook:** "Appeasement and Soviet choices shaped the opportunity structure; Hitler supplied the drive."
**Back:** *"Pact enabled, not caused."*

### Flashcard WH-2116 — Advanced: Hitler's aims and the Taylor challenge
**Front:** Was Hitler chiefly to blame for the Second World War?
**Definition (learn):** The classic case: Poland was attacked on all fronts; Hossbach Memorandum and Lebensraum show aims beyond revision. Taylor argued Hitler may not have intended a general war at once.
**Breakdown:**
- Hossbach Memorandum and Lebensraum (✅ owner 11 advanced §6c).
- Poland attacked on all fronts, not only over Danzig (✅).
- Taylor's challenge: limited war with Poland expected (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 11 advanced §6c.
**Links:** WH-2115; WH-2117; WH-2111.
**Trap:** Do not present Hitler as a blueprint actor without the Taylor challenge (✅ owner 11 advanced §6c).
**Mains hook:** "Hitler's expansionism remains the central driving force in Lowe's presentation."
**Back:** *"Hossbach, Lebensraum, Taylor's doubt."*

### Flashcard WH-2117 — Advanced: Chamberlain and the "peace for our time" conflict
**Front:** Did Chamberlain return from Munich claiming "peace for our time", and what does the owner say?
**Definition (learn):** Owner conflict. Advanced §5 states it as sourced; advanced §8.9 forbids attributing the phrase to Chamberlain because it is not sourced in the folder.
**Breakdown:**
- Advanced §5: "Chamberlain returned claiming 'peace for our time'" (✅ owner 11 advanced §5).
- Advanced §8.9: "Do not attribute the phrase 'peace for our time' ... to Chamberlain; it is not sourced in this folder" (✅ owner 11 advanced §8.9).
- Wikipedia not fetched for this quotation (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Munich, September 1938.
**Links:** WH-2110; WH-2112; WH-2118.
**Trap:** ⚠️ CONFLICT inside owner 11 advanced. Use the quotation only with a verified source; otherwise paraphrase.
**Mains hook:** Assess appeasement by its reasons and failures, not by a quotation.
**Back:** *"Quoted or not, Munich is the test."*

### Flashcard WH-2118 — Trap set: Locarno, Kellogg-Briand, Munich
**Front:** Which three interwar statements are the most common traps?
**Definition (learn):** Locarno guaranteed western frontiers only; Kellogg-Briand had no enforcement; Munich did not settle the Sudeten issue permanently.
**Breakdown:**
- Locarno: western frontiers, not eastern (✅ owner 11 basic §6).
- Kellogg-Briand: renounced war, no sanctions (✅).
- Munich: accelerated the destruction of Czechoslovakia (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1925; 1928; 1938.
**Links:** WH-2103; WH-2104; WH-2110.
**Trap:** These three statements are the usual distractors in prelims-style questions.
**Mains hook:** "Interwar peace was legal and conditional, not enforced."
**Back:** *"West only, no teeth, no permanence."*

### Flashcard WH-2119 — Trap: the League did not collapse only because of Hitler
**Front:** Did the League collapse because of Hitler alone?
**Definition (learn):** No. Manchuria and Abyssinia had already exposed the deeper weakness.
**Breakdown:**
- Manchuria 1931 and Abyssinia 1935 precede Hitler's aggression.
- The deeper weakness was the will of Britain and France (✅ owner 11 basic §3).
- Hitler's exit from the League in 1933 was a symptom, not the whole cause.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1931; 1933; 1935.
**Links:** WH-2106; WH-2107; WH-2099.
**Trap:** A "Hitler only" answer fails the failure-of-order directive (✅ owner 11 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "The League was only as strong as Britain's and France's willingness to support it."
**Back:** *"Manchuria first, Abyssinia second, Hitler third."*

### Flashcard WH-2120 — Advanced: Stresemann and the "Locarno spirit"
**Front:** Why did the Locarno spirit evaporate after 1929?
**Definition (learn):** The atmosphere depended on economic stability, and Stresemann's death and the Depression removed it.
**Breakdown:**
- Locarno reduced western tension (✅ owner 11 advanced §3).
- Stresemann died; Depression arrived; the spirit evaporated (✅ owner 11 advanced §3).
- Eastern frontiers left unguaranteed (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1925; 1929 (Depression); Stresemann's death date not in owner.
**Links:** WH-2103; WH-2113.
**Trap:** Do not date Stresemann's death from the owner (no date given; ⚠️).
**Mains hook:** "Locarno was genuine but conditional."
**Back:** *"Spirit needed prosperity."*

### Flashcard WH-2121 — Advanced: appeasement's causal engine (advanced 11 concept)
**Front:** What is the causal engine in the 1931–39 crisis sequence?
**Definition (learn):** Each unpunished act raised the aggressor's estimate of what was possible and lowered the defenders' estimate of what was worth fighting for.
**Breakdown:**
- Learning process on both sides (✅ owner 11 basic §8.7 chain).
- Sequence: Manchuria 1931, Hitler 1933, Abyssinia 1935, Naval Agreement 1935, Rhineland 1936, Spain 1936–39, Anschluss 1938, Munich 1938, Prague 1939, Pact 1939, Poland 1939.
- Hitler's aims and the permissive structure both matter (owner advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 11 basic §8.7.
**Links:** WH-2109; WH-2110; WH-2111; WH-2113.
**Trap:** Read the sequence as a learning process, not a list of dates.
**Mains hook:** "Repeated protest without effective force taught aggressors the next revision would be cheaper."
**Back:** *"Each concession teaches the next demand."*

### GROUP — Rise of Fascism: Italy, Germany, Japan (basic 12 + advanced 12)

### Flashcard WH-2122 — Fascism: core concept (basic 12)
**Front:** What conditions allowed authoritarian right-wing regimes to rise between the wars?
**Definition (learn):** Postwar grievance, economic breakdown, weak parliamentary systems, fear of the left, and mass propaganda plus coercion produced authoritarian takeover.
**Breakdown:**
- Postwar grievance and crisis.
- Economic distress and class fear.
- Weak parliamentary order.
- Mass propaganda and coercion -> authoritarian right gains ground (✅ owner 12 basic §6).
- Four variants: Italy (model), Germany (racial-totalitarian), Japan (army militarism), Spain (civil-war authoritarian) (✅ owner 12 basic §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 basic §1 and §6.
**Links:** WH-2077; WH-2130; WH-2136; WH-2137; WH-2139.
**Trap:** Do not write that all 1930s dictatorships were fascist (✅ owner 12 advanced §11 trap).
**Mains hook:** "Fascism occupied the space that democracies could not fill after 1918."
**Back:** *"Grief, slump, weak parliaments, fear, force."*

### Flashcard WH-2123 — Fasci di combattimento and the National Fascist Party
**Front:** When were the Fasci di combattimento and the National Fascist Party founded?
**Definition (learn):** Mussolini founded the Fasci in 1919; the National Fascist Party was organised in 1921.
**Breakdown:**
- Fasci di combattimento: 1919 (✅ owner 12 basic mini-timeline).
- National Fascist Party: 1921 (✅).
- Early programme fluid; turned toward property and business interests after electoral failure (✅ owner 12 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1919; 1921.
**Links:** WH-2124; WH-2126; WH-2140.
**Trap:** Fascism was not born as a fully fixed doctrine (✅ owner 12 advanced §2).
**Mains hook:** "Fascism's programme shifted as its base changed."
**Back:** *"Nineteen Fasci, twenty-one party."*

### Flashcard WH-2124 — March on Rome (October 1922)
**Front:** How did Mussolini come to power, and why was the March on Rome "more bluff than military revolution"?
**Definition (learn):** King Victor Emmanuel III refused to authorise resistance and invited Mussolini to form a government.
**Breakdown:**
- Oct 1922: March on Rome (✅ owner 12 basic §2).
- The king refused resistance (✅).
- Mussolini became prime minister (✅).
- Power given, not seized in battle (✅ owner 12 basic §10.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** October 1922.
**Links:** WH-2123; WH-2125; WH-2134.
**Trap:** Do not say Mussolini seized power by defeating the army (✅ owner 12 basic §7 and advanced §11).
**Mains hook:** "Democracy was handed over from within, not stormed."
**Back:** *"Twenty-two: the king said yes."*

### Flashcard WH-2125 — Acerbo Law and fascist parliamentary control
**Front:** What role did the Acerbo Law play in fascist consolidation?
**Definition (learn):** A law that helped the fascists dominate parliament.
**Breakdown:**
- Acerbo Law helped fascist parliamentary dominance (✅ owner 12 basic §7 and §10.5).
- Strengthened fascist parliamentary control (✅ owner 12 advanced §11).
- Year of the Acerbo Law is not stated in the owner (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Date not given in owner.
**Links:** WH-2124; WH-2127.
**Trap:** Do not date the Acerbo Law from memory; flag as ⚠️ if needed.
**Mains hook:** "Electoral engineering consolidated the March on Rome's result."
**Back:** *"Acerbo: parliament tilted."*

### Flashcard WH-2126 — Lateran Treaty (1929)
**Front:** What did the Lateran Treaty of 1929 settle, and how was it judged?
**Definition (learn):** A settlement between Mussolini and the Papacy that resolved the Church-State question.
**Breakdown:**
- Signed 1929 (✅ owner 12 basic mini-timeline and §7).
- Often seen as durable settlement of Church-State conflict (✅ owner 12 advanced §3).
- Church remained a rival focus of loyalty (✅ owner 12 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1929.
**Links:** WH-2123; WH-2127; WH-2140.
**Trap:** Do not present the Lateran Treaty as the regime's only success; the owner gives it as one "apparent" success.
**Mains hook:** "Spectacle and settlement: Lateran as a durable legitimacy device."
**Back:** *"Twenty-nine: Pope and Duce settle."*

### Flashcard WH-2127 — Italian fascist institutions and economy
**Front:** What were the main Italian fascist institutions, and what did the regime fail to deliver economically?
**Definition (learn):** The Corporate State, GIL youth organisation, Dopolavoro and public works, against incomplete autarky and an unchanged structural economy.
**Breakdown:**
- Institutions: Corporate State; GIL; strikes banned; Dopolavoro (✅ owner 12 basic §4, advanced §3).
- Public works, reclaimed marshes, roads, hydro-electric expansion (✅ owner 12 advanced §3).
- Failed: autarky incomplete (no coal, oil, enough steel); "Battle for Wheat" hurt other sectors; real wages fell; no welfare state (✅ owner 12 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 advanced §3.
**Links:** WH-2123; WH-2126; WH-2144.
**Trap:** Do not present Italian fascist economics as a success (✅ owner 12 advanced §3 verdict).
**Mains hook:** "Spectacle, discipline and short-term stabilisation, not structural solution."
**Back:** *"Corporate, GIL, Dopolavoro: order, not growth."*

### Flashcard WH-2128 — Italy: authoritarian with totalitarian ambitions
**Front:** How totalitarian was Mussolini's Italy in practice?
**Definition (learn):** Authoritarian with totalitarian ambitions, not fully totalitarian in realised practice.
**Breakdown:**
- Fascist features: one-party state, censorship, youth control, leader cult (✅ owner 12 advanced §2).
- Limits: the king remained head of state and dismissed Mussolini in 1943; Church was a rival focus; critics such as Benedetto Croce survived (✅ owner 12 advanced §2).
- System less pervasive and less brutal than Nazi Germany (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1943 (dismissal) ✅ owner 12 advanced §2.
**Links:** WH-2123; WH-2126; WH-2135.
**Trap:** Do not write that Mussolini created a completely totalitarian state (✅ owner 12 advanced §11).
**Mains hook:** "Authoritarian with totalitarian ambitions."
**Back:** *"Ambitious, not complete."*

### Flashcard WH-2129 — Italian anti-Jewish laws (1938–39)
**Front:** When did Italy adopt anti-Jewish measures, and what does the timing show?
**Definition (learn):** Anti-Jewish laws came late, in 1938–39, after more than fifteen years of fascist rule.
**Breakdown:**
- Italian anti-Jewish laws: 1938–39 (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6 and advanced §8).
- Alignment with Hitler deepened the turn (✅ owner 12 advanced §8).
- Compare Nuremberg Laws 1935 in Germany (WH-2134).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1938–39.
**Links:** WH-2134; WH-2135.
**Trap:** Do not attribute Holocaust content to this file; the owner points to basic/14 §6 (✅ owner 12 basic §8.10).
**Mains hook:** "Racial exclusion was derivative in Italy and constitutive in Germany."
**Back:** *"Thirty-eight: late and imported."*

### Flashcard WH-2130 — Weimar collapse: layers of causation
**Front:** What were the layers of Weimar's collapse?
**Definition (learn):** Versailles humiliation and stab-in-the-back myth; constitutional weakness; violence; hyperinflation; American loans; Depression; Nazi propaganda; elite bargaining.
**Breakdown:**
- Versailles humiliation; stab-in-the-back myth (✅ owner 12 basic §3).
- Constitutional weakness; fragmented coalitions (✅).
- Political violence from left and right (✅).
- Hyperinflation in 1923 (✅).
- Dependence on American loans after 1924 (✅).
- Depression after 1929 (✅).
- Nazi mass appeal plus conservative intrigue (✅).
- Lowe: the economic crisis after 1929 was probably the crucial factor (✅ owner 12 advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1923; 1924; 1929; 1933.
**Links:** WH-2131; WH-2132; WH-2113; WH-2146.
**Trap:** Do not argue Hitler won a majority by free election (✅ owner 12 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** "Hitler's rise required crisis, mass appeal and elite miscalculation together."
**Back:** *"Versailles, Weimar, 1923, 1929: elites let him in."*

### Flashcard WH-2131 — Enabling Act (23 March 1933)
**Front:** When was the Enabling Act passed, how did the Reichstag vote, and how long did it last?
**Definition (learn):** The law that gave Hitler's government dictatorial legislative powers.
**Breakdown:**
- Passed by Reichstag and commenced 23 March 1933 (✅ Wikipedia: Enabling Act of 1933, fetched).
- Reichstag vote: 444 for, 94 against (all Social Democrats), 109 absent (✅ Wikipedia).
- Duration: four years, lapsing 1 April 1937 or sooner; renewed in 1937 and 1939 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Hitler Chancellor January 1933 (✅ owner 12 basic §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 23 March 1933; 444–94–109; lapse 1 April 1937.
**Links:** WH-2130; WH-2132; WH-2134.
**Trap:** Do not date the Enabling Law to January 1933. Hitler became Chancellor in January; the Enabling Act was March (✅ owner 12 basic §10.8).
**Mains hook:** "Legal breakthrough, not electoral majority."
**Back:** *"Twenty-three March: four years, four-forty-four in."*

### Flashcard WH-2132 — The Nazis never won an overall majority
**Front:** Did the Nazi Party win an overall majority before Hitler became Chancellor?
**Definition (learn):** No. The NSDAP fell short of an absolute majority in March 1933, and needed its DNVP partner to reach 52 per cent.
**Breakdown:**
- NSDAP gained about five million more votes than before (✅ Wikipedia).
- Absolute majority not reached; DNVP partner needed to reach 52% (✅ Wikipedia).
- Conservative elites supplied the final step (✅ owner 12 basic §10.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** March 1933 election; 52% with DNVP (✅ Wikipedia). No vote-share percentage for NSDAP stated.
**Links:** WH-2131; WH-2130.
**Trap:** Do not claim a free-majority victory (✅ owner 12 basic §7 trap).
**Mains hook:** "Hitler was let in legally, not elected by majority."
**Back:** *"No majority, DNVP wanted in."*

### Flashcard WH-2133 — Night of the Long Knives (1934)
**Front:** What was the Night of the Long Knives, and what did it consolidate?
**Definition (learn):** A 1934 purge that consolidated Hitler's position.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1934 (✅ owner 12 basic mini-timeline).
- Consolidated Hitler's position (✅ owner 12 basic mini-timeline).
- Victims and numbers are not sourced in the owner (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1934.
**Links:** WH-2131; WH-2134.
**Trap:** Do not add victim counts (none is sourced).
**Mains hook:** "Consolidation through violence after legal takeover."
**Back:** *"Thirty-four: the knives settle it."*

### Flashcard WH-2134 — Nuremberg Laws (1935)
**Front:** When were the Nuremberg Laws passed, and why are they a discriminator?
**Definition (learn):** German racial laws of 1935 that marked the racial core of Nazism.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1935 (✅ owner 12 basic §7).
- Racial exclusion was constitutive in Germany (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6).
- Came within two years of Hitler's chancellorship (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1935.
**Links:** WH-2129; WH-2135; WH-2136.
**Trap:** Do not date the Nuremberg Laws loosely (✅ owner 12 basic §10.8).
**Mains hook:** "Race was the discriminator between Italian fascism and Nazism."
**Back:** *"Thirty-five: race writes the state."*

### Flashcard WH-2135 — Fascism versus Nazism: race as discriminator
**Front:** What separates Italian fascism from German Nazism?
**Definition (learn):** Both destroyed pluralism, but only Nazism made biological race the organising principle of the state.
**Breakdown:**
- Race: derivative in Italy; constitutive in Germany (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6).
- Thoroughness: Nazism more systematic, racialised and brutal (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6; advanced §8).
- Mass mobilisation: Japan, Spain and Portugal lacked the mass movement for national rebirth (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6).
- Family resemblance real, identity false (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 basic §10.6.
**Links:** WH-2128; WH-2134; WH-2129.
**Trap:** "Both were dictatorships" is not a comparison (✅ owner 12 basic §10.1).
**Mains hook:** "The difference is not one of degree."
**Back:** *"Same family, different race."*

### Flashcard WH-2136 — Japan: army dominance and expansion
**Front:** How did Japan move toward army-led militarism, and why is it not simply fascist?
**Definition (learn):** Parliamentary government weakened under elite hostility, corruption, economic crisis and army pressure; the army took control after Manchuria and Inukai's assassination.
**Breakdown:**
- Manchuria seized 1931 (✅ owner 12 basic §5).
- Prime Minister Inukai assassinated 1932; transition to army dominance (✅).
- Full-scale war in China 1937 (✅).
- Lowe: military dictatorship/right-wing nationalism, not a copy of Italy (✅ owner 12 basic §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1931; 1932; 1937.
**Links:** WH-2106; WH-2137; WH-2148.
**Trap:** Do not call Japan fascist without qualification (✅ owner 12 basic §10.8).
**Mains hook:** "Japan produced military dominance of a surviving constitutional structure, not a mass party-state."
**Back:** *"Thirty-one, thirty-two, thirty-seven: army runs Japan."*

### Flashcard WH-2137 — Spain: civil war and Franco
**Front:** How did Spain move to authoritarianism, and how is it classified?
**Definition (learn):** Monarchy fell in 1931; the republic faced Church-army-regional-social conflict; civil war 1936; Franco's Nationalists won in 1939 with German and Italian help.
**Breakdown:**
- Monarchy fell 1931 (✅ owner 12 basic §5).
- Civil war from 1936 (✅).
- Franco's victory 1939 with German and Italian help (✅).
- Franco's regime: Church power restored; clerical-authoritarian (✅ owner 12 advanced §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1931; 1936; 1939.
**Links:** WH-2109 (Spanish Civil War); WH-2138; WH-2148.
**Trap:** Do not equate Franco's Spain with Nazi Germany (✅ owner 12 basic §10.8).
**Mains hook:** "Clerical-authoritarian and civil-war-forged, related but not identical."
**Back:** *"Thirty-one falls, thirty-nine Franco wins."*

### Flashcard WH-2138 — Primo de Rivera (advanced 12)
**Front:** What was Primo de Rivera's regime in Spain, and what was its classification?
**Definition (learn):** A military dictatorship, not a fascist state.
**Breakdown:**
- Military dictatorship (✅ owner 12 advanced §9).
- Not fascist (✅ owner 12 advanced §9).
- Precedes the 1931 fall of the monarchy (⚠️ sequence from owner timeline; no date given).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No date given for Primo de Rivera in the owner.
**Links:** WH-2137; WH-2122.
**Trap:** Do not present Primo de Rivera's regime as fascist.
**Mains hook:** Use it to show that dictatorship is wider than fascism.
**Back:** *"Dictator, not fascist."*

### Flashcard WH-2139 — Four-layer comparison: ideology, base, institutions, coercion
**Front:** How should fascism, Nazism and Japanese militarism be compared?
**Definition (learn):** Four separable layers: ideology, social base, institutions and coercion.
**Breakdown:**
- Ideology: Italian nationalism and anti-communism; German race theory; Japanese emperor-centred nationalism (✅ owner 12 basic §10.5).
- Route to power: March on Rome; Chancellorship and Enabling Act; Manchuria and Inukai (✅).
- Social base: propertied classes and the king; Germany's broad electoral base; Japanese military and bureaucratic elites (✅).
- Institutions and coercion: Corporate State; Gleichschaltung-type control (⚠️ term not in owner); Nuremberg Laws; Japanese repression (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 basic §10.5 table.
**Links:** WH-2135; WH-2136; WH-2137.
**Trap:** Do not merge the four layers into one "dictatorship" verdict (✅ owner 12 basic §10.1).
**Mains hook:** "Four layers, three countries, one family resemblance."
**Back:** *"Ideology, base, office, force."*

### Flashcard WH-2140 — Advanced: Mussolini's fascist ambitions and limits
**Front:** What fascist ambitions did Mussolini have, and what limited them?
**Definition (learn):** One-party rule, leader cult, corporatism, propaganda, anti-communism, violence and autarky; limited by the king, the Church and critics.
**Breakdown:**
- Ambitions: one-party, leader cult, corporatism, propaganda, nationalism, anti-communism, violence, autarky (✅ owner 12 advanced §2).
- Limits: king as head of state; Church as rival; critics like Croce (✅).
- Lowe's verdict: authoritarian with totalitarian ambitions (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 advanced §2.
**Links:** WH-2128; WH-2123; WH-2122.
**Trap:** Do not write that Mussolini's Italy was fully totalitarian in practice (✅ owner 12 advanced §11).
**Mains hook:** "Mussolini's Italy: authoritarian, ambitious, incomplete."
**Back:** *"Ambitions, limits, verdict."*

### Flashcard WH-2141 — Advanced: Hitler's rise and elite intrigue
**Front:** Was Hitler's rise purely the result of economic collapse, elite intrigue or ideology?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe's multi-causal view: economic crisis after 1929 was probably crucial, but conservatives let Hitler in and propaganda and organisation mattered.
**Breakdown:**
- Economic crisis (✅ owner 12 advanced §4).
- Elite bargaining: Hitler appointed Chancellor in January 1933 (✅).
- Nazi propaganda and organisation offered unity, jobs and anti-Marxism (✅).
- Big business funding: Marxist claim; Fest's caution (✅ owner 12 advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** January 1933.
**Links:** WH-2130; WH-2132; WH-2143.
**Trap:** Do not say Germans loved Nazi ideology alone (✅ owner 12 advanced §11).
**Mains hook:** "Crisis, mass appeal and elite miscalculation together."
**Back:** *"Crisis plus consent plus conservatives."*

### Flashcard WH-2142 — Advanced: consent, coercion and the Hitler myth
**Front:** How did Nazi rule endure: consent, coercion or myth?
**Definition (learn):** A changing blend of achievement, myth, conformity and fear.
**Breakdown:**
- Consent: jobs, rearmament, welfare and leisure, foreign-policy successes (✅ owner 12 advanced §6; Aly on material benefits).
- Propaganda: Goebbels magnified Hitler's image; the Hitler myth (Kershaw) (✅).
- Coercion: Gestapo, camps, informers, censorship (✅).
- Evans: boredom, disengagement, survival strategies and fear explain absence of revolt (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 advanced §6.
**Links:** WH-2141; WH-2143; WH-2144.
**Trap:** Do not present Nazi full employment as success without the basic/20 §9.6 qualification (✅ owner 12 basic §8.10).
**Mains hook:** "Endured through achievement, myth, conformity and fear."
**Back:** *"Jobs, myth, Gestapo, boredom."*

### Flashcard WH-2143 — Advanced: Hitler as weak dictator (Mommsen, Broszat, Kershaw)
**Front:** Was Hitler a "weak dictator" or the decisive master figure?
**Definition (learn):** Mommsen's weak-dictator thesis and Broszat's "policies from below", against Rich and Jäckel's master-figure view; Kershaw's "working towards the Führer" as an intermediate formula.
**Breakdown:**
- Mommsen: weak dictator who exploited circumstances (✅ owner 12 advanced §7).
- Broszat: policies pushed from below (✅).
- Rich and Jäckel: Hitler decisive master figure (✅).
- Kershaw: officials "working towards the Führer" (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 advanced §7.
**Links:** WH-2142; WH-2141.
**Trap:** Do not present the weak-dictator debate as settled.
**Mains hook:** "The regime radicalised itself around his perceived wishes."
**Back:** *"Weak, pushed from below, but working toward the Führer."*

### Flashcard WH-2144 — Advanced: Italian economic and social verdict
**Front:** What did fascism deliver for Italy's economy and society, by Lowe's verdict?
**Definition (learn):** Spectacle, discipline and some short-term stability, without solving structural weakness.
**Breakdown:**
- Early successes: public works, reclaimed marshes, roads, hydro-electric expansion (✅ owner 12 advanced §3).
- Failures: autarky incomplete; Battle for Wheat; real wages fell in Depression years; no real welfare state; corporate state as labour control (✅).
- Corruption and inefficiency serious (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 advanced §3.
**Links:** WH-2127; WH-2140.
**Trap:** Do not call Italian economic policy a success without these qualifications.
**Mains hook:** "Fascism gave Italy spectacle and discipline, not a structural cure."
**Back:** *"Roads yes, autarky no."*

### Flashcard WH-2145 — 2021 PYQ: challenge to the democratic state system
**Front:** How should the 2021 GS-I demand "evaluate the challenge to the democratic state system between the two World Wars" be answered?
**Definition (learn):** The verified 2021 GS-I question, 15 marks, 250 words, in evaluative form.
**Breakdown:**
- Context: unmet war expectations, economic breakdown, institutional immobilism, political violence, fear of revolution, elite complicity.
- Core dimensions: four-layer comparison; Italy as model; Germany radicalised; Japan and Spain as variants.
- Counterpoint: the democracies were abandoned more than overthrown (✅ owner 12 basic §10.4 verdict).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-I, Q13 (✅ owner 12 advanced PYQ block, neutral rendering only).
**Links:** WH-2146; WH-2139; WH-2130.
**Trap:** The UN remembrance linkage concerns Nazism's consequences and is not evidence for Italian or Japanese regime development (✅ owner 12 basic §11).
**Mains hook:** "Democracies were not overthrown so much as abandoned by electorates and elites."
**Back:** *"Twenty-one: abandoned more than stormed."*

### Flashcard WH-2146 — Democratic failure: the mechanisms
**Front:** What mechanisms made parliamentary democracy lose legitimacy in the interwar period?
**Definition (learn):** Unmet war expectations, economic breakdown, institutional immobilism, normalised violence, fear of revolution, elite complicity, weak opposition, external dependence.
**Breakdown:**
- Unmet expectations: "mutilated victory"; stab-in-the-back myth (✅ owner 12 basic §10.4).
- Economic breakdown: German hyperinflation 1923; over six million German unemployed by 1932; Italian unemployment 1.1 million; Japanese raw silk below one-fifth of its 1923 price by 1932 (✅ owner 12 basic §10.4; mechanism owner basic/20 §9.5).
- Institutional design: Weimar fragmentation; contempt for weak Italian cabinets (✅).
- Elite complicity: king refused resistance; conservative intrigue let Hitler in legally (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Six million (Germany, 1932); 1.1 million (Italy); raw silk <1/5 (Japan, 1932).
**Links:** WH-2130; WH-2145; WH-2113; WH-2124.
**Trap:** Do not add other unemployment or economic figures; the owner limits these (✅ owner 12 basic §10.8).
**Mains hook:** "Democracy was blamed for immobilism as much as for outcomes."
**Back:** *"Hopes broken, money broken, elites broken."*

### Flashcard WH-2147 — Trap set: Mussolini, Hitler and the 1933 chronology
**Front:** Which factual claims about Mussolini and Hitler are the classic distractors?
**Definition (learn):** Mussolini did not defeat the army; Hitler never won a majority; the Enabling Act was March 1933, not January.
**Breakdown:**
- March on Rome: king's invitation, not battle (✅ owner 12 basic §7; advanced §11).
- Hitler: never a majority in free election (✅ owner 12 basic §7; Wikipedia WH-2132).
- Enabling Act: March 1933; Chancellor: January 1933 (✅ Wikipedia; owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Oct 1922; Jan 1933; 23 March 1933.
**Links:** WH-2124; WH-2131; WH-2132.
**Trap:** This is exactly the Prelims-style statement set in owner 12 basic §9.
**Mains hook:** "Accuracy on the three facts is the first mark."
**Back:** *"Invited not stormed; elected no; legal in March."*

### Flashcard WH-2148 — Trap: not every 1930s dictatorship was fascist
**Front:** Were all 1930s authoritarian regimes fascist?
**Definition (learn):** No. Japan and Spain shared features with fascism but lacked the mass mobilisation for national rebirth.
**Breakdown:**
- Japan: army-dominated militarism (✅ owner 12 advanced §9).
- Spain: clerical-authoritarian, civil-war forged (✅ owner 12 advanced §9).
- Primo de Rivera: military dictator (✅).
- Right-wing authoritarianism is the safer umbrella; fascism the narrower one (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 advanced §9.
**Links:** WH-2136; WH-2137; WH-2138; WH-2139.
**Trap:** "All 1930s dictatorships were fascist" is wrong (✅ owner 12 advanced §11).
**Mains hook:** "Mass mobilisation is the test."
**Back:** *"Not all dictators wear the black shirt."*

### Flashcard WH-2149 — Fascist features: Lowe's list
**Front:** What features did Lowe list as basic features of fascism?
**Definition (learn):** Authoritarian government, extreme nationalism, one-party rule, leader cult, economic self-sufficiency, propaganda, anti-communism and glorification of violence.
**Breakdown:**
- Authoritarian government (✅ owner 12 basic §2).
- Extreme nationalism; one-party rule; leader cult (✅).
- Economic self-sufficiency (autarky) (✅).
- Propaganda; anti-communism; glorification of violence (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 12 basic §2.
**Links:** WH-2122; WH-2128; WH-2139.
**Trap:** Use the list for family resemblance, not identity (✅ owner 12 basic §10.6).
**Mains hook:** "The family resemblance is real; the identity is false."
**Back:** *"Nation, one party, leader, self-sufficiency, force."*

### GROUP — Russian Revolution and USSR under Stalin (basic 13 + advanced 13)

### Flashcard WH-2150 — Russian Revolution: core sequence (basic 13)
**Front:** What is the Russian sequence from tsarism to Stalin's USSR?
**Definition (learn):** Autocratic weakness and war failure produced the 1917 revolutions, Bolshevik consolidation, and then the Stalinist economic revolution and terror state.
**Breakdown:**
- Tsarist weakness (1905 unfinished).
- War failure -> February 1917 -> October 1917.
- Bolshevik consolidation 1917–24.
- Stalinist industrialisation, collectivisation, purges (✅ owner 13 basic §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 13 basic §1.
**Links:** WH-2151; WH-2154; WH-2157; WH-2162; WH-2164.
**Trap:** Do not treat 1917 as one event; February and October are different (✅ owner 13 basic §1).
**Mains hook:** "Autocratic weakness plus war failure produced collapse; party control and terror shaped the Stalinist order."
**Back:** *"Weakness, war, two revolutions, consolidate, terror."*

### Flashcard WH-2151 — 1905: October Manifesto and the Dumas
**Front:** What did the 1905 unrest and the October Manifesto promise, and why was the settlement shallow?
**Definition (learn):** After Russia's defeat by Japan, Nicholas II promised a Duma and civil liberties; the settlement remained shallow.
**Breakdown:**
- Defeat by Japan triggered unrest (✅ owner 13 basic mini-timeline).
- October Manifesto promised a Duma and civil liberties (✅ owner 13 basic §2).
- First two Dumas dissolved quickly; Duma never a real check on the Tsar (✅ owner 13 basic §2, §9.4).
- Nicholas did not work sincerely within the spirit of the Manifesto (✅ owner 13 basic §9.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1905.
**Links:** WH-2152; WH-2153; WH-2171.
**Trap:** 1905 did stabilise the regime for a decade; do not read collapse back into it (✅ owner 13 basic §9.4).
**Mains hook:** "The 1905 settlement was never honoured, so legitimacy was not repaired."
**Back:** *"Five: promised, dissolved, unhonoured."*

### Flashcard WH-2152 — Stolypin and the agrarian question
**Front:** What did Stolypin's reforms achieve, and why did they not settle the land question?
**Definition (learn):** Stolypin abolished redemption payments, but his land reforms did not reach or satisfy enough peasants.
**Breakdown:**
- Abolished redemption payments (✅ owner 13 basic §2).
- Land reforms helped some peasants but did not solve agrarian strain (✅ owner 13 basic §2 table).
- Peasant land hunger remained the largest mobilisable grievance (✅ owner 13 basic §9.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the reform.
**Links:** WH-2151; WH-2153.
**Trap:** Stolypin's reforms had real effects for some peasants; do not present them as nothing (⚠️ owner 13 basic §9.4).
**Mains hook:** "The land question was unresolved by reform."
**Back:** *"Redemption gone, hunger stays."*

### Flashcard WH-2153 — Discredited monarchy and war exposure
**Front:** How did the Rasputin scandal and war weaken the Russian state?
**Definition (learn):** The royal family was discredited, especially by the Rasputin scandal; the war exposed military and administrative incompetence.
**Breakdown:**
- Rasputin scandal discredited the royal family (✅ owner 13 basic §2).
- Defeats, shortages and transport failures (✅ owner 13 basic §9.4).
- War as trigger, not substitute for structural causes (✅ owner 13 basic §9.4).
- Industrial unrest revived after 1912 (✅ owner 13 basic §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1912 (industrial unrest revival).
**Links:** WH-2151; WH-2154.
**Trap:** The dynasty's discredit was a symptom more than a cause (⚠️ owner 13 basic §9.4).
**Mains hook:** "The war converted grievance into breakdown."
**Back:** *"Rasputin, war, strikes: the crown falls apart."*

### Flashcard WH-2154 — February/March 1917 revolution
**Front:** What happened in February/March 1917, and when did Nicholas II abdicate?
**Definition (learn):** Bread riots in Petrograd widened into mass strikes; garrison mutiny, and the Duma and senior generals concluded Nicholas had to go.
**Breakdown:**
- Bread riots in Petrograd widened into strikes (✅ owner 13 basic §3a).
- Troops first fired on crowds; garrison mutinied (✅).
- Abdication: 2 March 1917 (✅ owner 13 basic §3a; ⚠️ Gregorian date; Julian Old Style would be 15 February).
- Character: a broad, largely unplanned collapse (✅ owner 13 advanced §9.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2 March 1917.
**Links:** WH-2155; WH-2157; WH-2169.
**Trap:** February was not the Bolshevik revolution; it overthrew the Tsar (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "February was a collapse with no single author."
**Back:** *"March two: Tsar goes, no author."*

### Flashcard WH-2155 — Why the Provisional Government failed
**Front:** Why did the Provisional Government collapse so quickly?
**Definition (learn):** Continued unpopular war, dual power with the soviet, delayed land reform and elections, economic chaos and the Kornilov affair.
**Breakdown:**
- Continued unpopular war (✅ owner 13 basic §3b).
- Shared authority with the Petrograd soviet (dual power) (✅).
- Delayed land reform and elections (✅).
- Economic chaos, inflation, shortages (✅).
- Kornilov affair discredited Kerensky and boosted Bolsheviks (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1917.
**Links:** WH-2156; WH-2154; WH-2157.
**Trap:** Do not say it was "just weak" (✅ owner 13 basic §8.1 directive map).
**Mains hook:** "Dual power: the government held office while the soviets held the soldiers and workers who could enforce anything."
**Back:** *"War, dual power, land, bread, Kornilov."*

### Flashcard WH-2156 — Lenin's April Theses
**Front:** What did Lenin's April Theses offer, and why did it help the Bolsheviks?
**Definition (learn):** A sharper line: peace, land and soviet power.
**Breakdown:**
- Peace (end the war) (✅ owner 13 basic §3b).
- Land (✅).
- Soviet power (✅).
- Offered an alternative to the Provisional Government (✅ owner 13 advanced §3b).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** April 1917 (owner names the April Theses; date by standard knowledge ⚠️).
**Links:** WH-2155; WH-2157.
**Trap:** Do not present the April Theses as the seizure of power; that came in October.
**Mains hook:** "A slogan set that the government could not match."
**Back:** *"Peace, land, soviets: the Bolshevik promise."*

### Flashcard WH-2157 — October/November 1917 seizure of power
**Front:** How did the Bolsheviks seize power in October/November 1917?
**Definition (learn):** The Petrograd soviet decided to seize power in mid-October; Red Guards and pro-soviet troops occupied key points on the night of 25–26 October and arrested Provisional Government ministers.
**Breakdown:**
- Mid-October: Petrograd soviet decides (✅ owner 13 basic §3c).
- Night of 25–26 October: key points occupied (✅).
- Ministers arrested (✅).
- Trotsky planned effectively; other socialist groups walked out of the Soviet Congress (✅ owner 13 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25–26 October (O.S.); 7–8 November (N.S.) by standard calendar (⚠️).
**Links:** WH-2154; WH-2156; WH-2171.
**Trap:** October was a disciplined minority seizure, not a mass insurrection alone (⚠️ owner 13 advanced §3b).
**Mains hook:** "October was both a disciplined seizure and the culmination of collapsing legitimacy."
**Back:** *"Twenty-fifth night: soviet seizes, ministers held."*

### Flashcard WH-2158 — Constituent Assembly dispersal (18–19 January 1918)
**Front:** When did the Constituent Assembly meet, and when was it dispersed?
**Definition (learn):** The elected assembly, without a Bolshevik majority, was dissolved by the Bolshevik-led executive after one sitting.
**Breakdown:**
- Convened 18 January 1918 (5 January Old Style) (✅ Wikipedia: Russian Constituent Assembly, fetched).
- Dissolved by Bolshevik-led executive in the early hours of 19 January 1918 (6 January O.S.) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Bolshevik vote share: about 24 per cent (partial count; overall 22–25 per cent) (✅ Wikipedia; SR share contested in the article).
- Owner: dispersed January 1918 (✅ owner 13 basic §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 18 Jan 1918; 19 Jan 1918; ~24% Bolshevik share.
**Links:** WH-2161; WH-2164; WH-2170.
**Trap:** Do not state a SR vote share as settled; the article itself flags its figures as inconsistent (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** "The decisive step away from parliamentary democracy."
**Back:** *"Eighteen-nineteen January: the assembly sits once."*

### Flashcard WH-2159 — Brest-Litovsk and "sacrificing space"
**Front:** Why did Lenin accept Brest-Litovsk despite its territorial cost?
**Definition (learn):** Lenin argued that space had to be sacrificed to gain time.
**Breakdown:**
- Treaty March 1918 (✅ owner 13 basic §4).
- Huge territorial cost (✅).
- Lenin: "sacrifice space to gain time" (✅ owner 13 basic §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** March 1918.
**Links:** WH-2073; WH-2160.
**Trap:** Do not present Brest-Litovsk as a victory; the owner shows it as a survival choice (✅ owner 13 advanced §2 context).
**Mains hook:** "Survival, not triumph, drove the decision."
**Back:** *"Give space, buy time."*

### Flashcard WH-2160 — Civil War: why the Reds won
**Front:** Why did the Bolsheviks win the Civil War (1918–20)?
**Definition (learn):** Firmer leadership, control of the central core, disunited Whites and foreign intervention that let the Reds appear as defenders.
**Breakdown:**
- Lenin and Trotsky firmer leadership (✅ owner 13 basic §4).
- Bolsheviks controlled central Russia (✅).
- Whites lacked unity of aim (✅).
- Foreign intervention helped Reds portray themselves as defenders (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1918–20.
**Links:** WH-2159; WH-2161.
**Trap:** Do not present the Civil War as only a military contest (✅ owner 13 basic §9.6).
**Mains hook:** "Survival and the party emerged militarised."
**Back:** *"Central core, unified leaders, divided Whites."*

### Flashcard WH-2161 — Cheka and Red Terror
**Front:** What was the Cheka, and what was the Red Terror?
**Definition (learn):** The early Bolshevik security police (Cheka), and a widened campaign of repression against former elites and rivals.
**Breakdown:**
- Cheka created (✅ owner 13 basic §7).
- Red Terror widened (✅ owner 13 basic §4).
- Coercion was constitutive from the start (✅ owner 13 basic §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the period.
**Links:** WH-2160; WH-2171 (advanced Lenin debate).
**Trap:** Do not present coercion as a later Stalinist deviation (✅ owner 13 basic §9.6 trap).
**Mains hook:** "Coercion was constitutive, not deviant."
**Back:** *"Cheka built, Red Terror widened."*

### Flashcard WH-2162 — New Economic Policy (NEP, 1921)
**Front:** What was the NEP, and was it a retreat from communism?
**Definition (learn):** A 1921 tactical retreat from war communism: peasants kept surplus after tax, small trade revived; heavy industry, banking and transport stayed state-controlled.
**Breakdown:**
- Launched 1921 (✅ owner 13 basic §4).
- Peasants keep surplus after tax (✅).
- Small trade revived; heavy industry, banking, transport state-controlled (✅).
- Tactical, under communist political control (✅ owner 13 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1921.
**Links:** WH-2163; WH-2171.
**Trap:** NEP did not restore capitalism (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "Tactical retreat, not abandonment of political control."
**Back:** *"Twenty-one: surplus kept, control kept."*

### Flashcard WH-2163 — Lenin's death and Stalin's General Secretary power
**Front:** How did Stalin use the General Secretary post to win the succession?
**Definition (learn):** Stalin's control of appointments and promotions tilted party conferences and central bodies toward him.
**Breakdown:**
- Lenin died January 1924 (✅ owner 13 basic mini-timeline).
- General Secretary post: appointments and promotions (✅ owner 13 advanced §5).
- Local party machines filled with supporters (✅).
- Rivals underestimated Stalin (✅ owner 13 basic §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** January 1924.
**Links:** WH-2164; WH-2166.
**Trap:** Do not claim Stalin won by ideology alone; organisation and patronage mattered (✅ owner 13 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** "Organisation beat oratory in the succession."
**Back:** *"Twenty-four: appointments win."*

### Flashcard WH-2164 — Stalin's successive defeats of rivals
**Front:** In what order did Stalin defeat Trotsky, Kamenev, Zinoviev and Bukharin?
**Definition (learn):** Stalin defeated successive divided oppositions, presenting each move as party orthodoxy.
**Breakdown:**
- Trotsky isolated first (✅ owner 13 basic §5).
- Then Kamenev and Zinoviev (✅).
- Finally Bukharin (✅).
