- Disputes: socialism in one country vs permanent revolution; NEP vs rapid industrialisation (✅ owner 13 advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond sequence.
**Links:** WH-2163; WH-2165.
**Trap:** Do not say Stalin defeated one united opposition (✅ owner 13 advanced §5).
**Mains hook:** "Successive divided oppositions, each presented as orthodoxy."
**Back:** *"Trotsky, then Kamenev and Zinoviev, then Bukharin."*

### Flashcard WH-2165 — Five Year Plans and collectivisation
**Front:** What did the Five Year Plans and collectivisation achieve, and at what cost?
**Definition (learn):** Rapid heavy industrialisation and forced collectivisation to mechanise farming and crush kulaks.
**Breakdown:**
- Five Year Plans: gains in coal, steel, pig-iron and electricity; harsh discipline; poor consumer supply (✅ owner 13 basic §5).
- Collectivisation: kolkhoz farms; kulaks targeted (✅ owner 13 basic §7).
- By 1937 most farmland collectivised; grain output not steadily up; livestock collapsed (✅ owner 13 basic §5).
- Violence, famine (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** By 1937 (collectivisation). No output figures (owner forbids).
**Links:** WH-2166; WH-2167; WH-2168.
**Trap:** "Collectivisation clearly solved agriculture" is wrong (✅ owner 13 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** "Stalin solved industrial weakness by transferring the burden onto peasants and workers."
**Back:** *"Plans build, farms break."*

### Flashcard WH-2166 — Famine of 1932–33: death toll estimates
**Front:** What death toll does the owner attribute to the 1932–33 famine?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe reports estimates of more than 5 million deaths while grain was still exported; totals and responsibility are contested.
**Breakdown:**
- Estimate: more than 5 million deaths (✅ owner 13 basic §5 and §9.7).
- Grain was still exported during famine (✅).
- Exact totals and responsibility contested (⚠️ owner 13 basic §9.7).
- Advanced: Lowe presents wider post-Soviet calculations; use ranges (✅ owner 13 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1932–33; "more than 5 million" (owner-attributed).
**Links:** WH-2165; WH-2167.
**Trap:** Do not give a falsely precise, uncontested total (✅ owner 13 advanced §6).
**Mains hook:** "Use ranges or source-attributed estimates."
**Back:** *"Thirty-two-thirty-three: five million-plus, contested."*

### Flashcard WH-2167 — Purges and the 1936 Constitution
**Front:** What did the Purges and the 1936 Constitution do?
**Definition (learn):** The Purges made Stalin unchallengeable and removed experienced officials; the 1936 Constitution presented the system as democratic but was a one-party dictatorship.
**Breakdown:**
- Purges eliminated alternative leaders and terrorised society (✅ owner 13 advanced §7a).
- Removed officials, officers and administrators (✅ owner 13 basic §9.7).
- 1936 Constitution: presented as democratic; elections not competitive (✅ owner 13 basic §5, §9.8).
- Robert Conquest's high estimates; post-Soviet lower but enormous totals (✅ owner 13 advanced §7a).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1936–38 (owner timeline).
**Links:** WH-2165; WH-2168; WH-2175.
**Trap:** Do not say the 1936 Constitution made the USSR a real democracy (✅ owner 13 basic §7 and §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "Terror at elite level removed alternatives; the wider system rested on mobility and wartime legitimacy."
**Back:** *"Thirty-six: democracy on paper, terror in practice."*

### Flashcard WH-2168 — Second World War, Fourth Five Year Plan and Stalin's death
**Front:** What happened in Stalin's final years after the Second World War?
**Definition (learn):** Victory, reconstruction, atomic bomb, persistent repression, and Stalin's death on 5 March 1953.
**Breakdown:**
- USSR survived and defeated Nazi Germany (✅ owner 13 basic §6).
- Fourth Five Year Plan (1946–50) rebuilt industry quickly on official statistics (✅).
- First Soviet atomic bomb 1949 (✅).
- Famine returned in 1946; 1952 grain output below 1940 (✅).
- Doctors' Plot; POWs suspicion; labour camps expanded; Stalin died 5 March 1953 (✅ owner 13 basic §6; date ✅ owner timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1946–50; 1949; 1946; 5 March 1953.
**Links:** WH-2167; WH-2176.
**Trap:** Do not treat war victory as softening (✅ owner 13 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** "Victory vindicated Stalin in his own eyes; the human cost was catastrophic."
**Back:** *"Forty-nine bomb, fifty-three death."*

### Flashcard WH-2169 — Advanced: were the 1917 revolutions inevitable?
**Front:** Were the 1917 revolutions inevitable?
**Definition (learn):** A historiographical question; Lowe reports traditional liberal, Soviet, later western and war-as-shock views.
**Breakdown:**
- Traditional liberal: recovery before 1914; monarchy might have survived without war (✅ owner 13 advanced §2).
- Soviet: Nicholas betrayed 1905; revolution bound to come (✅).
- Later western caution: Read, Service (✅).
- War-as-shock: Fitzpatrick and others (✅).
- Safe conclusion: vulnerable before 1914; war made collapse overwhelmingly more likely without predetermining each step (✅ owner 13 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 13 advanced §2.
**Links:** WH-2151; WH-2154; WH-2171.
**Trap:** "Inevitable revolution" is a historiographical question, not settled fact (✅ owner 13 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** "Vulnerable before 1914; war made collapse likely without making it predetermined."
**Back:** *"Vulnerable, war-shocked, not preordained."*

### Flashcard WH-2170 — Advanced: Lenin, evil genius or saviour?
**Front:** Was Lenin a saviour or the architect of later dictatorship?
**Definition (learn):** Admirers (A. J. P. Taylor), sympathetic revisionists (Moshe Lewin), and traditional critics who saw the one-party state and Cheka as preparing Stalin.
**Breakdown:**
- Admirers: Lenin preserved Soviet Russia under pressure (✅ owner 13 advanced §4).
- Lewin: violence forced by crisis; late Lenin sought a less brutal future (✅).
- Critics: one-party state, Cheka, terror and Assembly dispersal (✅).
- Lenin had dispersed the Assembly, created the Cheka, justified executions, banned factionalism (✅ owner 13 advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 13 advanced §4.
**Links:** WH-2158; WH-2161; WH-2175.
**Trap:** "Lenin was democratic until Stalin ruined everything" is wrong (✅ owner 13 advanced §4).
**Mains hook:** "Lenin solved survival problems by institutionalising party monopoly and coercion."
**Back:** *"Saviour or architect: both halves true."*

### Flashcard WH-2171 — Advanced: coup or mass insurrection (October)
**Front:** Was the October Revolution a coup or a mass insurrection?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe contrasts Soviet, traditional liberal and libertarian views; the best formulation is a disciplined seizure of strategic points inside a wider legitimacy collapse.
**Breakdown:**
- Soviet: mass support for Bolshevik slogans gave legitimacy (✅ owner 13 advanced §3b).
- Traditional liberal: disciplined minority exploiting chaos (✅).
- Libertarian: popular radicalism ran deeper than Bolshevik organisation (✅).
- Bolsheviks better organised; Trotsky planned effectively (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** October 1917.
**Links:** WH-2157; WH-2169.
**Trap:** Do not say the Bolsheviks were simply a mass party with majority support in October (owner gives three views).
**Mains hook:** "A disciplined seizure of strategic points and the culmination of a wider legitimacy collapse."
**Back:** *"Disciplined seizure, wider collapse."*

### Flashcard WH-2172 — Advanced: was the regime totalitarian?
**Front:** Was Stalin's USSR totalitarian in practice?
**Definition (learn):** Western view: totalitarian like Nazi Germany; social revisionists: society bargained from below; Getty-style: chaotic, inefficient practice.
**Breakdown:**
- Traditional western view: totalitarian (✅ owner 13 advanced §7b).
- Social revisionist: groups benefited and bargained from below (✅).
- Getty-style revisionism: inefficient and chaotic (✅).
- Lowe's balance: aims totalitarian; practice uneven except in terror, which was effective (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 13 advanced §7b.
**Links:** WH-2167; WH-2173.
**Trap:** Do not treat the totalitarian label as a single fixed view (✅ owner 13 advanced §7b).
**Mains hook:** "Totalitarian aims, chaotic practice, effective terror."
**Back:** *"Aims total, practice uneven, terror tight."*

### Flashcard WH-2173 — Advanced: Stalinism and Leninism
**Front:** Was Stalinism simply Leninism continued?
**Definition (learn):** Stalinism grew from Leninist structures but was Leninist one-party rule radicalised into a wider terror-state.
**Breakdown:**
- Continuities: party monopoly, violence, anti-pluralism (✅ owner 13 advanced §7c).
- Differences: scale, bureaucratic penetration, cult politics, systematic terror (✅).
- Formula: continuity of framework, discontinuity of degree (✅ owner 13 basic §9.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 13 advanced §7c.
**Links:** WH-2170; WH-2175; WH-2167.
**Trap:** Do not claim Stalin betrayed a liberal Lenin (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "Assess the extent to which Stalinism was a continuation of Leninism."
**Back:** *"Same frame, far darker."*

### Flashcard WH-2174 — Mensheviks, Bolsheviks and the Marxian formulations
**Front:** What do the owner's sourced Marxian formulations and the Menshevik-Bolshevik distinction say?
**Definition (learn):** Only two formulations are sourced: "dictatorship of the proletariat" and "from each according to his ability, to each according to his needs", both reported by Lowe.
**Breakdown:**
- Mensheviks: "the strict Marxists, believing in a proletarian revolution" (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
- Lenin "moving away from Marxism" (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
- Marx "had never described" how a socialist economy should be run; policy disputes followed (✅).
- Doctrine belongs to basic/01 §9 (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No publication dates (owner forbids).
**Links:** Topic 01 (basic/01 §9); WH-2150.
**Trap:** Do not cite The Communist Manifesto or Das Kapital or their dates from this owner (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "Lenin's party moved away from Marx's own expectations, which left room for dispute."
**Back:** *"Mensheviks stayed strict; Lenin moved."*

### Flashcard WH-2175 — Lenin versus Stalin: continuity of framework, discontinuity of degree
**Front:** How should the Lenin-to-Stalin relationship be stated?
**Definition (learn):** The one-party state, the dispersal of the Constituent Assembly and the political police were Lenin's; the scale of terror and the second revolution from above were Stalin's.
**Breakdown:**
- Lenin: one-party state, Constituent Assembly dispersal, Cheka (✅ owner 13 basic §9.8).
- Stalin: terror on a larger scale, second revolution from above (✅).
- Framework continuity; degree discontinuity (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jan 1918 (dispersal), 1921 (Cheka era NEP), 1928–29 (revolution from above) as owner dates.
**Links:** WH-2158; WH-2161; WH-2173.
**Trap:** Do not present Lenin's system as liberal democracy ruined by Stalin (✅ owner 13 basic §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "Continuity of framework, discontinuity of degree."
**Back:** *"Lenin built the cage, Stalin fitted it tighter."*

### Flashcard WH-2176 — Social transformation under Stalin
**Front:** How did Stalinism transform peasants, workers, women and nationalities?
**Definition (learn):** Collectivisation ended peasant proprietorship as a class; rapid industrialisation created a new working class; women entered industry alongside domestic burdens; nationalities faced tension between central control and national culture.
**Breakdown:**
- Peasants: proprietorship ended; rural labour tied to kolkhoz; rural-to-urban movement (⚠️ owner 13 basic §9.7).
- Workers: new working class from peasant recruits; upward mobility for some (⚠️).
- Women: mass industrial and professional entry; domestic burdens continued (⚠️).
- Nationalities: central control versus national-cultural policy; resurfaced in 1989–91 (⚠️ owner 13 basic §9.7; basic/21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No literacy, employment or urbanisation figures sourced (owner).
**Links:** WH-2165; WH-2168; Topic 21 (1989–91).
**Trap:** Keep these four dimensions qualitative (✅ owner 13 basic §9.7).
**Mains hook:** "Achievement and cost in the same answer."
**Back:** *"Peasants, workers, women, nations: all remade."*

### Flashcard WH-2177 — Trap: February, NEP, 1936 Constitution
**Front:** Which three Russian statements are the classic distractors?
**Definition (learn):** February 1917 was not the Bolshevik revolution; NEP did not restore capitalism; the 1936 Constitution did not make the USSR democratic.
**Breakdown:**
- February: broader uprising that overthrew the Tsar (✅ owner 13 basic §7 and §9.9).
- NEP: limited market mechanisms under communist control (✅).
- 1936 Constitution: elections not competitive (✅).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1917; 1921; 1936.
**Links:** WH-2154; WH-2162; WH-2167.
**Trap:** These are the most common error sets in this topic (✅ owner 13 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** "Precision on the three points is the first mark."
**Back:** *"Not Bolshevik, not capitalist, not democratic."*

### Flashcard WH-2178 — Trap: Lenin's system was not ruined by Stalin alone
**Front:** Was Lenin's system a liberal democracy that Stalin later ruined?
**Definition (learn):** No. Coercion, party monopoly and the dispersal of the Constituent Assembly already marked Lenin's system.
**Breakdown:**
- Constituent Assembly dispersed January 1918 (✅ owner 13 basic §9.6).
- Cheka and Red Terror (✅).
- Party monopoly and factionalism banned (✅ owner 13 advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** January 1918.
**Links:** WH-2158; WH-2161; WH-2170.
**Trap:** A "Lenin liberal, Stalin tyrant" answer loses the continuity point (✅ owner 13 basic §9.8).
**Mains hook:** "The continuity question is answered by the Assembly, not by the Stalin years."
**Back:** *"Assembly dispersed before Stalin."*

### Flashcard WH-2179 — Stalin's economic transformation: success or catastrophe?
**Front:** How far can Stalin's economic transformation be called a success?
**Definition (learn):** It built the industrial base that survived 1941 and failed by its own ideological criteria: it impoverished peasants, terrorised workers and destroyed expertise.
**Breakdown:**
- Industrial gains: coal, steel, pig-iron, electricity (✅ owner 13 advanced §6).
- Agrarian disaster and terror (✅).
- Wartime survival and postwar limits (✅ owner 13 advanced §9C).
- Lowe: "limited economic success argument" with Nove and McCauley-type positions (✅ owner 13 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No output figures (owner).
**Links:** WH-2165; WH-2166; WH-2168; WH-2176.
**Trap:** Do not use "industrialisation succeeded, therefore Stalin was justified" (✅ owner 13 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** "Stalin solved the industrial weakness by transferring the burden violently onto peasants and workers."
**Back:** *"Built the base, broke the people."*

## WORLD HISTORY — Part 3: WWII, Cold War, UN, China and Decolonisation

### GROUP — Second World War (basic 14 + advanced 14)

### Flashcard WH-3001 — Second World War: core idea and phases
**Front:** What is the single-sentence core idea of the Second World War, and what are its four phases?
**Definition (learn):** A mobile, global war (Europe, Russia, North Africa, the Atlantic, the Pacific) in which rapid Axis expansion gave way to overextension, Allied recovery and a war that reshaped society, empire, genocide studies and the global balance of power. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Phase 1 (1939–Dec 1940): Poland defeated; phoney war; Denmark and Norway occupied; France collapses; Battle of Britain prevents invasion.
- Phase 2 (1941–summer 1942): Axis offensive widens — Barbarossa, North Africa, Greece, Pearl Harbor, Japanese expansion.
- Phase 3 (summer 1942–summer 1943): Axis offensives checked at Midway, El Alamein and Stalingrad.
- Phase 4 (July 1943–Aug 1945): Italy falls, D-Day opens the second front, Germany collapses, Japan is defeated.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Lowe calls it "much more mobile, global and complex" than WWI. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3002 (Blitzkrieg), WH-3026 (why Axis lost), WH-3028 (consequences); Cold War WH-3033.
**Trap:** Do not compress the war into one theatre (Europe only). Asia-Pacific began earlier (1931/1937) — see WH-3026.
**Mains hook:** "Examine the phases of WWII." Answer: four phases with turning points; close with the structural shift to bipolarity.
**Back:** *"Four phases: Blitz-Bite, Bite-Back, Turn, Tide"* (phases: Expansion → Overreach → Turn → Collapse).

### Flashcard WH-3002 — Blitzkrieg
**Front:** What was Blitzkrieg and why did it make the early Axis advance so rapid?
**Definition (learn):** "Lightning war" — a combination of motorised divisions, tanks and air support that overwhelmed a poorly equipped defence through rapid, concentrated thrusts. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Components: tanks + motorised thrusts + air support.
- Poland: fell quickly to Blitzkrieg (Sept 1939).
- France: collapsed after German assault through Holland, Belgium and northern France.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Poland — Sept 1939; France surrendered June 1940 (Dunkirk evacuated large Allied numbers first). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3003 (Poland), WH-3004 (France & Dunkirk).
**Trap:** Do NOT say France fell "only because of cowardice". Owner: Blitzkrieg effectiveness, strategic surprise and Allied weakness explain it. (✅ owner 14)
**Mains hook:** Early Axis victories were operational (Blitzkrieg) — but operations cannot offset strategic overreach (WH-3026).
**Back:** *"Tank + Thrust + Air = Blitz"*

### Flashcard WH-3003 — Poland, September 1939 (German and Soviet occupation)
**Front:** What happened in Poland in September 1939 and why does it mark the European origin?
**Definition (learn):** Germany and the USSR occupied Poland in September 1939; the Nazi-Soviet Pact cleared the way. This is the immediate European origin of WWII. (✅ owner 14, ✅ owner 14 §10.4 citing basic/11)
**Breakdown:**
- Germany attacked from the west; USSR occupied from the east.
- Poland fell quickly to Blitzkrieg.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** September 1939 (owner). Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact signed 24 Aug 1939, backdated to 23 Aug 1939; Germany invaded Poland 1 Sept 1939; USSR followed 17 Sept 1939 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-3001, WH-3002, WH-3026 (European origin + Asian origin). Owner: basic/11 (International Relations 1919–39, collective security).
**Trap:** Do not say the war began with Pearl Harbor or with Barbarossa. The European war started with Poland (Sept 1939). Asia-Pacific war started earlier (Manchuria 1931, China 1937) — see WH-3026.
**Mains hook:** "The Second World War was not one war that spread but two regional wars…" — Poland is the European origin.
**Back:** *"Poland 1939: the spark in Europe."*

### Flashcard WH-3004 — Fall of France and Dunkirk (April–June 1940)
**Front:** Which countries fell to Germany in April–June 1940, and what was Dunkirk?
**Definition (learn):** Denmark, Norway, Holland, Belgium and France were defeated between April and June 1940. Dunkirk evacuated large numbers of Allied troops, but France still surrendered in June 1940. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Denmark and Norway occupied (phase 1).
- German assault through Holland, Belgium and northern France → France collapses.
- Dunkirk: Allied evacuation — men saved, France lost.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** April–June 1940; France surrendered June 1940. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3002 (Blitzkrieg), WH-3005 (Battle of Britain), WH-3019 (Vichy France after the fall).
**Trap:** Dunkirk ≠ French victory. Evacuation of troops was not a military win; France still capitulated.
**Mains hook:** Use France's fall to show why Britain's survival (WH-3005) was strategically crucial.
**Back:** *"Fall of Five: Denmark, Norway, Holland, Belgium, France (Apr–Jun 1940)."*

### Flashcard WH-3005 — Battle of Britain (July–Sept 1940)
**Front:** Why did the RAF's victory in the Battle of Britain matter, and what three reasons does Lowe give for German failure?
**Definition (learn):** The air battle over Britain (July–Sept 1940) in which the RAF held off the Luftwaffe, preventing a German invasion and keeping Britain in the war. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Result: first major check to Hitler; Britain stayed in the war.
- Lowe's reasons for German failure: (a) radar warning; (b) German range problems; (c) Hitler's shift to bombing London.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** July–Sept 1940. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3001 (phase 1), WH-3012 (D-Day later), WH-3023 (air power).
**Trap:** Battle of Britain prevented invasion — it was not the same as the Battle of the Atlantic (WH-3011), which kept Britain supplied. Do not swap them.
**Mains hook:** Air power as war-deciding (advanced 14 §3b) — the RAF's victory was the first proof that air power could decide.
**Back:** *"Radar + Range + London shift = RAF held the sky."*

### Flashcard WH-3006 — Operation Barbarossa (22 June 1941)
**Front:** What was Operation Barbarossa and why was it a turning point in the war's structure?
**Definition (learn):** Hitler's invasion of the USSR on 22 June 1941. It converted the European war into an existential ideological war and created the multi-front war Hitler could not win. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 22 June 1941.
- Effect: Germany fought Britain, then the USSR, then the USA — multi-front overreach.
- The USSR absorbed and then reversed the German eastern offensive (see WH-3010).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 22 June 1941. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3007 (Pearl Harbor and US entry), WH-3010 (Stalingrad), WH-3026 (overreach), WH-3002 (Blitzkrieg).
**Trap:** Barbarossa (USSR) ≠ Pearl Harbor (USA); both are 1941 but different theatres and dates.
**Mains hook:** "Hitler turned the war into an unwinnable multi-front struggle" — Barbarossa is the key escalation.
**Back:** *"22 June: Barbarossa breaks the East."*

### Flashcard WH-3007 — Pearl Harbor and US entry (7 December 1941)
**Front:** When and by whom was Pearl Harbor attacked, and what did it do to the war's resource balance?
**Definition (learn):** Japan attacked Pearl Harbor on 7 December 1941, bringing the USA directly into the war; Hitler then declared war on the USA, bringing overwhelming American industrial and financial resources. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Japan attacked Pearl Harbor (7 Dec 1941).
- USA entered the war directly.
- Hitler's declaration of war on the USA escalated the conflict further.
- Effect: decisive industrial and financial capacity for the Allies.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 7 Dec 1941. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3006 (Barbarossa), WH-3008 (Midway), WH-3026 (why Axis lost), WH-3023 (carrier air power).
**Trap:** Pearl Harbor is the trigger for US entry; Hitler's declaration on the USA was a separate escalating decision.
**Mains hook:** Pearl Harbor fused the Asian and European wars (WH-3026, "fusion, 1941").
**Back:** *"7 Dec: Pearl brought America in."*

### Flashcard WH-3008 — Battle of Midway (June 1942)
**Front:** What did Midway (June 1942) decide about naval power in the Pacific?
**Definition (learn):** The June 1942 battle that crippled Japanese carrier strength and shifted Pacific momentum. It showed that carrier-based air power, not battleships, decided the Pacific. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Crippled Japanese carriers.
- Pacific momentum passed to the Allies.
- Carrier air power dominated the Pacific (advanced 14 §3b).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** June 1942. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3023 (carrier vs battleship trap), WH-3007 (Pearl Harbor), WH-3009 (El Alamein — same year, different theatre).
**Trap:** The Pacific war was NOT mainly a battleship war (owner trap). Carrier air power was decisive.
**Mains hook:** Use Midway as evidence for "air and sea power ceased to be merely supporting arms and became war-deciding" (advanced 14 §3).
**Back:** *"Midway: carriers, not battleships."*

### Flashcard WH-3009 — El Alamein (October 1942)
**Front:** What did El Alamein (Oct 1942) save and what did it begin?
**Definition (learn):** The October 1942 battle that saved Egypt and the Suez Canal and led towards Axis expulsion from North Africa. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Saved Egypt and the Suez Canal.
- Began Axis expulsion from North Africa.
- North Africa had been revived by Rommel earlier in 1941–42 (owner §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** October 1942. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3008 (Midway, June 1942), WH-3010 (Stalingrad, Feb 1943), WH-3001 (phase 3).
**Trap:** El Alamein ≠ Stalingrad. El Alamein was North Africa; Stalingrad was the Soviet front.
**Mains hook:** The 1942–43 turning points are cumulative, not a single decisive battle (WH-3027).
**Back:** *"El Alamein: Egypt saved, Africa lost."*

### Flashcard WH-3010 — Stalingrad (February 1943)
**Front:** What did German surrender at Stalingrad (February 1943) achieve for the Soviet Union?
**Definition (learn):** The German surrender at Stalingrad in February 1943 shattered the German invincibility myth and opened the road to the Soviet advance westward. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Shattered German invincibility myth.
- Opened the road to Soviet advance.
- USSR absorbed and reversed the German eastern offensive (owner §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** February 1943 (German surrender). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3006 (Barbarossa, 1941), WH-3012 (D-Day, 1944), WH-3027 (turning point vs final victory).
**Trap:** Stalingrad is not "final victory" — Germany still fought until May 1945 (WH-3027).
**Mains hook:** The USSR's role in the Eastern Front is often under-weighted; Stalingrad shows Soviet agency.
**Back:** *"Stalingrad: the myth dies, the Soviets advance."*

### Flashcard WH-3011 — Battle of the Atlantic (1939–43)
**Front:** Why was the Battle of the Atlantic strategically vital, and what kept Britain supplied?
**Definition (learn):** The naval campaign against German U-boats that kept Britain supplied and made later Allied offensives possible. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Tools: convoy protection, radar and long-range aircraft.
- Result: U-boat threat defeated; Atlantic supply lines held; North Africa and D-Day were sustained.
- Advanced: "as strategically vital as the better-known land battles" (advanced 14 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1939–43 (owner §10.5). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3023 (air and sea power), WH-3005 (Battle of Britain), WH-3012 (D-Day needed sea-borne movement).
**Trap:** Not the same as the Battle of Britain (1940, air). Battle of the Atlantic was naval supply war.
**Mains hook:** "Assess the role of sea and air power in the Allied victory" (advanced 14 §8).
**Back:** *"Convoy, radar, long-range air — U-boats lose."*

### Flashcard WH-3012 — D-Day (6 June 1944)
**Front:** When did D-Day take place and what did it open?
**Definition (learn):** The 6 June 1944 Allied landings in Normandy that opened the western front in France and accelerated Germany's defeat — a genuine two-front collapse with the Soviet advance from the east. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 6 June 1944.
- Place: Normandy.
- Effect: second front; combined with Soviet eastern advance.
- Needed sea-borne mass movement (advanced 14 §3a).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 6 June 1944. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3010 (Stalingrad), WH-3011 (Atlantic supply), WH-3001 (phase 4), WH-3013 (German surrender May 1945).
**Trap:** D-Day ≠ end of war. Germany surrendered only in May 1945.
**Mains hook:** Two-front collapse vs earlier attrition — D-Day's effect is accelerative, not decisive by itself.
**Back:** *"6 June 1944: the western door opens."*

### Flashcard WH-3013 — End of the war in Europe and Japan (May and August 1945)
**Front:** When did Germany surrender, and what happened in August 1945 before Japan's surrender?
**Definition (learn):** Germany surrendered in May 1945. In August 1945 the atomic bombings and Soviet entry into the war against Japan preceded Japan's surrender. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- May 1945: German surrender.
- Aug 1945: atomic bombings (Hiroshima and Nagasaki) and Soviet entry against Japan.
- Japan's surrender followed the August sequence (owner mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** May 1945; August 1945. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3012 (D-Day), WH-3014 (Japan surrender causes), WH-3029 (nuclear age).
**Trap:** Do not give a single cause for Japan's surrender (WH-3014). Do not date the Soviet entry as before the bombs.
**Mains hook:** Japan's end is multi-causal; the nuclear age begins here (WH-3029).
**Back:** *"May: Germany falls. Aug: Bombs + Soviets, Japan yields."*

### Flashcard WH-3014 — Japan's surrender: multi-causal, not atomic-only
**Front:** What are the competing explanations for Japan's surrender in August 1945?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe foregrounds the atomic bombings but notes Japanese peace feelers and expected Soviet entry; historians debate the relative weight of the bombs, Soviet intervention, blockade, conventional destruction and the emperor-system question. (✅ owner 14, ⚠️ historiographical synthesis)
**Breakdown:**
- Atomic bombings (Hiroshima, Nagasaki).
- Soviet declaration of war and invasion of Manchuria (advanced 14 §4).
- Blockade; conventional bombing; Japanese internal politics; emperor-system question.
- Japanese peace feelers (contested).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1945. No casualty figures sourced in owner (⚠️). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3013 (August sequence), WH-3015 (bombing controversy in WH-3025), WH-3029 (nuclear age).
**Trap:** "Japan surrendered only because of the atomic bombs" is a trap. Also "the bombs were irrelevant" is a trap — present the debate.
**Mains hook:** "Japan's surrender is a multi-causal historiographical problem" (advanced 14 §7).
**Back:** *"Bombs, Soviets, blockade, emperor — weigh all, pick none alone."*

### Flashcard WH-3015 — The Holocaust: about 5.7 million murdered Jews (Lowe estimate)
**Front:** What figure does Lowe give for the Holocaust, and how should it be attributed?
**Definition (learn):** The systematic, state-organised murder of Jews across German-controlled Europe, one of the war's defining crimes. Lowe gives a source-specific estimate of about 5.7 million murdered Jews. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Lowe's figure: about 5.7 million Jews (attribute to Lowe).
- Estimates vary among historians (⚠️).
- Implemented through occupation machinery and racial policy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ~5.7 million (Lowe). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3016 (other victim groups), WH-3024 (intentionalist vs functionalist), WH-3017 (total war).
**Trap:** Do not invent or substitute another Holocaust figure. Do not merge other victims into the Jewish total (WH-3016).
**Mains hook:** Holocaust must be placed as a state project pursued through the war, not an isolated atrocity or a side-note (advanced 14 §5, §7).
**Back:** *"5.7 million, Lowe's count — attribute and admit variance."*

### Flashcard WH-3016 — Other Nazi victim groups (not compressed into the Jewish toll)
**Front:** Name the Nazi victim groups that must be listed separately from the Holocaust Jewish total.
**Definition (learn):** Nazi mass murder also targeted Roma, disabled people, Soviet prisoners of war, Poles, political opponents, homosexuals and others. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Roma; disabled people; Soviet prisoners of war; Poles; political opponents; homosexuals; "and others".
- Not compressed into one undifferentiated Holocaust total.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No figures sourced in owner. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3015 (Jewish toll), WH-3017 (total war).
**Trap:** Do not merge Roma, disabled, Soviet POW, Polish victims into the Jewish total.
**Mains hook:** Shows genocide's breadth — a bounded categorisation, not a number contest.
**Back:** *"Roma, disabled, POWs, Poles, dissenters, queer — name them."*

### Flashcard WH-3017 — Total war: what it means
**Front:** What did "total war" mean in 1939–45 and how did it differ in scope?
**Definition (learn):** A war in which civilian populations, industry, transport and food supply became military targets, whole societies were mobilised, and occupation and racial policy produced systematic atrocity. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Massive civilian bombing.
- Industry, transport and food supply as military targets.
- Whole societies mobilised.
- Occupation and racial policy → systematic atrocity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No bombing casualty figures sourced (⚠️ owner caution). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3025 (bombing debate), WH-3015 (Holocaust), WH-3018 (collaboration/resistance), WH-3021 (forced labour).
**Trap:** Total war's distinguishing feature is targeting, not scale (owner verdict scaffold). Do not give bombing casualty numbers.
**Mains hook:** "The distinguishing feature was not scale but targeting" (owner §10.8).
**Back:** *"Total = targets: civilians, factories, food."*

### Flashcard WH-3018 — Vidkun Quisling and Norway (collaboration anchor)
**Front:** Who was Vidkun Quisling and why is his name a generic term?
**Definition (learn):** Quisling led the local Nazis in Norway who "gave the invaders every assistance". His name became the generic term for a collaborator. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Context: German invasion of Norway (1940, phase 1).
- Norwegian population resisted the invasion (✅ owner 14).
- Local Nazis under Quisling assisted the invaders.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Norway's invasion is in the April–June 1940 phase (WH-3004). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3019 (Vichy), WH-3020 (Italian partisans), WH-3004 (fall of France and Norway).
**Trap:** Bounded — do not extend to other collaborators, parties or officials (owner §10.6).
**Mains hook:** Resistance and collaboration are the same question asked of occupied societies — the structure of the choice matters, not nation grades.
**Back:** *"Quisling = collaborator, the word itself."*

### Flashcard WH-3019 — Vichy France as a collaborationist state
**Front:** What was Vichy France and why is it the decisive collaboration case?
**Definition (learn):** A functioning collaborationist authority whose forces were "technically on the same side" as the Axis; it allowed the Japanese to occupy French Indo-China. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Collaboration through a state (with colonies and armed forces).
- Permitted Japanese occupation of French Indo-China.
- Strategic consequences as far as south-east Asia.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Vichy regime (post-June 1940 — per WH-3004). No other figures sourced. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3004 (fall of France), WH-3018 (Quisling), WH-3026 (Asian war).
**Trap:** Do not extend to other collaborationist regimes; do not quantify collaboration (owner bound).
**Mains hook:** Collaboration through a state had strategic effects in Asia — proof that the war was a single system.
**Back:** *"Vichy let Japan into Indo-China."*

### Flashcard WH-3020 — Italian partisans and Mussolini's execution
**Front:** What happened to Mussolini at the end of the war, and what does it show about resistance?
**Definition (learn):** Italian partisans captured and shot Mussolini and took his body to Milan, where it was strung up by the feet in a public square. It shows resistance movements executing the political settlement, not merely harassing the occupier. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Italian partisans captured and shot Mussolini.
- Body taken to Milan and strung up in a public square.
- Italy falls (phase 4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Italy falls from July 1943 (WH-3001, phase 4). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3001 (phase 4), WH-3018 (Quisling), WH-3016 (wider victim groups).
**Trap:** Bounded — use only this named anchor; do not add partisan operations, commanders or casualty figures (owner §10.6).
**Mains hook:** Resistance = agency; but partisan execution also shows the political settlement being made by occupied peoples.
**Back:** *"Milan: partisans end the Duce."*

### Flashcard WH-3021 — Forced labour and displacement
**Front:** Why are forced labour and mass displacement primary, not incidental, features of WWII?
**Definition (learn):** The war forced "millions of decent, innocent men and women" into forced labour and produced mass displacement; these are named among the war's effects. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Forced labour: millions.
- Displacement and reconstruction as social consequences.
- Women were among those deported into forced labour (owner §10.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No figures sourced in owner (⚠️). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3017 (total war), WH-3022 (no Versailles-type peace), WH-3028 (consequences), WH-3016 (victim groups).
**Trap:** Do not invent forced-labour or displacement numbers; the owner gives none.
**Mains hook:** Population movement and coerced labour as core features of total war.
**Back:** *"Forced labour and displacement — the war's silent mass."*

### Flashcard WH-3022 — No single Versailles-type peace (separate treaties)
**Front:** How did the post-WWII settlement differ from the post-WWI Versailles model?
**Definition (learn):** The 1945 settlement took the form of separate treaties rather than one all-inclusive peace, a deliberate lesson from 1919. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Versailles-type peace (1919): one inclusive treaty.
- WWII: separate treaties → lesson from 1919.
- But European settlement was made by occupation and Cold War division, not by treaty (owner §10.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-1945 (no treaty dates given in owner). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3001 (consequences), WH-3028 (post-war order), WH-3036 (Yalta/Potsdam) and WH-3049 (Berlin Wall).
**Trap:** Do not say "the 1945 peace was a second Versailles". The owner's point is the opposite: separate treaties and occupation.
**Mains hook:** Separate treaties did not produce a single European peace; it was made by occupation and division.
**Back:** *"Separate treaties, not one Versailles."*

### Flashcard WH-3023 — Carrier air power vs battleships (Pacific)
**Front:** Why did carrier-based air power dominate the Pacific, not battleships?
**Definition (learn):** In the Pacific, carrier aircraft decided naval battles; Midway (June 1942) crippled Japanese carriers and shifted momentum. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Carrier air power dominated the Pacific.
- Midway: carrier strength crippled.
- Air power as a war-deciding instrument (advanced 14 §3b).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** June 1942 (Midway). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3008 (Midway), WH-3011 (Battle of the Atlantic), WH-3005 (Battle of Britain).
**Trap:** "The Pacific war was mainly a battleship war" — wrong. (✅ owner 14 §8 trap)
**Mains hook:** "Air and sea power ceased to be supporting arms and became war-deciding instruments."
**Back:** *"Carriers ruled the Pacific."*

### Flashcard WH-3024 — Intentionalist vs functionalist debate (Holocaust)
**Front:** What are the intentionalist and functionalist views on the Holocaust, and what is Lowe's combined verdict?
**Definition (learn):** Intentionalists: Hitler always intended extermination; the war provided opportunity. Functionalists: wartime circumstances, conquest and bureaucratic radicalisation drove the Final Solution. Lowe's overall direction: a combined explanation — long-term anti-Semitic intent plus wartime opportunity and administrative escalation. (✅ owner 14 advanced §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Intentionalist = intent first, opportunity second.
- Functionalist = circumstances and bureaucratic radicalisation.
- Lowe combined: intent + opportunity + escalation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner. (✅ owner 14 advanced)
**Links:** WH-3015 (5.7 million), WH-3016 (victim groups), WH-3025 (bombing debate for contrast).
**Trap:** Do not treat Holocaust as isolated from war (error 1) or as a spontaneous by-product with no ideological core (error 2).
**Mains hook:** "How should the Holocaust be located within the history of WWII?" Answer: combined reading, ideology + opportunity.
**Back:** *"Intent + opportunity: the combined reading."*

### Flashcard WH-3025 — Strategic bombing: useful, excessive, or both?
**Front:** What are the three views on strategic bombing in Lowe's account?
**Definition (learn):** Critics: area bombing killed huge civilian populations and is morally indefensible. Defenders: in total war it was a grim but necessary instrument. Modified verdict: early bombing was wasteful, but by 1944–45 attacks on oil, transport and armaments became decisively effective. (✅ owner 14 advanced §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Critics: moral indefensibility.
- Defenders: necessity in total war.
- Modified: wasteful early, decisive late (oil, transport, industry).
- Ruhr bombing and later synthetic oil and armaments attacks had major material effects (✅ archives per Lowe).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1944–45 decisive phase. Ruhr named. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3017 (total war), WH-3014 (Japan's surrender), WH-3024 (historiography pattern).
**Trap:** Bombing was neither wholly useless nor wholly clean. Do not give bombing casualty figures (owner caution).
**Mains hook: "Strategic bombing was morally contentious but, in Lowe's verdict, materially decisive once accuracy and targeting improved."**
**Back:** *"Early waste, late decisive: oil, rail, arms."*

### Flashcard WH-3026 — Why the Axis lost (cumulative, not single-cause)
**Front:** What are the cumulative reasons for Axis defeat?
**Definition (learn):** Axis defeat came from cumulative causes: strategic overreach (multi-front war), Hitler's escalations, Allied resource superiority, sea and air power, and self-defeating occupation brutality. (✅ owner 14, ⚠️ compact answer line)
**Breakdown:**
- Strategic overreach: Germany fought Britain, USSR, USA; Japan widened the war.
- Hitler's decisions: Barbarossa and declaration on the USA.
- Resource imbalance: US, Soviet, British/Commonwealth resources.
- Sea and air power.
- Brutal occupation alienated subject peoples.
- Germany failed to defeat Britain before attacking the USSR.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early Axis victories (1939–42); defeat 1942–45. Asian origin 1931 (Manchuria) and 1937 (China full-scale war) (owner §10.4). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3001, WH-3006 (Barbarossa), WH-3007 (Pearl Harbor), WH-3023 (air/sea), WH-3027 (turning points).
**Trap:** Do not say the war was won by one battle or one ally. The Asian war started earlier — say so to avoid a Eurocentric answer.
**Mains hook:** (⚠️ owner) "The Axis lost because early operational brilliance could not compensate for strategic overreach, weaker resources and self-defeating occupation policies."
**Back:** *"Overreach, Resources, Sea-Air, Brutality."*

### Flashcard WH-3027 — Turning points are not final victory
**Front:** How do turning points differ from final victory?
**Definition (learn):** A turning point changes momentum; it is not the end. Battle of Britain checked Germany; Stalingrad shattered eastern momentum; D-Day accelerated the end. (✅ owner 14 advanced §7)
**Breakdown:**
- Battle of Britain → checked Germany's single-front war (1940).
- Midway → Pacific momentum (June 1942).
- El Alamein → Egypt saved; Axis expulsion (Oct 1942).
- Stalingrad → Soviet advance (Feb 1943).
- Battle of the Atlantic → supply (1939–43).
- D-Day → Western front (6 June 1944).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** See WH-3005, WH-3008, WH-3009, WH-3010, WH-3011, WH-3012. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3026, WH-3005, WH-3010, WH-3012.
**Trap:** "Turning point ≠ final victory" (owner trap). Do not write one battle won the war.
**Mains hook:** "Discuss the major turning points" — answer by mechanism for each, not a list (owner §10.1).
**Back:** *"Turning ≠ Winning: each battle does a different job."*

### Flashcard WH-3028 — Consequences of WWII (eight effects)
**Front:** List the eight major consequences of WWII that Lowe identifies.
**Definition (learn):** Human loss; no single Versailles-type peace; social change; nuclear age; end of European dominance; superpowers; decolonisation; UN as League's successor. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- Human loss: enormous military and civilian deaths.
- No Versailles-type peace: separate treaties (WH-3022).
- Social change: displacement, reconstruction, welfare pressures.
- Nuclear age: Hiroshima and Nagasaki (WH-3029).
- End of European dominance: Britain, France, Germany, Italy weakened.
- Superpowers: USA and USSR (→ Cold War, WH-3033).
- Decolonisation: European invincibility myth damaged (WH-3146, WH-3149).
- UN: successor to the League (→ WH-3078).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No casualty totals (owner caution). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3022, WH-3029, WH-3033, WH-3146, WH-3078.
**Trap:** Do not give a total death toll; do not say the war ended colonialism immediately (WH-3185).
**Mains hook:** "Effects on colonial empires and the post-1945 order" — mechanism is delegitimation + European exhaustion.
**Back:** *"Eight effects: loss, peace, society, nuclear, Europe, superpowers, empire, UN."*

### Flashcard WH-3029 — Hiroshima and Nagasaki: the nuclear age
**Front:** What did Hiroshima and Nagasaki introduce to international politics?
**Definition (learn):** The atomic bombings introduced a new kind of deterrent threat and redefined great-power conflict thereafter. (✅ owner 14)
**Breakdown:**
- New deterrent threat.
- Redefines great-power conflict (link to Cold War WH-3035).
- Bombings date: August 1945 (WH-3013).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1945. No casualty figures sourced. (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3013, WH-3014, WH-3035 (deterrence in Cold War).
**Trap:** Do not present the bombs as the single cause of Japan's surrender (WH-3014).
**Mains hook:** The nuclear age is a direct bridge to the Cold War's deterrence logic.
**Back:** *"Bombs made deterrence the new war."*

### Flashcard WH-3030 — Probable WWII Mains questions and answer frame
**Front:** What is the standard four-step answer frame for WWII Mains questions?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Divide the war into phases; (2) explain turning points and why early Axis advantage faded; (3) add the social-moral dimension (Holocaust, bombing, displacement); (4) conclude with superpowers, decolonisation and the UN. (⚠️ owner 14 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: thesis → two turning points or two reasons for defeat → one consequence → verdict.
- 15 marks: thesis → phases → turning points → why Axis lost → effects → verdict.
- 20 marks: all of the above + total war & Holocaust, resistance/collaboration, boundary consequences → verdict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner ⚠️ probable questions: "Why did the Axis win early but lose in 1945?", "Discuss turning points", "How did WWII transform world politics and society?", "Effects on colonial empires and post-1945 order". (⚠️)
**Links:** WH-3026, WH-3027, WH-3028.
**Trap:** Zero direct PYQ routes in owner (2018–2025). Probable questions remain original practice (owner §11).
**Mains hook:** Use the frame as scaffolding; the answer must connect campaigns to consequences.
**Back:** *"Phases, Turns, Moral cost, Post-war order."*


### Flashcard WH-3031 — Naval and air power as decision-makers
**Front:** Why does Lowe treat sea and air power as decision-makers, not background?
**Definition (learn):** The war turned on naval control (convoys, U-boats, Mediterranean supply, D-Day sea-borne landings) and air power (Battle of Britain, carrier dominance, anti-U-boat air, strategic bombing) as decision-makers. (✅ owner 14 advanced §3)
**Breakdown:**
- Sea power: convoys survived; U-boats beaten; Mediterranean and North African supply; D-Day mass movement.
- Air power: Battle of Britain blocked invasion; carrier aircraft in the Pacific; air crippled U-boats; strategic bombing hit oil, transport and armaments.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cards: WH-3005, WH-3008, WH-3011, WH-3023, WH-3025.
**Links:** WH-3011, WH-3023, WH-3025.
**Trap:** Air and sea were not mere support arms in this war (owner exam use).
**Mains hook:** "Assess the role of sea and air power in the Allied victory."
**Back:** *"Sea and sky decided the war."*

### Flashcard WH-3032 — Second World War concept card: "two regional wars fused"
**Front:** Why is the WWII origin described as two regional wars fused by 1941?
**Definition (learn):** The Asian war (Japan: Manchuria 1931, China full-scale war 1937) and the European war (Germany: Poland 1939) were separate and earlier in Asia; they fused into one global war in 1941 (Barbarossa 22 June; Pearl Harbor 7 Dec; Hitler's declaration on the USA). (✅ owner 14 §10.2, §10.4)
**Breakdown:**
- European revisionism: Poland (Sept 1939); Nazi-Soviet Pact (WH-3003).
- Collapse of collective security: Manchuria 1931, Abyssinia 1935, Rhineland 1936, Munich 1938, Prague 1939 (basic/11).
- Asian origin: 1931 Manchuria; 1937 full war in China (basic/12).
- Fusion: 1941.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1931, 1937, 1939, 1941 (all owner). (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** WH-3003, WH-3006, WH-3007, WH-3026.
**Trap:** A Europe-only account is wrong (owner: "say so, or the answer is Eurocentric").
**Mains hook:** "The Second World War was not one war that spread but two regional wars that were fused into one…"
**Back:** *"Asia 1931/37 + Europe 1939 = fused 1941."*

### GROUP — Cold War and International Relations (basic 15 + advanced 15)

### Flashcard WH-3033 — Cold War: organised hostility under the nuclear shadow
**Front:** How does Lowe define the Cold War, and what is the exam line?
**Definition (learn):** A period in which the USA and USSR did not fight each other in open general war, but confronted each other through propaganda, arms competition, alliance-building, economic pressure and limited wars. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Forms of confrontation: propaganda; arms race; alliances; economic pressure; limited (proxy) wars.
- Core exam line: "the Cold War was not peace; it was organised hostility under the shadow of nuclear weapons." (✅ owner 15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Period roughly 1945–1991 (end phase 1985–91, owner timeline). (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3034 (causes), WH-3064 (periodisation), WH-3071 (end).
**Trap:** "Cold War" is not a period of peace. Also not a purely two-power contest (WH-3067, WH-3073).
**Mains hook:** "Define the Cold War as rivalry without direct general war" is the first step in the standard frame.
**Back:** *"No hot war, all hostility."*

### Flashcard WH-3034 — Five causes of the Cold War (Lowe)
**Front:** List Lowe's five causes of the Cold War.
**Definition (learn):** Differences of principle; Soviet security aims; Western suspicion; the atomic factor; and wartime legacy. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Ideology: communist planning and collective ownership vs capitalist liberal-democracy.
- Security: Stalin's protective belt in eastern Europe after vast wartime losses.
- Suspicion: Truman and many Western leaders saw Soviet moves as expansionist.
- Atomic: USA had the bomb first (WH-3035).
- Wartime legacy: delay over second front, occupation issues, postwar planning (WH-3001 link: second front = D-Day 1944).
- Each side read its own acts as defensive and the other's as aggressive (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none additional. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3033, WH-3035, WH-3036 (Yalta/Potsdam), WH-3065 (weighting), WH-3012 (D-Day, second front).
**Trap:** Do not blame one side alone. Lowe's framing is mutual perception.
**Mains hook:** "What caused the Cold War? Assess the relative role of ideology and power politics." Use the weighting in WH-3065.
**Back:** *"Ideology, Security, Suspicion, Atom, Wartime debt."*

### Flashcard WH-3035 — Atomic asymmetry and the Soviet bomb (1949)
**Front:** How did the atomic factor shape Cold War mistrust, and when did the Soviet Union get the bomb?
**Definition (learn):** The USA had the atomic bomb first; mistrust deepened sharply. The Soviet atomic bomb in 1949 began the nuclear arms race in earnest. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Asymmetry → every conventional concession looked like strategic surrender (⚠️ owner analytical line).
- 1949: Soviet bomb → arms race in earnest.
- Hiroshima/Nagasaki's deterrent threat was introduced in WH-3029 (basic 14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1949 (Soviet atomic bomb). (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3034, WH-3029 (nuclear age), WH-3050 (Cuba: nuclear risk), WH-3044 (China 1949).
**Trap:** The Soviet bomb did not end the Cold War or create parity overnight; the arms race "in earnest" began in 1949 — don't date the start of the arms race to 1945 or 1962.
**Mains hook:** Atomic deterrence restrained direct war but intensified the arms competition (advanced/basic 15 §11 mechanism control).
**Back:** *"'49: Russia gets the bomb; the race is on."*

### Flashcard WH-3036 — Yalta and Potsdam (1945): agreement with tension
**Front:** What did the Yalta and Potsdam conferences (1945) agree and leave unresolved?
**Definition (learn):** Wartime allies met at Yalta and Potsdam in 1945; they agreed on some points but tension remained over Poland, Germany and eastern Europe. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Agreed: some points of settlement.
- Unresolved: Poland, Germany, eastern Europe.
- Stalin then established pro-communist governments across eastern Europe; the West saw breach of promise of free elections.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1945. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3034 (wartime legacy), WH-3037 (Truman Doctrine), WH-3040 (Czechoslovakia 1948), WH-3022 (no Versailles-type peace).
**Trap:** Yalta/Potsdam were not a settled peace — the Cold War started from their unresolved disputes.
**Mains hook:** Use Yalta/Potsdam to show that the 1945 settlement began with a deficit of trust.
**Back:** *"1945: agreed in part, tense in all."*

### Flashcard WH-3037 — Truman Doctrine (1947)
**Front:** What did the Truman Doctrine pledge, and in which year?
**Definition (learn):** A 1947 US pledge of support to states resisting communist pressure. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1947.
- Pledge: support for states resisting communist pressure.
- Part of the containment strategy with the Marshall Plan (WH-3038).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1947. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3038 (Marshall Plan), WH-3036 (Yalta/Potsdam), WH-3033.
**Trap:** Truman Doctrine = political-military pledge; Marshall Plan = economic programme. Do not swap.
**Mains hook:** Containment in practice began in 1947; cite it as the first step of escalation (advanced 15 §9).
**Back:** *"Truman: stand by those who resist."*

### Flashcard WH-3038 — Marshall Plan (1947): economic recovery as anti-communist strategy
**Front:** What was the Marshall Plan and what purposes did it serve?
**Definition (learn):** The US economic recovery programme for Europe, using economic recovery as an anti-communist strategy. Lowe notes it had both humanitarian and political purposes. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1947.
- Type: economic programme for Europe.
- Purposes: humanitarian + political.
- Stalin's response: Cominform and tighter control over eastern Europe (WH-3039).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1947. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3037, WH-3039, WH-3042 (NATO).
**Trap:** Marshall aid also served US economic and political interests (⚠️ owner §10.4). Economic power was a Cold War instrument from the outset.
**Mains hook:** The first tool of Cold War economic statecraft.
**Back:** *"Marshall: money for Europe, against communism."*

### Flashcard WH-3039 — Cominform: Stalin's response to Marshall and the Iron Curtain
**Front:** What did Stalin do in response to the US economic and political measures of 1947?
**Definition (learn):** Stalin responded with the Cominform and tighter control over eastern Europe. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Cominform = Communist Information Bureau (⚠️ full name: standard knowledge, not in owner).
- Effect: tighter control over eastern Europe.
- Followed by Czechoslovakia 1948 (WH-3040).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Established 5 October 1947 after an unofficial conference in Poland, September 1947; nine founding parties (✅ Wikipedia): USSR, Bulgaria, Czechoslovakia, Hungary, Poland, Romania, Yugoslavia, France, Italy; Yugoslavia expelled June 1948; dissolved 17 April 1956 (✅ Wikipedia). Owner gives no date.
**Links:** WH-3038, WH-3040, WH-3036.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Cominform (Soviet-led bloc coordination body) with the Comintern (dissolved 1943) — ⚠️ standard knowledge.
**Mains hook:** Economic aid answered by bloc consolidation — mutual reinforcement.
**Back:** *"Cominform: Moscow's answer to Marshall."* ⚠️

### Flashcard WH-3040 — Czechoslovakia (1948) and the Iron Curtain
**Front:** What did the 1948 communist seizure of power in Czechoslovakia do to the European settlement?
**Definition (learn):** Communist seizure of power in Czechoslovakia (1948) hardened the "Iron Curtain" between East and West. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1948.
- Event: communist seizure of power.
- Effect: Iron Curtain hardened.
- Part of 1945–48 spheres of influence enforced, not negotiated (owner §10.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1948. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3036, WH-3039, WH-3041 (Berlin blockade).
**Trap:** Czechoslovakia 1948 ≠ Hungary 1956 (WH-3048) ≠ Prague 1968 (not in owner — do not include without flag).
**Mains hook:** Spheres of influence were enforced, not negotiated, from 1948.
**Back:** *"'48: Czechoslovakia goes communist; the Iron Curtain hardens."

### Flashcard WH-3041 — Berlin blockade and airlift (1948–49)
**Front:** What was the first great Cold War crisis, and how was it answered?
**Definition (learn):** The Berlin blockade (1948–49) and the Western airlift formed the first great Cold War crisis. The blockade was answered by airlift, not by direct land-war escalation. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Years: 1948–49.
- Response: airlift (not land-war escalation).
- Founding rule: both sides would risk confrontation but avoid direct war (owner §10.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1948–49. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3040, WH-3042 (NATO 1949), WH-3049 (Berlin Wall 1961), WH-3065.
**Trap:** Blockade ≠ Wall: 1948–49 blockade vs 1961 Wall. Do not merge them.
**Mains hook:** Berlin's crises show that the Cold War's rule was "confrontation without direct war" — an origin of détente.
**Back:** *"Berlin '48: blockade met with airlift."*

### Flashcard WH-3042 — NATO (1949): collective western defence
**Front:** What was NATO and in which year was it formed?
**Definition (learn):** The North Atlantic Treaty Organisation, formed in 1949 as a collective Western defence arrangement. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1949.
- Nature: collective Western defence.
- Formal alliance bloc → division institutionalised (owner §10.5).
- Response to NATO: Warsaw Pact (1955, WH-3047).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Treaty signed 4 April 1949 (✅ Wikipedia). Twelve original signatories (✅ Wikipedia; count tallied from listed names): France, UK, Belgium, Netherlands, Luxembourg (Western Union), USA, Canada, Portugal, Italy, Norway, Denmark, Iceland.
**Links:** WH-3047 (Warsaw Pact), WH-3041 (Berlin), WH-3046 (1955 Austrian treaty), WH-3069 (SEATO, 1954).
**Trap:** NATO (1949) vs Warsaw Pact (1955) — both bloc alliances, different years and sides; SEATO (1954) is a non-European Asian bloc.
**Mains hook:** "Division institutionalised": NATO formed in 1949 for the West; the East answered in 1955.
**Back:** *"1949: West bands together — NATO."*

### Flashcard WH-3043 — Korean War (1950–53) — Cold War turned into proxy armed conflict
**Front:** How did the Korean War make the Cold War militarised, and where did the frontier end?
**Definition (learn):** North Korea invaded South Korea in June 1950; the UN, in the absence of the Soviet delegation, authorised intervention; American troops formed the bulk. UN forces moved north; Chinese forces entered; the frontier remained near the 38th parallel by 1953. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- June 1950: North Korea invasion.
- UN authorisation during Soviet absence (cross-link WH-3083 UN Security Council).
- American troops: bulk of UN force.
- UN forces moved north → Chinese entry.
- 1953: frontier near 38th parallel.
- Lowe: entrenched bloc politics in Asia as well as Europe (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** June 1950; 1953; 38th parallel. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3044 (China 1949), WH-3083 (UN Security Council), WH-3069 (SEATO), WH-3068 (non-alignment).
**Trap:** Korea was neither a pure civil war nor a pure proxy war (owner §10.9). Ended roughly where it began.
**Mains hook:** Korea is the best "non-European theatre" for the Cold War answer (basic 15 §9 frame).
**Back:** *"'50–'53: war to the 38th again."*

### Flashcard WH-3044 — Communist victory in China (1949)
**Front:** What did communist victory in China (1949) mean for American fears?
**Definition (learn):** Communist victory in China (1949) widened American fears of a global communist advance. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1949.
- American response: fear of a global advance → containment expanded to Asia (Korea, WH-3043).
- Full revolutionary chronology in WH-3113 (China chain).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1949. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3043, WH-3035 (Soviet bomb 1949), WH-3113 (Chinese Revolution chain), WH-3120 (1 Oct 1949).
**Trap:** Communist victory 1949 ≠ Sino-Soviet alliance (1950 treaty) — separate events.
**Mains hook:** 1949 was a double year: the Soviet bomb and communist China.
**Back:** *"1949: China turns red; fear turns global."*

### Flashcard WH-3045 — Stalin's death and the limited thaw (1953–56)
**Front:** What were the signs of the post-1953 thaw, and why was it partial?
**Definition (learn):** After Stalin's death the thaw included the Korean armistice, the Austrian State Treaty, easing with Yugoslavia and Khrushchev's "peaceful coexistence". The thaw remained partial (Warsaw Pact 1955; Hungary 1956; Berlin Wall 1961; Cuba 1962). (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Signs: Korean armistice; Austrian State Treaty (1955 in timeline); Yugoslavia; "peaceful coexistence".
- Limits: WH-3047 (Warsaw Pact), WH-3048 (Hungary), WH-3049 (Wall), WH-3050 (Cuba).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Austrian State Treaty and Warsaw Pact both 1955 (owner timeline). (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3046 (1955 Austrian treaty card below), WH-3047, WH-3048, WH-3049, WH-3050.
**Trap:** The thaw did not end Cold War confrontation; advanced 15 §2 "thaw's limits".
**Mains hook:** "How far was the post-1953 thaw real?" — answer by listing the thaw signals and their limits.
**Back:** *"Stalin dies, thaw comes, walls stay."*

### Flashcard WH-3046 — Austrian State Treaty (1955)
**Front:** What was the Austrian State Treaty's place in the Cold War?
**Definition (learn):** The Austrian State Treaty of 1955 is listed by Lowe among the post-1953 thaw signals. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1955.
- Signal of thaw (not a German settlement).
- Contrast: Berlin remained a flashpoint (WH-3049).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1955. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3045 (thaw), WH-3047 (Warsaw Pact, same year).
**Trap:** Austria was treated as a settlement; Germany was not — "Berlin remained a flashpoint".
**Mains hook:** Use Austria vs Germany to show a settled and an unsettled division.
**Back:** *"Austria freed in '55; Germany divided on."*

### Flashcard WH-3047 — Warsaw Pact (1955): Eastern bloc alliance
**Front:** What was the Warsaw Pact and in which year was it created?
**Definition (learn):** The Warsaw Pact was created in 1955 as the Soviet-led Eastern military alliance, the counterpart to NATO (WH-3042). (✅ owner 15 for 1955; Soviet-led nature is ⚠️ standard knowledge.)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1955.
- Side: Eastern bloc (USSR).
- Institutionalised the division of Europe with NATO (owner §10.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Signed 14 May 1955 in Warsaw (✅ Wikipedia). Eight founding members (✅ Wikipedia): USSR, Albania, Bulgaria, Czechoslovakia, East Germany, Hungary, Poland, Romania. Declared disbanded 25 February 1991; treaty formally ended 1 July 1991 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-3042 (NATO), WH-3046 (Austrian treaty), WH-3048 (Hungary 1956 under the Pact).
**Trap:** Warsaw Pact (1955) ≠ NATO (1949). Do not date the Pact to 1949.
**Mains hook:** "Division institutionalised" — Warsaw Pact (1955) and NATO (1949).
**Back:** *"'55 Warsaw answers NATO."*

### Flashcard WH-3048 — Hungarian rising (1956) crushed by USSR
**Front:** What happened in Hungary in 1956 and what did it show?
**Definition (learn):** The USSR crushed the Hungarian rising in 1956. It showed that the West would not contest the Soviet sphere militarily. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1956.
- Event: Soviet crushing of rising.
- Inference: Soviet sphere not contested militarily by West.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1956. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3047 (Warsaw Pact), WH-3052 (Suez 1956, same year), WH-3040 (Czechoslovakia 1948).
**Trap:** Hungary 1956 (crushed, Soviet sphere) vs Suez 1956 (West/Egypt crisis) — same year, different theatres.
**Mains hook:** Hungary and Suez in 1956 show the two superpowers' spheres: Soviet force in East, Western retreat at Suez.
**Back:** *"'56: Hungary crushed, West silent."*

### Flashcard WH-3049 — Berlin Wall (1961): division made physical
**Front:** Why was the Berlin Wall built in 1961, and what did it mean?
**Definition (learn):** The Berlin Wall (1961) was built to stop movement; division was stabilised by making it physical. It froze one aspect of the German crisis but symbolised continuing division. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1961.
- Purpose: stop movement (from East to West).
- Effect: division stabilised.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1961. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3041 (Berlin blockade), WH-3050 (Cuba 1962), WH-3036 (Potsdam).
**Trap:** The Wall did NOT solve the German question (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Berlin "was about spheres" (advanced 15 §3d summary).
**Back:** *"Wall '61: stops the flow, freezes the split."*

### Flashcard WH-3050 — Cuban missile crisis (1962): nuclear risk and restraint
**Front:** What was the Cuban missile crisis and what did it establish?
**Definition (learn):** The 1962 crisis brought the world close to nuclear war. Lowe presents it as deterrence logic at its sharpest: Soviet missiles, American blockade, UN mediation pressure and last-minute compromise. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1962.
- Sequence: Soviet missiles → US blockade → UN mediation pressure → last-minute compromise (advanced 15 §3c).
- Established that nuclear risk itself forces restraint → origin of détente (owner §10.5).
- Follow-ups: hotline and test-ban (advanced 15 §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1962. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3035 (Soviet bomb), WH-3049 (Wall, 1961), WH-3058 (détente), WH-3064 (periodisation), WH-3065 (crisis summary).
**Trap:** Cuba ≠ Berlin (1961 Wall). Cuba showed western-hemisphere rivalry.
**Mains hook:** "Discuss the significance of the Cuban missiles crisis" — answer: deterrence + hotline + origin of détente.
**Back:** *"'62 Cuba: nuclear brink, then compromise."*

### Flashcard WH-3051 — Vietnam, Chile and Afghanistan as Cold War theatres
**Front:** What do Vietnam, Chile and Afghanistan illustrate in Lowe's account?
**Definition (learn):** Vietnam shows the limits of American military power against communist movements; Chile shows a shorter, fragile Marxist experiment (intervention inside politics, coups and ideological fear); Afghanistan (1979) revived East-West tension and showed détente was reversible. (✅ owner 15 §5b, §3d, §10.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Vietnam: limits of US power.
- Chile: Marxist experiment; internal politics, coups, ideological fear (⚠️ names/dates not in owner: Salvador Allende, president 3 Nov 1970–11 Sept 1973, Popular Unity coalition, Socialist Party (✅ Wikipedia; coup 11 Sept 1973).
- Afghanistan: Soviet invasion 1979.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Afghanistan 1979 (✅ owner 15). Vietnam and Chile dates not in owner (⚠️).
**Links:** WH-3055 (Camp David), WH-3069 (SEATO), WH-3054 (Yom Kippur), WH-3070 (détente limits), WH-3065 (crisis summary).
**Trap:** Use these as Cold War framework illustrations, not as region-specific substitutes (owner §5).
**Mains hook:** Cold War penetrated domestic politics in the western hemisphere — Chile.
**Back:** *"Vietnam limits power, Chile limits ballots, Afghanistan limits détente."*

### Flashcard WH-3052 — Suez War (1956): decolonisation crisis + Cold War episode
**Front:** What did the 1956 Suez War show about European power and the superpowers?
**Definition (learn):** Nasser's nationalisation of the canal provoked a British-French-Israeli attack; US/USSR pressure exposed declining European imperial power. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: Nasser's canal nationalisation.
- Actors: British-French-Israeli attack.
- Pressure: US and USSR.
- Triple nature: decolonisation crisis, UN test, Cold War episode (advanced 15 §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1956. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3048 (Hungary 1956), WH-3053 (Six-Day War), WH-3066 (proxy-label), WH-3083 (UN test).
**Trap:** Suez ≠ Six-Day War (1967). Suez: British-French-Israeli attack. Six-Day: Israel's occupation.
**Mains hook:** Local nationalism (Nasser) drove the event; superpowers entered (WH-3066).
**Back:** *"Suez '56: canal taken, empire exposed."*

### Flashcard WH-3053 — Six-Day War (1967): territorial transformation
**Front:** What did the 1967 Six-Day War change?
**Definition (learn):** Israel occupied the Sinai, Gaza, West Bank, East Jerusalem and the Golan Heights, transforming the territorial question. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1967.
- Territories: Sinai; Gaza; West Bank; East Jerusalem; Golan Heights.
- Effect: territorial question transformed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1967. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3052 (Suez), WH-3054 (Yom Kippur 1973), WH-3055 (Camp David), WH-3057 (1948–49).
**Trap:** Six-Day War (1967) ≠ Yom Kippur (1973). Keep in sequence (owner §8).
**Mains hook:** "Why can the Arab-Israeli conflict not be explained as a Cold War proxy struggle alone?" (WH-3066).
**Back:** *"'67: five areas taken — Sinai, Gaza, West Bank, East Jerusalem, Golan."*

### Flashcard WH-3054 — Yom Kippur War (1973): Arab leverage and oil politics
**Front:** What did the 1973 Yom Kippur War restore, and what did it widen?
**Definition (learn):** The Egyptian and Syrian attack restored some Arab leverage; superpower tension and oil politics widened the crisis. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1973.
- Attackers: Egypt and Syria.
- Effect: restored some Arab leverage.
- Widened by superpower tension and oil politics (owner §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1973. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3053, WH-3055 (Camp David), WH-3066 (proxy-label).
**Trap:** Yom Kippur (1973) is not a 1967 war; oil politics is a separate economic layer.
**Mains hook:** Oil and strategic routes are an autonomous layer (advanced 15 §4).
**Back:** *"'73: Egypt and Syria strike back."*

### Flashcard WH-3055 — Camp David (1978–79): Egypt–Israel peace, Palestinian question unresolved
**Front:** What did Camp David (1978–79) achieve and leave unresolved?
**Definition (learn):** Egypt and Israel made peace, but the Palestinian question remained unresolved. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Years: 1978–79.
- Achievement: Egypt–Israel peace.
- Unresolved: Palestinian question (occupation, refugees, statehood).
- Egypt-Israel peace narrowed interstate war but did not solve statehood (advanced 15 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1978–79. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3053, WH-3054, WH-3066.
**Trap:** Camp David ≠ full Arab-Israeli settlement.
**Mains hook:** Peace treaty between states ≠ solution of the Palestinian question.
**Back:** *"Camp David: Egypt signs, Palestine waits."*

### Flashcard WH-3056 — Iran–Iraq War (1980–88) and Gulf War (1990–91)
**Front:** What do the Iran–Iraq War and the Gulf War show about the Middle East after the classic Arab–Israeli cycle?
**Definition (learn):** Regional rivalry, oil security and outside intervention outlasted the classic Arab-Israeli war cycle (owner §6). Iran–Iraq 1980–88; Gulf War 1990–91.
**Breakdown:**
- Iran–Iraq War: 1980–88.
- Gulf War: 1990–91.
- Drivers: regional rivalry, oil security, outside intervention.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1980–88; 1990–91. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3054, WH-3066.
**Trap:** Gulf War (1990–91) is not an Arab–Israeli war; do not place it in the 1967–73 cycle.
**Mains hook:** Middle East wars were not all proxy conflicts (WH-3066).
**Back:** *"'80–88 Iran-Iraq, '90–91 Gulf: oil and rivals."*

### Flashcard WH-3057 — Arab–Israeli War (1948–49): Israel survives, refugee problem
**Front:** What did the 1948–49 Arab–Israeli War establish?
**Definition (learn):** Israel survived; the Palestinian refugee problem and Arab-Israeli hostility became enduring regional issues. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Years: 1948–49.
- Israel survived.
- Palestinian refugee problem: enduring.
- Start of cycle: 1948–49 → Suez 1956 → 1967 → 1973 → Camp David 1978–79 → Lebanon → Iran–Iraq → Gulf War.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1948–49. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3053, WH-3066, WH-3055.
**Trap:** Do not treat Middle East conflict as "one unchanging war" (owner trap; advanced 15 §8).
**Mains hook:** "Assess the interaction of regional causes and superpower rivalry in the Middle East after 1948."
**Back:** *"'48: Israel survives, refugees remain."*

### Flashcard WH-3058 — Détente (1970s): why it happened
**Front:** Why did détente emerge in the 1970s, and what are the markers?
**Definition (learn):** Détente emerged because both sides feared nuclear catastrophe and faced strategic and economic strain. Markers: SALT I (1972), summits, Helsinki Agreement (1975), improvement in US-China relations. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Reasons: fear of nuclear catastrophe; Soviet economic strain; American exhaustion after Vietnam; West European desire for stability; triangular pressure from China-US rapprochement (advanced 15 §5).
- Markers: SALT I (1972); summits; Helsinki (1975); US–China rapprochement (1972 opening).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** SALT I 1972; Helsinki 1975. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3059 (Helsinki), WH-3060 (INF), WH-3050 (Cuba), WH-3070 (limits).
**Trap:** Détente is not peace or ended rivalry (WH-3070).
**Mains hook:** "Explain the causes, course and consequences of détente." Frame: risk management (owner §10.2).
**Back:** *"Détente: fear + strain = talks, not peace."*

### Flashcard WH-3059 — Helsinki Agreement (1975): norm-building, not settlement
**Front:** What was the Helsinki Agreement of 1975, and what did it not do?
**Definition (learn):** A 1975 East–West agreement that marked a détente marker and norm-building; it was an agreement, not a political settlement of the German or European question. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1975.
- Type: norm-building agreement.
- Not: a political settlement of Germany or Europe (owner §10.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1975. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3058 (détente), WH-3070 (limits).
**Trap:** Helsinki (1975) ≠ SALT I (1972) — arms control vs norm-building.
**Mains hook:** Helsinki shows détente's soft-norm side; SALT I shows its hard-arms side.
**Back:** *"Helsinki '75: norms, not a settlement."*

### Flashcard WH-3060 — INF Treaty (1987): Gorbachev and Reagan
**Front:** What did the 1987 INF Treaty do?
**Definition (learn):** The INF Treaty (1987), signed by Gorbachev with Reagan, marked destruction of a class of nuclear weapons. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1987.
- Signatories: Gorbachev and Reagan.
- Effect: destruction of a class of nuclear weapons (owner §8).
- Stage of Gorbachev's arms reduction (WH-3071).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1987. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3058 (INF earlier in detente line), WH-3071 (end), WH-3070 (SS-20).
**Trap:** INF (1987) ≠ SALT I (1972) ≠ SALT II (not ratified, WH-3070).
**Mains hook:** Gorbachev's arms reduction is one of four converging causes of the Cold War's end.
**Back:** *"'87 INF: a whole class of missiles gone."*

### Flashcard WH-3061 — Historiography: orthodox, revisionist, post-revisionist
**Front:** What are the three schools of Cold War blame in Lowe's account?
**Definition (learn):** Orthodox (Stalin's expansionism; containment defensive); revisionist (Soviet moves largely defensive; US atomic and economic power aggravated conflict); post-revisionist (both sides contributed; each read its own acts as defensive and the other's as aggressive). (✅ owner 15 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Orthodox: George Kennan central (✅ owner 15).
- Revisionist: William Appleman Williams — US economic hegemony and atomic leverage (✅ owner 15).
- Post-revisionist: wider archival access; mixed responsibility.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** no dates given. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3062 (Williams), WH-3063 (post-revisionist), WH-3034 (causes).
**Trap:** "Revisionists say Stalin was innocent" is a trap — they mainly stress Soviet security fears and US provocation (advanced 15 §8).
**Mains hook:** "Which side was more responsible for the Cold War?" — answer with the post-revisionist synthesis (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Kennan blames Moscow; Williams blames Washington; post-revisionists blame the mirror."*

### Flashcard WH-3062 — George Kennan and containment (orthodox)
**Front:** Who is central to the orthodox interpretation and what is the containment argument?
**Definition (learn):** George Kennan is the central figure of the orthodox line in Lowe's account; orthodox historians hold that Stalin's expansionism caused the Cold War, and containment was defensive. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Figure: George Kennan.
- Claim: Stalin's expansionism; containment defensive.
- Contrast: revisionists (WH-3062 Williams).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none in owner. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3061, WH-3037 (Truman Doctrine), WH-3038 (Marshall).
**Trap:** Kennan (orthodox) is not Williams (revisionist). Keep the schools apart.
**Mains hook:** Containment is the orthodox answer's core instrument.
**Back:** *"Kennan: containment is defence."*

### Flashcard WH-3063 — William Appleman Williams and revisionism
**Front:** Who represents the revisionist line and what does it stress?
**Definition (learn):** William Appleman Williams represents the revisionist line that stresses US economic hegemony and atomic leverage as aggravating the conflict. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Figure: William Appleman Williams.
- Stresses: US economic hegemony; atomic leverage.
- Soviet moves largely defensive (in revisionist view).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none in owner. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3061, WH-3038 (Marshall), WH-3035 (atomic asymmetry).
**Trap:** Williams ≠ "Stalin innocent" in strict sense; revisionists mainly stress Soviet security fears and US provocation.
**Mains hook:** Revisionism links the Marshall Plan and atomic bomb to Cold War escalation.
**Back:** *"Williams: dollars and bombs."*

### Flashcard WH-3064 — Cold War periodisation (phases)
**Front:** How should the Cold War be periodised for a Mains answer?
**Definition (learn):** Six phases: 1945–49 settlement disputes to Berlin; 1950–53 Korea; 1953–62 thaw and limits; 1970s détente; 1979–85 Afghanistan and "second Cold War"; 1985–91 Gorbachev to collapse. (✅ owner 15 advanced §2)
**Breakdown:**
- 1945–49: settlement disputes; Truman; Marshall; Berlin.
- 1950–53: Korea militarises.
- 1953–62: thaw with Berlin and Cuba.
- 1970s: détente, SALT, Helsinki, US-China.
- 1979–85: Afghanistan; missile tension.
- 1985–91: Gorbachev; INF; Afghanistan withdrawal; collapse.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner timeline. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3033, WH-3065, WH-3071.
**Trap:** The Cold War is not one frozen condition (owner ⚠️ line).
**Mains hook:** "How did the Cold War develop between 1945 and 1962?"
**Back:** *"Settle, Korea, Thaw, Détente, Second, Collapse."*

### Flashcard WH-3065 — Crises as bipolarity mechanisms (Berlin, Korea, Cuba, Vietnam, Chile)
