**Front:** What does each Cold War crisis illustrate about bipolarity?
**Definition (learn):** Berlin — spheres of influence; Korea — containment; Cuba — deterrence; Vietnam — overreach (limits of US power); Chile — intervention inside Third World political field. (✅ owner 15 advanced §3; ⚠️ synthesis)
**Breakdown:**
- Berlin: occupation arrangements, economic policy, symbolic prestige.
- Korea: proxy war under UN cover; risk of escalation to China.
- Cuba: deterrence; missiles, blockade, UN mediation, compromise.
- Vietnam: limits of US power.
- Chile: internal politics, coups, ideological fear.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates per cards WH-3041, WH-3043, WH-3050, WH-3051.
**Links:** WH-3041, WH-3043, WH-3050, WH-3051.
**Trap:** Do not use these crises as region-specific substitutes (owner §5).
**Mains hook:** "Discuss a crisis" — answer by what it revealed about bipolar systems (owner §10.1).
**Back:** *"Berlin: spheres. Korea: containment. Cuba: deterrence. Vietnam: overreach. Chile: intervention."*

### Flashcard WH-3066 — The proxy-war label is insufficient (Middle East)
**Front:** Why is the Middle East not explicable as a US–USSR proxy war alone?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe shows local actors had autonomous causes: Arab nationalism and decolonisation; Israeli security and Palestinian self-determination; interstate Arab politics; oil and strategic routes. Superpower involvement is a modifier, not the cause. (✅ owner 15 §4, §6, §10.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Cold War rivalry: arms, diplomacy, crisis management.
- Decolonisation/Arab nationalism: Suez; Nasserism.
- Competing national projects: Israeli security and Palestinian self-determination.
- Oil and strategic routes: Suez Canal, Gulf security, 1973 oil shock.
- Lowe's sequence 1948–49, Suez, 1967, 1973, Camp David, Lebanon, Iran–Iraq, 1990–91 Gulf.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3052, WH-3053, WH-3054, WH-3055, WH-3056, WH-3057.
**Trap:** Do not write that "ancient hatred" made conflict inevitable, nor that superpowers manufactured it (advanced 15 §4).
**Mains hook:** "Why can the Arab-Israeli conflict not be explained as a Cold War proxy struggle alone?" Answer: combined reading — post-imperial state formation, nationalism, security dilemmas, territory and external intervention.
**Back:** *"Superpowers amplify; locals originate."*

### Flashcard WH-3067 — Communist bloc was not one actor: the Sino-Soviet split
**Front:** What does the Sino-Soviet split show about the "communist bloc"?
**Definition (learn):** The bloc's two largest powers quarrelled openly; China accused the USSR of revisionism; the rift was territorial as well as doctrinal; communist states fought each other (China–Vietnam, Feb 1979). (✅ owner 15 §10.6A; full chain in owner 17 §8.7A — see WH-3136 to WH-3140)
**Breakdown:**
- 1950 Sino-Soviet treaty of mutual assistance and friendship → deterioration "steadily after 1956".
- China: "revisionism" by Khrushchev; rejected "peaceful coexistence" and non-violent path.
- USSR cut economic aid to China in retaliation.
- Territory: China demanded return of areas Russia had taken in the 19th century north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang.
- Feb 1979: China attacked Vietnam after Vietnam's invasion of Kampuchea removed Pol Pot; China withdrew after three weeks. Vietnam supported Russia.
- Both Russia and China courted Washington by end of 1970s.
- Formal reconciliation: five-year trade agreements July 1985; Gorbachev visited Beijing May 1989; Vietnam withdrew from Kampuchea same year.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1950; after 1956; Feb 1979; July 1985; May 1989. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3072 (Cold War ending), WH-3058 (1972 US–China opening), WH-3136 (Sino-Soviet split).
**Trap:** "The communist bloc acted as one" is wrong. "Bipolarity described the distribution of weapons, not the distribution of loyalty." (⚠️ owner formulation)
**Mains hook:** Use the Sino-Soviet split to explain why US–China opening (1972) was possible.
**Back:** *"Two reds, one quarrel: 1950 pact, 1956 rift, 1979 war."*

### Flashcard WH-3068 — Non-alignment as agency, not indecision (Cold War)
**Front:** Why did many newly independent states refuse bloc alignment, and what did it offer them?
**Definition (learn):** Many new states after WWII wanted to remain neutral or non-aligned; Non-alignment was a positive strategy of sovereignty; it gave bargaining power (aid, arms and diplomatic support from both blocs) but also exposed them to pressure from both. (✅ owner 15 §10.6; ⚠️ analytical)
**Breakdown:**
- Lowe: neutral/non-aligned states in communism–capitalism struggle.
- Asia: Korean War and SEATO showed many preferred to stay uncommitted (WH-3069).
- Bargaining power: aid, arms and diplomatic support from both.
- Exposure: pressure and destabilisation.
- India's NAM belongs to Polity cross-link (owner §7, §10.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** no dates. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3069 (SEATO), WH-3043 (Korea), WH-3140 (China and the bloc), WH-3150 (decolonised states).
**Trap:** Do not present NAM content in full here. Mention as India's answer to bipolar pressure only.
**Mains hook:** "The Cold War and the decolonised world": local agency and non-alignment (owner §10.1).
**Back:** *"Non-aligned: neither pawn nor bystander."*

### Flashcard WH-3069 — SEATO (Southeast Asia Treaty Organisation)
**Front:** What was SEATO and when was it set up?
**Definition (learn):** SEATO was the Southeast Asia Treaty Organisation, an Asian collective-defence alliance that Lowe cites as showing that many states in Asia wanted to stay uncommitted. (✅ owner 15 for the cross-reference; year and members ⚠️ — see Links.)
**Breakdown:**
- Type: collective defence alliance in Asia (Western-led).
- Lowe's use: Korea and SEATO showed states preferred not to enter bloc politics (owner §7).
- Treaty signed in Manila 8 September 1954; organisation formally established 19 February 1955 (Bangkok); dissolved 30 June 1977 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Eight member states (✅ Wikipedia): Australia, France, New Zealand, Pakistan, Philippines, Thailand, UK, USA.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Manila Pact 8 Sept 1954; organisation 19 Feb 1955; dissolved 30 June 1977 (✅ Wikipedia). Owner gives no date.
**Links:** WH-3068 (non-alignment), WH-3043 (Korea), WH-3042 (NATO for Europe).
**Trap:** SEATO ≠ ASEAN (1967, ⚠️ standard date). Do not confuse SEATO's 1954 defence treaty with the regional ASEAN grouping.
**Mains hook:** SEATO shows the Western bloc's Asian arm — and why many Asian states avoided it.
**Back:** *"SEATO: Asia's NATO-lite, in '54." ⚠️*

### Flashcard WH-3070 — Détente's limits: SS-20, SALT II, Afghanistan
**Front:** Why did détente not become peace?
**Definition (learn):** Rivalry continued in new theatres: SS-20 deployment versus NATO missile responses (Pershing/Cruise), failure of SALT II ratification, Soviet invasion of Afghanistan (1979), and persistent mutual suspicion. (✅ owner 15 advanced §5, basic §7)
**Breakdown:**
- SS-20 vs Pershing/Cruise (NATO response).
- SALT II ratification failed (owner).
- Afghanistan 1979 revived tension.
- Helsinki was an agreement, not a settlement (WH-3059).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1979 (Afghanistan). SALT II dates not given in owner (⚠️ standard 1979 signing; not verified).
**Links:** WH-3058, WH-3059, WH-3051 (Afghanistan), WH-3060 (INF).
**Trap:** Détente ≠ friendship; it managed rivalry.
**Mains hook:** "Explain the causes and consequences of détente" — answer with markers and limits.
**Back:** *"SS-20, SALT II stalled, Afghanistan: détente cracks."*

### Flashcard WH-3071 — End of the Cold War: four converging causes
**Front:** What are the four converging causes of the Cold War's end, and what was the dating?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Soviet economic burden (arms and bloc maintenance too costly); (2) Gorbachev's diplomacy (INF 1987; withdrawal from Afghanistan; readiness to negotiate); (3) collapse of legitimacy (popular opposition in eastern Europe); (4) no repeat of old-style Soviet repression (no Hungary or Czechoslovakia-scale response). Between 1988 and 1991 communist systems collapsed across eastern Europe and then in the USSR. (✅ owner 15 advanced §6; basic §7, §10.7)
**Breakdown:**
- Gorbachev pushed arms reduction; signed INF (1987); withdrew from Afghanistan; did not use force in eastern Europe.
- 1988–91 collapse in eastern Europe and USSR.
- Deeper economic causes → owned by basic 21 §2 (⚠️ cross-link).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1985–91 (Gorbachev period); 1987 INF; 1988–91 collapse. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3060 (INF), WH-3067 (Sino-Soviet reconciliation 1989), WH-3072 (traps).
**Trap:** Do not say "Gorbachev alone ended it"; do not say "peace broke out" — new nationalist conflicts emerged (advanced 15 §6).
**Mains hook:** "Why did the Cold War end?" — multi-causal; "a reforming Soviet leadership ... declined to use force" (owner §10.8).
**Back:** *"Money, Gorbachev, Legitimacy, No tanks: four, none alone."*

### Flashcard WH-3072 — Cold War: common traps (summit, détente, Gorbachev, peace)
**Front:** What are the standard traps about the Cold War's end and détente?
**Definition (learn):** (a) "Détente ended the Cold War permanently in the 1970s" — wrong; relaxed tension without ending rivalry. (b) "One summit solved everything" — wrong; longer structural change. (c) "Gorbachev alone ended it" — wrong; structural exhaustion and legitimacy collapse mattered. (d) "Peace broke out after 1989" — wrong; nationalist conflicts emerged. (✅ owner 15 §8, §6)
**Breakdown:**
- Trap 1: détente ≠ end.
- Trap 2: one summit ≠ end.
- Trap 3: Gorbachev alone ≠ end.
- Trap 4: post-1989 peace ≠ end of conflict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3058, WH-3071.
**Trap:** As above.
**Mains hook:** Use the traps as anti-patterns in a conclusion.
**Back:** *"Thaw ≠ peace; summit ≠ end; Gorbachev ≠ alone."*

### Flashcard WH-3073 — Cold War was not just Europe or US–USSR
**Front:** Why is the Cold War not only a US–USSR contest in Europe?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe shows Asia (Korea, China), Latin America (Cuba, Chile), Vietnam, Afghanistan, the Middle East and the decolonising world as major theatres of Cold War competition. (✅ owner 15 §8 trap)
**Breakdown:**
- Asia: Korea; China; Vietnam.
- Latin America: Cuba; Chile.
- Middle East: Suez, Arab–Israeli.
- Africa: Angola and Congo (per owner §11 link to basic 18 §7.6A — WH-3150, WH-3156).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** see individual cards.
**Links:** WH-3043, WH-3050, WH-3051, WH-3066, WH-3150.
**Trap:** Europe-only narrative is wrong (owner §8).
**Mains hook:** "Was it only a European conflict?" — "European in origin, global in operation."
**Back:** *"Global theatres, not one European chessboard."*

### Flashcard WH-3074 — Cuban crisis follow-ups: hotline and test-ban
**Front:** What follow-up measures did the 1962 Cuban crisis produce?
**Definition (learn):** The Cuban missiles crisis produced hotline and test-ban follow-up (advanced 15 §8). It established that nuclear risk itself would force restraint. (✅ owner 15)
**Breakdown:**
- Hotline (US–USSR communication line).
- Test-ban follow-up.
- Origin of détente (owner §10.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1962 (cross-ref). Hotline and test-ban dates not given in owner (⚠️).
**Links:** WH-3050, WH-3058, WH-3065.
**Trap:** Do not claim the 1963 Partial Test Ban Treaty date without verification — ⚠️ not in owner.
**Mains hook:** Crises can produce institutionalised restraint; Cuba is the origin of détente.
**Back:** *"Cuba → hotline → test-ban."*

### Flashcard WH-3075 — Second Cold War (1979–85)
**Front:** What is the "second Cold War" in Lowe's periodisation?
**Definition (learn):** The 1979–85 renewed confrontation after Afghanistan and missile tension, when détente was reversed. (✅ owner 15 advanced timeline)
**Breakdown:**
- 1979: Soviet invasion of Afghanistan.
- SS-20 and NATO missile responses.
- Ended with Gorbachev's reforms (1985–91).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1979–85. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3051, WH-3064, WH-3070, WH-3071.
**Trap:** Second Cold War is a phase label, not a separate war.
**Mains hook:** "How and why did the Cold War come to an end?" — the second Cold War is the bridge to Gorbachev.
**Back:** *"'79–'85: renewed chill before the thaw."*

### Flashcard WH-3076 — Cold War Mains frame and probable questions
**Front:** What is the standard Mains frame and what are the probable questions?
**Definition (learn):** Six-step frame: (1) define as rivalry without direct general war; (2) causes via ideology, security and mistrust; (3) escalation (Truman Doctrine, Marshall Plan, Berlin, NATO, Korea); (4) thaw, Cuba, détente and collapse; (5) one non-European theatre (Korea, Vietnam or Middle East); (6) structure without erasing local causes. (⚠️ owner §9)
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: thesis → two causes or two crises → one qualification → verdict.
- 15 marks: thesis → causes → escalation 1945–53 → crisis phase → détente → verdict.
- 20 marks: all + non-European theatre with local agency + multi-causal end.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Probable Q's (⚠️): causes; 1945–62 development; Cuban crisis; détente; Arab–Israeli not proxy alone; blame; thaw; end.
**Links:** WH-3033, WH-3064, WH-3065, WH-3066, WH-3071.
**Trap:** Zero direct topic-only PYQ routes 2018–2025 (owner §11).
**Mains hook:** Use the frame; do not write one-sided blame.
**Back:** *"Define, cause, escalate, thaw, detente, end, theatre, verdict."*

### Flashcard WH-3077 — Cold War concept: "organised hostility" and the security dilemma
**Front:** What is the standard qualified thesis for the origins of the Cold War?
**Definition (learn):** The Cold War arose from a security dilemma between two powers whose defensive measures were structurally indistinguishable from offensive ones — ideology determined how each read the other's moves. (⚠️ owner §10.2 thesis)
**Breakdown:**
- Soviet defensive expansion → Western containment → confirmed Soviet suspicion.
- Atomic asymmetry made each step feel irreversible.
- Ideology made it non-negotiable and global (⚠️ owner verdict: "Power politics created the confrontation; ideology made it non-negotiable and global").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none additional. (✅ owner 15)
**Links:** WH-3033, WH-3034, WH-3035, WH-3061.
**Trap:** Do not give a blame verdict (owner §10.1).
**Mains hook:** "What caused the Cold War? Assess the relative role of ideology and power politics." (⚠️ owner verdict)
**Back:** *"Security dilemma + ideology = non-negotiable."*

### GROUP — United Nations and Global Governance (basic 16 + advanced 16)

### Flashcard WH-3078 — UN as successor to the League: origin and design lesson
**Front:** How does Lowe present the UN in relation to the League of Nations, and what design lesson did the founders draw?
**Definition (learn):** The UN is the post-1945 successor to the League; its founders wanted to remove the League's weaknesses and build an organisation better equipped to preserve peace. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- League defects and UN responses (owner §9.4): USA never joined → UN wider membership; tied to Versailles → founded on own Charter aims; unanimity paralysis → more decisive procedures (veto still blocks); no League army → Security Council can authorise enforcement (no permanent army); weak social remit → far more economic/social work and individual human rights.
- Two layers for exam use: historical origin after WWII; institutional structure and mandate (GS-II).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3079 (Dumbarton Oaks/San Francisco), WH-3089 (UN vs League comparison), WH-3028 (WWII consequence: UN as successor), basic/11 (League record).
**Trap:** The UN is not simply a "League with a new name": the veto and absence of a standing army survived (WH-3089).
**Mains hook:** "How far has the UN been more successful than the League?" — answer by design improvement plus persisting defect (⚠️ owner §9.2).
**Back:** *"UN = League fixed in scope, not in enforcement."*

### Flashcard WH-3079 — Dumbarton Oaks (1944), San Francisco (1945) and the UN's creation
**Front:** What were the founding milestones of the UN in 1944–45?
**Definition (learn):** Dumbarton Oaks proposals (1944); UN Charter framed at San Francisco (1945); the UN (UNO) came into existence in October 1945. (✅ owner 16 mini-timeline)
**Breakdown:**
- 1944: Dumbarton Oaks proposals.
- 1945: Charter framed at San Francisco.
- 26 June 1945: Charter signed at San Francisco by 50 countries; 24 October 1945: UN comes into existence (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1944 (Dumbarton Oaks, owner); 26 June 1945 Charter signed; 24 October 1945 in force (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-3078, WH-3080 (aims), WH-3081 (six organs).
**Trap:** Do not date the UN's establishment other than Charter framed at San Francisco in 1945; UNO in existence from October 1945 (owner §9.10). Do not conflate the Charter's framing date with the existence date.
**Mains hook:** The UN's creation is a product of the wartime great-power alliance (Dumbarton Oaks, San Francisco).
**Back:** *"'44 Dumbarton, '45 San Francisco, Oct '45 UN exists."*

### Flashcard WH-3080 — UN's three broad aims (Lowe)
**Front:** What are the three broad aims that Lowe attributes to the UN?
**Definition (learn):** (1) preserve peace and eliminate war; (2) remove causes of conflict by encouraging economic, social, educational, scientific and cultural progress; (3) safeguard the rights of individuals, peoples and nations. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Aim 1: peace/war.
- Aim 2: developmental causes of conflict.
- Aim 3: rights.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3078, WH-3083 (Security Council role), WH-3098 (non-military work).
**Trap:** Aim 2 is why the UN does non-military governance; do not reduce the UN to the Security Council (WH-3098).
**Mains hook:** Use the three aims as the normative frame: peace, development, rights.
**Back:** *"Peace, progress, rights: the three aims."*

### Flashcard WH-3081 — Six principal UN bodies (Charter) today
**Front:** Name the six principal UN bodies under the Charter as per the official UN main-bodies page.
**Definition (learn):** General Assembly; Security Council; ECOSOC; Trusteeship Council; International Court of Justice; Secretariat. (✅ owner 16; ✅ official UN main-bodies page, rechecked 21 July 2026 per owner)
**Breakdown:**
- General Assembly — deliberation, budget, elections, broad resolutions.
- Security Council — primary responsibility for peace and security.
- ECOSOC — economic, social and development coordination.
- Trusteeship Council — trust-territory oversight, now inactive.
- ICJ — principal judicial organ.
- Secretariat — administration led by the Secretary-General.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Six organs. Official page rechecked 21 July 2026 (owner). (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3082–WH-3087 (each organ), WH-3088 (ICC trap).
**Trap:** Lowe's "seventh organ" (ICC) is not Charter-accurate. Lowe lists six organs plus the ICC in his discussion (owner §2).
**Mains hook:** "Explain the structure and mandate of the UN" — organs with functions and legal categories.
**Back:** *"GA, SC, ECOSOC, Trust, ICJ, Secretariat — six, no seventh."*

### Flashcard WH-3082 — General Assembly
**Front:** What are the functions of the UN General Assembly?
**Definition (learn):** The General Assembly is the deliberative organ of the UN; it handles deliberation, debate, budget, elections and broad resolutions. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Deliberation and debate.
- Budget.
- Elections.
- Broad resolutions (political weight even without binding force) — e.g. "Uniting for Peace" (WH-3090).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3081, WH-3090 (Uniting for Peace), WH-3083 (Security Council), WH-3105 (structural reform argument).
**Trap:** "The General Assembly is merely symbolic" is wrong — it matters for legitimacy, pressure and mechanisms like Uniting for Peace (owner §7).
**Mains hook:** The GA matters for legitimacy when the SC is blocked.
**Back:** *"GA: debate, budget, elections, broad voice."*

### Flashcard WH-3083 — Security Council: 15 members, 5 permanent, veto
**Front:** What is the composition of the Security Council, and what is its key brake?
**Definition (learn):** The Security Council has primary responsibility for peace and security; it has 15 members — 5 permanent and 10 non-permanent. The veto is the core institutional brake on decisive action. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Membership: 15 (5 permanent, 10 non-permanent).
- Primary responsibility: peace and security.
- Veto: central brake (owner §7).
- Authorises enforcement by member-state forces (WH-3092, WH-3098).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 15 members; 5 permanent; 10 non-permanent. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3084 (ECOSOC), WH-3096 (Hungary/Czechoslovakia veto), WH-3101 (Gulf War), WH-3105 (reform argument).
**Trap:** Do not reduce the UN to the SC alone (owner §7). Do not say veto makes GA irrelevant (WH-3082).
**Mains hook:** "The veto is not a flaw in the design; it is the design — the price of getting great powers inside" (⚠️ owner §9.2).
**Back:** *"Fifteen seats: five with the veto, ten rotating."*

### Flashcard WH-3084 — ECOSOC
**Front:** What does ECOSOC do?
**Definition (learn):** The Economic and Social Council coordinates economic, social and development matters across the UN system. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Function: coordination on economic, social, development matters.
- Links to specialised agencies (WH-3093 category card).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3081, WH-3098 (non-military work), WH-3093.
**Trap:** ECOSOC is a principal organ; do not confuse with specialised agencies (WH-3093).
**Mains hook:** ECOSOC-linked agencies widen the meaning of governance (advanced §4).
**Back:** *"ECOSOC: economy and society, coordinated."*

### Flashcard WH-3085 — Trusteeship Council (historical; inactive)
**Front:** What was the Trusteeship Council's role and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** The Trusteeship Council oversaw trust territories towards independence; it is now inactive. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Function (historic): trust-territory oversight.
- Status: inactive (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3081, WH-3087 (Secretariat), WH-3105 (reform argument).
**Trap:** Trusteeship is historical — not a current active organ (owner §2 & §7).
**Mains hook:** The Trusteeship Council is a reminder of decolonisation's institutional route.
**Back:** *"Trust Council: historic, now quiet."*

### Flashcard WH-3086 — International Court of Justice (principal judicial organ)
**Front:** What is the ICJ and how does it differ from the ICC?
**Definition (learn):** The International Court of Justice is the UN's principal judicial organ. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- ICJ: principal UN organ.
- ICC: independent treaty-based court with a cooperation relationship with the UN (not a principal organ) (⚠️ owner §2, §9.5; ✅ standard).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none additional. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3088 (ICC trap), WH-3081.
**Trap:** ICJ and ICC are not interchangeable (owner §7).
**Mains hook:** Institutional precision marks: name ICJ as organ; ICC as treaty court.
**Back:** *"ICJ is inside; ICC is beside."*

### Flashcard WH-3087 — Secretariat
**Front:** What is the Secretariat's function?
**Definition (learn):** The Secretariat is the administrative machinery of the UN, led by the Secretary-General. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Function: administration.
- Head: Secretary-General.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none; current Secretary-General not stated (owner: do not state current holder in a history answer). (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3081, WH-3093.
**Trap:** Do not give the current office-holder unless dated and verified (owner §9.8).
**Mains hook:** Secretariat is the machinery; GA/SC are the decision-makers.
**Back:** *"Secretariat: the UN's engine room."*

### Flashcard WH-3088 — ICC is not a UN principal organ (Lowe's seventh-organ trap)
**Front:** Why is the ICC not one of the UN's principal organs even though Lowe discusses it as a "seventh organ"?
**Definition (learn):** The ICC is an independent treaty-based court with a cooperation relationship with the UN; the official UN page lists six principal bodies. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Lowe: discusses ICC as "seventh organ" — not Charter-accurate (owner §2).
- Official: six principal bodies.
- ICC: Rome Statute court (⚠️ standard — Rome Statute; not fetched).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3081, WH-3086.
**Trap:** Lowe's seven-organ list is not current Charter law (advanced §7).
**Mains hook:** "Institutional precision" — separate courts from principal organs.
**Back:** *"Six organs, not seven."*

### Flashcard WH-3089 — UN vs League: six comparison points
**Front:** What does Lowe say the UN improved on, and what persisted, compared with the League?
**Definition (learn):** The UN spent far more effort on economic and social work; committed itself to individual human rights; Assembly and SC procedures allowed more decisive action; membership was wider. Persisting weaknesses: SC veto and no permanent army. (✅ owner 16 §3)
**Breakdown:**
- Scope: economic and social work.
- Human rights: committed to individual protection.
- Decision-making: more decisive procedures.
- Membership: far wider than League.
- Weakness 1: SC veto.
- Weakness 2: no permanent army.
- Verdict: more successful, but structural weaknesses remain (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3078, WH-3083, WH-3100 (Somalia and Bosnia failures).
**Trap:** Do not say the UN failed "because wars continue" (owner trap §7 "UN fails because wars" line in advanced).
**Mains hook:** "How far has the UN been more successful than the League?" — use the six comparison points, then the persisting defect.
**Back:** *"UN: wider, kinder, quicker — still vetoed, still armless."*

### Flashcard WH-3090 — Uniting for Peace (1950) and the General Assembly's role
**Front:** What is "Uniting for Peace" and where did it emerge from?
**Definition (learn):** The logic that emerged from the Korean War context (1950), allowing the General Assembly to act when the Security Council is blocked. Owner notes Assembly resolutions can still matter politically. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Context: Korean War (1950).
- Mechanism: GA political resolutions as response to SC paralysis (owner §7).
- Not a substitute for the veto; a political alternative.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1950. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3043 (Korea), WH-3082 (GA), WH-3083 (veto).
**Trap:** "Veto means GA irrelevant" (owner trap) — wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use Uniting for Peace to show that GA authority rises when SC is blocked.
**Back:** *"'50 Korea: when the Council is stuck, the Assembly speaks."*

### Flashcard WH-3091 — UN peacekeeping and collective enforcement: distinctions
**Front:** What are the three legal categories that candidates must not merge?
**Definition (learn):** (1) UN authorisation (SC approval), (2) UN command (UN-led forces), (3) UN peacekeeping (consent-based, UN-commanded). They are not interchangeable. Korea 1950 and the 1991 Gulf War were authorised collective enforcement by member-state forces, not blue-helmet peacekeeping. (✅ owner 16 advanced §7; basic §9.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Consent-based peacekeeping: ceasefire observation; separation; supervision.
- Authorised enforcement: SC authorises; member states act (Korea 1950; Gulf 1991).
- UN command: rare; no permanent UN army.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Korea 1950; Gulf 1991. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3092, WH-3098, WH-3096, WH-3082.
**Trap:** Korea/Gulf War are not blue-helmet missions (owner §9.10).
**Mains hook:** Match a case to the mechanism before judging success.
**Back:** *"Authorised ≠ commanded ≠ peacekeeping."*

### Flashcard WH-3092 — Korea 1950: collective enforcement made possible by Soviet absence
**Front:** Why was Korea a case of collective enforcement, and why was the SC able to act?
**Definition (learn):** Security Council-authorised collective enforcement, not consent-based peacekeeping; action was possible because the Soviet delegation was absent. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Type: authorised collective enforcement.
- Enabler: Soviet absence (cross-ref WH-3043).
- Result: contested success, non-repeatable (owner §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1950. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3043 (Korea war), WH-3091, WH-3090 (Uniting for Peace), WH-3083 (veto).
**Trap:** "Korea was a UN-commanded army" — not in owner's framing; authorised member-state force (WH-3091).
**Mains hook:** Korea shows that great-power absence can enable UN action — but cannot be relied upon.
**Back:** *"Korea: Moscow absent, the Council acts."*

### Flashcard WH-3093 — Specialised agencies vs UN funds vs UN agencies/programmes (legal categories)
**Front:** How do WHO, FAO, UNESCO, UNICEF, UNRWA, IMF, World Bank, UNCTAD and WTO differ in legal category?
**Definition (learn):** WHO, FAO and UNESCO are specialised agencies. UNICEF is a UN fund. UNRWA is a General Assembly-created agency/programme. IMF and World Bank are UN specialised agencies (owner). UNCTAD is a UN body. WTO is not a UN specialised agency. OCHA is a humanitarian coordination body. (✅ owner 16 §5, §9.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Specialised agencies: WHO, FAO, UNESCO (owner); IMF, World Bank (owner).
- UN fund: UNICEF.
- GA-created agency/programme: UNRWA.
- UN body: UNCTAD.
- Humanitarian coordination: OCHA.
- Not UN specialised agency: WTO (⚠️ owner: part of wider multilateral trade system).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3084 (ECOSOC), WH-3087 (Secretariat), WH-3098 (non-military).
**Trap:** Do not flatten every entity into one legal category (owner §7). WTO ≠ UN specialised agency (owner §9.10).
**Mains hook:** "Explain the structure and mandate of the United Nations" — distinguish organs, specialised agencies, funds and programmes.
**Back:** *"Specialised: WHO, FAO, UNESCO, IMF, WB. Fund: UNICEF. Not UN: WTO."* ⚠️ (IMF/WB status per owner)

### Flashcard WH-3094 — West New Guinea: mediation and supervised transfer
**Front:** What did the UN do in West New Guinea, and what does the case show?
**Definition (learn):** The UN helped negotiations and supervised the transfer in West New Guinea. The case shows that UN action works when a compromise exists. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Role: helped negotiations; supervised transfer.
- Lesson: works when compromise exists (advanced §3).
- Great-power posture: agreed or indifferent (owner §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates in owner (⚠️ not stated). (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3095 (Suez), WH-3104 (success-condition matrix).
**Trap:** Do not invent a date for West New Guinea; the owner gives none.
**Mains hook:** Peacekeeping succeeds where the parties agree and great powers are indifferent or aligned.
**Back:** *"West New Guinea: negotiate, supervise, transfer."*

### Flashcard WH-3095 — Suez 1956: UN's strongest moment
**Front:** Why does Lowe call Suez 1956 one of the UN's strongest moments?
**Definition (learn):** Suez was one of the UN's strongest moments: a ceasefire and a peacekeeping force followed world pressure on the aggressors. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Ceasefire and peacekeeping force followed.
- Aggressors yielded to world pressure (advanced §3).
- Same year as Hungary (WH-3096) — the paired contrast.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1956. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3052 (Suez war), WH-3096 (Hungary), WH-3104 (matrix).
**Trap:** Suez (success) vs Hungary (failure), both 1956 — the "classic paired contrast" (owner §7).
**Mains hook:** "Works when aggressors yield to world pressure" — the success condition.
**Back:** *"Suez '56: UN wins; Hungary '56: UN fails."*

### Flashcard WH-3096 — Hungary 1956 and Czechoslovakia 1968: UN failure under Soviet force
**Front:** Why did the UN fail in Hungary (1956) and Czechoslovakia (1968)?
**Definition (learn):** Soviet veto and Soviet force made the UN ineffective; in the matrix, "directly opposed" great power leads to complete ineffectiveness. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Hungary 1956: Soviet veto and force (WH-3048).
- Czechoslovakia 1968: Soviet force (WH-3040 for 1948 Czechoslovakia; 1968 date is from owner).
- Lesson: veto plus superpower defiance defeats the UN.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1956; 1968. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3048, WH-3083 (veto), WH-3104 (matrix), WH-3095 (Suez).
**Trap:** Czechoslovakia 1968 is not the 1948 coup (WH-3040). Two different events.
**Mains hook:** The veto makes the UN powerless against a permanent member's own sphere.
**Back:** *"Veto + tanks = UN silent."*

### Flashcard WH-3097 — Congo 1960–64: costly but substantial
**Front:** What does the Congo operation (1960–64) show about complex peacekeeping?
**Definition (learn):** A complex and costly UN operation that restored some order; complex missions require resources. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Years: 1960–64.
- Result: restored some order.
- Lesson: complex missions need resources (advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1960–64 (✅ owner 16 timeline).
**Links:** WH-3104 (matrix), WH-3100 (Somalia, Bosnia), WH-3108 (Lowe reform logic).
**Trap:** Do not give troop or budget figures for the Congo operation (owner §9.10).
**Mains hook:** Congo shows the cost of complexity; UN success needs resources.
**Back:** *"Congo: costly, not failed."*

### Flashcard WH-3098 — UN non-military work: the balance bank
**Front:** What non-military work does Lowe list to balance a "UN fails" answer?
**Definition (learn):** Human rights (UDHR and rights publicity); labour (ILO); health (WHO, disease control); food (FAO); education and culture (UNESCO); children and refugees (UNICEF, UNRWA); finance and trade (IMF, World Bank, UNCTAD); humanitarian relief (OCHA). (✅ owner 16 §5, §9.7)
**Breakdown:**
- Human rights: Universal Declaration of Human Rights.
- Labour: ILO.
- Health: WHO.
- Food: FAO.
- Education/culture: UNESCO.
- Children/refugees: UNICEF; UNRWA.
- Finance/trade: IMF; World Bank; UNCTAD (WTO not UN agency).
- Humanitarian: OCHA.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** UDHR adopted 10 December 1948 as GA Resolution 217 A(III); 48 in favour, none against, 8 abstentions (✅ Wikipedia). (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3093 (categories), WH-3080 (aims), WH-3109 (verdict).
**Trap:** The UN is not only the Security Council; use at least two of these in a 15-mark answer (owner §9.7).
**Mains hook: "The greatest strength of the UN lies outside the battlefield." — Discuss.** Yes, if you measure by lives affected (owner §9.9).
**Back:** *"Rights, Labour, Health, Food, Learning, Children, Money, Relief."*

### Flashcard WH-3099 — Cyprus and Kashmir: ceasefire without settlement
**Front:** What did the UN achieve in Cyprus and Kashmir?
**Definition (learn):** Ceasefires and supervision were possible in Cyprus and Kashmir, but not a final settlement. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Achievement: ceasefires, supervision.
- Non-achievement: final settlement.
- Category: "present but insufficient" (owner §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none in owner. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3104 (matrix), WH-3089 (UN limits), WH-3110 (fire-break).
**Trap:** Do not say the UN "solved" Palestine, Kashmir or Cyprus (owner §7, §9.10).
**Mains hook:** Containment without resolution — UN's crisis management, not settlement.
**Back:** *"Cyprus, Kashmir: ceasefire yes, peace no."*

### Flashcard WH-3100 — Somalia and Bosnia (1990s): too little force, weak backing
**Front:** Why did UN missions fail in Somalia and Bosnia?
**Definition (learn):** Too little force, too complex a civil-war environment, and weak political backing; these were failure or humiliation cases. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Somalia: failure/humiliation; weak mandate.
- Bosnia: failure; complex civil war; weak backing.
- Lesson: weak mandate + insufficient force invite collapse (advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1990s (owner timeline). (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3097 (Congo), WH-3104 (matrix), WH-3089.
**Trap:** More peacekeeping ≠ more success (advanced §7).
**Mains hook:** Mandate must match force; the civil-war environment changes peacekeeping's nature.
**Back:** *"Somalia, Bosnia: thin mandates, thinner force."*

### Flashcard WH-3101 — Gulf War 1991: Council-authorised coalition, not UN army
**Front:** What was the UN role in the 1991 Gulf War?
**Definition (learn):** The Security Council authorised force by a member-state coalition; it was not a UN-commanded peacekeeping army. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Type: authorised coalition enforcement.
- Not: UN command; not blue-helmet peacekeeping (owner §9.10).
- Lesson: authorisation did not create a UN-commanded army (advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1991. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3091 (distinctions), WH-3083 (SC), WH-3103 (Iraq 2003 contrast).
**Trap:** Gulf War 1991 (authorised) ≠ Iraq 2003 (unauthorised, bypassed) — owner.
**Mains hook:** Post-Cold War: great-power alignment made enforcement possible.
**Back:** *"'91 Gulf: Council says go, coalition goes."*

### Flashcard WH-3102 — Cambodia and Mozambique: post-Cold-War transitions
**Front:** Why did the UN succeed in Cambodia and Mozambique?
**Definition (learn):** The post-Cold-War UN helped stabilise transitions; peace implementation can succeed where combatants are exhausted. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Cambodia and Mozambique: transitions supported.
- Condition: exhausted combatants (advanced §3).
- Great-power posture: agreed/aligned after Cold War (owner §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1990s (owner timeline). (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3104 (matrix), WH-3100 (1990s failure contrast).
**Trap:** Cambodia and Mozambique success ≠ Somalia/Bosnia failure; the difference is condition and force.
**Mains hook:** Peace implementation succeeds where fighters are exhausted.
**Back:** *"Cambodia, Mozambique: exhausted fighters, supported transition."*

### Flashcard WH-3103 — Iraq 2003: UN bypassed
**Front:** What did the 2003 Iraq crisis expose about UN authority?
**Definition (learn):** The US–UK invasion without clear Security Council authorisation exposed UN limits sharply. Unilateral great-power action can bypass the UN. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 2003.
- Actors: US and UK.
- Authorisation: not clear (owner).
- Effect: prestige damaged; legitimacy questions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2003 (✅ owner 16). No figures given (owner caution).
**Links:** WH-3101 (Gulf War 1991), WH-3104 (matrix).
**Trap:** Do not say the UN "failed" in Iraq as if it had no role; the owner's point is bypass and exposed limits.
**Mains hook:** "Under what conditions has the UN succeeded or failed?" — Iraq 2003 is the "bypassed" case.
**Back:** *"Iraq 2003: bypassed, not beaten."*

### Flashcard WH-3104 — Peacekeeping success condition: the great-power matrix
**Front:** Under what conditions did UN peacekeeping succeed or fail?
**Definition (learn):** State the condition first, then use the cases. Great-power posture decides: agreed or indifferent → success (West New Guinea, Suez 1956, Cambodia and Mozambique); temporarily absent → success but non-repeatable (Korea 1950); aligned after Cold War → enforcement success (Gulf 1991); directly opposed → ineffectiveness (Hungary 1956, Czechoslovakia 1968); divided or disengaged → failure (Somalia, Bosnia); bypassed → exposed limits (Iraq 2003); present but insufficient → containment (Cyprus, Kashmir); complex partially effective → mixed (Congo). (✅ owner 16 §9.6)
**Breakdown:**
- UN success rises when consent, clarity and great-power backing converge; falls when one powerful actor rejects the institution or the mandate exceeds force (owner §3 pattern ⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cases listed above. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3094–WH-3103 (each case), WH-3092 (Korea), WH-3101 (Gulf), WH-3109 (verdict).
**Trap:** Do not list missions without a success condition (owner §9.1 directive).
**Mains hook:** "Assess the UN as a peacekeeping organisation with suitable examples." Answer via the matrix.
**Back:** *"Agree, absent, aligned, opposed, divided, bypassed, stuck, complex."*

### Flashcard WH-3105 — Structural reform argument: 1945 Council vs decolonised Assembly
**Front:** What durable, undated reform argument does the owner recommend?
**Definition (learn):** The Security Council's permanent membership reflects the power distribution of 1945, while the General Assembly reflects a membership transformed by decolonisation. The mismatch between the composition of authority and the composition of the organisation is the durable, historically demonstrable reform argument. (⚠️ owner §9.8 synthesis)
**Breakdown:**
- SC: 1945 power distribution.
- GA: post-decolonisation membership (see WH-3127 for decolonisation and UN membership).
- Mismatch → reform logic.
- Do not state current reform negotiations or outcomes (owner §9.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1945. No current reform status stated. (✅ owner 16 for structure; ⚠️ synthesis)
**Links:** WH-3083, WH-3082, WH-3108 (Lowe reform logic), WH-3106 (current).
**Trap:** Do not assert any current SC reform outcome or negotiating position (owner §9.10).
**Mains hook:** "Reform of the Security Council" — answer with the structural logic only.
**Back:** *"1945 seats, decolonised members."*

### Flashcard WH-3106 — Pact for the Future (2024) and UN80 (current link)
**Front:** What are the current reform markers in the owner, and what do they support?
**Definition (learn):** The UNGA adopted Resolution 79/1 (Pact for the Future) on 22 September 2024; its annexes include the Global Digital Compact and the Declaration on Future Generations. The UN's 2025 observance linked its 80th anniversary to the Pact and the UN80 initiative. (✅ owner 16 advanced §6; owner rechecked 21 July 2026.)
**Breakdown:**
- 22 Sep 2024: GA Res 79/1.
- Annexes: Global Digital Compact; Declaration on Future Generations.
- 2025: UN80 and 80th anniversary.
- Use: frames reform as adaptation, not abolition (owner §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 22 Sep 2024; 2025; rechecked 21 July 2026 (owner). (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3105, WH-3108.
**Trap:** Do not state outcome or budget figures for UN80; owner excludes unsupported claims (basic §10). No current SC expansion claims (owner §6).
**Mains hook:** Post-Lowe evidence of reform — but keep it status-disciplined.
**Back:** *"Pact 2024, UN80 2025: adapt, not abolish."*

### Flashcard WH-3107 — China's UN seat: 1971 change
**Front:** What happened to China's UN seat in 1971?
**Definition (learn):** The People's Republic of China took China's UN seat in 1971. (✅ owner 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1971.
- Change: PRC replaces the Republic of China's seat (⚠️ Res 2758 number not verified; date 25 Oct 1971 ✅ Wikipedia).
- Link: China's 1949 revolution (WH-3044) and US–China opening (1972, WH-3058).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 October 1971 — PRC given the Chinese seat in place of the Republic of China (✅ Wikipedia). Resolution 2758 number not verified in this card (⚠️).
**Links:** WH-3044, WH-3058, WH-3067.
**Trap:** 1971 (UN seat) ≠ 1972 (Nixon's China opening) — two events.
**Mains hook:** The UN seat change reflected China's shift from Cold War isolation to participation.
**Back:** *"'71: Beijing takes the UN seat."*

### Flashcard WH-3108 — Lowe's reform logic (post-Cold-War, pre-2012 frame)
**Front:** What reforms does Lowe's "future of the UN" discussion propose?
**Definition (learn):** Stronger and quicker peacekeeping capacity; more dependable financing; better co-ordination across agencies; clearer handling of terrorism and humanitarian crises; a more workable relationship between the UN and overwhelming US power. (✅ owner 16 advanced §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Capacity: stronger, quicker peacekeeping.
- Finance: dependable.
- Coordination: agencies.
- Terrorism and humanitarian crises.
- US relationship: workable.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Lowe's discussion is pre-2012 context (advanced §5 ⚠️). (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3105, WH-3106, WH-3109.
**Trap:** Lowe's reform frame is historical; do not present it as current negotiation status.
**Mains hook:** Reform logic is reformist, not abolitionist (owner §5).
**Back:** *"Quicker troops, steadier funds, joined-up agencies."*

### Flashcard WH-3109 — UN verdict: forum, norm-setter, humanitarian coordinator
**Front:** What is Lowe's balanced verdict, and what is the safe UPSC summary?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe is neither celebratory nor dismissive. Weaknesses: no permanent army; delays over when and how to intervene; veto politics; funds shortage; agency overlap and friction; tension with powerful states. Strengths: a forum where small states can speak; some wars shortened or contained; human-rights pressure; contribution to health, welfare, refugees, literacy and development. Safe summary: the UN is strongest as a forum, norm-setter and humanitarian coordinator; weakest when hard enforcement collides with great-power politics. (✅ owner 16 §6; ⚠️ summary)
**Breakdown:**
- Weaknesses (6).
- Strengths (4).
- Verdict line (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3098, WH-3104, WH-3108.
**Trap:** "The UN fails because wars continue" is wrong (owner §7 trap).
**Mains hook:** "The greatest strength of the UN lies outside the battlefield." — Discuss (owner §9.1).
**Back:** *"Forum yes, enforcer no."*

### Flashcard WH-3110 — Peacekeeping metaphor: fire-break, not fire brigade
**Front:** What is the standard verdict on UN peacekeeping?
**Definition (learn):** The UN is effective as a fire-break (separating combatants, supervising transitions) and ineffective as a fire brigade (cannot compel a great power or resolve a civil war it has not been empowered to settle). (⚠️ owner §9.9 verdict scaffold)
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: separation of combatants; supervising transitions.
- Limit: cannot compel great power; cannot resolve civil war without mandate.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (⚠️)
**Links:** WH-3104, WH-3099, WH-3100.
**Trap:** Do not write that the UN is a world government (owner §9.2 verdict: judged as world government it is a failure).
**Mains hook:** "Assess the UN as a peacekeeping organisation with suitable examples."
**Back:** *"Fire-break yes, fire brigade no."*

### Flashcard WH-3111 — UN as "more successful than the League": structural reason
**Front:** Why is the UN more successful than the League, and why can it rarely act against great powers?
**Definition (learn):** The UN kept great powers inside the organisation, which is precisely why it can rarely act against them. (⚠️ owner §9.9 verdict scaffold)
**Breakdown:**
- The League: USA outside.
- The UN: great powers inside, with veto.
- Consequence: restraint but not enforcement against them.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 16)
**Links:** WH-3078, WH-3083, WH-3089.
**Trap:** "The UN improved on the League" must be paired with "the veto remains" (owner).
**Mains hook:** Answer both halves: improved scope, same core enforcement problem.
**Back:** *"Great powers inside, so the UN cannot push them."*

### Flashcard WH-3112 — UN Mains frame and probable questions
**Front:** What is the standard UN answer frame and the probable questions?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Begin with WWII background and League failure; (2) structure — GA, SC, Secretariat, ICJ, ECOSOC, Trusteeship; (3) use 2–3 cases for mixed peacekeeping; (4) add non-peacekeeping work; (5) balanced verdict on veto, funding and great-power politics. (⚠️ owner §8)
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: thesis → two organs/functions or two cases → success condition → verdict.
- 15 marks: thesis → League lesson → structure → mixed peacekeeping → non-military → verdict.
- 20 marks: plus structural limits, status-disciplined reform, post-Cold-War change.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Probable questions (⚠️): "How far has UN been more successful than the League?"; "Assess the UN as a peacekeeping organisation"; "Greatest strength outside the battlefield"; "Explain structure and mandate".
**Links:** WH-3078, WH-3081, WH-3104, WH-3098, WH-3109.
**Trap:** Zero direct PYQ routes 2018–2025 in owner §10 (original practice).
**Mains hook:** Use the frame; keep current GS-II detail as optional garnish only.
**Back:** *"League, Organs, Cases, Beyond, Verdict."*

### GROUP — China, Communism and Asia (basic 17 + advanced 17)

### Flashcard WH-3113 — Chinese Revolution: from imperial crisis to communist victory (causal chain)
**Front:** What is the causal chain from the Qing crisis to the 1949 communist victory?
**Definition (learn):** Foreign pressure and Qing decline → 1911 revolution → Yuan dictatorship → warlord chaos → KMT vs CCP rivalry → anti-Japanese resistance war → CCP victory in 1949. (✅ owner 17 §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Foreign humiliation discredited the dynasty (Opium Wars, concessions, Japanese victories).
- 1911: Manchu collapse; Yuan Shikai turned republic into dictatorship.
- Warlord era: central authority weak.
- KMT (Sun Yat-sen) vs CCP (1921).
- Anti-Japanese war (1937).
- 1949: PRC proclaimed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1911; 1921; 1927; 1934–35; 1937; 1949. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3114 (1911), WH-3117 (CCP/KMT), WH-3119 (1937), WH-3120 (1949), WH-3121 (why CCP won).
**Trap:** 1911 ended the Manchu dynasty but did not create a stable democratic order (owner §6).
**Mains hook:** "Assess the reasons for the communist triumph in 1949" — show each link is state-building failure, not only ideology.
**Back:** *"Insult → Revolt → Yuan → Warlords → Rivals → War → Red."*

### Flashcard WH-3114 — 1911 Revolution: removed a dynasty, not the problem
**Front:** What did the 1911 Revolution achieve and what did it fail to achieve?
**Definition (learn):** The 1911 revolution ended the Manchu dynasty and a republic was proclaimed, but Yuan Shikai turned republican change into military dictatorship; warlordism and civil conflict followed. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Achieved: end of Manchu dynasty; republic proclaimed.
- Failed: stable democratic order; state-building.
- Yuan Shikai → military dictatorship.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1911. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3113, WH-3115 (warlord era), WH-3116 (Sun's Three Principles).
**Trap:** "1911 immediately solved China's political crisis" is wrong (owner §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Regime change without state-building (owner §8.4).
**Back:** *"1911: dynasty gone, state not built."*

### Flashcard WH-3115 — Yuan Shikai and the Warlord Era
**Front:** What was the Warlord Era and how did Yuan Shikai contribute to it?
**Definition (learn):** After Yuan turned republican change into dictatorship, China fragmented; provincial generals fought each other and central authority remained weak. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Yuan Shikai: military dictatorship.
- Warlord Era: fragmentation; provincial generals; weak centre.
- Problem: "who can build a state" (owner §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Warlord era (no dates in owner). (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3114, WH-3113, WH-3117 (KMT–CCP rivalry).
**Trap:** Do not present Yuan Shikai as an early republican hero; the owner calls his rule military dictatorship.
**Mains hook:** Fragmentation created the conditions for the KMT–CCP contest.
**Back:** *"Yuan dictates, warlords divide."*

### Flashcard WH-3116 — Sun Yat-sen's Three Principles and the KMT
**Front:** What were Sun Yat-sen's Three Principles and why did they matter?
**Definition (learn):** Nationalism, democracy and land reform / people's livelihood — a republican alternative to the CCP path. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Nationalism.
- Democracy.
- Land reform / people's livelihood.
- Shows CCP victory was not the only available path (owner §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3117 (KMT–CCP), WH-3122 (KMT weaknesses), WH-3113.
**Trap:** The KMT did not lose only because of foreign interference; corruption and weak governance mattered (owner §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Use Sun's principles to show a non-communist route existed.
**Back:** *"Sun: nation, democracy, livelihood."*

### Flashcard WH-3117 — CCP founding (1921), KMT–CCP alliance and rupture (1927)
**Front:** When was the CCP founded and when did the KMT–CCP alliance break?
**Definition (learn):** The CCP was founded in 1921, first co-operated with the KMT and then fought it; the KMT–CCP alliance broke in 1927 and civil war deepened. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- 1921: CCP founded.
- 1927: alliance breaks; civil war deepens.
- After 1927: civil war becomes organising conflict (owner §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1921; 1927. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3113, WH-3116, WH-3118 (Long March).
**Trap:** 1921 (CCP founding) ≠ 1927 (rupture) ≠ 1949 (PRC).
**Mains hook:** Civil war is the organising conflict of 1927–49.
**Back:** *"'21 CCP born, '27 alliance dies."*

### Flashcard WH-3118 — Long March (1934–35): survival that became legitimacy
**Front:** What did the Long March (1934–35) achieve for the CCP?
**Definition (learn):** The Long March preserved the communist leadership and elevated Mao; survival turned into political legitimacy. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Years: 1934–35.
- Result: leadership preserved; Mao dominant.
- Rural base as survival adaptation that became strategy (owner §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1934–35. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3117, WH-3123 (Mao's peasant strategy), WH-3121 (why CCP won).
**Trap:** Long March (1934–35) ≠ Japanese invasion (1937).
**Mains hook:** Long March is political myth + organisational survival (advanced §2).
**Back:** *"Long March: leadership saved, Mao crowned."*

### Flashcard WH-3119 — Japanese full-scale invasion (1937) and the anti-Japanese war
**Front:** How did the 1937 Japanese invasion reshape the Chinese contest?
**Definition (learn):** The full-scale Japanese invasion of China (1937) turned an ideological civil war into a competition over national defence; the anti-Japanese war damaged the KMT and helped the CCP appear more patriotic. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- 1937: full-scale invasion.
- KMT bore conventional war and was weakened (owner §8.5).
- CCP gained patriotic credibility.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3032 (Asian origin: 1931/1937), WH-3113, WH-3121.
**Trap:** Japanese war in China (1937) is part of the Asian origin of WWII (WH-3032) — do not date the Asian war from 1941.
**Mains hook:** Civil war and external war interacting — an advanced link (advanced §2).
**Back:** *"'37: invasion makes patriotism the prize."*

### Flashcard WH-3120 — 1 October 1949: PRC proclaimed; Chiang to Taiwan
**Front:** When was the People's Republic of China proclaimed, and where did Chiang's government retreat?
**Definition (learn):** Mao proclaimed the People's Republic of China on 1 October 1949 after the CCP's civil-war victory; Chiang and his supporters retreated to Taiwan. (✅ owner 17 basic §2 & advanced §8)
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1 October 1949 (advanced §8).
- Victor: CCP.
- Loser's retreat: Chiang and supporters to Taiwan.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 Oct 1949. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3044 (communist China widened US fears), WH-3035 (Soviet bomb 1949), WH-3121.
**Trap:** Do not date the PRC's proclamation to 1947 or 1950 — owner: 1949.
**Mains hook:** The PRC changed Cold War Asia (WH-3044).
**Back:** *"1 Oct 1949: Mao proclaims; Chiang flees to Taiwan."*

### Flashcard WH-3121 — Why the CCP won in 1949 (balance sheet, both sides)
**Front:** Why did the CCP win in 1949? Give the CCP strengths and the KMT weaknesses.
**Definition (learn):** The CCP won because it built, in the countryside and during a war of national defence, administrative discipline and popular legitimacy — the things the KMT lost in the cities. (⚠️ owner §8.2 thesis; ✅ owner §8.5 evidence)
**Breakdown:**
- CCP strengths: rural strategy and social base; discipline and reform; Long March legitimacy; patriotic credibility.
- KMT weaknesses: corruption; poor administration; inflation; weak mass appeal; war exhaustion.
- Decisive: land reform and administrative honesty, not doctrine (⚠️ owner §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1949. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3122, WH-3123, WH-3118, WH-3119.
**Trap:** "The KMT lost only to foreign interference" is wrong (owner §8.9). "The CCP won by doctrine alone" is also a trap — organisation mattered.
**Mains hook:** "Discuss the causes of the communist victory in China in 1949" — balance-sheet answer.
**Back:** *"Peasants, discipline, Long March, patriotism beat corruption and inflation."*

### Flashcard WH-3122 — KMT weaknesses under Chiang Kai-shek
**Front:** What weakened the Kuomintang (KMT) under Chiang Kai-shek?
**Definition (learn):** Corruption, poor administration, inflation and weak mass appeal eroded KMT support; the KMT bore the conventional war and was weakened by it. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Corruption.
- Poor administration.
- Inflation.
- Weak mass appeal; loss of urban support.
- War exhaustion (conventional war burden).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3121, WH-3116, WH-3119.
**Trap:** Do not present KMT loss as only external (WH-3121).
**Mains hook:** The KMT lost the mandate before it lost the war (⚠️ owner §8.5).
**Back:** *"KMT: corrupt, broke, unpopular, worn out."*

### Flashcard WH-3123 — Mao's peasant-based strategy
**Front:** How did Mao's strategy differ from the urban-worker model?
**Definition (learn):** Mao rooted communist survival in the countryside and among peasants rather than in a small urban working class. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Social base: peasants.
- Rural strategy → survival and power.
- Useful against the claim that communism was only urban-worker ideology (advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3118 (Long March), WH-3121, WH-3124 (land reform).
**Trap:** Do not repeat Marxist theory; answer by the strategic logic (owner §8.1).
**Mains hook:** "Peasant-based revolution: why a rural strategy worked where an urban one had failed."
**Back:** *"Mao went to the fields."*

### Flashcard WH-3124 — Land reform and early consolidation (1949–50s)
**Front:** What did land reform and early consolidation achieve after 1949?
**Definition (learn):** Land reform, reconstruction and tighter one-party control; old landlord power weakened; the party-state expanded rapidly. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Land reform: landlord power weakened.
- Reconstruction.
- One-party control tightened.
- Owner social transformation (⚠️): land reform destroyed the landlord class as a social force.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** no figures sourced (⚠️ owner §8.6). (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3123, WH-3125 (Hundred Flowers), WH-3130 (Mao legacy).
**Trap:** Do not give land-reform figures (owner caution).
**Mains hook:** Land reform was the CCP's decisive social asset (owner §8.5).
**Back:** *"Land reform: landlords out, party in."*

### Flashcard WH-3125 — Hundred Flowers and Anti-Rightist turn
**Front:** What was the Hundred Flowers episode and its outcome?
**Definition (learn):** A brief criticism followed by repression — the Anti-Rightist turn — which made the limits of dissent clear. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Phase 1: brief criticism allowed.
- Phase 2: repression (Anti-Rightist).
- Lesson: limits of dissent.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates given in owner (⚠️ 1956–57 standard; not verified here).
**Links:** WH-3124, WH-3126 (GLF), WH-3127 (Cultural Revolution).
**Trap:** Do not confuse the brief criticism with genuine liberalisation.
**Mains hook:** Mao-era campaigns displaced administrative rationality (advanced §3).
**Back:** *"Flowers opened, then closed."*

### Flashcard WH-3126 — Great Leap Forward (1958–61)
**Front:** What was the Great Leap Forward and what was its cost?
**Definition (learn):** A crash industrialisation and commune experiment; severe disruption and famine; Mao's prestige suffered. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Years: 1958–61.
- Method: crash industrialisation; commune experiment.
- Effect: severe disruption and famine.
- Effect on Mao: prestige suffered.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1958–61. No famine figures sourced (⚠️ owner §8.9). (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3125, WH-3127 (CR), WH-3130 (legacy).
**Trap:** Great Leap (1958–61) ≠ Cultural Revolution (1966–76). Never merge (advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** "Assess the impact of Maoism on Chinese society and state formation."
**Back:** *"Leap: communes, crash industry, famine."*

### Flashcard WH-3127 — Cultural Revolution (1966–76)
**Front:** What was the Cultural Revolution and what did it damage?
**Definition (learn):** Mass mobilisation against alleged enemies inside the system; education, administration and social stability were badly damaged. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Years: 1966–76.
- Method: mass mobilisation.
- Damage: education, administration, social stability.
- Owner ⚠️: assault on education produced a lost cohort (qualitative only).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1966–76. No figures (owner). (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3126 (GLF), WH-3130 (legacy), WH-3128 (Deng reform).
**Trap:** Do not merge with the Great Leap (WH-3126).
**Mains hook:** Campaign politics vs pragmatic growth (advanced §8 trap).
**Back:** *"Cultural: mobilise, purge, damage schools."*

### Flashcard WH-3128 — Deng-era reform from 1978: economic, not political
**Front:** What did Deng-era reform (from 1978) change, and what did it leave untouched?
**Definition (learn):** Pragmatic opening; agricultural decollectivisation; foreign investment and market mechanisms; rapid growth; no multi-party democracy. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Economic: opening; decollectivisation; FDI; market.
- Political: one-party rule unchanged.
- Economic pragmatism replaced permanent mass campaign politics (advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** from 1978 (owner timeline); after Mao's death 1976 (advanced §8). (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3129 (Tiananmen), WH-3131 (Deng paradox), WH-3127.
**Trap:** Deng reformed the economy; he did not democratise (owner §6 trap, §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Reform without democracy" — separate economic from political liberalisation.
**Back:** *"Deng: markets yes, multiparty no."*

### Flashcard WH-3129 — Tiananmen Square (1989): economic reform and political repression
**Front:** What did the 1989 Tiananmen crackdown show?
**Definition (learn):** The 1989 protest movement was crushed by force; economic reform continued but political liberalisation was blocked. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 1989.
- Event: protest crushed by force.
- Outcome: economic reform continued; politics blocked.
- Same year as the Gorbachev visit to Beijing (WH-3139).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1989. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3128, WH-3131, WH-3139.
**Trap:** "Tiananmen ended economic reform" is wrong (owner §6, §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Tiananmen was the moment that separation was enforced by force" (⚠️ owner §8.2).
**Back:** *"1989: reform runs, protest is crushed."*

### Flashcard WH-3130 — Mao's legacy: achievement and catastrophe together
**Front:** How does Lowe's account balance Mao's legacy?
**Definition (learn):** Mao gave China unity, independence and revolutionary purpose, but at huge human and institutional cost when campaigns displaced administrative rationality. (⚠️ owner advanced §3; ✅ owner basic §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Achievements: political unification; foreign domination rolled back; party-state authority; land reform and mass mobilisation.
- Costs: Great Leap Forward (ideological overreach); Cultural Revolution (stability shattered); blurred criticism/disloyalty/class war.
- Verdict: the capacity that unified China made the disasters possible (⚠️ owner §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3124, WH-3126, WH-3127.
**Trap:** Do not present Mao's legacy as either wholly positive or wholly negative.
**Mains hook:** "Mao made modern China, but he also deeply damaged it." — Comment.
**Back:** *"Unity and ruin, same hand."*

### Flashcard WH-3131 — Deng's paradox: marketisation inside a one-party shell
**Front:** What is the "Deng paradox" in UPSC terms?
**Definition (learn):** Market incentives and foreign investment coexisted with the Party's unquestioned political centre; marketisation inside a one-party Leninist shell. (⚠️ owner advanced §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Allowed: market incentives; FDI; decollectivisation.
- Not allowed: organised political opposition (Tiananmen).
- Verdict line: "China proved that market reform does not require political liberalisation — and Tiananmen was the price of proving it" (⚠️ owner §8.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3128, WH-3129.
**Trap:** Deng's reform ≠ democratisation (owner §6). Post-Mao reform ≠ Mao's policy style (advanced §8 trap).
**Mains hook:** "Reform without democracy" — the central answer distinction.
**Back:** *"Market yes, party-monopoly stays."*

### Flashcard WH-3132 — North Korea: Kim Il-sung and centralised communism
**Front:** How did communism emerge in North Korea and what makes it distinctive?
**Definition (learn):** Kim Il-sung built a tightly controlled communist state after the division of Korea; extreme centralisation and one-party control endured; dynastic personalism (⚠️ owner §8.7). (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Route: division of Korea (external division, not internal revolution — owner §8.7).
- Character: extreme centralisation; dynastic personalism; enduring one-party control.
- Most rigid, security-centric model (advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Division of Korea (WH-3043 for war). (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3043 (Korean War), WH-3141 (comparison).
**Trap:** North Korea ≠ Chinese copy (WH-3141).
**Mains hook:** Compare with Chinese model: NK was produced by division, not revolution alone.
**Back:** *"Division made Kim; dynasty made North Korea."*

### Flashcard WH-3133 — Vietnam: anti-colonial war, Geneva division and reunification
**Front:** What path did Vietnam take to communism?
**Definition (learn):** Anti-colonial war; division after Geneva; communist victory and reunification. Nationalism and communism fused more strongly than in China. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Route: anti-colonial war → division after Geneva → communist victory → reunification.
- Asset: anti-colonial legitimacy (owner §8.7).
- 1975–76: reunified Vietnam (owner timeline).
- Dec 1978: Vietnam's invasion of Kampuchea (WH-3136/3138).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Geneva division date not in owner (⚠️ 1954 standard, not verified). 1975–76 reunification. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3134 (Cambodia), WH-3138 (Sino-Vietnamese war), WH-3141 (comparison).
**Trap:** Vietnam ≠ China: nationalism fused more strongly.
**Mains hook:** "Anti-colonial legitimacy could be the primary asset" (owner §8.7).
**Back:** *"Vietnam: war on France, then on the South."* ⚠️ (flagged: Geneva date not in owner)

### Flashcard WH-3134 — Cambodia: Khmer Rouge under Pol Pot
**Front:** What made Cambodia the starkest warning case of Asian communism?
**Definition (learn):** Khmer Rouge rule under Pol Pot became brutally extreme — ideological radicalism without institutional restraint produced mass atrocity. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Route: Khmer Rouge rule.
- Character: brutally extreme; not a Chinese copy.
- Overthrown by Vietnam's December 1978 invasion (WH-3138).
- 1975–76 communist victory in Cambodia (owner timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1975–76 victory; Dec 1978 Vietnamese invasion (owner §8.7A). (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3133, WH-3138, WH-3141.
**Trap:** "Cambodia is just another Vietnam" — wrong (advanced §8). No casualty figures sourced (owner).
**Mains hook:** Radicalism without institutional restraint.
**Back:** *"Pol Pot's Cambodia: the warning case."*

### Flashcard WH-3135 — Laos: quieter, adaptive one-party model
**Front:** How did communism come to Laos and how did it adapt?
**Definition (learn):** Communist takeover in 1975, later moderated by pragmatic economic adjustment; a quieter but durable one-party model. (✅ owner 17)
**Breakdown:**
- Takeover: 1975.
- Later: pragmatic economic adjustment.
- Character: less dramatic than Vietnam or Cambodia; more adaptive over time (advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1975. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3133, WH-3134, WH-3141.
**Trap:** Laos is not a Vietnam copy; the owner says local histories differed.
**Mains hook:** Small-state communism survived by adaptation.
**Back:** *"Laos: 1975, quiet, adaptive."*

### Flashcard WH-3136 — Sino-Soviet split: from 1950 treaty to 1956 rift
**Front:** What was the Sino-Soviet relationship at its start, and when did it deteriorate?
**Definition (learn):** A treaty of mutual assistance and friendship (1950); relations deteriorated steadily after 1956. (✅ owner 17 §8.7A)
**Breakdown:**
- 1950: treaty of mutual assistance and friendship.
- After 1956: steady deterioration (de-Stalinisation era).
- Doctrinal charge: China disapproved Khrushchev's "peaceful coexistence" and his claim that communism could be reached by non-violent methods; accused Russians of "revisionism" (reinterpreting Marx and Lenin).
- Strategic charge: China angry at Khrushchev's "soft" line towards the USA.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1950; after 1956. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3137 (territory and aid), WH-3138 (1979), WH-3139 (reconciliation), WH-3067 (Cold War summary), WH-3140 (verdict).
**Trap:** Do not attribute the breach to a 1964 event or to Khrushchev's fall — owner: "steadily after 1956" only (§8.9).
**Mains hook:** Ideology operative, not decorative (⚠️ owner §8.7A).
**Back:** *"1950 pact, 1956 rift, doctrine first."*

### Flashcard WH-3137 — Sino-Soviet split: territory and aid cut
**Front:** What material and territorial grievances deepened the Sino-Soviet split?
**Definition (learn):** The USSR reduced economic aid to China in retaliation; China demanded back territory Russia had taken in the nineteenth century, north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang. (✅ owner 17 §8.7A)
**Breakdown:**
- Aid cut: retaliation (point of rupture).
- Frontier: north of Vladivostok; Sinkiang.
- Once China softened towards USA, territory became "the main bone of contention" (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** nineteenth-century Russian acquisitions (owner). (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3136, WH-3067 (Cold War summary), WH-3140.
**Trap:** Do not add aid or trade figures; owner gives none (§8.9).
**Mains hook:** Imperial-era grievance outliving ideology — strong 20-mark observation (owner §8.7A).
**Back:** *"Aid cut, border claimed."*

### Flashcard WH-3138 — Sino-Vietnamese War (February 1979)
**Front:** Why did China attack Vietnam in February 1979, and how long did it last?
**Definition (learn):** China attacked Vietnam in February 1979, partly in retaliation for Vietnam's December 1978 invasion of Kampuchea (which overthrew Pol Pot, a protégé of China), and partly over the frontier; China withdrew after three weeks. Vietnam supported Russia. (✅ owner 17 §8.7A)
**Breakdown:**
- Feb 1979: China attacks Vietnam.
- Trigger: Dec 1978 Vietnamese invasion of Kampuchea.
- Also: frontier dispute.
- Duration: three weeks.
- Alignment: Vietnam supported Russia.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dec 1978; Feb 1979; three weeks. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3134 (Cambodia), WH-3136, WH-3140 (non-monolithic).
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1979 Sino-Vietnamese war with the 1979 Soviet invasion of Afghanistan (WH-3051).
**Mains hook:** "Communist states fighting each other" — the decisive empirical refutation of a monolithic bloc.
**Back:** *"Feb '79: China hits Vietnam, leaves in three weeks."*

### Flashcard WH-3139 — Sino-Soviet reconciliation: July 1985 and May 1989
**Front:** How did the Sino-Soviet rift end, and what was the bookend?
**Definition (learn):** Gorbachev began a new era; five-year trade and economic co-operation agreements were signed in July 1985; a formal reconciliation took place in May 1989 when Gorbachev visited Beijing; Vietnam withdrew from Kampuchea the same year. (✅ owner 17 §8.7A)
**Breakdown:**
- 1984: China's three grievances (Russian troops in Afghanistan; Soviet backing for Vietnam in Kampuchea; Soviet troop build-up on Chinese frontiers of Mongolia and Manchuria).
- July 1985: five-year trade agreements.
- May 1989: Gorbachev in Beijing.
- Same year: Vietnam withdrew from Kampuchea.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1984; July 1985; May 1989. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3129 (Tiananmen, same year), WH-3071 (end of Cold War), WH-3136.
**Trap:** Do not state a 1969 border clash narrative; owner excludes it (§8.9).
**Mains hook:** The bloc reconciled in 1989 — months before it dissolved (⚠️ owner §8.8).
**Back:** *"'85 deals, '89 Beijing summit."*

### Flashcard WH-3140 — Communist bloc was never monolithic (verdict)
**Front:** Why is treating international communism as one actor the commonest analytical error?
**Definition (learn):** China and the USSR quarrelled over doctrine, then strategy, then territory, and finally fought each other's allies. The communist world behaved like a states system, not a church. (⚠️ owner §8.7A formulation)
**Breakdown:**
- Doctrine (1956 onward).
- Strategy (soft US line).
- Territory (Vladivostok; Sinkiang).
- Competition for US favour by 1979.
- Proxy fight (Vietnam–China, 1979).
- Reconciliation (1985–89).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** as above. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3136–WH-3139, WH-3067, WH-3141.
**Trap:** Do not describe the communist world as a single bloc (owner §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Was international communism a single bloc?" — "No" with §8.7A chain.
**Back:** *"States, not church: doctrine, strategy, territory, war."*

### Flashcard WH-3141 — Four distinct communist trajectories in Asia
**Front:** How do China, North Korea, Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos differ?
**Definition (learn):** China: peasant-based revolution, civil-war victory 1949, market reform without democratisation. North Korea: division created the regime; extreme centralisation; dynasty. Vietnam: anti-colonial nationalism fused with communism; reunified 1975–76. Cambodia: Khmer Rouge extremism; starkest warning. Laos: 1975 takeover; quieter and adaptive one-party model. (✅ owner 17 §5, §8.7)
**Breakdown:**
- China: out-governing won (WH-3121).
- North Korea: external division (WH-3132).
- Vietnam: anti-colonial legitimacy (WH-3133).
- Cambodia: ideological radicalism (WH-3134).
- Laos: adaptation (WH-3135).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1949; 1975–76; 1975; Dec 1978. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3132–WH-3135, WH-3140.
**Trap:** "Communism in Asia" is not one story (advanced §5 trap); none is a Chinese copy.
**Mains hook:** "Compare the spread of communism in China with one South-East Asian case." Use the four-part comparison.
**Back:** *"China out-governs, NK divides, Vietnam nationalises, Cambodia radicalises, Laos adapts."*

### Flashcard WH-3142 — Panchsheel (1954), 1962 border war and India's strategic caution
**Front:** What is the India-linkage sequence the owner gives for China?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's sequence is 1949 PRC → 1954 Panchsheel → 1962 border war → long-term strategic caution. (⚠️ advanced §6 synthesis; cross-link Polity)
**Breakdown:**
- 1949: PRC proclaimed (WH-3120).
- 1954: Panchsheel (⚠️ cross-check in Polity).
- 1962: border war (⚠️ not in owner beyond the sequence).
- Long-term strategic caution (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1954; 1962 (owner advanced §6). (✅ owner 17 advanced — ⚠️ details in Polity)
**Links:** WH-3120, WH-3068 (non-alignment), WH-3044.
**Trap:** Do not re-narrate India's China policy here (owner §6); cross-link Polity.
**Mains hook:** Use as a bridge to India's strategic autonomy answer.
**Back:** *"'49 PRC, '54 Panchsheel, '62 war, then caution."*

### Flashcard WH-3143 — CCP Fourth Plenary Session (October 2025): current doctrine link
**Front:** What does the October 2025 CCP Fourth Plenary communique show, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** The official communique (20–23 October 2025) said the meeting adopted recommendations for the Fifteenth Five-Year Plan, framing policy around high-quality development, technological capacity and centralised Party leadership. (📰 owner 17 advanced §7; ⚠️ official Chinese statement, not independent proof of target achievement.)
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 20–23 October 2025.
- Content: recommendations for the Fifteenth Five-Year Plan.
- Doctrine: high-quality development; technological capacity; centralised Party leadership.
- Caution: official statement; not independent verification.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 20–23 Oct 2025 (owner as-of date). (📰 owner)
**Links:** WH-3131 (Deng paradox), WH-3128.
**Trap:** Do not present an official communique as proof of achieved targets (owner §7).
**Mains hook:** Post-Mao economic adaptation without surrender of one-party rule.
**Back:** *"Oct 2025: plan adopted, party still central."*

### Flashcard WH-3144 — China and Asia: Mains frame and probable questions
**Front:** What is the standard answer frame for China/Asian communism questions?
**Definition (learn):** CCP victory: background crisis → KMT failures → CCP rural strategy → effect of Japanese war. Mao's legacy: achievements → policy disasters → social effect → balanced judgement. Spread in Asia: common appeal → local context → regime divergence → conclusion. (✅ owner 17 advanced §9)
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: thesis → two reasons or two Mao-era policies → qualification → verdict.
- 15 marks: thesis → 1911–49 chain → why CCP won → Mao balance → verdict.
- 20 marks: plus reform transition and one non-Chinese Asian comparison.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Probable Q's (owner advanced §9): "Assess the reasons for the communist triumph in 1949"; "Mao made modern China…"; "Compare Chinese communism with Vietnamese or North Korean communism."
**Links:** WH-3113, WH-3121, WH-3130, WH-3141.
**Trap:** Zero direct PYQ routes 2018–2025 (owner §10).
**Mains hook:** Use structure, then one comparative case.
**Back:** *"Crisis, KMT fail, peasants, war; Mao's ledger; compare one."*

### Flashcard WH-3145 — China traps: Deng, 1911, Great Leap vs Cultural Revolution, Cambodia
**Front:** What are the standard traps in the China/Asia topic?
**Definition (learn):** (1) "Deng democratised China" — wrong; (2) "1911 solved the crisis" — wrong; (3) "Great Leap and Cultural Revolution are one event" — wrong (1958–61 vs 1966–76); (4) "Cambodia is just another Vietnam" — wrong; (5) "Tiananmen ended economic reform" — wrong; (6) "Mao and Deng belong to the same policy style" — wrong (campaigns vs pragmatic growth). (✅ owner 17 §6, §8)
**Breakdown:**
- Deng reformed economy, not politics.
- 1911 → warlordism.
- GLF (1958–61) ≠ CR (1966–76).
- Cambodia ≠ Vietnam.
- Tiananmen did not end reform.
- Mao ≠ Deng style.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** as above. (✅ owner 17)
**Links:** WH-3114, WH-3126, WH-3127, WH-3128, WH-3129, WH-3134.
**Trap:** As above.
**Mains hook:** Use traps as conclusions' anti-patterns.
**Back:** *"Deng ≠ democracy; 1911 ≠ peace; GLF ≠ CR; Cambodia ≠ Vietnam."*

### GROUP — Decolonization of Africa and Asia (basic 18 + advanced 18)

### Flashcard WH-3146 — Why the European empires ended after 1945 (interaction, five causes)
**Front:** Why did European empires end after 1945, and what is the safest causal answer?
**Definition (learn):** Empires ended through the interaction of five causes: metropolitan exhaustion, colonial nationalism, ideological delegitimation, international climate and the cost of repression. No single cause is sufficient. (✅ owner 18 §2, §7.4; ⚠️ combined answer)
**Breakdown:**
- Metropolitan exhaustion: less money, coercive capacity and confidence after WWII (WH-3147).
