- Colonial nationalism: elites, workers, soldiers and peasants challenged foreign rule.
- Ideological delegitimation: anti-fascist rhetoric and self-determination (WH-3149).
- International climate: USA, USSR and UN pressure (⚠️ both superpowers also intervened later).
- Cost of repression: the decisive mechanism (WH-3148).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Decolonisation accelerated sharply after 1945 (owner §5). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3026 (Japan widened the war), WH-3028 (WWII consequences), WH-3147, WH-3148, WH-3149.
**Trap:** "Empire ended because Europeans became generous" — wrong (owner §5 trap). "Violent decolonisation was universal" — wrong; some were negotiated (WH-3151).
**Mains hook:** "Analyse the causes of the collapse of the European empires after 1945." Answer as interaction, not a list (owner §7.1).
**Back:** *"Tired, Angry, Shamed, Watched, Costly: five causes, one exit."*

### Flashcard WH-3147 — Metropolitan exhaustion after the Second World War
**Front:** How did WWII weaken the European metropoles' capacity to hold empire?
**Definition (learn):** Britain, France and others had less money, less coercive capacity and less confidence to sustain empire after the war. (✅ owner 18 §2, §7.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Money: reduced.
- Coercive capacity: reduced (supply of repression falls).
- Confidence: reduced.
- Britain, France, Germany and Italy all weakened (WH-3028).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** after 1945. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3028 (end of European dominance), WH-3146, WH-3148.
**Trap:** Exhaustion alone would have produced reform, not withdrawal (owner §7.4 caution).
**Mains hook:** The supply of repression fell; nationalist demand rose — the interaction is the answer.
**Back:** *"Exhaustion cut the supply of force."*

### Flashcard WH-3148 — Cost of repression: Algeria as the decisive mechanism
**Front:** Why does the "cost of repression" count as the decisive mechanism of decolonisation?
**Definition (learn):** Maintaining empire required levels of force that became politically expensive (Lowe's example: Algeria). This mechanism converts the other causes into a decision to leave. (✅ owner 18 §2, §7.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Colonial nationalism raised the demand for repression.
- Metropolitan exhaustion limited supply.
- When the price in force became politically unbearable, withdrawal followed (⚠️ owner analysis).
- The calculation differed by colony, not by empire (⚠️ owner caution).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Algeria: independence 1962 (WH-3154). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3146, WH-3147, WH-3154 (Algeria).
**Trap:** Do not present repression as the only cause; it converts other causes (owner §7.4).
**Mains hook:** "Empire ended where the cost of holding it exceeded the benefit" (⚠️ owner §7.2).
**Back:** *"Repression's price made leaving cheaper."*

### Flashcard WH-3149 — Ideological delegitimation: anti-fascist rhetoric and the myth of invincibility
**Front:** How did WWII ideology and Japanese victories weaken the moral case for empire?
**Definition (learn):** Empire became harder to defend morally after anti-fascist war rhetoric and the language of self-determination; Japanese victories damaged the myth of European invincibility. (✅ owner 18 §2, §7.4; ✅ owner 14 §7)
**Breakdown:**
- Anti-fascist war rhetoric.
- Self-determination language.
- Japanese victories (1941–42) → myth of European invincibility damaged (WH-3014 and WH-3028).
- Asian peoples less willing to return under European rule (owner 14 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1941–45 (Japan's victories). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3028, WH-3146, WH-3026.
**Trap:** Delegitimation is slow and sets no date by itself (owner §7.4 caution).
**Mains hook:** Removed the moral argument at home, not only abroad.
**Back:** *"Self-determination + lost myth = moral retreat."*

### Flashcard WH-3150 — Angola (1975): Cold War extends a civil war
**Front:** How did the Cold War turn Angola's post-independence succession into a long war, and how was it settled?
**Definition (learn):** Civil war broke out "immediately after gaining independence from Portugal in 1975" between the MPLA (Neto), UNITA (Savimbi) and the FNLA. The USA backed the FNLA; Cuba sent troops for the MPLA; South African troops invaded via Namibia; Mobutu sent troops from Zaire. Settlement came with the December 1988 UN-brokered deal trading South African withdrawal from Namibia against the departure of 50,000 Cuban troops. (✅ owner 18 §7.6A #4)
**Breakdown:**
- 1975: independence from Portugal; three-way civil war.
- MPLA (Neto): Marxist-style, appealing across tribal divisions.
- UNITA (Savimbi): Ovimbundu base.
- FNLA: weaker, Bakongo base.
- External patrons: USA (FNLA); Cuba (MPLA); South Africa; Zaire (Mobutu).
- Lowe: fighting would have happened anyway, but outside interference "certainly made the conflict much worse."
- Dec 1988 settlement.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1975; December 1988; 50,000 Cuban troops (owner). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3067 (Cold War non-monolithic), WH-3073 (non-European theatres), WH-3174 (international pressure), WH-3146.
**Trap:** Do not claim the Cold War caused African civil wars; it lengthened a war it did not start (owner §7.6A).
**Mains hook:** "External patrons convert a finite internal contest into an indefinitely resourced one" (owner mechanism).
**Back:** *"Angola: three movements, three patrons, one deal in '88."*

### Flashcard WH-3151 — Negotiated / constitutional transfer: Ghana, 1957
**Front:** What made Ghana the model of a negotiated transfer, and what happened afterwards?
**Definition (learn):** Ghana (Gold Coast) became independent in 1957 — the first black African colony south of the Sahara to gain independence from Britain — through a relatively smooth constitutional transfer. Independence alone did not prevent later economic strain and coups. (✅ owner 18 §5, §7.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Conditions: few settlers; no strategic asset the metropole would fight for; an organised movement able to negotiate.
- Outcome: economic strain; coups (Nkrumah removed by army in 1966, WH-3163).
- Mini-timeline: 1957 Ghana; 1960 Congo; 1962 Algeria.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1957. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3157 (settler variable), WH-3163 (Ghana after independence), WH-3151.
**Trap:** Ghana's smooth transfer ≠ smooth state (owner §7.5, §7.6A #1).
**Mains hook:** "Preparation is necessary but not sufficient" (owner comparison table).
**Back:** *"Ghana 1957: smooth door, rocky house."*

### Flashcard WH-3152 — Negotiated but partitioned: India and Pakistan (1947)
**Front:** What characterised the 1947 transfer in India and Pakistan?
**Definition (learn):** British withdrawal with partition — an organised mass movement plus unresolved communal-political division. Independence came with communal division. (✅ owner 18 §3, §7.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Route: negotiated but partitioned.
- Outcome: two states, 1947.
- Armed conflict: communal violence around partition (⚠️ owner table).
- Indian detail: see Modern-Indian-History cross-link (not duplicated).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1947 (India/Pakistan). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3157, WH-3158 (Malaya), WH-3161 (comparison table); Modern-Indian-History basic/27 (cross-link).
**Trap:** "Partition is the whole story of Asian decolonisation" — wrong (owner §5). Indo-China and Malaya show diverse routes.
**Mains hook:** Power transfer can be fast yet deeply violent (advanced §2).
**Back:** *"1947: quick transfer, divided house."*

### Flashcard WH-3153 — Prolonged anti-colonial war: French Indo-China
**Front:** How did French Indo-China show that decolonisation in Asia could be militarised?
**Definition (learn):** Anti-colonial war weakened French control; the metropole refused concession against an armed nationalist movement. The case feeds into the Vietnamese communist path (WH-3133). (✅ owner 18 §3, §7.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Route: prolonged anti-colonial war.
- Metropolitan refusal to concede.
- Link: Vietnam's communist victory (basic 17 §5).
- Link: Malaya — anti-Japanese resistance became anti-British insurgency (WH-3158).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** no dates in owner for Indo-China war (⚠️ not verified here). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3133 (Vietnam), WH-3146, WH-3187 (wartime resistance → postwar insurgency).
**Trap:** "French Indo-China" is not interchangeable with "Algeria" — Algeria was settler colonialism (WH-3154).
**Mains hook:** Compare routes: non-settler war vs settler war.
**Back:** *"Indo-China: war, not talks."*

### Flashcard WH-3154 — Algeria (1962): settler-colonial war
**Front:** Why was Algeria's decolonisation long and violent?
**Definition (learn):** Settler colonialism made the struggle long and violent; a large settler population legally and politically integrated with the metropole made withdrawal non-negotiable; independence came in 1962 after a prolonged violent struggle. (✅ owner 18 §2, §3, §7.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Route: settler-colonial war.
- Settler variable: large settler population, integrated legally and politically.
- Outcome: independence 1962 (one state).
- Lesson: "Settlers make withdrawal non-negotiable" (owner table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1962. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3148 (cost of repression), WH-3157 (settler variable), WH-3161 (comparison), WH-3151 (Ghana).
**Trap:** Algeria ≠ Ghana (opposite means; same continent, same era).
**Mains hook:** Ghana and Algeria "by opposite means"; the difference lies in whether Europeans lived there (owner §7.5 comparison sentence).
**Back:** *"Algeria 1962: settlers make the war."*

### Flashcard WH-3155 — Late imperial collapse: Portuguese Africa (Angola and Mozambique, 1975)
**Front:** Why did Portuguese Africa decolonise later, and what did its collapse produce?
**Definition (learn):** Portuguese rule collapsed in Angola and Mozambique in 1975, later than many British and French colonies; the metropole was authoritarian and had no domestic mechanism for conceding withdrawal; the result was rapid exit plus factional conflict. (✅ owner 18 §5, §7.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Timing: 1975 (later than British and French colonies).
- Metropole: authoritarian; no domestic mechanism to concede.
- Results: rapid exit; factional conflict (Angola, WH-3150).
- Mozambique: independence 1975 (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1975. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3150 (Angola), WH-3173 (Portuguese independence's effect on South Africa — "surrounded by hostile black states").
**Trap:** "Portuguese Africa decolonised with the British and French" — wrong; it was late.
**Mains hook:** Authoritarian metropoles delayed and then accelerated exit.
**Back:** *"Portugal late, Angola and Mozambique 1975."*

### Flashcard WH-3156 — Hurried exit into crisis: Congo (1960)
**Front:** What made the Congo's independence a classic case of collapse into state crisis?
**Definition (learn):** The Belgians "suddenly allowed" independence with no prepared successor class; there were only 17 graduates; Katanga seceded with the wealthiest part of the state; Lumumba was destroyed as "a dangerous communist"; by mid-1961 there were 20,000 UN troops; General Mobutu took power "using white mercenaries and backed by the USA". (✅ owner 18 §7.6A #3)
**Breakdown:**
- Administrative decapitation: 17 graduates.
- Secession: Katanga (mineral-rich).
- Cold War hostility to elected leader (Lumumba).
- Mobutu's rule; Amnesty International reported at least a thousand political prisoners held without trial and several hundred dead in 1978–79.
- 1981 Nationality Law restricted citizenship to those with family connection at the time of the 1885 Berlin Conference.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1960 (independence); mid-1961 (20,000 UN troops); 17 graduates; 1978–79 (Amnesty); 1981; 1885. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3157 (hurried exit), WH-3150 (Angola), WH-3161 (comparison with Ghana).
**Trap:** Do not treat Congo as typical; Lowe contrasts it with Ghana's preparation (owner §7.6A #3).
**Mains hook:** "A rushed exit with no trained successor class produces state failure, not independence."
**Back:** *"Congo 1960: 17 graduates, one rich secession, one patron."*

### Flashcard WH-3157 — The settler variable: the most useful analytical variable
**Front:** Why is the presence of a settler population the most useful variable for predicting the route of decolonisation?
**Definition (learn):** Whether a substantial settler population and its property claims stood between the colonial power and withdrawal decided how negotiable independence was. Few settlers and no strategic asset → negotiated (Ghana); settlers and strategic assets → war (Algeria). (✅ owner 18 §7.5 callout; ⚠️ synthesis)
**Breakdown:**
- Negotiated: Ghana (few settlers).
- Partitioned: India/Pakistan (organised movement + communal division).
- Settler war: Algeria.
- Late collapse: Portuguese Africa (authoritarian metropole).
- Hurried exit: Congo.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** see route cards. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3151–WH-3156, WH-3158, WH-3161, WH-3182 (West Africa).
**Trap:** "The coloniser's intentions decide" is weaker than the settler variable (owner §7.2 Route).
**Mains hook:** "The variable that decides the route — settler presence and strategic value."
**Back:** *"Settlers decide: no settlers, negotiation; settlers, war."*

### Flashcard WH-3158 — Malaya: plural society and the successor-state problem
**Front:** Why does Malaya constitute a third route to decolonisation?
**Definition (learn):** Britain intended to leave but would not leave to the insurgents; the route is defined by communal composition plus armed communist insurgency. The problem was who would inherit the state, not whether Britain would go. (✅ owner 18 §7.5A, Lowe §24.3(b))
**Breakdown:**
- Territory: nine states each ruled by a sultan; Malacca and Penang (British settlements); Singapore separate.
- Population: multiracial — mostly Malays and Chinese; some Indians and Europeans.
- Economy: exports of rubber and tin; "most prosperous in south-east Asia" (Lowe).
- Federation of Malaya (1948) grouped the states; Singapore remained a separate colony.
- Universal adult suffrage → Malays, the largest group, usually dominated affairs (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Federation of Malaya 1948 (✅ owner 18 §5, §7.5A).
**Links:** WH-3159 (Emergency), WH-3161 (comparison), WH-3160 (Malaysia), WH-3152 (India).
**Trap:** Malaya ≠ Ghana or Algeria; the route is neither negotiated-simple nor settler war (owner §7.5A callout).
**Mains hook:** "Malaya's difficulty was not persuading Britain to go but deciding who would be there when it went." (⚠️ owner verdict)
**Back:** *"Malaya: many communities, one rubber-tin state."*

### Flashcard WH-3159 — Malayan Emergency (1948–60) and the promise of independence
**Front:** How did Britain defeat the Malayan communist insurgency?
**Definition (learn):** A state of emergency was declared in 1948 and remained in force until 1960. Chinese communist guerrillas led by Chin Peng, who had resisted the Japanese, turned on the British. Britain resettled suspected Chinese into specially guarded villages and made clear that independence would follow as soon as the country was ready. (✅ owner 18 §7.5A)
**Breakdown:**
- 1948: emergency declared.
- Insurgents: Chinese communist guerrillas (Chin Peng); anti-Japanese resistance became anti-British insurgency.
- Tactic: resettlement of suspected helpers into guarded villages.
- Political promise: independence "as soon as the country was ready"; kept Malays pro-British.
- Alliance Party (Tunku Abdul Rahman) won 51 of 52 seats in 1955.
- Full independence 1957; admitted to the Commonwealth.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1948–60 emergency; 1955 (51 of 52); 1957 independence. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3187 (wartime → insurgency), WH-3158, WH-3160.
**Trap:** Do not present the Emergency as repression alone — the credible political timetable was the decisive mechanism (owner §7.5A).
**Mains hook:** Counter-insurgency plus a credible independence promise.
**Back:** *"Resettle, promise, win: Malaya '48–'60."*

### Flashcard WH-3160 — Malaysia (1963), Singapore's exit (1965), Brunei (1984)
**Front:** How did Malaya's decolonisation continue after 1957?
**Definition (learn):** The Federation of Malaysia was proposed by the Tunku in 1961 and proclaimed in September 1963, joining Malaya with Singapore, North Borneo (Sabah), Brunei and Sarawak, after a UN investigation team reported a large majority in favour. Brunei decided not to join and became independent within the Commonwealth in 1984. Singapore left in 1965 to become an independent republic. (✅ owner 18 §7.5A)
**Breakdown:**
- 1961: Tunku proposes Malaysia.
- Sept 1963: Federation of Malaysia proclaimed (with Singapore, Sabah, Brunei, Sarawak).
- Brunei: did not join; independent 1984.
- Singapore: separate republic 1965.
- Shape of successor state contested for eight years (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1961; September 1963; 1965; 1984. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3158, WH-3159.
**Trap:** Singapore's 1965 exit ≠ Federation's 1948 exclusion of Singapore (separate colony at the start). Do not mix.
**Mains hook:** Decolonisation did not end at independence — the successor state's shape was settled by separation, not war.
**Back:** *"'63 Malaysia formed, '65 Singapore exits, '84 Brunei independent (never joined)."*

### Flashcard WH-3161 — Comparison table: India, Ghana, Algeria and Malaya
**Front:** How do India, Ghana, Algeria and Malaya differ on the core problem, coloniser stance, decisive instrument and outcome?
**Definition (learn):** India — communal division inside a mass movement; ended by negotiation and partition. Ghana — building a state on a single commodity; mass campaign and constitutional preparation. Algeria — a settler population; prolonged war. Malaya — which community inherits a state not yet in existence; counter-insurgency plus a credible independence promise. (✅ owner 18 §7.5A comparison table)
**Breakdown:**
- Outcomes: India two states 1947; Ghana one state 1957; Algeria one state 1962; Malaya Federation 1957 → Malaysia 1963 → Singapore separate 1965.
- Armed conflict: India communal violence (⚠️); Ghana limited (boycotts, demonstrations, general strike); Algeria full colonial war; Malaya insurgency against coloniser and majority's future state.
- Teaching line: partition as price of deferred question; preparation necessary but not sufficient; settlers make withdrawal non-negotiable; plural society → contest over boundaries and franchise.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** as above. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3151–WH-3159.
**Trap:** Don't generalise one route to all; owner stresses diverse routes.
**Mains hook:** "Compare a relatively peaceful case of decolonisation with a violent one."
**Back:** *"India splits, Ghana prepares, Algeria fights, Malaya franchises."*

### Flashcard WH-3162 — Why post-colonial states struggled: inherited constraints (structural, not cultural)
**Front:** What six inherited constraints explain post-colonial instability?
**Definition (learn):** Arbitrary borders; narrow economies; thin administrative capacity; authoritarian drift; external intervention; colonial identity-fixing (chiefs). The causal formula: colonial borders + weak institutions + mono-crop economies + army/elite rivalry + Cold War interference → coups, civil wars and unstable states. (✅ owner 18 §4, §7.6; ⚠️ identity-fixing mechanism from basic/07 §8.7)
**Breakdown:**
- Borders: colonial creations grouping communities with weak shared identity (basic/07).
- Narrow economies: few exports; price shocks become regime-threatening (basic/20).
- Thin administration: too few officials; army most organised institution.
- Authoritarian drift: one-party rule; coups; corruption.
- External intervention: Cold War arms and money.
- Identity-fixing: colonial chiefs hardened fluid identities (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none additional. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3163 (Ghana), WH-3164 (Nigeria), WH-3165 (Rwanda), WH-3166 (Zimbabwe), WH-3150 (Angola).
**Trap:** "Post-colonial instability is tribalism" — wrong; Lowe points to borders, institutions, military politics and intervention (owner §7 trap). Structural, not cultural (owner §7.1).
**Mains hook:** "Why did independent Africa face coups, civil wars and one-party rule so often?"
**Back:** *"Borders, Brains, Beans, Boots, Big-power meddling."*

### Flashcard WH-3163 — Ghana after independence: Nkrumah, cocoa and the closing of politics
**Front:** What were Nkrumah's achievements and failures in Ghana (1957–66)?
**Definition (learn):** Nkrumah ruled from 1957 until the army removed him in 1966. He achieved real gains (cocoa output doubled, Volta dam begun 1961), but dependence on cocoa exports turned a world price fall into a huge balance-of-payments deficit, and from 1959 he closed politics (deportations and jail without trial; in 1964 all parties except his own banned). (✅ owner 18 §7.6A #1)
**Breakdown:**
- Achievements: cocoa output doubled; forestry, fishing, cattle-breeding expanded; gold and bauxite better exploited; Volta dam begun 1961 (irrigation and hydro-electric power); village road and school projects.
- Failures: cocoa dependence → payments deficit after a steep fall in world cocoa price; 1959 detention without trial; 1964 one-party ban; personal fortune through corruption (owner).
- Lowe: Ghana had a small but well-educated group of politicians who gained five years' government experience before 1957 — "unique to Ghana".
- Lowe: the American CIA gave the 1966 coup its full backing because the USA disapproved of Nkrumah's links with communist states (attributed to Lowe).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1957–66; 1959; 1961; 1964; 1966. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3151 (Ghana route), WH-3162 (structural constraints), WH-3179 (Pan-Africanism), WH-3180 (elite).
**Trap:** Ghana had the best preparation in British Africa and still failed — "they were not ready" is not the explanation (owner §7.6A #1).
**Mains hook:** A single-commodity export base converts a price fall into a fiscal crisis; closing politics leaves the army as the only instrument of change.
**Back:** *"Ghana: cocoa up, politics down, army in."*

### Flashcard WH-3164 — Nigeria and Biafra (1960–70): federal design meets territorial identity
**Front:** Why did Nigeria's federal constitution fail to contain regional identity, and what did the Biafra war cost?
**Definition (learn):** Nigeria became independent in 1960 as Britain's largest African colony, with a federal constitution of three regions. Regions felt the centre did not protect them; recession by 1964; the 1964 elections were accused of being "fixed"; a January 1966 coup by mainly Ibo officers killed Balewa; massacres of Ibos followed in the north; Colonel Ojukwu declared the eastern region independent as Biafra (May 1967); the war lasted to January 1970. (✅ owner 18 §7.6A #2)
**Breakdown:**
- Independence: 1960.
- Recession 1964 (prices up 15 per cent; wages below calculated minimum living wage).
- Coup: January 1966 (Balewa killed).
- Biafra: declared May 1967.
- Outside arms: Britain and USSR armed the federal side; France armed Biafra.
- Lowe: "Biafra lost more civilians from disease and starvation than troops killed in the fighting."
- The UN, the Commonwealth and the OAU could not mediate.
- Counter-evidence: recovery after 1970 "remarkably swift"; Gowon took no revenge, reconciled Ibos, expanded states from 12 to 19.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1960; January 1966; May 1967; January 1970; 15 per cent; 12 → 19 states. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3162, WH-3150 (Angola: outside arms), WH-3167 (civil war definition).
**Trap:** Ethnic division is not a permanent verdict — institutional redesign worked after 1970 (owner §7.6A #2 counter-evidence).
**Mains hook:** "Federalism distributes power territorially; where identity is also territorial, every distributive dispute becomes a secession question."
**Back:** *"Nigeria 1960, Biafra 1967, war to 1970, states 12 → 19."*

### Flashcard WH-3165 — Burundi (1993) and Rwanda (1994): identity made into genocide
**Front:** How does Lowe's account of Burundi and Rwanda show that "tribal conflict" is a political construction?
**Definition (learn):** Tutsi and Hutu spoke the same language and looked alike; Hutu were the majority and Tutsi the elite ruling group, differentiated by occupation (cattle vs crops). Burundi: a Hutu president elected in the first democratic elections of 1993 was murdered by Tutsi soldiers in October 1993, and reprisal killings followed. Rwanda: fighting from 1990 between the Tutsi-dominated FPR and the Hutu-dominated army; an Arusha settlement in 1993 with 2,500 UN troops; in April 1994 the aircraft carrying the Rwandan and Burundian presidents was brought down; the Interahamwe militia launched a campaign of genocide; about 800,000 Tutsi and moderate Hutu were murdered, backed by the Hutu government; about a million Tutsi refugees fled to Tanzania and Zaire. (✅ owner 18 §7.6A #5)
**Breakdown:**
- Burundi: OAU sent a peacekeeping force ("first time it had ever taken such action") and could not stop massacres; great powers "showed little concern".
- Burundi 1993: about 50,000 Tutsi killed (owner §7.9 permitted list).
- Rwanda 1994: UN force "not equipped" and soon withdrew.
- Mechanism: colonial and post-colonial politics hardened a social-occupational hierarchy into a political identity; competitive elections made it the currency of power; militia and state backing converted violence into genocide.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Oct 1993 (Burundi); 1990; 1993 Arusha; April 1994; ~800,000; ~1 million refugees. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3162 (colonial identity-fixing), WH-3167 (genocide vs civil war), WH-3150 (external arms and money).
**Trap:** Do not call Rwanda 1994 "tribal violence" or "civil war" without qualification; it is genocide on evidence of intent, planning and state backing (owner §7.7A).
**Mains hook:** "'Tribal conflict' is a political construction with a datable history, not an ancient given."
**Back:** *"Same tongue, different jobs, made into rival identities; 1994 genocide."*

### Flashcard WH-3166 — Zimbabwe (1980): Lancaster House and the unresolved land question
**Front:** What did the 1979 Lancaster House settlement and 1980 independence secure, and what did they leave unresolved?
**Definition (learn):** Lancaster House Conference (1979) led to independence in 1980. Mugabe's first decade earned praise for moderation and reconciliation; the Lancaster House guarantee gave whites 20 of 100 parliamentary seats. Later: ZANU–ZAPU hostility, Nkomo's forced resignation in 1982, suppression in Matabeleland, the 1987 Unity Accord; IMF structural adjustment cuts; about 4,000 white farmers still owned about half the arable land; from February 2000 violent occupation of white-owned farms, "clearly a deliberate policy organized by the government". (✅ owner 18 §7.6A #6)
**Breakdown:**
- 1979: Lancaster House Conference.
- 1980: independence.
- Early achievements: wage increases, food subsidies, better health and education.
- Constraint: 20 of 100 parliamentary seats for whites.
- 1982: Nkomo's forced resignation; 1987: Unity Accord.
- Feb 2000: land occupations after rejection of Mugabe's draft constitution (owner: political strategy under electoral pressure).
- Britain offered extra compensation if land went "to ordinary peasant farmers rather than to members of Mugabe's ruling elite" (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1979; 1980; 20 of 100 seats; 1982; 1987; February 2000; ~4,000 farmers; about half of arable land. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3162, WH-3167, WH-3162 (land question), WH-3179 (Pan-Africanism context).
**Trap:** Do not read 1980 backwards from 2000 — Lowe records genuine early achievements (owner §7.6A #6).
**Mains hook:** "A settlement that secures political power while leaving the colonial distribution of productive assets untouched leaves the land question available as a political instrument."
**Back:** *"Zimbabwe 1980: power won, land deferred."*

### Flashcard WH-3167 — Genocide, apartheid, civil war and neo-colonialism: keep them apart
**Front:** How should the four terms (genocide, apartheid, civil war, neo-colonialism) be distinguished?
**Definition (learn):** Genocide: intent to destroy a group as such, with planning and state backing (Rwanda 1994). Apartheid: legal construction and maintenance of racial hierarchy (South Africa from 1948). Civil war: organised armed contest for control of the state or for secession (Nigeria 1967–70; Angola from 1975; Congo/DRC from 1998). Neo-colonialism: continuing economic dependence after formal sovereignty. (✅ owner 18 §7.7A)
**Breakdown:**
- Test for genocide: intent to destroy group; planning; state backing.
- Test for apartheid: statutory hierarchy, not extermination.
- Test for civil war: armed contest for state control or secession; not one-sided mass killing.
- Test for neo-colonialism: after sovereignty; not formal rule.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rwanda 1994; SA 1948; Nigeria 1967–70; Angola 1975; Congo/DRC 1998 (owner's list).
**Links:** WH-3165, WH-3168, WH-3164, WH-3150, WH-3181.
**Trap:** Do not use "genocide" for apartheid, nor "apartheid" as a loose synonym for discrimination; do not call Rwanda "civil war" without qualification (owner §7.7A).
**Mains hook:** Precision of category marks the answer.
**Back:** *"Genocide = intent; apartheid = law; civil war = contest; neo-colonialism = after flag."*

### Flashcard WH-3168 — Apartheid: origins (1910, 1948) and the statutory system
**Front:** How did the apartheid system come about in 1948, and what did it codify?
**Definition (learn):** Apartheid codified and extended an existing racial hierarchy; the Afrikaner Nationalist Party under Dr Malan won the 1948 election promising to preserve white supremacy. The Union of South Africa (1910) was about 70 per cent black African, 18 per cent white, 9 per cent "coloured" and 3 per cent Asian. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(a))
**Breakdown:**
- Pre-existing order: reserved areas about 7 per cent of country; pass laws; 1911 law forbade black workers' strikes and skilled jobs.
- Trigger: India and Pakistan's 1947 independence alarmed white South Africans — apartheid as reaction to decolonisation elsewhere.
- Vehicle: Afrikaner Nationalist Party (Malan), 1948 win; Dutch Reformed Church support; Broederbond (secret organisation).
- Doctrine + party + church + secret organisation = a system.
- Separation at every level: reserves, townships, facilities, schools (inferior education for blacks); classification and identity cards; pass laws; ban on interracial marriage and sexual relations.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1910; 1911; 1947; 1948; ~7 per cent reserves. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3169 (contradiction), WH-3170 (resistance), WH-3167 (apartheid definition), WH-3152 (India/Pakistan 1947).
**Trap:** Apartheid did not "invent" racial hierarchy; it codified (owner).
**Mains hook:** Apartheid as a reaction to decolonisation — why it belongs in this topic.
**Back:** *"1947 alarm, 1948 law, 1910 roots."*

### Flashcard WH-3169 — Apartheid's internal contradiction: economy vs exclusion
**Front:** What is the single best analytical sentence on apartheid, and what did Bantustans involve?
**Definition (learn):** Lowe: "complete separation was impossible because over half the non-white population worked in white-owned mines, factories and other businesses; the economy would have collapsed if all non-whites had been moved to reserves." The system's economic dependence on the people it excluded made it unsustainable. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(b))
**Breakdown:**
- Bantu Self-Government Act (1959): seven "homelands".
- Transkei declared "independent" in 1969; Bophuthatswana and Venda by 1980.
- Bantustans: about 13 per cent of the country for over 8 million people; "vastly overcrowded"; "very little better than rural slums"; "the outside world dismissed this with contempt".
- Africans lost all political rights; white-held parliamentary representation for Africans abolished.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1959; 1969; 1980; 13 per cent; 8 million. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3168, WH-3176 (Botha), WH-3177 (transition), WH-3178 (economic apartheid).
**Trap:** Bantustans were not real independence; the "independent" label was a fiction (owner: outside world dismissed).
**Mains hook:** "Apartheid failed because it could neither dispense with black labour nor survive the loss of its regional buffer states, international respectability and its own church's blessing." (⚠️ owner verdict)
**Back:** *"Labour kept it; homelands could not."*

### Flashcard WH-3170 — Non-violent protest: Luthuli, Defiance and the Freedom Charter
**Front:** What constitutional protest did the South African movement undertake before 1960?
**Definition (learn):** Chief Albert Luthuli organised stay-away protests; the 1952 defiance campaign led to over 8,000 arrests and floggings; the 1955 Kliptown meeting proclaimed the Freedom Charter ("South Africa belongs to all who live in it, black and white"); the 1957 bus boycott was sustained for three months. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(c))
**Breakdown:**
- Stay-away protests (Luthuli).
- 1952 defiance: over 8,000 arrests and floggings.
- 1955 Kliptown: Freedom Charter (equality before law; freedoms of assembly, movement, speech, religion, press; vote; equal pay; 40-hour week; minimum wage; unemployment benefit; free medical care; free compulsory equal education).
- 1957 bus boycott (three months).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1952; 1955; 1957; 8,000+. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3171 (Sharpeville), WH-3172 (armed turn), WH-3180 (elite plus mass).
**Trap:** The Freedom Charter claimed inclusion, not reversal of white citizenship (owner: "constitutional document").
**Mains hook:** Shows the movement's claim was inclusion, not reversal.
**Back:** *"1955 Charter: all who live here."*

### Flashcard WH-3171 — Sharpeville (1960): repression produced the armed turn
**Front:** What happened at Sharpeville in 1960 and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** Police fired on an anti-pass-law demonstration at Sharpeville in 1960, killing 67 and wounding many; 15,000 arrests followed; the brutal treatment convinced many black leaders that violence could only be met with violence. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(c))
**Breakdown:**
- Event: police fire on anti-pass demonstration.
- Deaths: 67; wounded: many.
- Aftermath: 15,000 arrests.
- Result: armed turn (causal claim).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1960; 67 killed; 15,000 arrests. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3170, WH-3172 (MK 1961), WH-3168.
**Trap:** Sharpeville (1960) ≠ Soweto (1976) — different turning points.
**Mains hook:** "Repression, not doctrine, produced the armed turn" — the causal claim to make.
**Back:** *"Sharpeville '60: 67 dead, violence chosen."*

### Flashcard WH-3172 — Umkhonto we Sizwe (1961) and the twin strategies
**Front:** What was Umkhonto we Sizwe, who was prominent, and what was the sentence?
**Definition (learn):** Umkhonto we Sizwe (MK) sabotaged strategic targets, with bomb attacks in Johannesburg, Port Elizabeth and Durban in 1961; Nelson Mandela was prominent and was sentenced to life imprisonment on Robben Island; Luthuli continued non-violent protest and was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(c))
**Breakdown:**
- MK (1961): sabotage; bomb attacks in three cities.
- Mandela: life imprisonment; Robben Island.
- Luthuli: non-violent; *Let My People Go*; Nobel Peace Prize.
- Both strategies persisted simultaneously.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1961. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3171, WH-3170, WH-3177 (Mandela released 1990).
**Trap:** Do not write a simple shift from non-violence to violence (owner).
**Mains hook:** "Critically examine" — balance both tracks.
**Back:** *"'61 MK bombs; Mandela jailed; Luthuli prizes."*

### Flashcard WH-3173 — Soweto (1976) and the death of Steve Biko (1977)
**Front:** What was the Soweto uprising and what does it show about state policy?
**Definition (learn):** Mass demonstrations in 1976 against Afrikaans as the language of instruction; police opened fire, killing at least 200; protests spread nationwide and a further 500 were killed over six months; Steve Biko, "encouraging young people to be proud of their blackness", was beaten to death by police in 1977. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(c))
**Breakdown:**
- Trigger: Afrikaans as medium of instruction.
- Deaths: at least 200 at first; further 500 in six months.
- Biko: beaten to death in police custody in 1977 (owner: "beaten to death by police").
- Lesson: the state's own education policy created the generation that broke it.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1976 (Soweto); 200; 500; 1977 (Biko). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3171, WH-3170, WH-3176 (Botha reforms).
**Trap:** Soweto (1976) is not Sharpeville (1960).
**Mains hook:** A generational, education-based revolt.
**Back:** *"Soweto '76: Afrikaans lessons, 200 dead."*

### Flashcard WH-3174 — International pressure on apartheid: Commonwealth, UN, OAU and the trade gap
**Front:** What international measures targeted apartheid, and where did they fail?
**Definition (learn):** The Commonwealth: Macmillan's 1960 "wind of change" speech in Cape Town; criticism at the 1961 Commonwealth Conference led Verwoerd to withdraw South Africa's application for continued membership (ending its Commonwealth status). UN and OAU condemned apartheid; the UN voted an economic boycott in 1962 but it proved useless because not all states supported it. Britain, the USA, France, West Germany and Italy condemned apartheid publicly but kept trading, and sold arms, hoping South Africa would be a bastion against communism. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(d))
**Breakdown:**
- 1960: Macmillan, "wind of change", Cape Town.
- 1961: SA leaves the Commonwealth (Verwoerd withdraws application).
- 1962: UN economic boycott — ineffective.
- Cold War trade contradiction: public condemnation, private arms and trade.
- Regional change: Angola and Mozambique 1975; Zimbabwe 1980 left SA "surrounded by hostile black states".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1960; 1961; 1962; 1975; 1980. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3175 (sanctions 1986), WH-3150 (Cold War in Africa), WH-3166 (Zimbabwe), WH-3067 (bloc logic).
**Trap:** "The West opposed apartheid" is incomplete — Cold War priorities protected it for two decades (owner's essential qualification).
**Mains hook:** Cold War priorities delayed sanctions.
**Back:** *"Wind of change, boycott fails, trade goes on."*

### Flashcard WH-3175 — Sanctions bite: August and September 1986
**Front:** What sanctions package did the Commonwealth agree in August 1986, and what did the US Congress do in September 1986?
**Definition (learn):** In August 1986 the Commonwealth (except Britain) agreed a strong package: no further loans; no sales of oil, computer equipment or nuclear goods; no cultural and scientific contacts. Margaret Thatcher committed Britain only to a voluntary investment ban, arguing severe sanctions would cost black Africans their jobs; Rajiv Gandhi accused her of compromising on basic principles. In September 1986 the US Congress voted over President Reagan's veto to stop loans, cut air links and ban imports of South African iron, steel, coal, textiles and uranium. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(d))
**Breakdown:**
- Aug 1986: Commonwealth (except Britain) package.
- Britain: voluntary investment ban only (Thatcher).
- Rajiv Gandhi's criticism.
- Sept 1986: US Congress overrides Reagan veto.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1986; September 1986. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3174, WH-3176 (Botha 1986 pass law abolition), WH-3177.
**Trap:** Britain (Thatcher) was not part of the August 1986 Commonwealth package; keep the exception.
**Mains hook:** Sanctions became decisive once regional change and Cold War logic shifted.
**Back:** *"Aug '86 Commonwealth; Sept '86 Congress overrides."*

### Flashcard WH-3176 — Botha's reform chain (1979–89): reform to preserve control
**Front:** What did P. W. Botha reform from 1979, and why did partial reform fail?
**Definition (learn):** Botha reformed apartheid in order to preserve white control: unions and strikes legalised for blacks (1979); township councils (1981); tricameral constitution for whites, coloureds and Asians but not Africans (1984); mixed marriage allowed (1985); pass laws abolished (1986). Concessions refused the vote; anger rose; states of emergency in 1985 and 1986; mass arrests; reporting banned; de Klerk (1989) concluded apartheid must go completely. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(e))
**Breakdown:**
- Why Botha moved: Commonwealth/UN/OAU criticism; late-1970s recession and white emigration; failure of homelands; American criticism.
- Botha's September 1979 words: "Either we adapt or we perish. White domination and legally enforced apartheid are a recipe for permanent conflict."
- Why partial reform failed: no vote; new constitution excluded Africans; "black Africans were incensed".
- Lowe's generalisation: an authoritarian regime that tries to reform itself cannot stop the process of change (same as USSR under Gorbachev) — link to WH-3071.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1979; 1981; 1984; 1985; 1986; 1989. Whites 16 per cent by 1980 (vs 21 per cent between wars). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3071 (Gorbachev parallel), WH-3177, WH-3175.
**Trap:** Do not treat Botha's reforms as democratic reform; they excluded the vote for Africans.
**Mains hook:** "Reform does not stop halfway" — the Gorbachev parallel.
**Back:** *"Botha: adapt to survive, but no vote."*

### Flashcard WH-3177 — Negotiated transition to majority rule (1989–94)
**Front:** Sequence the negotiated transition from de Klerk to Mandela's presidency.
**Definition (learn):** de Klerk (1989) concluded that apartheid had to go; Mandela released in 1990 after 27 years; ANC legalised; apartheid laws dropped; Namibia independent in 1990; constitutional talks from 1991; 1993 power-sharing agreement; the ANC won almost two-thirds in the 1994 election; a coalition of ANC, National Party and Inkatha; Mandela president in 1994. (✅ owner 18 §7.7(e))
**Breakdown:**
- Constraints: de Klerk faced right-wing Afrikaner opposition and his own National Party; ANC power struggle with Inkatha (Buthelezi).
- ANC reassurance: disclaimed wholesale nationalisation; Mandela condemned violence and called for reconciliation.
- Outcome: transition to majority rule "without civil war" — the analytical achievement.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1989; 1990 (Mandela released after 27 years); 1990 (Namibia); 1991 (talks); 1993 (power-sharing); 1994 (election and Mandela presidency). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3176, WH-3172, WH-3178.
**Trap:** Do not state the transition was achieved by one actor alone (de Klerk and Mandela both mattered; owner).
**Mains hook:** Why negotiated transition avoided civil war (owner's verdict).
**Back:** *"'89 de Klerk, '90 Mandela, '94 Mandela president."*

### Flashcard WH-3178 — Post-1994 South Africa: political apartheid ended, economic apartheid did not
**Front:** What did the TRC choose, what delivery was achieved, and what persisted?
**Definition (learn):** The Truth and Reconciliation Commission, assisted by Tutu, chose amnesty and acknowledgement over revenge. Real delivery: 70 per cent of black households had electricity; access to pure water rose by 9 million people since 1994; education free and compulsory; exports diversified away from gold; inflation down to 4 per cent. Persistent problems: high unemployment, crime, job-loss protest, growth slowing to 1.5 per cent in 2001 from 3.1 in 2000, the AIDS crisis, migration pressure and the anti-immigrant riots of May 2008 (at least 80 killed). (✅ owner 18 §7.7(f))
**Breakdown:**
- TRC: amnesty and acknowledgement.
- Delivery: electricity; water; education; exports; inflation.
- Problems: unemployment; crime; privatisation protests; growth slowdown; AIDS; migration riots.
- Verdict: political apartheid ended completely and at once; economic apartheid did not (⚠️ owner §7.7(f)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 70 per cent; 9 million; 1.5 per cent (2001); 3.1 per cent (2000); 4 per cent inflation; May 2008; at least 80 killed. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3177, WH-3169, WH-3186 (transfer vs rupture).
**Trap:** Do not describe post-2013 South African politics from this file (owner §7.9).
**Mains hook:** "Political independence without economic decolonisation" applied to a state that never left the map.
**Back:** *"Politics freed, economy still apartheid-shaped."*

### Flashcard WH-3179 — Pan-Africanism and the OAU (1963): Nkrumah's federal dream
**Front:** What was Nkrumah's pan-African project and why did it fail?
**Definition (learn):** Nkrumah was a strong believer in pan-Africanism and sought a continental federation; he supported the Organization of African Unity (1963); an economic union with Guinea and Mali was formed "though nothing much came of it"; his dream of an African federal state quickly faded; other leaders suspected he was pursuing his own leadership. (✅ owner 18 §7.6B)
**Breakdown:**
- Continental federation: aim.
- OAU (1963): supported.
- Economic union with Guinea and Mali: little result.
- Limitation: territorial states inherited could not accommodate transnational ambition.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1963 (OAU). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3163 (Ghana), WH-3180 (elite), WH-3165 (OAU in Burundi).
**Trap:** Do not present the OAU as a successful peace or federation project (owner: OAU could not stop Burundi massacres).
**Mains hook:** The elite's transnational project failed against the territorial states they inherited.
**Back:** *"Nkrumah's United States of Africa faded; OAU survived."*

### Flashcard WH-3180 — Western-educated elite plus urban mass action
**Front:** Why did African nationalism need both an educated elite and urban mass action?
**Definition (learn):** Education abroad (Britain and the USA) gave Africans the vocabulary and awareness of racial discrimination; named leaders: Kwame Nkrumah (Convention People's Party from 1949), Nnamdi Azikiwe ("Zik"), Julius Nyerere (TANU), and Marcus Garvey (self-educated Jamaican; Universal Negro Improvement Association). The press, the party and the general strike were the elite's instruments; urban workers supplied disruptive capacity. (✅ owner 18 §7.6B)
**Breakdown:**
- Education abroad created the leadership.
- Nkrumah: 1950 boycotts, violent demonstrations and general strike; released by British "realizing that he had mass support".
- Azikiwe: 1945 general strike in Nigeria "enough to prompt the British" to prepare independence.
- Mass of Africans in new towns "particularly receptive".
- Correction: elite did not win by argument alone; combined argument with urban mass action.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1945 (Azikiwe strike); 1949 (CPP); 1950 (Nkrumah strike). (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3170, WH-3179, WH-3182 (West Africa first).
**Trap:** Do not homogenise: Garvey is self-educated; not all were Western-educated in the same way.
**Mains hook:** Education explains the leadership, not the mass force.
**Back:** *"Elite speaks, workers strike."*

### Flashcard WH-3181 — Neo-colonialism: economic influence after formal sovereignty
**Front:** What does neo-colonialism mean in Lowe's usage?
**Definition (learn):** The practice of exercising influence over former colonies after independence by economic means; Britain expected to exercise influence through trade links; the term "became widespread in most of the new states of the Third World". (✅ owner 18 §7.6B)
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: economic influence through trade.
- Scope: most new Third World states.
- Not formal colonial rule.
- Lowe presents it as a description of a practice, not a completed theory; do not attribute it to a named theorist from this file (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3167, WH-3186, WH-3162.
**Trap:** Neo-colonialism ≠ continuing formal rule (owner §7.7A).
**Mains hook:** Concept for the gap between formal and economic independence.
**Back:** *"Neo-colonial = influence without flags."*

### Flashcard WH-3182 — West Africa moved first: few Europeans and no settler estates
**Front:** Why did West Africa move to independence comparatively straightforwardly?
**Definition (learn):** In West Africa there were relatively few Europeans, and they tended to be administrators rather than permanent settlers with profitable estates to defend; this made the move to independence comparatively straightforward (owner quoting Lowe). (✅ owner 18 §7.6B)
**Breakdown:**
- Settler variable explains the region.
- Educated elite explains the leadership (WH-3180).
- Keep the two variables separate.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3157 (settler variable), WH-3180, WH-3151 (Ghana).
**Trap:** Do not merge "why the region moved first" with "who led it".
**Mains hook:** Region vs leadership.
**Back:** *"Few settlers, early exit."*

### Flashcard WH-3183 — Lowe's verdict debate: Brendon and Gott vs Ferguson
**Front:** What is the historiographical dispute in Lowe's verdict chapter on decolonisation?
**Definition (learn):** Piers Brendon and Richard Gott stress coercion, extraction and imperial violence; Niall Ferguson emphasises legal, commercial and institutional legacies. The two positions must not be treated as equivalent by default; a balanced answer tests legacies against who benefited, the violence of conquest and rule, and the constrained sovereignty inherited at independence. (✅ owner 18 advanced §3)
**Breakdown:**
- Brendon, Gott: coercion, extraction, violence.
- Ferguson: legal, commercial, institutional legacy.
- Test: who benefited; violence; constrained sovereignty.
- Lowe: the problem is not "unready" peoples; empire left artificial borders, distorted economies, narrow administrative capacity, elite-dominated states (advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3162, WH-3186, WH-3146.
**Trap:** Do not quote one side as settled consensus (advanced §7 trap).
**Mains hook:** Political freedom historically legitimate and unavoidable; state-building after freedom immensely difficult (owner UPSC-safe synthesis).
**Back:** *"Brendon/Gott: extraction. Ferguson: legacy. Test both."*

### Flashcard WH-3184 — African Union 2025 reparations theme (current afterlife)
**Front:** What current anchor links decolonisation to modern reparations debates?
**Definition (learn):** In 2025 the African Union officially adopted "Justice for Africans and People of African Descent Through Reparations" as its theme of the year. (📰 owner 18 advanced §6; ⚠️ continuity hook only.)
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 2025 (theme of the year).
- Debates: memory, reparations, return of artefacts, whether political independence ended colonial inequality.
- Static answer must rest on the historical process itself.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025. (📰 owner)
**Links:** WH-3183, WH-3186.
**Trap:** Do not use this as a substitute for historical analysis (owner advanced §6).
**Mains hook:** Use as a continuity hook in a conclusion.
**Back:** *"2025: AU reparations theme."*

### Flashcard WH-3185 — Decolonisation traps: peace, generosity, mistake, partition
**Front:** What are the standard traps about decolonisation?
**Definition (learn):** (1) "All colonies became independent peacefully" — many did not; (2) "Empire ended because Europeans became generous" — nationalism and global pressure were central; (3) "African problems prove decolonisation was a mistake" — Lowe treats them as state-building crises; (4) "Partition is the whole story of Asian decolonisation" — Indo-China and Malaya show diversity; (5) "Political empire ended faster than economic dependence" — correct; economic dependence persisted. (✅ owner 18 §5, §7.9, advanced §7)
**Breakdown:**
- Trap 1: peaceful everywhere — false.
- Trap 2: generosity — false.
- Trap 3: mistake — false.
- Trap 4: partition as whole story — false.
- Trap 5: political vs economic ending (advanced §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3146, WH-3152, WH-3153, WH-3158, WH-3186.
**Trap:** As above.
**Mains hook:** Use these as anti-patterns in conclusions.
**Back:** *"Not peaceful, not generous, not a mistake, not only partition."*

### Flashcard WH-3186 — Transfer or rupture? Political decolonisation vs economic dependence
**Front:** Was decolonisation a transfer or a rupture?
**Definition (learn):** A real rupture in sovereignty and a much weaker one in economic structure — political decolonisation was rapid; economic decolonisation was not; formal independence coexisted with continuing commodity dependence. (⚠️ owner §7.2 continuity thesis, §7.8 transfer-or-rupture scaffold)
**Breakdown:**
- Sovereignty: real rupture.
- Economy: continuity (commodity dependence; inherited borders; outside leverage).
- Evidence: Ghana's cocoa (WH-3163); Nigeria's oil and coup history (WH-3164); Zimbabwe's land (WH-3166); South Africa's economic apartheid (WH-3178).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3162, WH-3178, WH-3181, WH-3183.
**Trap:** Do not give a verdict without evidence (owner §7.1).
**Mains hook:** "Decolonisation transferred sovereignty faster than it built stable states." Discuss.
**Back:** *"Flags moved fast; economies moved slowly."*

### Flashcard WH-3187 — Wartime resistance becomes post-war insurgency (Malaya and Indo-China)
**Front:** How did wartime anti-Japanese resistance become post-war insurgency?
**Definition (learn):** The Chinese communist guerrillas who had led the anti-Japanese resistance in Malaya turned on the British in 1948 (the Malayan case); the same sequence is noted for Indo-China; resistance in WWII became post-war insurgency. (✅ owner 14 §10.6 link; owner 18 §7.5A and study link)
**Breakdown:**
- Resistance: anti-Japanese (WWII).
- Post-war: anti-British insurgency (Malaya, 1948).
- Indo-China: same sequence with opposite outcome (owner §7.5A).
- Use only the link; do not extend to unsourced cases (owner 14 §10.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1948 (Malaya insurgency). (✅ owner 14 & 18)
**Links:** WH-3158, WH-3159, WH-3153, WH-3018 (Quisling and resistance), WH-3020 (Italian partisans).
**Trap:** Do not present every resistance movement as becoming insurgency.
**Mains hook:** Why Britain would not simply hand over Malaya — because the insurgents had the anti-Japanese record.
**Back:** *"Resist Japan, then fight Britain."*

### Flashcard WH-3188 — Decolonisation: Mains frame, probable questions and one-line verdicts
**Front:** What is the standard Mains frame and what are the probable questions on decolonisation?
**Definition (learn):** Why empires ended: war exhaustion → nationalist pressure → international climate → conclusion. Problems after independence: weak state → economic fragility → army/faction → external intervention. Assess decolonisation: necessity → achievements → limitations → balanced verdict. (✅ owner 18 advanced §8)
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: two causes → one route → verdict.
- 15 marks: unsustainability → two routes with cases → instability → verdict.
- 20 marks: plus settler vs non-settler, border legacy, Cold War, economic dependence → verdict.
- Probable: "Decolonisation transferred sovereignty faster than it built stable states." Discuss; "Analyse causes of collapse of European empires after 1945"; "Why did independent Africa face coups, civil wars and one-party rule?"
- Malaya one-line verdict: "not persuading Britain to go but deciding who would be there when it went".
- Apartheid verdict: "ended by negotiation because both sides concluded that the alternative was civil war."
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2015 Malaya and 2016 Western-educated-Africans demands are neutral (owner §10); no verbatim PYQ text held. (✅ owner 18)
**Links:** WH-3146, WH-3157, WH-3162, WH-3177, WH-3186.
**Trap:** Zero verbatim PYQ routes in owner ledgers 2018–2025 (⚠️ owner §10).
**Mains hook:** Use the frame; avoid breadth without mechanism.
**Back:** *"Causes, Routes, Instability, Verdict."*

### Flashcard WH-3189 — Decolonisation verdict scaffolds: causation, routes, instability, transfer
**Front:** What are the standard verdict scaffolds for decolonisation?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Why empires collapsed: the equation changed on both sides — colonial nationalism raised the price of staying; war and reconstruction reduced the metropole's willingness to pay. (2) Route: where the coloniser had only officials it negotiated; where it had settlers and strategic assets it fought. (3) Instability: independence handed nationalist movements a state built to extract and control. (4) Transfer or rupture: a real rupture in sovereignty and a weaker one in economic structure. (⚠️ owner §7.8)
**Breakdown:**
- Causation: nationalist price vs metropolitan capacity.
- Routes: negotiation vs war, length predicts successor violence.
- Instability: inherited structures narrowed options.
- Transfer: political rupture, economic continuity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** none. (⚠️)
**Links:** WH-3146, WH-3157, WH-3162, WH-3186, WH-3188.
**Trap:** Do not present one cause (owner §7.1).
**Mains hook:** Use one scaffold per conclusion; add named case.
**Back:** *"Price, Route, Structure, Rupture."*

## WORLD HISTORY — Part 4: 20th-Century Latin America, World Economy and the End of the Cold War

> Section note: (Owners 19–21, basic and advanced; IDs WH-4001 onward). Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` = grounded in the repository owner; `(✅ Wikipedia)` = confirmed on a fetched Wikipedia page (fetched 2026-11-01); `⚠️` = synthesis, memory-only, or an owner-vs-source conflict flagged inside the card. Owner text is Lowe-based and carries no figures for Latin American debt totals, GDP or trade shares; none is added here. Where the owner gives a figure that a fetched page contradicts, both are shown and the conflict is flagged.

---

### GROUP — Latin America in the Twentieth Century

### Flashcard WH-4001 — Latin America in the 20th century: informal empire and constrained sovereignty
**Front:** What is the right analytical label for US power in twentieth-century Latin America, and why is "colony" the wrong word?
**Definition (learn):** Informal empire (or hegemony without formal rule): the USA rarely governed directly but set the outer limits of what sovereign Latin American governments could do, through economic, strategic, financial and covert leverage.
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanisms: economic leverage (investment, trade, finance) → strategic intervention (anti-communism) → political signalling against hostile governments → local partnership through elites, armies and anti-left coalitions.
- Structural problems that interacted with it: unequal landholding, export dependence, militarised politics, US influence, debt.
- The region was sovereign in form; the constraint was on policy space.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 19 §1, §7.5 (✅ owner 19; ✅ owner 19 adv §2). Period framed from the Mexican Revolution (1910) to the 1990s–2000s challenges (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-4002 (Mexico), WH-4009 (four instruments), WH-4010–4013 (structural problems), WH-4023 (dependency debate), WH-4025 (ladder).
**Trap:** "The USA controlled Latin America" is wrong; so is "US influence meant no local agency". Domestic elites, armies, parties and social movements mattered throughout (✅ owner 19 §7.9).
**Mains hook:** "Analyse the nature of US domination in twentieth-century Latin America." Answer: informal empire / hegemony rather than empire, with local collaborators and limits named. Qualified conclusion: external leverage set the boundaries; inside them, oligarchies and armies made the choices.
**Back:** *"Not a ruler, a referee with a veto — the veto is what the region lived under."*

### Flashcard WH-4002 — Mexican Revolution and the PRI: institutionalised revolution
**Front:** What did the Mexican Revolution begin in 1910, and what did it become?
**Definition (learn):** The Mexican Revolution is the opening upheaval of the twentieth-century Latin American story (from 1910). Its legacy was a long single-party dominance by the PRI rather than a quick pluralist democracy.
**Breakdown:**
- Start: 1910 onward (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline).
- Outcome: dominance of the PRI and slow political opening (✅ owner 19 §3).
- Lesson: revolutionary legitimacy can harden into one-party rule.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mexican Revolution from 1910 (✅ owner 19). PRI dominance: "long" (no end year given in owner; do not add one) (✅ owner 19).
**Links:** WH-4001, WH-4021 (Brazil vs Mexico pair), WH-4009.
**Trap:** "Revolution always solved inequality" is false; Mexico and Nicaragua both show limits (✅ owner 19 §5). Do not date the PRI's end here.
**Mains hook:** Use Mexico to show that a revolution can institutionalise into a durable one-party system. Qualified conclusion: Mexico postponed the social question by institutionalising the revolution, not by resolving it (✅ owner 19 §7.8 scaffold, ⚠️ synthesis).
**Back:** *"Revolution that became a party: the 1910 fire ended in one-party furniture."*

### Flashcard WH-4003 — Guatemala 1954: reform overturned
**Front:** What happened to Guatemala's reform experiment in 1954, and who was involved?
**Definition (learn):** A reformist government's land and foreign-property policies met organised US-backed anti-communist intervention; the reform was overturned in 1954. The classic Cold War intervention case in the region.
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: reform that touched land or foreign assets (✅ owner 19 §7.5).
- Mechanism: covert and proxy intervention against a reform government.
- Outcome: reformist government overthrown (✅ owner 19 §7.5; ✅ owner 19 adv §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1954 (✅ owner 19). Invasion 18 June 1954; Árbenz resigned 27 June 1954; CIA operation PBSuccess, authorised by Eisenhower in August 1953 (✅ Wikipedia, "1954 Guatemalan coup d'état").
**Links:** WH-4001, WH-4009 (covert intervention instrument), WH-4022 (patron-swap pair), WH-4025.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Guatemala 1954 with Cuba 1959 (a successful revolution that survived). Guatemala's reform did not find an external patron; Cuba did. Owner calls the reformer "Arbenz" (✅ owner adv 19 §7); the spelling with accent is Árbenz (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** "Why did revolution rarely succeed in Latin America?" Use Guatemala: reform converted a domestic dispute into an international one. ⚠️ The Eisenhower-era authorisation is a fetched date, not owner text.
**Back:** *"Fifty-four: land reform met a CIA codename — PBSuccess."*

### Flashcard WH-4004 — Cuban Revolution (1959–62): the clearest successful challenge
**Front:** Why is the Cuban Revolution the decisive revolutionary challenge to US dominance in the region?
**Definition (learn):** Fidel Castro's revolution (1959) broke with the US-aligned order, faced US hostility, and aligned with the Soviet Union; it is the one revolution in the region that survived.
**Breakdown:**
- 1959: revolution (Castro) (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline, owner 19 adv §7).
- 1959–62: break with US-aligned order → missile crisis internationalises the confrontation (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline).
- Structure: US hostility + Soviet alignment (✅ owner 19 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cuban Revolution 1959 (✅ owner 19); 1959–62 span (✅ owner 19 §7.5).
**Links:** WH-4005 (missile crisis), WH-4022 (Cuba vs Guatemala/Nicaragua), WH-4015 (1980s debt), WH-4025 (ladder).
**Trap:** "Cuba was the region's only revolution" is wrong; Cuba is the only one that survived US hostility (✅ owner 19 §7.2 scaffold, ⚠️ analytic). Do not date the missile crisis as the revolution itself.
**Mains hook:** "Assess the Cuban Revolution's significance." Qualified line: it survived by exchanging one external patron for another (⚠️ owner 19 §7.2 template).
**Back:** *"Fifty-nine: the only one that lived — it swapped patrons, not threats."*

### Flashcard WH-4005 — Cuban missile crisis (1962): the confrontation internationalised
**Front:** What did the 1962 missile crisis do to the Cuban confrontation?
**Definition (learn):** The 1962 crisis is the point where the US–Cuba quarrel became a superpower confrontation, pulling the Cold War's central powers into the region.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 1962 (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline and §10 date control).
- Effect: internationalised the confrontation.
- Keep it separate from the 1959 revolution (owner date control: "Cuban Revolution (1959), missile crisis (1962) … remain distinct episodes").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1962 (✅ owner 19 §10).
**Links:** WH-4004; WH-4023; WH-4025.
**Trap:** Do not merge 1959 and 1962 into one "Cuban event". Do not add dates for the Soviet–US settlement (not in owner; ⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use it as evidence for the "Cold War made intervention international" point; not for the claim that Latin America was a Soviet satellite.
**Back:** *"Fifty-nine made Cuba; sixty-two made the world look at Cuba."*

### Flashcard WH-4006 — Nicaragua: Sandinistas, 1979
**Front:** What did the Sandinista victory of 1979 in Nicaragua illustrate?
**Definition (learn):** The Sandinistas came to power in Nicaragua in 1979. Their experiment met counter-revolutionary pressure, showing local revolution meeting external hostility.
**Breakdown:**
- 1979: Sandinistas take power (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline).
- Pressure: counter-revolutionary (external hostility) (✅ owner 19 §3, §7.7).
- Lesson: a revolution can succeed locally and still be contained by outside pressure.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1979 (✅ owner 19 §7.5). No later election or defeat dates in owner; do not add them.
**Links:** WH-4003 (Guatemala as comparison), WH-4022, WH-4001.
**Trap:** "Revolution solved inequality" fails in Nicaragua (✅ owner 19 §5). Do not confuse the Sandinistas with the Cuban regime (different year and path).
**Mains hook:** Use Nicaragua in the "anti-intervention case study" answer: Nicaragua and Guatemala are the two classic cases (✅ owner 19 §5).
**Back:** *"Seventy-nine: a local revolution met a foreign counter-revolution."*

### Flashcard WH-4007 — Venezuela: oil wealth, instability, and Chávez (from 1998)
**Front:** Why is Venezuela the owner's warning case on resource wealth, and when does Chávez become the anti-US symbol?
**Definition (learn):** Venezuela's oil wealth funded populist politics and instability; Chávez from 1998 became the leading symbol of anti-US politics in the region.
**Breakdown:**
- Pattern: oil wealth → instability → later populist challenge to US influence (✅ owner 19 §3).
- Chávez: key symbol of anti-US politics from 1998 (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline).
- Oil-price link: Venezuela's revenue fell with world oil prices in the 1980s debt story (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, Lowe Ch. 26).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Chávez from 1998 (✅ owner 19). Oil-revenue fall: ✅ owner 19 §7.6A (no figure given).
**Links:** WH-4015 (1980s debt), WH-4020 (Argentina), WH-4024 (China-CELAC).
**Trap:** "Commodity wealth guarantees stable prosperity" is false; Venezuela is the warning example (✅ owner 19 §5). Do not say Chávez started the challenge to US power (earlier nationalist and revolutionary challenges existed; ✅ owner 19 adv §7).
**Mains hook:** "Resource wealth can distort politics rather than stabilise it." Use Venezuela; qualify with the 1990s–2000s regional challenges.
**Back:** *"Oil paid for the populist song, and the price fall changed the tune."*

### Flashcard WH-4008 — Brazil: industrial growth without social justice
**Front:** What is Brazil's twentieth-century pattern and why is it the region's central lesson?
**Definition (learn):** Brazil combined industrial growth with inequality and authoritarian phases. Development was not the same as social justice.
**Breakdown:**
- Pattern: industrial growth alongside inequality and authoritarian phases (✅ owner 19 §3).
- Lesson: development and social justice are separable (✅ owner 19 §7.7, ⚠️ analytic).
- Debt aftermath: Brazil later brought government debt under control, "a considerable achievement" (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, Lowe Ch. 26).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No specific years in owner beyond the regional 20th-century frame. Do not add Brazilian dates (⚠️ none sourced).
**Links:** WH-4021 (Brazil vs Mexico pair), WH-4015, WH-4017.
**Trap:** Do not claim Brazil was a stable democracy throughout; the owner records authoritarian phases. Do not give Brazilian debt totals (none sourced).
**Mains hook:** "Development did not automatically mean social justice." Use Brazil plus the 1980s debt story; qualify with the later recovery, which was uneven.
**Back:** *"Grew the factories, kept the gap — industry without equity."*

### Flashcard WH-4009 — Four instruments of US domination (how power worked)
**Front:** Through which four instruments did US domination work in the region?
**Definition (learn):** Informal domination worked through four channels: covert/proxy intervention against reform; hostility and containment of revolution; financial leverage; and continuous commercial and strategic weight.
**Breakdown:**
- Covert and proxy intervention against reform → Guatemala 1954 (WH-4003); Nicaragua 1979 counter-pressure (WH-4006).
- Hostility and containment of revolution → Cuba 1959–62 (WH-4004, WH-4005).
- Financial leverage → 1980s debt crisis; "economic conditionality achieved what intervention could not" (⚠️ owner 19 §7.5 table reading).
- Commercial and strategic weight → structural, continuous influence (✅ owner 19 §7.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 19 §7.5 (✅ owner 19). Lowe presents the USA as the decisive outside power (✅ owner 19 §4).
**Links:** WH-4001, WH-4003–4006, WH-4015.
**Trap:** Financial leverage is not a separate "neutral" channel; the owner treats it as US-linked in the same section. Do not attribute the debt crisis solely to Washington.
**Mains hook:** "Analyse the nature of US domination." Structure: four instruments → local partners → limits. Qualified conclusion: hegemony rather than empire.
**Back:** *"Reform, revolution, reserves, rapport — the four Rs of the referee."*

### Flashcard WH-4010 — Unequal landholding
**Front:** How did unequal landholding shape twentieth-century Latin American politics?
**Definition (learn):** Concentrated landownership gave oligarchic elites control that blocked broad social reform.
**Breakdown:**
- Oligarchic control blocked broad social reform (✅ owner 19 §2).
- Mechanism: a narrow domestic market and a political class opposed to redistribution (⚠️ owner 19 §7.4).
- Caution: land structures differed by country (⚠️ owner 19 §7.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner (no land-share figures sourced; do not add).
**Links:** WH-4001, WH-4003 (land reform overturned), WH-4013.
**Trap:** Do not treat land structure as identical across countries; the owner warns about variation.
**Mains hook:** Use with the US-influence strand: landholding created governments that needed external backing, and external backing preserved the landholding (⚠️ owner 19 §7.2 "Interaction" template).
**Back:** *"Few owned the land, so few could let the reform in."*

### Flashcard WH-4011 — Export dependence on a narrow commodity base
**Front:** Why did export dependence make Latin American economies fragile?
**Definition (learn):** Economies relied heavily on a narrow commodity base, so price swings turned into fiscal and political crises.
**Breakdown:**
- Structure: narrow commodity exports (✅ owner 19 §2).
- Mechanism: commodity price swings → fiscal and political crisis (⚠️ owner 19 §7.4).
- Symmetry examiners reward: commodity boom and bust are the same structural vulnerability (⚠️ owner 20 §8.5A, cross-linked).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4014 (ISI as response), WH-4015 (debt), WH-4031 (globalisation).
**Trap:** Some economies diversified substantially (⚠️ owner 19 §7.4). Do not say all commodity exporters were equally trapped.
**Mains hook:** "Commodity wealth guarantees prosperity?" Answer: no; Venezuela warns; the boom-bust symmetry is the point.
**Back:** *"One crop, one crash: price falls travel from the port to the palace."*

### Flashcard WH-4012 — Militarised politics and caudillismo
**Front:** How did armies enter Latin American politics, and what is the 19th-century inheritance?
**Definition (learn):** Armies repeatedly entered politics through coups or pressure, acting as arbiters. This is the twentieth-century successor to nineteenth-century caudillismo, the rule of personal strongmen (caudillos).
**Breakdown:**
- 20th c.: armies entered politics through coups or pressure (✅ owner 19 §2).
- Lineage: caudillismo (✅ owner 19 §7.4 linked to owner 08 §8.6).
- Caution: not all coups had the same social base (⚠️ owner 19 §7.4).
- Rule: do not name individual caudillos (owner 19 §7.9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 08 §8.6 (cross-link, not reopened here).
**Links:** WH-4001, WH-4013, WH-4014. Basic owner 08 for caudillismo.
**Trap:** Do not treat the army as one ideological bloc; "military rule" covered different social coalitions. Do not name a caudillo as an example here (owner rule).
**Mains hook:** "Why was Latin American politics unstable?" Qualify: militarised politics was a symptom of oligarchic structure and external leverage, not an independent cause.
**Back:** *"The army became the arbiter the caudillo once was."*

### Flashcard WH-4013 — US influence worked through local partners
**Front:** Why does the owner insist that US influence did not erase local agency?
**Definition (learn):** US power usually worked through domestic elites, armies and anti-left coalitions. Domestic actors chose many of the outcomes within the limits US power set.
**Breakdown:**
- Partnership model: elites, armies, anti-left coalitions (⚠️ owner 19 adv §2).
- Agency remained with elites, parties and movements throughout (✅ owner 19 §7.9).
- Structural problem listed separately as "US influence" (✅ owner 19 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4001, WH-4009, WH-4010.
**Trap:** "US influence meant no local agency" is a defeated answer (✅ owner 19 §5, §7.9).
**Mains hook:** Write the interaction: external domination and internal inequality were one mechanism, not two explanations (⚠️ owner 19 §7.2).
**Back:** *"Washington chose the menu; Latin American elites ordered the meal."*

### Flashcard WH-4014 — Import-substituting industrialisation (ISI)
**Front:** What was import-substituting industrialisation and why did it run into trouble?
**Definition (learn):** ISI deliberately replaced imported manufactures with domestic ones behind tariff and licensing protection, with state investment and subsidised credit. It is the region's characteristic mid-century development strategy (⚠️ analytical category used by the owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: protection, state investment, subsidised credit (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6).
- Chain: small domestic markets, import bills for capital goods and fiscal deficits → external borrowing through the 1970s → 1980s debt crisis (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6).
- Ends in neoliberal turn: trade opening, privatisation, austerity (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No ISI dates beyond "mid-century" and "sustained through the 1970s by borrowing" (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6). Do not attribute ISI to a named economist (owner 19 §7.9).
**Links:** WH-4011, WH-4015, WH-4017, WH-4018.
**Trap:** ISI is not "socialism"; it is protectionist state-led industrialisation. Do not assign it to India's Second Five-Year Plan here; that belongs to Economy.
**Mains hook:** "Economic strategy since 1945": the ISI → debt → liberalisation sequence (✅ owner 19 §7.1 demand map). Qualify: liberalisation restored openness without resolving inequality.
**Back:** *"Make at home what was bought abroad — until the bills came due."*

### Flashcard WH-4015 — 1980s debt crisis: Mexico 1982 and the regional scale
**Front:** When and how did Mexico's debt crisis erupt, and what figures does the record give for regional debt?
**Definition (learn):** The 1980s debt crisis engulfed much of Latin America. Mexico's 1982 declaration that it could no longer service its debt is the fetched marker of the regional break.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: debt crisis engulfed much of Latin America in the 1980s; it weakened states, intensified austerity and increased dependence on outside lenders (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline, §4).
- Mexico, August 1982: Finance Minister Jesús Silva-Herzog declared Mexico could no longer service its debt; a unilateral 90-day moratorium followed (✅ Wikipedia, "Latin American debt crisis").
- Owner case: Mexico sought an IMF loan and rescheduling of half of a $96 billion overseas debt in 1982 (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, Lowe Ch. 26).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1980s (✅ owner 19). Mexican moratorium August 1982 (✅ Wikipedia). Debt service $66 billion in 1982, up from $12 billion in 1975 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-4016 (compounding), WH-4017 (social doors), WH-4020 (Argentina), WH-4041 (the 1930s slump and its political transmission, for cross-era comparison).
**Trap:** **Conflict flagged:** owner 19 adv §7 gives "by 1985 Latin American external debt had reached roughly $368 billion (Lowe)"; the fetched Wikipedia page gives "more than $315 billion in 1983" (✅ Wikipedia). These are different years and sources; cite each with its year and do not merge them. ⚠️ Both are Lowe/Wikipedia figures, not verified against a primary dataset.
**Mains hook:** "Why did debt become a central political issue in Latin America?" Qualify: debt converted an economic condition into a loss of policy autonomy (✅ owner 19 §7.4).
**Back:** *"Eighty-two: Mexico said stop — the region's debt wall went up."*

### Flashcard WH-4016 — Compounding interest and the debt trap (1979–81)
**Front:** How did the debt trap work mechanically in 1979–81?
**Definition (learn):** Debtor nations could not pay the interest. Unpaid interest was added to existing debt, so the total grew, and they had to borrow from new sources just to keep up payments.
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: many debtor nations "could not even pay the interest, let alone repay the debts" (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, Lowe).
- Stage 2: unpaid interest "was added on to the existing debt" (✅ owner 19 §7.6A).
- Stage 3: borrowing "merely to keep up the payments" (✅ owner 19 §7.6A).
- Social effect: debt service crowds out health, education, subsidies and wages (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Period 1979–81 (✅ owner 19 §7.6A). No totals beyond the Wikipedia figures on WH-4015.
**Links:** WH-4015, WH-4017, WH-4018.
**Trap:** The mechanism, not the totals, is what an answer needs (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6A). Do not say the debt crisis was purely financial; it had political and social consequences (✅ owner 19 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Debt and dependency": the compounding trap converts a financial stress into a sovereignty question.
**Back:** *"Interest unpaid becomes principal — the snowball starts rolling downhill."*

### Flashcard WH-4017 — Four doors: how the debt crisis reached society
**Front:** Through which four "doors" did the debt crisis reach ordinary people?
**Definition (learn):** The budget, the currency, the price level and the employment contract. Adjustment imposed by creditors, not chosen by electorates, reached households through these four channels.
**Breakdown:**
- Budget: debt service crowds out social spending (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6A).
- Currency: devaluation and import limits raise living costs (✅ owner 19 §7.6A; African case WH-4019).
- Price level: hyperinflation destroys savings and wages (✅ owner 19 §7.6A; Argentina WH-4020).
- Employment contract: austerity and cuts in jobs and services (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, Zimbabwe WH-4018).
- Political effect: the social contract of the ISI era was withdrawn under conditionality (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, ⚠️ analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Formula quoted in owner 19 §7.6A (⚠️ synthesis).
**Links:** WH-4016, WH-4018, WH-4019, WH-4020.
