**Trap:** Do not claim the adjustment was purely harmful. The owner records Ghana's programme "soon seemed to be working" (production up 7 per cent, inflation down to 40 per cent). State both halves (✅ owner 19 §7.6A).
**Mains hook:** "The debt crisis had deep social consequences." Structure by four doors; verdict: deepest effect was on the legitimacy of the developmental state (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6A formulation).
**Back:** *"Budget, money, prices, jobs — four doors, one knock."*

### Flashcard WH-4018 — Conditionality and austerity: Zimbabwe ESAP and Ghana's IMF deal
**Front:** What did the owner cite for "rescheduling comes with conditions", and what is the Zimbabwe programme's name?
**Definition (learn):** Debtor countries rescheduled debts under agreement, and creditors' conditions (austerity) became the transmission from a balance-of-payments problem to household welfare.
**Breakdown:**
- Zimbabwe accepted an IMF loan and, against public opinion, agreed to an Economic Structural Adjustment Programme (ESAP) involving "unpopular cuts in public spending on social services and jobs" (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, Lowe).
- Ghana's Rawlings "reluctantly… turned to the IMF" and had to accept austerity conditions (✅ owner 19 §7.6A).
- Use as African illustration of the mechanism only (⚠️ owner 19 §7.9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ESAP named for Zimbabwe (✅ owner 19 §7.9 permits this name only). No ESAP date in owner; do not add.
**Links:** WH-4016, WH-4017, WH-4019; African cross-owner basic/18 §7.6A.
**Trap:** The owner allows only the **Economic Structural Adjustment Programme** name for Zimbabwe. Do not add other programme names for Latin America (✅ owner 19 §7.9).
**Mains hook:** "Economic strategy since 1945": ISI → debt → conditional austerity → liberalisation (✅ owner 19 §7.1).
**Back:** *"Reschedule the loan, reschedule the life: the cuts arrive with the cheque."*

### Flashcard WH-4019 — Ghana: devaluation and inflation (African illustration, not Latin America)
**Front:** What are the Ghana figures the owner permits, and why must they not be transferred to Latin America?
**Definition (learn):** Ghana's 1971 devaluation and 1981 inflation are used only as African illustrations of the same mechanism (currency and price channels).
**Breakdown:**
- 1971: imports limited and the currency devalued by nearly 50 per cent (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, Lowe).
- 1981: inflation at 125 per cent, with labour unrest "as wages remained low" (✅ owner 19 §7.6A).
- After adjustment: production up 7 per cent; inflation down to 40 per cent (✅ owner 19 §7.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1971; 1981; figures as cited (✅ owner 19 §7.6A and §7.9 permitted list).
**Links:** WH-4017 (currency door), WH-4018, WH-4057 (oil shock importer strain; Ghana cited there too).
**Trap:** **Explicit owner rule:** do not transfer Ghana's figures to Latin America (✅ owner 19 §7.6A). An answer that says "Latin American inflation was 125 per cent" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use as mechanism illustration within an African answer, or as a "cost of adjustment" example in a comparative question.
**Back:** *"Ghana's numbers, not Latin America's: fifty, one-twenty-five, forty."*

### Flashcard WH-4020 — Argentina: hyperinflation and a debt-driven crisis
**Front:** What does the owner say about Argentina's 1980s position?
**Definition (learn):** Argentina was left with an unprecedentedly high foreign debt and inflation of around 900 per cent. Inflation works as a regressive tax on cash and fixed wages.
**Breakdown:**
- Inflation around 900 per cent (✅ owner 19 §7.6A, Lowe; sourced for Argentina only).
- Social effect: inflation falls on holders of cash and fixed wages (⚠️ owner 19 §7.6A).
- Later recovery: Argentina repaid its IMF debts under Kirchner and won popularity by doing so (✅ owner 19 §7.6A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ~900 per cent (✅ owner 19 §7.6A). Do not give a year for the peak (owner gives none).
**Links:** WH-4017, WH-4015, WH-4016.
**Trap:** Do not extend the 900 per cent figure to other countries. Recovery was uneven and later; do not use it to soften the 1980s (✅ owner 19 §7.6A).
**Mains hook:** Use the "later recovery must be conceded" line to avoid determinism.
**Back:** *"Nine hundred per cent: savings melted before the cheque cleared."*

### Flashcard WH-4021 — Comparison pair: Brazil vs Mexico ("two ways of postponing one question")
**Front:** How does the owner pair Brazil and Mexico to show variety within Latin America?
**Definition (learn):** Brazil industrialised without democratising; Mexico institutionalised a revolution without pluralising. Two different ways of postponing the same social question.
**Breakdown:**
- Brazil: industrial growth, inequality and authoritarian phases (✅ owner 19 §3).
- Mexico: PRI dominance, slow political opening (✅ owner 19 §3).
- Common thread: social reform postponed (⚠️ owner 19 §7.8 verdict scaffold).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4002, WH-4008.
**Trap:** Do not treat "Latin America" as one political unit; the owner calls that the trap that loses marks (✅ owner 19 §7.7).
**Mains hook:** "Compare two Latin American states." Use the pair, name both paths, then the verdict (⚠️ owner 19 §7.8).
**Back:** *"Brazil built factories without votes; Mexico built a party without rivals."*

### Flashcard WH-4022 — Comparison pair: Cuba vs Guatemala and Nicaragua (the patron question)
**Front:** Why did Cuba survive US hostility when Guatemala and Nicaragua faced pressure?
**Definition (learn):** Cuba found an external patron (the Soviet Union) willing to underwrite the consequences. Reform in Guatemala and the Sandinista experiment in Nicaragua did not have that backing in the same way.
**Breakdown:**
- Cuba: revolution, US hostility, Soviet alignment (✅ owner 19 §3).
- Guatemala: reform overturned 1954 (WH-4003).
- Nicaragua: Sandinistas 1979 with counter-revolutionary pressure (WH-4006).
- Owner verdict: "Because reform that touched land or foreign assets converted a domestic dispute into an international one, and only Cuba found an external patron willing to underwrite the consequences" (⚠️ owner 19 §7.8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1954; 1959; 1979 (✅ owner 19).
**Links:** WH-4003, WH-4004, WH-4006.
**Trap:** Do not say Cuba's survival proves the Soviet model worked. The owner's claim concerns the external patron, not the economy.
**Mains hook:** "Why did revolution rarely succeed?" Use the patron logic; qualify that Cuba's survival came at the price of external dependence (⚠️ owner 19 §7.2).
**Back:** *"Guatemala lost its patron, Cuba found one — survival is a sponsorship question."*

### Flashcard WH-4023 — Dependency debate and agency (advanced)
**Front:** What is the advanced framing of Latin American instability, and how should it be qualified?
**Definition (learn):** Dependency (structural dependence on outside powers, unequal trade and domestic oligarchies) explains constraints. The good answer combines dependency with domestic institutional weakness; the region was not without agency.
**Breakdown:**
- Explanations to choose among: excess borrowing and fragile export earnings; global financial shocks; structural dependency; domestic governance failures (✅ owner 19 adv §3, ⚠️ analytical use).
- Best answer: combine dependency and domestic institutional weakness (✅ owner 19 adv §3).
- Later left turns built on old frustrations: dependency, inequality, debt, resentment of intervention (⚠️ owner 19 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4001, WH-4013, WH-4015, WH-4024.
**Trap:** Dependency does not mean "no political agency" (❌ owner 19 adv §7; Lowe shows constant local conflict). Do not say the region moved in one ideological direction.
**Mains hook:** "Latin American instability was produced jointly by domestic inequality and external intervention." Comment: agree with the joint mechanism, then qualify each side.
**Back:** *"Dependency is the leash; the local hand is still on it."*

### Flashcard WH-4024 — China-CELAC Forum, 13 May 2025 (📰 current link, advanced)
**Front:** What dated current-affairs anchor does the advanced owner use for Latin American external alignment?
**Definition (learn):** On 13 May 2025 the Fourth Ministerial Meeting of the China-CELAC Forum in Beijing adopted the Beijing Declaration and the China-CELAC Joint Action Plan for Cooperation in Key Areas (2025–2027), per China's Ministry of Foreign Affairs.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 13 May 2025 (📰 owner 19 adv §6, citing China's MFA).
- Content: Beijing Declaration; Joint Action Plan 2025–2027.
- Limit: confirms meeting and framework only; not proof of implementation or displacement of US influence (⚠️ owner 19 adv §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 📰 as of owner date; forum meeting date 13 May 2025 (owner citing MFA; not fetched in this pass).
**Links:** WH-4023, WH-4007; Topic 15 for the wider Cold War.
**Trap:** Do not treat a 2025 framework as proof that US primacy has ended. Owner says this is an echo of contested influence, not a verdict.
**Mains hook:** Use as "modern echo": contestation of US influence is repeated, not new. Qualify with implementation evidence.
**Back:** *"Thirteen May, twenty-five: a framework signed, not a power shift proven."*

### Flashcard WH-4025 — Latin America chronology ladder (1910 to 1998)
**Front:** What is the chronology ladder for twentieth-century Latin America?
**Definition (learn):** A ladder to memorise the separate episodes, not one regional cycle.
**Breakdown:**
- 1910 onward — Mexican Revolution opens the upheaval (WH-4002).
- 1954 — Guatemala reform overturned (WH-4003).
- 1959–62 — Cuban Revolution and missile crisis 1962 (WH-4004, WH-4005).
- 1979 — Sandinistas in Nicaragua (WH-4006).
- 1980s — debt crisis; Mexico August 1982 (WH-4015).
- 1990s–2000s — new challenges to US dominance (✅ owner 19 mini-timeline).
- 1998 onward — Chávez in Venezuela (WH-4007).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 19 §10 date control (✅ owner 19).
**Links:** WH-4001, WH-4026.
**Trap:** Keep the episodes distinct; Mexico 1910, Guatemala 1954, Cuba 1959, missile crisis 1962, Sandinistas 1979, Mexico debt 1982 and liberalisation are separate.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to avoid "one regional cycle" errors in a chronology-based answer.
**Back:** *"Ten, fifty-four, fifty-nine, seventy-nine, eighty-two, ninety-eight — one date per episode."*

### Flashcard WH-4026 — Trap bundle: four Latin American myths
**Front:** Which four popular myths about twentieth-century Latin America does the owner reject?
**Definition (learn):** A single card listing the four myths and their corrections.
**Breakdown:**
- Myth 1: "Latin America had no local agency." Correction: elites, armies, parties and movements mattered (WH-4013).
- Myth 2: "Commodity wealth guarantees prosperity." Correction: Venezuela (WH-4007).
- Myth 3: "Revolution always solved inequality." Correction: Mexico and Nicaragua show limits (WH-4002, WH-4006).
- Myth 4: "Debt crisis was purely financial." Correction: it had political and social consequences (WH-4016, WH-4017).
- Also: "Latin America was politically uniform" (✅ owner 19 §5 trap note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 19 §5, §7.9.
**Links:** WH-4007, WH-4013, WH-4002, WH-4006, WH-4016, WH-4017.
**Trap:** The four myths are the usual examiner traps for this topic.
**Mains hook:** Use the corrections as the counter-argument paragraph of any answer.
**Back:** *"No agency, no gold, no fix, no pure finance — four myths, four corrections."*

### GROUP — The World Economy and Population since 1900

### Flashcard WH-4027 — World economy since 1900: the core theme
**Front:** What is the core thesis of the world-economy and population owner, and what does it test?
**Definition (learn):** The world economy moved from European-centred liberal integration, through war-and-depression fragmentation, to a US-led rules-based order, and then to a globalised system that spread growth and contagion by the same channels (⚠️ owner 20 §8.2 template).
**Breakdown:**
- Capitalist, communist and developing-world economies, tracked by Lowe (✅ owner 20 §1).
- Population change is inseparable from mortality decline, urbanisation, fertility change and health shocks (⚠️ owner 20 §1).
- Syllabus anchor: political philosophies "their forms and effect on the society" (✅ owner 20 §3 and §1, official GS-I line).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mini-timeline spans 1900s–2008 (✅ owner 20 mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-4028 to WH-4033 (phases), WH-4060 (demography), WH-4065 (capitalism vs socialism), WH-4067 (ladder).
**Trap:** "Economy since 1900" is not a century-long chronicle; the directive tests the phase logic and the drivers of each transition (✅ owner 20 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** "Trace the major changes in the world economy since 1900." Answer by phases, with the driver of each transition named.
**Back:** *"Europe's centre, war's collapse, a designed order, one connected shock machine."*

### Flashcard WH-4028 — Phase 1: early twentieth century to 1945
**Front:** What shattered the older liberal world economy before 1945?
**Definition (learn):** Wars and depression shattered the older liberal world economy. Europe weakened; the USA emerged far stronger.
**Breakdown:**
- Pattern: total war and the 1930s collapse of trade and credit (✅ owner 20 §8.4).
- Outcome: Europe weakened, USA emerged stronger (✅ owner 20 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early 20th c. to 1945 (✅ owner 20 §2). Depression 1929–33 (WH-4034 to WH-4041).
**Links:** WH-4034, WH-4038, WH-4054.
**Trap:** Do not treat the USA as already dominant in 1900; it "emerged" stronger after the wars and depression (✅ owner 20 §2).
**Mains hook:** Use as the first phase in any "changes since 1900" answer.
**Back:** *"War and slump broke the old liberal engine; America took the wheel."*

### Flashcard WH-4029 — Phase 2: 1945 to the 1970s, two economies apart
**Front:** How did the capitalist West and the communist bloc develop between 1945 and the 1970s?
**Definition (learn):** The two blocs developed separately. Cold War economics was also ideological competition (✅ owner 20 §2, §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- US-led capitalist order expands; communist bloc separate (✅ owner 20 mini-timeline).
- Ended by: oil shocks and 1970s strains (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
- Post-1944 architecture: Bretton Woods and GATT (WH-4054, WH-4056).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1945 onwards (✅ owner 20 mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-4054, WH-4055, WH-4057, WH-4065.
**Trap:** Do not present the bloc division as ideological only; the owner treats it as a development divide too (⚠️ owner 20 §8.8).
**Mains hook:** Use to set up the North-South dispute of the 1970s: the first attempt to design the world economy.
**Back:** *"Two economies, one Cold War, a shared fear of each other's ledger."*

### Flashcard WH-4030 — Phase 3: 1970s–1990s oil, debt and North-South conflict
**Front:** What characterised the 1970s–1990s phase of the world economy?
**Definition (learn):** Oil shocks, debt, uneven development and North-South conflict. "Third World" unity weakened as some states industrialised faster than others.
**Breakdown:**
- Oil shocks → debt pressures → North-South conflict (✅ owner 20 §2, §8.4).
- Internal divergence: some industrialised faster (✅ owner 20 §3).
- Ended by: end of the Cold War and opening of former communist economies (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1970s–80s oil shocks and debt pressures (✅ owner 20 mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-4033, WH-4057, WH-4058, WH-4015 (Latin American debt).
**Trap:** "The Third World" is not one economic category (✅ owner 20 §3, §8.9).
**Mains hook:** "Why did the North-South divide persist?" Use this phase to explain divergence.
**Back:** *"Oil bought the bills, debt bought the crisis, and the South split apart."*

### Flashcard WH-4031 — Phase 4: 1990s–2008 globalisation after the Cold War
**Front:** How did globalisation accelerate after the Cold War, and what did it deepen?
**Definition (learn):** Globalisation accelerated after the Cold War. Trade, finance and production chains deepened integration but also vulnerability.
**Breakdown:**
- Channels: trade, finance, production chains (✅ owner 20 §2).
- Institutions: GATT then WTO from 1995 (WH-4056).
- Ended by: the 2008 financial crisis (✅ owner 20 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1990s (✅ owner 20 mini-timeline); WTO start 1995 (✅ owner 20 §8.4).
**Links:** WH-4032, WH-4056, WH-4067.
**Trap:** Do not say globalisation "removed" the North-South divide; the owner shows uneven gains (❌ owner 20 §6). Do not describe present-day trade policy here; that belongs to IR.
**Mains hook:** "Globalisation widened both opportunity and vulnerability." Use the phase and the two channels.
**Back:** *"Trade, money and parts crossed borders — and so did the shocks."*

### Flashcard WH-4032 — 2008 financial crisis: contagion, not reversal of integration
**Front:** What did the 2008 crisis show about an integrated world economy?
**Definition (learn):** The 2008 crisis is Lowe's standard example of a shock exposing the fragility of an integrated world economy. It did not reverse integration.
**Breakdown:**
- Lesson: "global integration spreads shocks quickly" (✅ owner 20 §8.5).
- Integration transmits losses as efficiently as gains (✅ owner 20 §8.5).
- The 2008 aftermath is the last phase in the owner's sequence (✅ owner 20 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2008 (✅ owner 20 mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-4031, WH-4067.
**Trap:** "2008 disproved globalisation completely" is wrong; it exposed vulnerability (❌ owner 20 §6).
**Mains hook:** Use as the contagion example in a "globalisation: gains and vulnerability" answer.
**Back:** *"Two thousand eight: the shock crossed every border the gains had crossed."*

### Flashcard WH-4033 — North-South divide and the "Third World" as a false single category
**Front:** What is the North-South divide, and why is "the Third World" an unhelpful single category?
**Definition (learn):** The North-South divide is the twentieth-century fault line between industrialised and developing economies, distinct from the East-West Cold War divide. The developing world did not move together.
**Breakdown:**
- Lowe: some developing states industrialised much more successfully than others (✅ owner 20 §3).
- Constraints on poorer states: debt, commodity dependence, weak industrial bases (✅ owner 20 §3).
- Integration rewards those already able to supply manufactures (⚠️ owner 20 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4030, WH-4031, WH-4064, WH-4066.
**Trap:** Do not treat "the Third World" as one bloc; internal divergence was large (❌ owner 20 §8.9). Do not say "the South ceased to exist" either.
**Mains hook:** "Why did globalisation not remove the North-South divide?" Answer: mechanisms of uneven gain, with one country-level contrast.
**Back:** *"Same label, different ladders: the South climbed unevenly."*

### Flashcard WH-4034 — Great Depression, causes (1): overproduction and unequal income
**Front:** What were the two domestic structural causes of the 1929 slump that the owner names first?
**Definition (learn):** Overproduction and an income distribution too narrow to absorb output. Industrialists, with mechanisation, produced more than the home market could absorb. Unsold stock built up, men were laid off, and families bought less.
**Breakdown:**
- Overproduction: self-reinforcing contraction (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
- Income: average industrial wages rose about 8 per cent between 1923 and 1929 while industrial profits rose about 72 per cent; the 8 per cent was about 1.4 per cent in real terms (✅ owner 20 §9.4, Lowe).
- Lowe's concession: a slump was still "not inevitable" if exports had held up (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1923–29 wages +8 per cent; profits +72 per cent; real +1.4 per cent (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
**Links:** WH-4035, WH-4036, WH-4037.
**Trap:** Do not present overproduction as an industry-only story; Lowe applies the logic to farmers (⚠️ owner 20 §9.4). Do not write "the Crash caused the Depression" (WH-4036).
**Mains hook:** "What caused the Great Depression?" Structure: structural imbalance first, then the Crash as symptom.
**Back:** *"Too much made, too little bought: profits ran away from wages."*

### Flashcard WH-4035 — Great Depression, causes (2): speculation and self-fulfilling expectation
**Front:** What role did speculation play, and what mechanism does the owner name?
**Definition (learn):** Speculation grew from about 1926. Shares were bought on credit, brokers sold on credit, and banks speculated with deposits. The mechanism is a self-fulfilling expectation: investors' own selling produced the collapse they feared.
**Breakdown:**
- Average share value rose from $9 (1924) to $26 (1929) (✅ owner 20 §9.4, Lowe).
- RCA stock rose from $85 (early 1928) to $505 (September 1929) though it paid no dividends (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
- Mechanism named: self-fulfilling expectation (⚠️ owner 20 §9.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1924, 1926 (momentum), 1928, 1929 figures as cited (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
**Links:** WH-4034, WH-4036.
**Trap:** Do not moralise about "greed"; the owner names the expectation mechanism (⚠️ owner 20 §9.4).
**Mains hook:** Use speculation as one of four causes, not the only cause.
**Back:** *"Nine to twenty-six, then buyers turned sellers — expectation did the rest."*

### Flashcard WH-4036 — Wall Street Crash, 24 October 1929: symptom, not cause
**Front:** What did the Crash of October 1929 do, and why is it a symptom rather than the cause?
**Definition (learn):** "Black Thursday", 24 October 1929: nearly 13 million shares were dumped at very low prices. By mid-1930 prices averaged about 25 per cent of the previous year's peak; rock bottom came in 1932. Lowe is explicit that the Crash did not cause the depression.
**Breakdown:**
- 24 October 1929, "Black Thursday", nearly 13 million shares dumped (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
- Mid-1930: prices about 25 per cent of prior year's peak (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
- 1932: rock bottom (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
- Lowe quote: "It was just a symptom of a problem whose real causes lay much deeper" (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 24 Oct 1929; 13 million; 25 per cent; 1932 (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
**Links:** WH-4034, WH-4035, WH-4037.
**Trap:** The single most examined discrimination in this topic: "The Wall Street Crash caused the Depression" is wrong (❌ owner 20 §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "What caused the Great Depression?" Open with this discrimination.
**Back:** *"The Crash was the alarm, not the fire."*

### Flashcard WH-4037 — Export contraction and the Fordney-McCumber Tariff (1922)
**Front:** How did American protectionism turn a domestic problem into a world crisis?
**Definition (learn):** The Fordney-McCumber Tariff (1922) kept foreign goods out of the US market. That denied Europeans the dollar earnings they needed to buy American goods or to service war debts. Many states retaliated with their own tariffs.
**Breakdown:**
- American tariff barriers, Fordney-McCumber (1922) (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
- Consequence: Europeans lacked dollars to buy US goods or service war debts (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
- Retaliation: many states raised tariffs (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
- Causal bridge from domestic to world crisis (⚠️ owner 20 §9.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1922 (✅ owner 20 §9.4).
**Links:** WH-4034, WH-4040, WH-4043 (New Deal trade agreements).
**Trap:** Protection was self-defeating for a creditor power: you cannot be paid in goods you refuse to admit (⚠️ owner 20 §9.4). Do not describe the 1922 Act as the sole cause.
**Mains hook:** "How did it become a world crisis?" Use the export channel.
**Back:** *"Shut the door on goods, then wonder why the dollars don't come back."*

### Flashcard WH-4038 — Transmission channel 1: credit withdrawal
**Front:** How did US credit withdrawal transmit the slump abroad?
**Definition (learn):** After the Crash, the USA stopped further loans and began to call in short-term loans to Germany. The Dawes-era recovery had been built on that lending.
**Breakdown:**
- Fastest channel: needs no fall in trade at all (⚠️ owner 20 §9.5).
- Named evidence: US "stopped any further loans and began to call in many of the short-term loans already made to Germany" (✅ owner 20 §9.5, Lowe).
- Lowe: Germany's dependence was recognised as a danger before 1929 (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-October 1929 (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
**Links:** WH-4036, WH-4039, WH-4041.
**Trap:** Do not present credit as a trade channel; it is financial. Do not invent the Dawes Plan's terms (not in owner).
**Mains hook:** "The Depression became a world crisis through American credit rather than American trade alone." Qualify with the trade channel.
**Back:** *"Called-in loans travel faster than a falling export order."*

### Flashcard WH-4039 — Transmission channel 2: banking failure, 25,000 banks to fewer than 15,000
**Front:** What do the bank-count figures in the owner show about the financial channel?
**Definition (learn):** Banking collapse destroyed savings and credit at the same time, so the shock hit consumption and investment together.
**Breakdown:**
- USA: over 25,000 banks in 1929, fewer than 15,000 by 1933 (✅ owner 20 §9.5, Lowe).
- Germany: "a crisis of confidence in the currency and… a run on the banks, many of which had to close" (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1929 and 1933 bank counts (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
**Links:** WH-4038, WH-4040, WH-4043, WH-4044.
**Trap:** Do not give German bank counts; none is sourced (⚠️ owner 20 §9.5).
**Mains hook:** Use to show confidence collapse as a transmission channel.
**Back:** *"Twenty-five thousand banks, fewer than fifteen thousand — savings went with them."*

### Flashcard WH-4040 — Transmission channels 3 and 4: trade contraction, tariff retaliation and commodity collapse
**Front:** How did export collapse, tariff retaliation and commodity prices carry the slump to ordinary producers?
**Definition (learn):** Export-dependent economies imported the depression whether or not their banks failed. Each country's rational defence (tariffs) made the aggregate contraction worse. Japan's raw silk price collapse reached villages.
**Breakdown:**
- Trade contraction: German exports "severely reduced"; Japan "exports shrank disastrously"; Italy "exports fell further" (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
- Tariff retaliation: "other countries introduced or raised tariffs against Japanese goods" (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
- Commodity: Japanese raw silk, mostly sold to the USA, fell by 1932 to less than one-fifth of its 1923 price; about half of Japan's farmers depended on silk as well as rice (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1932; 1923 base; one-fifth (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
**Links:** WH-4037, WH-4039, WH-4041.
**Trap:** Tariffs are both a cause and an amplifier; say which you mean (⚠️ owner 20 §9.5). Do not generalise the silk ratio.
**Mains hook:** Use the collective-action failure: each rational tariff made the aggregate worse.
**Back:** *"Each wall helped one house and sank the street."*

### Flashcard WH-4041 — Political transmission: the Depression and extreme politics
**Front:** What was the Depression's largest world-historical output, according to the owner?
**Definition (learn):** The crisis caused extreme right-wing governments to come to power in Japan and Germany. Its largest output was constitutional and political, not economic.
**Breakdown:**
- Germany: over six million unemployed by 1932 (✅ owner 20 §9.5, §9.7).
- Italy: unemployment rose to 1.1 million (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
- Lowe: without the crisis "Hitler would probably never have been able to come to power" (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
- Necessary, not sufficient; the fuller argument is in World History basic/12 §10.4 (✅ owner 20 §9.5 note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1932 German unemployment; 1.1 million Italy (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
**Links:** WH-4038, WH-4040, WH-4051.
**Trap:** Do not say the Depression alone caused fascism; it was a necessary condition (⚠️ owner 20 §9.5).
**Mains hook:** "Effects of the Depression abroad." Use with the democratic-versus-authoritarian comparison (WH-4051).
**Back:** *"Six million jobless in Germany: the slump handed the extremists their stage."*

### Flashcard WH-4042 — Currency policy under pressure: Mussolini and the lira (refusal to devalue until 1936)
**Front:** What currency-policy evidence does the owner give, and what does it show?
**Definition (learn):** Mussolini refused to devalue the lira until 1936. Instead "wages and salaries were cut", and because wages fell faster than prices, workers suffered a real loss. Refusing external devaluation forces internal devaluation.
**Breakdown:**
- Choice: refusal to devalue until 1936 (✅ owner 20 §9.5).
- Result: wage cuts; real wage loss; Italian workers lost over 10 per cent in real terms (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- Boundary: the sources do not discuss the gold standard; do not write a gold-standard mechanism (⚠️ owner 20 §9.9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1936; over 10 per cent real loss (✅ owner 20 §9.5, §9.7).
**Links:** WH-4040, WH-4041, WH-4052.
**Trap:** Do not write a gold-standard causal argument; this folder's sources do not contain it (❌ owner 20 §9.9). ⚠️ Know the standard monetary context externally only if separately verified.
**Mains hook:** Use the lira as evidence that currency choices shift the cost of adjustment onto wages.
**Back:** *"Hold the lira, cut the wage: the adjustment went to the pay packet."*

### Flashcard WH-4043 — New Deal: Roosevelt's three aims (relief, recovery, reform)
**Front:** What three aims did Roosevelt state for the New Deal, and what period do the measures cover?
**Definition (learn):** Roosevelt's own classification: relief (direct help to the destitute), recovery (reduce unemployment, stimulate demand, restart the economy) and reform (prevent recurrence). Measures ran 1933 to 1940.
**Breakdown:**
- Relief: direct help to the destitute (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Recovery: reduce unemployment and restart the economy (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Reform: prevent recurrence (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Brain Trust: economists and academics; a "complete change from those of the laissez-faire Republicans" (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1933–1940 (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
**Links:** WH-4044 to WH-4050 (instruments grouped by function).
**Trap:** Do not present "First/Second/Third New Deal" periodisation as established; Michael Heale calls it an oversimplification (✅ owner 20 §9.9). Do not attribute to Keynes.
**Mains hook:** "What instruments were deployed?" Structure by function; the three aims are a ready classification.
**Back:** *"Relief for today, recovery for tomorrow, reform so it never happens again."*

### Flashcard WH-4044 — New Deal banking and finance: SEC 1934 and the 50 versus 10 per cent margin
**Front:** What did the Securities Exchange Commission (1934) change about share purchases on credit?
**Definition (learn):** The SEC (1934) reformed the stock exchange by requiring a down payment of at least 50 per cent instead of 10 per cent on shares bought on credit. Temporary government takeover of banks and guarantee of depositors restored confidence.
**Breakdown:**
- Down payment: at least 50 per cent (from 10 per cent) on shares bought on credit (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Banks: temporarily taken over; depositors guaranteed (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Success: "money began to flow into the banks again" (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** SEC 1934; 50 vs 10 per cent (✅ owner 20 §9.6, §9.9).
**Links:** WH-4035, WH-4039, WH-4043.
**Trap:** Do not name the deposit-insurance agency; the owner does not name it (⚠️ owner 20 §9.6 "guaranteed depositors"). The 50/10 figure is the card's central number.
**Mains hook:** Financial stabilisation is the clearest success; contrast it with the recovery failure in WH-4050.
**Back:** *"Fifty not ten: the margin fell and the money came home."*

### Flashcard WH-4045 — New Deal agricultural support: Farmers' Relief Act (1933) and the AAA
**Front:** What did the agricultural measures of 1933 pay farmers to do, and what did they fail to fix?
**Definition (learn):** The Farmers' Relief Act (1933) and the Agricultural Adjustment Administration (AAA) paid farmers compensation for reducing output, raising prices. Farm distress was overproduction in another form.
**Breakdown:**
- Compensation for reduced output raised prices (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Limits: "certainly helped farmers, but it threw many farm labourers out of work"; little help for the dustbowl areas of Kansas, Oklahoma and Texas (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Dustbowl: mid-1930s drought and soil erosion (⚠️ owner 20 §9.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1933 (✅ owner 20 §9.9).
**Links:** WH-4037, WH-4043, WH-4047.
**Trap:** The AAA paid farmers to reduce output; do not describe it as subsidising production (⚠️ owner 20 §9.6).
**Mains hook:** "Effects on society": farmers, labourers and the dustbowl (WH-4052).
**Back:** *"Pay to plough less, and the labourers pay the price."*

### Flashcard WH-4046 — New Deal industrial coordination: NRA and the Supreme Court
**Front:** What was the National Recovery Administration, and why did the Supreme Court rule against it?
**Definition (learn):** The NRA coordinated prices, output and labour conditions in the first phase. The Supreme Court ruled several measures, including the NRA, unconstitutional, halting their operation. The instrument was defeated by the constitutional order it was meant to save.
**Breakdown:**
- NRA: coordinate prices, output and labour conditions (✅ owner 20 §9.6; the full name is ⚠️ as the owner gives only the acronym).
- Court: held the president taking too much power; ruled NRA unconstitutional (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- Later: the Court became more amenable after Roosevelt appointed five more co-operative judges (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Five appointments in the second term (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Links:** WH-4043, WH-4052.
**Trap:** Do not claim the Court blocked all New Deal measures; it struck "several" (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Mains hook:** Constitutional limits bind emergency economics, and they shift over time (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Back:** *"The NRA was struck down by the very Constitution it meant to save."*

### Flashcard WH-4047 — New Deal public works: TVA (1933) and lasting assets
**Front:** What did the Tennessee Valley Authority (1933) build, and why does the owner call public works durable?
**Definition (learn):** The TVA built dams for cheap electricity and organised conservation, irrigation and afforestation across a region ruined by soil erosion. Public works created jobs and left durable assets.
**Breakdown:**
- TVA (1933): dams, cheap electricity, conservation, irrigation, afforestation (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Other public works: slum clearance, house and flat building, mortgage relief loans (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Owner verdict: "provided services of lasting value" (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1933 (✅ owner 20 §9.9).
**Links:** WH-4045, WH-4043, WH-4052.
**Trap:** Public works are not the same as recovery; the 1938 cut shows the limit (WH-4050).
**Mains hook:** Use TVA as the ecological link: soil erosion and the dustbowl.
**Back:** *"Dams for dignity: the Valley got power and soil that stayed put."*

### Flashcard WH-4048 — New Deal labour: Fair Labour Standards Act (1938) and collective bargaining
**Front:** What did the Fair Labour Standards Act (1938) set, and which firms were forced to recognise unions?
**Definition (learn):** The FLSA set a maximum working week, a minimum wage in certain low-paid trades, and made most child labour illegal. Collective bargaining became accepted; General Motors and US Steel were forced to recognise unions.
**Breakdown:**
- FLSA 1938: maximum week; minimum wage in certain low-paid trades; most child labour illegal (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Collective bargaining "became accepted" (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- General Motors and US Steel forced to recognise unions (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Labour standards were what made business the New Deal's most determined opponent (⚠️ owner 20 §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1938 (✅ owner 20 §9.9).
**Links:** WH-4052, WH-4053.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the 1935 Social Security Act with the 1938 labour act (WH-4049).
**Mains hook:** "Effects on society and the business response": labour standards and opposition.
**Back:** *"Thirty-eight: week, wage, child — and GM and Steel had to sit down."*

### Flashcard WH-4049 — New Deal social insurance: Social Security Act (1935)
**Front:** What did the Social Security Act (1935) introduce, and what did the owner judge it to be?
**Definition (learn):** The Social Security Act (1935) introduced welfare benefits described by Lowe as "an important step towards a welfare state". Its purpose was reform: protect households against the next downturn.
**Breakdown:**
- 1935; welfare benefits (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Principle: "the American government had accepted that it had a duty to help those in need" (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- Limit: "rugged individualism" remained a vital ingredient; a step, not a completed welfare state (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1935 (✅ owner 20 §9.9).
**Links:** WH-4043, WH-4048, WH-4052.
**Trap:** Do not call it a completed welfare state (❌ owner 20 §9.6).
**Mains hook:** Use to show the New Deal changed what government was for.
**Back:** *"Thirty-five: a step toward the welfare state, not the destination."*

### Flashcard WH-4050 — New Deal fiscal expansion and the 1938 spending cut
**Front:** What did the 1938 reduction in government spending do, and why is the spending evidence delicate?
**Definition (learn):** Roosevelt was prepared to spend government cash to pull the country out of depression. Lowe judges he was too cautious; when he reduced spending in 1938 the result was another recession, with unemployment up to 10.5 million.
**Breakdown:**
- Doctrinal break: government spending as a recovery tool (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- 1938 cut → another recession; unemployment 10.5 million (✅ owner 20 §9.6, §9.7).
- Unemployment fell to under 8 million by 1937 but remained "a serious problem" (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- Interpretation: the instrument worked in proportion to its size (⚠️ owner 20 §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1937 under 8 million; 1938 10.5 million; 1943 below 1 million through war effort (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Links:** WH-4043, WH-4051, WH-4052.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the expansion to Keynes or "Keynesian economics" (❌ owner 20 §9.9). Do not claim the New Deal ended the Depression (❌ owner 20 §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "Did the New Deal end the Depression?" Use the 1938 episode as the decisive concession.
**Back:** *"Cut in thirty-eight, back to ten million — spending was the medicine."*

### Flashcard WH-4051 — Authoritarian counter-case: Nazi rearmament-led recovery
**Front:** How did Nazi Germany cut unemployment, and what qualification must any answer make?
**Definition (learn):** Vast public works (slum clearance, land drainage, autobahn building) combined with rearmament from 1934 and conscription from 1935. Unemployment fell from over 6 million at Hitler's accession to about 2 million by end-1935 and to negligible levels by 1939. The military budget was 52 per cent of government spending in 1938–9.
**Breakdown:**
- Public works plus rearmament (from 1934) and conscription (from 1935) (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Unemployment: over 6 million → about 2 million (end 1935) → negligible (1939) (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Military budget 52 per cent of government spending, 1938–9 (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
- Qualification: abolition of trade unions; purges of Jews and anti-Nazis (✅ owner 20 §9.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1934 rearmament; 1935 conscription; 52 per cent (✅ owner 20 §9.6, §9.9).
**Links:** WH-4041, WH-4050, WH-4052.
**Trap:** Never present this as a successful alternative without the purge and trade-union sentence (❌ owner 20 §9.6, §9.9). Full employment through exclusion is not a policy model.
**Mains hook:** "Compare democratic and authoritarian responses." Use the constrained American programme against this; the price must be named.
**Back:** *"Full jobs bought with guns and gaps in the lists — not a model."*

### Flashcard WH-4052 — New Deal verdict: legitimacy saved, recovery incomplete
**Front:** What is the graded verdict on whether the New Deal saved capitalism?
**Definition (learn):** It saved the political legitimacy of capitalism without restoring its output. Confidence in banks, government and the constitutional order returned; the American constitutional system survived intact. Unemployment stayed in the millions until war production; Lowe says the New Deal did not rescue the USA from the depression.
**Breakdown:**
- Legitimacy: confidence restored; "some historians think it may even have prevented a violent revolution" (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- Achievements: relief, millions of jobs, public works, social security, collective bargaining, federal authority (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- Limits: business opposition; states; Supreme Court; the left (Socialists; Huey Long); economic outcome (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- War: US GNP almost doubled 1939–45; June 1940 8 million out of work; by end-1942 almost full employment; about 7 million extra jobs by 1945 (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Above (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Links:** WH-4043 to WH-4051, WH-4053.
**Trap:** Do not say the New Deal achieved nothing, or that it ended the Depression (❌ owner 20 §9.9).
**Mains hook:** "Did the New Deal save capitalism?" Verdict: decisive for the survival of democratic capitalism, insufficient for recovery.
**Back:** *"Saved the system's nerve, not its payroll — war did the hiring."*

### Flashcard WH-4053 — Huey Long and the left critique of the New Deal (1934–35)
**Front:** What did the left say about the New Deal, and what happened to Huey Long?
**Definition (learn):** Socialists felt the New Deal "was not drastic enough and still left too much power in the hands of big business". Huey Long, governor of Louisiana and US senator, launched Share Our Wealth in 1934, financed by taxing the rich. He was assassinated in September 1935.
**Breakdown:**
- Socialist critique: not drastic enough (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- Share Our Wealth (1934), financed by taxing the rich (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
- Assassination: September 1935 (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1934; September 1935 (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Links:** WH-4052, WH-4048.
**Trap:** Do not frame the New Deal as "the radical option"; the left attacked it from the other side (✅ owner 20 §9.7).
**Mains hook:** Use the left and the right to show the New Deal was squeezed from both sides.
**Back:** *"Share the wealth, lose the senator — the left pushed from the other side."*

### Flashcard WH-4054 — Bretton Woods (1944): the designed post-war economic order
**Front:** What did the 1944 Bretton Woods conference create, and what were its two institutions?
**Definition (learn):** The conference created the framework of the post-war economic order: a fixed-exchange-rate system, with the IMF to manage balance-of-payments adjustment and the World Bank (IBRD) to finance reconstruction and development. Both are UN specialised agencies (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4; ✅ owner 16 §9.5).
**Breakdown:**
- Conference: Mount Washington Hotel, Bretton Woods, New Hampshire, 1–22 July 1944 (✅ Wikipedia, "Bretton Woods system").
- Fixed exchange rates with the dollar linked to gold; IMF for balance of payments; IBRD for reconstruction and development (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
- IMF: Articles of Agreement signed 27 December 1945 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Analytical point: the first attempt to design the world economy rather than let it emerge (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1944; 1–22 July 1944; 27 Dec 1945 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Conflict flagged:** Wikipedia gives conflicting IMF operation dates: "began operations in 1946" and "began financial operations on 1 March 1947"; the IBRD "became operational in 1945" (✅ Wikipedia, mixed). Do not give an operational start year without checking.
**Links:** WH-4055, WH-4056, WH-4029, WH-4033.
**Trap:** Do not supply quota shares, voting weights, lending figures or programme names (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4). Do not describe current IMF/World Bank governance in a history answer (❌ owner 20 §8.9). The WTO is not a UN specialised agency (✅ owner 20 §8.4).
**Mains hook:** "The post-1945 order was the first designed world economy"; the North-South debate is an argument about who designed it (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
**Back:** *"Forty-four: fix the rates, fund the rebuild, and watch the balance."*

### Flashcard WH-4055 — End of the fixed-rate system: 1971 and the move to floating rates
**Front:** When did the fixed-rate element of the post-war system break, and what replaced it?
**Definition (learn):** The fixed-rate element broke down in the early 1970s; floating exchange rates and freer capital movement became the norm. This was the institutional precondition for the financial globalisation of the 1990s (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
**Breakdown:**
- Early 1970s breakdown (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
- US ended dollar-to-gold convertibility on 15 August 1971; the page says this effectively brought the system to an end (✅ Wikipedia).
- Jamaica Accords, 1976, formally ratified the end (✅ Wikipedia).
- Smithsonian Agreement: year not confirmed in the fetched extract (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 15 Aug 1971; 1976 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-4054, WH-4031.
**Trap:** The owner's "early 1970s" and Wikipedia's 1971 are consistent; do not claim floating rates began in 1944 or before 1971.
**Mains hook:** Use to connect the 1971 break to 1990s financial globalisation (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
**Back:** *"Seventy-one: the gold link snapped and the currencies floated."*

### Flashcard WH-4056 — GATT to WTO (1995): trade liberalisation and the trap of "UN agency"
**Front:** How did trade liberalisation proceed after 1945, and what is the WTO's status?
**Definition (learn):** Trade liberalisation proceeded through GATT and, from 1995, through the WTO. The WTO belongs to the wider multilateral trade system but is not a UN specialised agency.
**Breakdown:**
- GATT: the post-war trade-liberalisation vehicle (⚠️ owner 20 §8.4).
- WTO: from 1995 (✅ owner 20 §8.4, §8.9).
- Status: not a UN specialised agency (✅ owner 20 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1995 (✅ owner 20 §8.9).
**Links:** WH-4054, WH-4031, WH-4066.
**Trap:** "WTO is a UN agency" is wrong (✅ owner 20 §8.4). Do not start the WTO before 1995 (✅ owner 20 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Use to separate the Bretton Woods institutions from the trade regime when answering on institutions.
**Back:** *"GATT began the talks; the WTO came in 1995 and sits outside the UN family."*

### Flashcard WH-4057 — Oil shock 1973: OPEC price rise and the OAPEC embargo
**Front:** What was the 1973 oil shock, and what dates does the record give?
**Definition (learn):** Producers raised the price of oil in 1973, partly to conserve supplies. The Arab oil embargo (OAPEC, declared October 1973) ran to March 1974. The price shock sharply raised the cost of imported energy.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: in 1973 OPEC members "partly in an attempt to conserve oil supplies, began to charge more for their oil" (✅ owner 20 §8.5A).
- Embargo: OAPEC declared an embargo in October 1973; the embargo ended in March 1974 (✅ Wikipedia, "1973 oil crisis"). One section gives 18 March 1974 as King Faisal's ending.
- Price (conflict): Wikipedia's OPEC announcement of 16 October 1973 gives "from $3.01 … to $5.12" in one place and "a 17% rise … to $3.65" in the chronology; a later figure reached nearly $12 by year-end (✅ Wikipedia, mixed).
- Importers: rising oil prices worsened America's balance-of-payments deficit (✅ owner 20 §8.5A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1973; October 1973 – March 1974 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-4058, WH-4030, WH-4019.
**Trap:** **Conflict flagged:** the fetched page gives inconsistent price figures for 16 October 1973; cite only the owner's qualitative "price rise" unless a single figure is confirmed. The stated motive includes conservation, not only revenue (✅ owner 20 §8.5A).
**Mains hook:** "Effects of the oil shocks": supply shock → surplus → borrowing → debt vulnerability (WH-4058).
**Back:** *"Seventy-three: producers said conserve and charge; importers paid."*

### Flashcard WH-4058 — Oil shock as a causal chain: from energy price to debt crisis
**Front:** What is the owner's causal chain from the 1973 oil shock to the 1980s debt crisis?
**Definition (learn):** The oil shock did not simply make energy expensive. It redistributed purchasing power towards producers, financed a decade of importer borrowing, and exposed commodity-dependent states when terms of trade turned.
**Breakdown:**
- Supply action → price shock → importer costs up and demand weaker (✅ owner 20 §8.5A).
- Producer surpluses (Middle East, Nigeria, Libya) (✅ owner 20 §8.5A).
- Recycled as lending → borrowing looks cheap while commodity prices hold (⚠️ owner 20 §8.5A, the plumbing not sourced).
- Commodity prices fall or interest costs rise → sovereign-debt crisis (⚠️ owner 20 §8.5A).
- Lowe's own qualification: Libya's oil profits "did not necessarily mean that their governments spent the money wisely" (✅ owner 20 §8.5A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Tanzania's oil bill "used up almost half her earnings from exports" (✅ owner 20 §8.5A).
**Links:** WH-4057, WH-4015, WH-4011, WH-4018.
**Trap:** Do not say oil surpluses were the specific source of the loans; the folder does not establish that link (⚠️ owner 20 §8.5A). Do not write "stagflation" as a folder fact (❌ owner 20 §8.5A).
**Mains hook:** "The oil shock … explains why an energy event of 1973 is read as the origin of a debt crisis of the 1980s" (⚠️ owner 20 §8.5A formulation).
**Back:** *"Price up, surplus flows, loans follow, terms turn, debt bites."*

### Flashcard WH-4059 — Environment as a world-economy dimension: growth, scarcity and warming
**Front:** What environmental dimensions does Lowe place inside the world-economy chapter, and what must an answer avoid?
**Definition (learn):** Growth that ignored side effects, awareness arriving in the 1970s, resource exhaustion, pollution and global warming. Lowe defines global warming as "the uncontrollable warming of the Earth's atmosphere caused by the large quantities of gases emitted from industry."
**Breakdown:**
- Growth motive: wealth and profit; "very little attention was paid to the side effects" (✅ owner 20 §8.5B).
- 1970s: people "became increasingly aware that all was not well with their environment" (✅ owner 20 §8.5B).
- Resource exhaustion: fossil fuels "cannot be replaced"; OPEC conserved oil in the 1970s; Britain drilled in the North Sea; nuclear power as response (✅ owner 20 §8.5B).
- Ecosystem and ecology defined (✅ owner 20 §8.5B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1970s awareness (✅ owner 20 §8.5B).
**Links:** WH-4057, WH-4058, WH-4034 (dustbowl).
**Trap:** Do not supply temperature, emission, concentration or treaty figures from this file (❌ owner 20 §8.9). Live climate policy belongs to Environment-and-Ecology.
**Mains hook:** Closing line for a 20-mark answer: growth, inequality and the physical limits of the growth model (⚠️ owner 20 §8.5B).
**Back:** *"Profit first, side effects later — and the bill arrived as warming."*

### Flashcard WH-4060 — Demographic transition: four stages (historical use)
**Front:** What are the four stages of the demographic transition, and what does the owner use them for?
**Definition (learn):** A framework from high births and high deaths to low births and low deaths. The "population explosion" is the widening of the gap in Stage 2, when deaths fall but births stay high.
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: high births, high deaths → slow growth.
- Stage 2: deaths fall (medicine, clean water, public health), births stay high → rapid growth.
- Stage 3: births begin to fall (education, urbanisation, women's employment, child survival, family planning) → growth slows.
- Stage 4: low births, low deaths → stabilisation, then ageing (✅ owner 20 §8.6 diagram; ⚠️ the stage framing).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4061, WH-4062, WH-4063, WH-4064.
**Trap:** The model's owner is Geography basic/26 §2; World History keeps the historical role (✅ owner 20 §8.6). Do not attribute it to a named demographer (❌ owner 20 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Use the model to convert description into analysis; regional variation is mandatory (⚠️ owner 20 §8.6).
**Back:** *"High, high, then deaths fall, births lag, and finally both low — the gap is the story."*

### Flashcard WH-4061 — Population explosion: mortality decline before fertility decline
**Front:** Why did world population grow so fast in the twentieth century?
**Definition (learn):** Better medicine, cleaner water and lower death rates, combined with a slower fall in fertility in many regions, produced rapid growth. The explosion is about the gap, not "more births".
**Breakdown:**
- Falling death rates (✅ owner 20 §4).
- Continued high fertility; population surged before fertility declined (✅ owner 20 §4).
- Pressure on food, housing, employment and urban services (✅ owner 20 §4).
- Owner formulation: "a mortality event before it was a fertility event" (⚠️ owner 20 §8.2 template).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4060, WH-4062, WH-4063.
**Trap:** Rapid growth is linked to falling death rates, not simply to "more births" (✅ owner 20 §6).
**Mains hook:** "Explain the causes and consequences of rapid population growth in the twentieth century." Structure: mortality decline → fertility lag → consequences → policy response.
**Back:** *"Deaths fall first, births follow late — that lag is the explosion."*

### Flashcard WH-4062 — Population control routes: welfare versus coercion (China's one-child policy)
**Front:** What are the two routes to lower fertility, and which case does the owner use for the coercive route?
**Definition (learn):** The welfare or development route (education, health, urbanisation and women's empowerment) reduces fertility gradually. Direct state control tries to lower births faster. China's one-child control policy is the owner's key case of direct state control.
**Breakdown:**
- Welfare route: gradual, through development (✅ owner 20 §4).
- Coercive route: direct state intervention (✅ owner 20 §4).
- China: effective in reducing births but raises major ethical and social questions (⚠️ owner 20 §4).
- Lowe: states tried varied measures, some voluntary and some coercive (✅ owner 20 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No China dates in owner; do not add (⚠️).
**Links:** WH-4060, WH-4061, WH-4063.
**Trap:** Do not discuss population policy only numerically; the human consequences are the examinable point (✅ owner 20 adv §4). Do not add India's population policy here (❌ owner 20 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Answer: education and women's employment rather than coercion; the coercive route raises ethics (⚠️ owner 20 §8.8).
**Back:** *"Schools change families; decrees change births — and the ethics follow the decree."*

### Flashcard WH-4063 — HIV/AIDS as a development and population shock
**Front:** Why is HIV/AIDS treated as a development issue, not a medical one?
**Definition (learn):** A late-twentieth-century epidemic, especially destructive in parts of Africa. It affected labour, family structure, life expectancy and development. It removes the working-age cohort that produces output and raises children.
**Breakdown:**
- Major late-20th-century population issue (✅ owner 20 §5).
- Affected labour, families, life expectancy and development (✅ owner 20 §5).
- International campaigns mattered; burden highly unequal (✅ owner 20 §5).
- Health crises reverse gains even without war (⚠️ owner 20 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None; prevalence and funding figures not sourced (❌ owner 20 §8.7).
**Links:** WH-4060, WH-4061, WH-4033.
**Trap:** HIV/AIDS does not belong outside population history; Lowe treats it within the population chapter (❌ owner 20 §6).
**Mains hook:** "HIV/AIDS as a development issue": use the working-age cohort argument (⚠️ owner 20 §8.7).
**Back:** *"The epidemic took the workers and the parents together."*

### Flashcard WH-4064 — Dated current anchors: IMF July 2026 and UN WPP 2024 (📰)
**Front:** What are the dated current-affairs anchors for growth and population, and what must be attached to them?
**Definition (learn):** IMF World Economic Outlook Update (July 2026): world growth projected at 3.0 per cent for 2026 and 3.4 per cent for 2027. UN World Population Prospects 2024: world population about 8.2 billion, projected peak about 10.3 billion in the mid-2080s.
**Breakdown:**
- IMF: 3.0% (2026), 3.4% (2027), vintage July 2026 (📰 owner 20 adv §7; not fetched in this pass).
- UN WPP 2024: about 8.2 billion; peak about 10.3 billion mid-2080s (📰 owner 20 adv §7; not fetched in this pass).
- Use: long-run themes (fragile integration; slowing but large population growth) (⚠️ owner 20 adv §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As-of: IMF July 2026 vintage; UN WPP 2024 (✅ owner 20 adv §7).
**Links:** WH-4032, WH-4061, WH-4033.
**Trap:** Keep forecast vintage attached; later updates are not interchangeable (✅ owner 20 adv §7). Do not merge these figures into Lowe's pre-2013 narrative (✅ owner 20 adv §8).
**Mains hook:** Use only as a dated current-affairs anchor, not as historical evidence.
**Back:** *"Dated figures, dated forecasts — never mixed into the history."*

### Flashcard WH-4065 — Capitalism versus socialism: the social outcome test
**Front:** How should capitalism and socialism be compared in a world-history answer?
**Definition (learn):** The comparison cannot be settled on output alone. The capitalist West produced higher consumption; the communist bloc produced faster early industrialisation at far higher social cost. Both left inequality unresolved in different forms.
**Breakdown:**
- Lowe links systems to employment, inequality and development, not production alone (✅ owner 20 adv §3).
- Capitalist dynamism, socialist planning and mixed-economy experiments each gave gains and distortions (⚠️ owner 20 adv §3).
- Syllabus phrase: "political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism etc.— their forms and effect on the society" (✅ owner 20 adv §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4029, WH-4027, WH-4001 (ideological framing in basic/01).
**Trap:** Do not present an ideological verdict; the owner asks for comparative performance and social outcome (❌ owner 20 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** "Capitalism versus socialism in practice": comparative economic performance and social outcome.
**Back:** *"Compare the outputs and the people — the scoreboard has two columns."*

### Flashcard WH-4066 — India's 1991 reform as a world-history cross-link (not a repeat)
**Front:** How should India's 1991 liberalisation be used in the world-economy topic?
**Definition (learn):** India's 1991 liberalisation belongs to the Economy folder. In world-history terms it is an example of how post-Cold-War globalisation pulled states deeper into world markets.
**Breakdown:**
- Use as an example only (⚠️ owner 20 adv §6).
- Cross-link: Economy basic/16 and advanced/16 (✅ owner 20 adv §6).
- Do not retell LPG reforms here (⚠️ owner 20 adv §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1991 (✅ owner 20 adv §6).
**Links:** WH-4056, WH-4031, WH-4065.
**Trap:** Do not import India's population-policy or 1991-reform detail into the world-history answer (❌ owner 20 §8.9).
**Mains hook:** Use as one example in a globalisation answer, with a cross-link to Economy.
**Back:** *"One line for 1991, a pointer to Economy, no re-run of the reforms."*

### Flashcard WH-4067 — World economy chronology ladder (1929 to 2008)
**Front:** What is the chronology ladder for the world economy and population since 1900?
**Definition (learn):** A ladder that keeps the economic stages separate.
**Breakdown:**
- Oct 1929: Crash (WH-4036).
- 1929–33: transmission (WH-4038 to WH-4041).
- 1933–40: New Deal (WH-4043).
- 1944: Bretton Woods (WH-4054).
- 1971: end of gold convertibility (WH-4055).
- 1973–74: oil shock and embargo (WH-4057).
- 1990s: globalisation; WTO 1995 (WH-4031, WH-4056).
- 2008: financial crisis (WH-4032).
- 20th century: population growth; HIV/AIDS late 20th century (WH-4061, WH-4063).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 20 §10 date control (✅ owner 20).
**Links:** WH-4027, WH-4068.
**Trap:** Keep the stages separate: the Crash is not the Depression, and Bretton Woods is not the WTO.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to structure any "since 1900" answer.
**Back:** *"Twenty-nine, forty-four, seventy-one, seventy-three, ninety-five, two-oh-eight — the spine of the century."*

### Flashcard WH-4068 — Trap bundle: globalisation and population myths
**Front:** Which popular myths about globalisation and population does the owner reject?
**Definition (learn):** The owner rejects four pairs of myths with corrections.
**Breakdown:**
- "Globalisation benefited all regions equally" → uneven gains (WH-4033).
- "The Third World moved as one bloc" → divergence (WH-4033).
- "Population growth proves economic failure" → fast-growing populations coexisted with development; ageing is the reverse problem (✅ owner 20 adv §8).
- "HIV/AIDS belongs outside population history" → it is inside (WH-4063).
- "2008 disproved globalisation" → it exposed vulnerability (WH-4032).
- "Development can be inferred from GDP alone" → distribution matters (✅ owner 20 adv §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 20 §6, §8.9; adv §8.
**Links:** WH-4032, WH-4033, WH-4061, WH-4063.
**Trap:** Myth-correction pairs are the examiner's favourite distractors.
**Mains hook:** Use as a counter-argument structure.
**Back:** *"Equal gains, one bloc, growth-equals-failure, GDP-equals-development — four myths, four cracks."*

### GROUP — End of the Cold War and the New World Order

### Flashcard WH-4069 — End of the Cold War and the new world order: three linked stories
**Front:** What three linked stories does the owner say this topic contains?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Dissolution of Soviet power; (2) negotiated and violent redrawing of European boundaries; (3) the promise and limits of a US-led post-Cold-War order (✅ owner 21 §1, ⚠️ framing).
**Breakdown:**
- Scope: the long Cold War belongs to Topic 15; world economy since 1900 to Topic 20; present-day IR to IR owners (✅ owner 21 §1).
- Syllabus anchors: GS-I "redrawing of national boundaries" and political ideologies; GS-II "important international institutions, agencies and fora" (✅ owner 21 §1).
- This file uses the Gulf War, Yugoslavia, NATO, European integration and globalisation only to test the new-order claim (✅ owner 21 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Chronology 1985 (Gorbachev) to 2004 (eastern enlargement) (✅ owner 21 mini-timeline).
**Links:** WH-4070, WH-4079, WH-4083, WH-4086, WH-4091, WH-4095, WH-4096, WH-4100.
**Trap:** Do not extend this file's historical narrative beyond Lowe's 2013 boundary; post-2013 claims must be separately dated (❌ owner 21 §14.11).
**Mains hook:** Structure any answer on the three stories: dissolution, redrawing, and the contested order.
**Back:** *"Dissolve, redraw, reorder — three stories, one ending that was never final."*

### Flashcard WH-4070 — Gorbachev (1985): rescue, not abolition
**Front:** Did Gorbachev intend to abolish communism, and what did he try to do?
**Definition (learn):** Gorbachev came to power in 1985 and wanted a humane, democratic socialism inside a reformed one-party framework. He did not enter office intending to abolish communism or dissolve the USSR.
**Breakdown:**
- 1985: Gorbachev comes to power (✅ owner 21 mini-timeline).
- Aim: humane, democratic socialism; reform and save (✅ owner 21 §2).
- Sequencing problem: glasnost raised expectations faster than perestroika improved daily life (⚠️ owner 21 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1985 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** WH-4071, WH-4072, WH-4079.
**Trap:** "Gorbachev intended to destroy communism" is wrong; he tried to reform and save it (❌ owner 21 §14.11).
**Mains hook:** "Gorbachev tried to reform communism but ended by dissolving it." Comment: the dissolution was a consequence of reform outrunning control, not its aim.
**Back:** *"Eighty-five: he meant to repair the house, and the walls moved."*

### Flashcard WH-4071 — Glasnost and perestroika: openness versus restructuring
**Front:** How do glasnost and perestroika differ in mechanism and effect?
**Definition (learn):** Glasnost (openness) released dissidents, widened public discussion and exposed bureaucratic failure. Perestroika (restructuring) decentralised and modernised party, government and economy.
**Breakdown:**
- Glasnost → criticism became legitimate before a trusted replacement order existed (✅ owner 21 §2).
- Perestroika → partial reform disrupted command mechanisms without a functioning market (✅ owner 21 §2).
- Political reform: televised debate and competitive elements weakened the CPSU's monopoly (✅ owner 21 §2).
- Lowe: glasnost released criticism faster than perestroika delivered results (✅ owner 21 adv §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4070, WH-4073.
**Trap:** Do not treat glasnost and perestroika as synonyms; they had different mechanisms (❌ owner 21 §14.11).
**Mains hook:** Use the sequencing point: openness outran restructuring, which made collapse a possibility.
**Back:** *"Glasnost opened the mouth; perestroika failed to fill the plate."*

### Flashcard WH-4072 — New foreign-policy thinking and the Soviet non-intervention choice
**Front:** What did Gorbachev's new foreign-policy thinking remove, and why did it matter for Eastern Europe?
**Definition (learn):** New foreign-policy thinking reduced confrontation, arms burdens and coercive control over Eastern Europe. Satellite regimes lost the expectation of Soviet military rescue, and the Soviet Union did not use old-style force to preserve communist rule in eastern Europe.
**Breakdown:**
- Reduce confrontation and arms burdens (✅ owner 21 §2).
- Without Soviet intervention, eastern European communist systems weakened rapidly (✅ owner 21 §14.4).
- The decision not to coerce was a choice, not an inevitability (⚠️ owner 21 §14.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4073, WH-4074, WH-4075, WH-4098.
**Trap:** Do not present Western pressure as the decisive cause alone (❌ owner 21 §11).
**Mains hook:** "The Cold War ended through interaction between structural decay and political choice." The non-coercion choice is the pivot.
**Back:** *"No tanks to the rescue: the satellites lost their guarantee."*

### Flashcard WH-4073 — Why the Cold War ended: six causal families and the balanced verdict
**Front:** What are the six causal families for the end of the Cold War, and what is the balanced verdict?
**Definition (learn):** Economic stagnation; defence and imperial burdens; Gorbachev's agency; society from below; nationality mobilisation; Western pressure and diplomacy. None is sufficient alone.
**Breakdown:**
- Stagnation: loss of performance legitimacy (✅ owner 21 §3).
- Burdens: defence and Afghanistan (✅ owner 21 §3).
- Agency: reform, arms diplomacy, non-intervention (✅ owner 21 §3).
- Society: Solidarity, civic groups, churches, workers (✅ owner 21 §3).
- Nationalities: republics offered alternative loyalties (✅ owner 21 §3).
- Western pressure: arms competition and engagement; Reagan responded to Gorbachev (✅ owner 21 §3).
- Balanced verdict: structural weakness made reform urgent; reform released opposition; societies and republics supplied alternatives; diplomacy and non-use of mass force made reversal hard (✅ owner 21 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4070, WH-4074, WH-4079, WH-4097.
**Trap:** A "West defeated the USSR" thesis ignores Soviet and Eastern European agency (✅ owner 21 §3).
**Mains hook:** "Was the Cold War ended more by Soviet weakness, Western pressure or political choice?" Answer: a combined explanation with a ranked emphasis.
**Back:** *"Six causes, none alone: stagnation, burden, leader, society, nation, pressure."*

### Flashcard WH-4074 — Solidarity and Poland (August 1988 to June 1989)
**Front:** How did Solidarity start the eastern European sequence, and what did it secure?
**Definition (learn):** Solidarity's huge anti-government strikes in August 1988 forced the government to negotiate. Organised opposition and bargaining produced elections in June 1989.
**Breakdown:**
- August 1988: strikes organised by the trade union Solidarity (✅ owner 21 §4, §14.4).
- Negotiation and elections in June 1989 (✅ owner 21 §4).
- Agency: the decisive omission created the opportunity; organised society supplied the agency (⚠️ owner 21 §14.4).
- Caution: do not generalise Poland's model to every state (⚠️ owner 21 §14.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1988; June 1989 (✅ owner 21 §4).
**Links:** WH-4072, WH-4075, WH-4073.
**Trap:** Do not add Solidarity's membership, leadership or round-table detail; none is sourced (❌ owner 21 §14.11).
**Mains hook:** Use Poland to show that Moscow's restraint and society's mobilisation are the two halves of one explanation (⚠️ owner 21 §14.4).
**Back:** *"Eighty-eight strikes, eighty-nine votes: the union turned restraint into revolution."*

### Flashcard WH-4075 — Eastern Europe 1989: one regional wave, different routes
**Front:** What different national routes did the 1989 revolutions take?
**Definition (learn):** Communist systems weakened rapidly once Moscow no longer guaranteed repression, but the routes differed. Hungary opened its border with Austria; East Germany's demonstrations and elite division led to the Wall's opening; Czechoslovakia's Velvet Revolution transferred power with little violence; Romania's overthrow was violent.
**Breakdown:**
- Hungary: reform communists accepted competitive change; opened the border with Austria, weakening East Germany's containment of exit (✅ owner 21 §4).
- East Germany: demonstrations, elite division, Berlin Wall opened 9 November 1989 (✅ owner 21 §4).
- Czechoslovakia: Velvet Revolution, power transferred with little violence (✅ owner 21 §4; date not given in owner ⚠️).
- Romania: violent overthrow (✅ owner 21 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1989 (✅ owner 21 §4). Hungary, Czechoslovakia and Romania dates not given in owner; ⚠️ if added.
**Links:** WH-4074, WH-4076, WH-4098.
**Trap:** "1989" was not one peaceful script; Romania was violent (✅ owner 21 §4). Do not treat "1989" as one event.
**Mains hook:** "Analyse the causes of the collapse of communism in the USSR and Eastern Europe." Use the four routes as the variation evidence (⚠️ owner 21 §14.11).
**Back:** *"One wave, four routes: border, wall, velvet, and the violent one."*

### Flashcard WH-4076 — Berlin Wall opened: 9 November 1989
**Front:** What did the opening of the Berlin Wall on 9 November 1989 end, and what did it not do?
**Definition (learn):** The opening ended the physical symbol of European division. It did not itself reunify Germany.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 9 November 1989 (✅ owner 21 mini-timeline).
- Context: East German demonstrations and elite division (✅ owner 21 §4).
- Not sufficient: reunification required the Two Plus Four settlement and legal reunification later (✅ owner 21 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 9 November 1989 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** WH-4077, WH-4078, WH-4075.
**Trap:** Wall opening equals reunification is wrong (❌ owner 21 adv §10).
**Mains hook:** "Explain how German reunification combined popular revolution and great-power diplomacy." Start with the Wall.
**Back:** *"Nine November opened the gate, not the treaty."*

### Flashcard WH-4077 — Two Plus Four Treaty (12 September 1990)
**Front:** What did the Two Plus Four Treaty settle, and who were its parties?
**Definition (learn):** The treaty settled Germany's external status, borders and sovereignty. Signed on 12 September 1990 by the two German states and the four former occupying powers.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 12 September 1990 (✅ owner 21 §5; ✅ Wikipedia, "Two Plus Four Treaty").
- Place: Moscow (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parties: the Federal Republic of Germany and the German Democratic Republic; France, the Soviet Union, the United Kingdom and the United States (✅ Wikipedia).
- Entry into force: 15 March 1991, when the last ratification instrument was deposited (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: "controls German Federal Foreign Office chronology" (✅ owner 21 §12, citing German official records).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 12 Sept 1990; 15 Mar 1991 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-4076, WH-4078.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the treaty's signing (12 Sept 1990) with its entry into force (15 Mar 1991) or with legal reunification (3 Oct 1990).
**Mains hook:** Use to show the settlement was interstate, not only popular.
**Back:** *"Twelve September: six signatures, two Germanys, one sovereignty."*

### Flashcard WH-4078 — German reunification (3 October 1990) and the trap with 1871
**Front:** When did German reunification take legal effect, and why must it not be confused with 19th-century unification?
**Definition (learn):** Reunification of the Federal Republic and the German Democratic Republic took legal effect on 3 October 1990. Nineteenth-century unification under Prussia is a separate event.
**Breakdown:**
- Legal reunification: 3 October 1990 (✅ owner 21 §5).
- Sequence: Wall (9 Nov 1989) → Two Plus Four (12 Sept 1990) → reunification (3 Oct 1990) (✅ owner 21 §5).
- Verdict: popular pressure opened the question; interstate diplomacy settled it (⚠️ owner 21 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 3 Oct 1990 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** WH-4076, WH-4077, WH-4094 (Global South context).
**Trap:** Do not confuse 1990 reunification of FRG and GDR with the 19th-century unification under Prussia (❌ owner 21 §5). The 19th-century event is in World History basic/06.
**Mains hook:** Use the two-stage sequence to rebut both "diplomacy alone" and "street alone" errors (⚠️ owner 21 adv §3).
**Back:** *"Three October: the flag changed, the Prussia question did not come back."*

### Flashcard WH-4079 — Soviet dissolution: the stages from Baltic claims to 25 December 1991
**Front:** What was the sequence of Soviet dissolution, and what is its end date?
**Definition (learn):** Dissolution was a state process, not a single resignation. It moved from republican sovereignty claims, through a rival Russian power centre, the August 1991 coup, republican exits, and the end of the union.
**Breakdown:**
- Lithuania declared restoration of independence in March 1990 (✅ owner 21 §6).
- Yeltsin's election as leader of the Russian republic created a rival source of authority (✅ owner 21 §6).
- August 1991 failed hard-line coup (✅ owner 21 §6).
- Belovezha Accords, 8 December 1991, by Russia, Byelorussia and Ukraine (✅ Wikipedia, "Dissolution of the Soviet Union").
- Alma-Ata Protocol, 21 December 1991, eight more republics joined (✅ Wikipedia).
- Soviet of the Republics formally dissolved the state on 26 December 1991 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Gorbachev resigned 25 December 1991, handing power including nuclear codes to Yeltsin (✅ owner 21 §6; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** March 1990; August 1991; 8 Dec 1991; 21 Dec 1991; 25 Dec 1991; 26 Dec 1991 (✅ owner 21 and Wikipedia).
**Links:** WH-4080, WH-4081, WH-4082.
**Trap:** Do not merge the Berlin Wall, Two Plus Four, reunification and Soviet dissolution into one event (❌ owner 21 §14.11).
**Mains hook:** "Explain how the Soviet Union dissolved." Structure: stagnation → republics → Yeltsin → coup → exits → 25 December.
**Back:** *"March to Baltic, August to coup, December to the end — eight, twenty-one, twenty-five."*

### Flashcard WH-4080 — The failed August 1991 coup: it accelerated dissolution
**Front:** What did the failed hard-line coup of August 1991 do to the Soviet order?
**Definition (learn):** The coup failed, and it accelerated the collapse of union and party authority and the republican exits. Coercive restoration destroyed the authority it sought to save.
**Breakdown:**
- Failed hard-line coup (✅ owner 21 §5, §14.5).
- Destroyed much of the CPSU's remaining legitimacy (✅ owner 21 §6).
- Accelerated republican exits (✅ owner 21 §6).
- Wikipedia: the page refers to it as the "failed 1991 August coup"; start date and duration not confirmed in the extract (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** August 1991 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** WH-4079, WH-4073.
**Trap:** The coup did not prevent dissolution; it accelerated it (✅ owner 21 §11). Do not omit it from dissolution dynamics (❌ owner 21 §14.11).
**Mains hook:** Coercive restoration can destroy legitimacy and accelerate exit.
**Back:** *"The coup came to save the union and sped up its funeral."*

### Flashcard WH-4081 — Fifteen union republics: the USSR did not simply become Russia
**Front:** How many union republics made up the USSR, and what did Russia inherit?
**Definition (learn):** The USSR was a federation of fifteen union republics. Each could become a vehicle for sovereignty once central control weakened. Russia inherited only part of the former union's identity and authority.
**Breakdown:**
- Fifteen union republics (✅ owner 21 §6; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Federal institutions became state-building platforms (✅ owner 21 §14.5).
- Russia inherited part of the authority (✅ owner 21 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 15 republics (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** WH-4079, WH-4082.
**Trap:** "The USSR simply became Russia" is wrong (❌ owner 21 §11).
**Mains hook:** Use to answer any redrawing-of-boundaries question: successor states multiplied.
**Back:** *"Fifteen flags, not one: Russia was the largest heir, not the whole estate."*

### Flashcard WH-4082 — Post-Soviet Russia: shock therapy and Putin's restoration
**Front:** What does the owner say about Russia after 1991?
**Definition (learn):** Yeltsin favoured much faster market change and a looser union. Post-1991 reform produced inflation, unemployment, oligarchic wealth and social misery ("shock therapy"). The Putin era restored state authority more effectively than liberal credibility.
**Breakdown:**
- Yeltsin: faster market change; looser union (✅ owner 21 adv §2).
- Shock therapy outcomes: inflation, unemployment, oligarchs, social misery (✅ owner 21 adv §2).
- Putin era: state authority restored more than liberal credibility (✅ owner 21 adv §2).
- Collapse of communism did not automatically create healthy democracy or fair market (⚠️ owner 21 adv §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner.
**Links:** WH-4079, WH-4081, WH-4098.
**Trap:** "USSR breakup equals democratic success" is wrong; the 1990s Russian experience was turbulent (❌ owner 21 adv §10).
**Mains hook:** Post-Soviet Russia: Yeltsin chaos → oligarchs and shock therapy → Putin restoration → balanced judgement (✅ owner 21 adv §11).
**Back:** *"Yeltsin's shock, the oligarch's wealth, Putin's order — freedom came with a bill."*

### Flashcard WH-4083 — UN Security Council Resolution 678 (29 November 1990)
**Front:** What did Resolution 678 authorise, and when?
**Definition (learn):** On 29 November 1990, Resolution 678 authorised member states cooperating with Kuwait to use "all necessary means" if Iraq did not withdraw by the deadline.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 29 November 1990 (✅ owner 21 mini-timeline).
- Content: all necessary means if Iraq does not withdraw (✅ owner 21 mini-timeline).
- Category: Council-authorised coalition enforcement (✅ owner 21 §7, §12 "UN Resolution 678 controls the Gulf legal category").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 29 Nov 1990 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** WH-4084, WH-4085, WH-4086.
**Trap:** Do not call the Gulf War ordinary UN peacekeeping (❌ owner 21 §14.11).
